The Things That Carry Us

The Things That Carry Us

The Short Measure

1.1 Breakfast at the Ninth Weigh House

The fish had come off the wrong boat, and everyone at breakfast knew it except the woman who had bought it.

“It is the same fish,” said Adesuwa.

Her sister turned the head on the edge of the platter until its one remaining eye looked at her.

“It cannot be the same fish. We ate that fish yesterday.”

“The same kind.”

“The kind that comes off a boat nobody asked for.”

Across the courtyard, two boys carrying a shutter stopped to listen. Adesuwa pointed at the empty opening in the store wall. They carried on. One had plaster in his hair and the other had none on him anywhere, which told her how the morning had gone without requiring an inquiry.

The porridge was thick enough to stand against the side of the bowl. Her sister had put a thumb of ground pepper on it and left Adesuwa to stir it in herself. Beyond the courtyard wall the first porters were passing toward the canal. Their empty carrying frames knocked when they walked abreast; a loaded procession went more quietly. Somewhere a woman was calling for a child who had already been called twice. The third summons included the name of the child’s father, who was dead and could do very little about it.

Adesuwa drew the platter nearer.

“You asked me to buy fish.”

“I asked you to collect the fish from the blue awning.”

“The blue awning had fallen down.”

“So you bought fish from a stranger.”

“He was underneath it.”

Her sister looked away. For a moment Adesuwa thought she had won. Then her sister began to laugh, without sound, leaning on the table until the platter moved.

“You bought the awning-mender’s dinner.”

“He sold it readily enough.”

“He has six children. He could have sold you two of those if you had stood still.”

One of the boys laughed. Adesuwa put another piece of fish in her bowl and chose not to hear him.

Her sister’s name was Dede. In the district where they had been children she was still spoken of as Adesuwa’s little sister, because she had once been small and the people who decided such things had not revised their opinions. She now had a household of eleven, a dead husband who remained useful in arguments, and a voice that carried across a canal without effort. Adesuwa rented two rooms from her and was frequently accused of having married the Ninth Weigh-House instead of anybody with a bed.

The boys set the shutter down. Dede rose to inspect it.

They were renewing the store’s outer coat before the rains. The old surface had been wetted and scraped where it had lifted; yesterday the younger children had been set to picking fibres out of the chopped straw, and had made a competition of finding things that were not straw. Half a buckle, a dried lizard, and a piece of blue thread were arranged on a brick beside the doorway. Adesuwa had forbidden the lizard at the breakfast table. It had subsequently appeared beside her pillow.

“Too much sand,” Dede said.

The clean boy began an explanation.

“Show me,” she said.

He put his fingers into the mixture. She watched him work it and waited. He added water without looking at her.

Adesuwa ate the rest of the fish. The canal outside the wall smelled warm already, the wet smell mixed with grain dust from the warehouses. A white bird stood on the far parapet, lifting one foot from the sun-heated coping. She could see the Granary Tower above the roofs. Its eastern face was still in shadow. By the time the shadow reached the public steps she was supposed to have heard three appeals, examined a damaged beam, and decided whether her youngest clerk had really lost his voice or simply disliked reading the correction book aloud.

Dede came back with plaster on her wrist.

“The workers eat here at noon,” she said.

“I am at the house.”

“Your mouth does not have to be here for your share to be.”

“I gave you coin yesterday.”

“For the sand.”

“And the day before.”

“For the straw.”

Adesuwa reached for the small purse inside her sash. Dede held out her hand before she had found it.

“You could ask the men who own the store to feed the men who coat it.”

“I did. You are the last owner still pretending this is a conversation.”

Adesuwa gave her the coin. The store served six households. Two families had brought baskets of millet, one had supplied the sand, and the potter whose back wall joined it had lent the boys. Her own share was the upper room in which she kept dry-season grain against a year’s salary paid late. She had never needed to open it for that purpose. Dede borrowed from it regularly, always announcing the loan in front of somebody and returning grain from a different harvest, which Adesuwa then had to examine while her sister stood nearby making remarks about sisters who inspected sisters.

At the courtyard gate a porter cleared his throat.

He was a broad man with a carrying pad hanging from his neck. The pad had been mended in pale cloth, fresh against the old brown. Behind him stood a taller man holding a shallow wooden box against his chest.

“The Ninth House?” the porter asked.

“Across the lane,” Adesuwa said.

“They sent us here.”

Dede looked at the sun.

“Because it is open and she is not in it.”

The tall man’s eyes moved from Dede’s hand to the coin on the table, then to the plaster. His clothes were clean at the shoulders and grey below the knee. He had the look of somebody who had washed what could be washed without taking anything off.

“Inspector Adesuwa?”

She put down her spoon.

“Let me finish.”

“We have waited three days.”

“Then you have come at a good time. I have three mouthfuls.”

Dede brought two stools out of the shade. The porter took one. The tall man remained standing until she set the other directly behind him and said that standing guests made her workers think they were not allowed to rest.

He sat.

Adesuwa finished her porridge. The last mouthful was mostly pepper. She swallowed, drank, and found the man watching the wooden box rather than her.

“What district?”

“Tembe Reach.”

She knew the name. Everyone in the Ninth knew Tembe grain: a small pale millet that ran well off a scoop and fooled new hands by settling lower in the vessel than they expected. It had been a heavy contribution in the wet season before this one. She remembered a string of barges with red mats over the holds, but could not immediately remember which receiving house had taken them.

“And you are?”

“Belaku, speaking for the lower stores.”

“All of them?”

“Those who sent me.”

“That is what I am asking.”

“Five.”

He named them. He had a deliberate speaking voice, each household given enough space to be heard separately. The porter shifted on the stool. He knew the names and was waiting for a later part.

Adesuwa wiped her hands before accepting the box.

Inside lay two folded papers and a round wooden measure, small enough to lift in one hand. Its rim was bound with brass. Under the handle somebody had carved three short lines. She turned it toward the light. The bottom had been repaired, properly, with a fitted piece of the same wood. There was no obvious distortion.

She unfolded the first paper. The contribution named was familiar now: nine barges, received in the month when the east lock had been under repair. A licensed receiving house had entered the delivered quantity, allowed the ordinary wet-season adjustment and passed the record through the Ninth for certification.

Her stamp stood beside the clerk’s mark.

The second paper refused a request for relief. The district was in default of its contribution.

“How much have you in store?” she asked.

Belaku did not answer at once.

“Enough to feed the district for how long?”

“You have the request.”

“Requests are written to persuade.”

“It was written to obtain grain.”

“Which is why I ask.”

“Eight days if the people away cutting the canal banks do not come home. Five if they do. They are coming home.”

Dede had stopped listening with the air of a woman respecting official business, but Adesuwa could tell from the stillness of her hands that she had heard. The clean boy came to ask about the second mixing pit. She sent him back with a gesture.

Adesuwa compared the two dates.

“The refusal has probably been entered against the wrong season.”

“We took it to the receiving house.”

“Yes, but this was issued from—”

“They said the same thing. Then they sent us to the water office. The water office sent us to the Ninth. Your clerk sent us back to the receiving house. Yesterday the receiving house told us to wait for you.”

“My clerk should have kept the papers.”

“He kept the first copies.”

There was anger now, held low. Adesuwa found herself disliking the effort it required her not to answer it. She had not been the person who sent him around Mbaro. She had been across the western canal inspecting a split drying floor while her clerk pretended to have no voice.

“Bring these with you,” she said. “I will have the original opened.”

Belaku did not take the box.

“Tell him first.”

He indicated the porter.

“What?”

“What is wrong.”

“I haven’t established what is wrong.”

“You said the season was wrong.”

“I said probably.”

The porter looked at her with an expression she could not read. The pale patch on his carrying pad lay over his heart. One edge had begun to lift; he kept pressing it down with his thumb.

“I carry for the Five Steps,” he said. “We are coating our store before the rain. The people who cut the straw were to be fed from what came back with this man. They are working now.”

Adesuwa looked past him at Dede’s open store.

“Your work is paid in meals?”

“And help when it is their turn. The meals were promised. They have children who eat before they come. Now we have asked them to bring their own.”

“Who is issuing the remaining grain?”

“My wife. She is better at it than I am.”

He smiled a little, without asking her to share it.

“She wants to know what to tell them.”

Adesuwa set the refusal on top of the contribution. The paper edges did not match. The first clerk had cut his sheet square; the second had used a narrow strip torn from a larger document. There was enough room for the refusal and the required titles, and none for an explanation.

“Tell them I am examining both entries today.”

“We leave at noon,” the porter said.

“Then tell them that. I cannot give you an answer I have not found.”

He nodded. He seemed neither grateful nor offended. It troubled her more than Belaku’s anger.

She rose. Dede brought the clean cloth Adesuwa wore over her shoulder when she went to the house. The brass stamp was in its pouch beside the doorway, where she had put it the evening before. She checked the seal on the pouch before lifting it. Dede watched her do that every morning and had never once refrained from looking amused.

“Take the fish,” her sister said to the porter.

“We ate.”

“Then you will not be eating it hungry.”

She wrapped what remained in a leaf and pushed it into his hand. Belaku began to object and found himself holding the empty bowls instead. Dede had a way of making an offered kindness part of the work already underway. By the time he had put the bowls beside the washing basin, the porter had thanked her.

At the gate Adesuwa paused.

“The measure stays with me while we compare it.”

Belaku tightened his hold on the box.

“It belongs to Five Steps.”

“Yes.”

“It was my grandfather’s.”

“Then I will take care of it.”

“I would like that written.”

She almost told him that her custody required no additional paper. Then she remembered how many offices he had visited with her stamp already in his box.

“Across the lane,” she said. “You can watch me write it.”

The Ninth Weigh-House had three doors. The large central opening admitted loads and remained barred until the morning weights had been checked. The narrow door to its left was for people with disputes. The one on the right led into a little court with a water jar and an old bench, where people who had finished disputing often sat longer than those still waiting. Adesuwa entered there, put the box on the bench, and went to find her clerk.

He was reciting a love song to the woman who swept the floors. His voice was excellent.

She waited until he saw her.

“Good,” she said. “You can read corrections today.”

Behind her, for the first time, Belaku laughed.

1.2 Two Honest Measures

The receiving house lay below the old east lock, on a bend where barges had to be poled away from the bank even in a gentle current. Its outer wall had been rubbed smooth by shoulders. A man on the roof was pulling drying mats up through a hatch, swearing each time the rolled end caught.

Adesuwa arrived with Belaku, the box and a clerk named Eru who had recovered his health sufficiently to complain about the walk.

“You would complain about being carried,” she told him.

“Only if the carrier stopped.”

“Then keep up.”

She had left the Ninth under her deputy and sent the porter home with a written undertaking to report by the following evening. That was sooner than she would ordinarily have committed herself. Dede had given the porter another parcel. Adesuwa had not asked what was in it.

At the receiving house, the master measurer was eating standing up. She recognized him from the wet-season inspections: short, round at the waist, with a scar running through one eyebrow and a habit of looking at a person’s hands before their face. He looked at hers now, saw the stamp pouch and put his bowl on a ledge.

“If you have come about the floor, I sent it.”

“I have come about Tembe Reach.”

He did not look at Belaku.

“We answered that.”

“I would like to see what you answered.”

“The office of allocation—”

“Your receiving record first.”

He wiped his hands carefully. It was not reluctance to touch the paper dirty. She had worked with him before. He was giving himself time to decide how much trouble this was likely to be.

“Eru,” she said, “ask for a tray of the retained grain.”

“We have no retained grain from that season.”

“Then a current lot of the same district. And the house measure. And a level board.”

The measurer watched her.

“You think I took it.”

“I think you should finish your breakfast while somebody brings what I asked for.”

The retained grain had been planted out the previous season. That was normal; no house kept sacks merely to prove that old grain had once been sound. A sample strip sewn into the receiving record held a few dry grains, too few to measure and sufficient to identify the crop. Eru brought it to her between his palms as if he had unearthed something precious.

Belaku leaned close.

“That is ours.”

“You cannot know that from six grains,” the measurer said.

“I know the kind.”

“We receive that kind from three districts.”

“Then it is a pity you have lost all three.”

The man put his breakfast aside again.

Adesuwa placed the record flat between them.

“We will do this without performing it for each other.”

“I have been doing it for three days without anyone looking,” Belaku said.

The measuring court had shade until noon. They took a table to the end nearest the wall and set the district vessel beside the receiving-house vessel. The latter was new compared with Belaku’s, turned in pale wood and marked at the handle. Its rim showed a small knock. Adesuwa ran her thumb along it and asked when it had happened.

“Last week.”

“Witness?”

A woman carrying a winnowing tray raised her hand without slowing.

“I dropped it. Ask me about it while I have another thing to drop.”

“The vessel used for Tembe?”

“Inside. Red cord on the handle.”

The old one came out. Eru put the two house vessels together. They matched in height and were slightly narrower than the district measure, which ought to have worried a stranger and did not worry Adesuwa. Shape meant very little until the capacities were compared. A wide, shallow vessel made levelling awkward but emptied cleanly. A deep one could be held against the hip. Houses argued about which was better with the passion other people reserved for marriages.

She had Eru fill the district measure from the tray, strike it level and pour it into the old house vessel. The grain came below the rim. He frowned.

“Again,” she said.

He repeated it. Same result.

Belaku’s face changed.

“Now do it as you ordinarily would,” she told him.

He took the measure, scooped low, let the grain fall into it, struck it once with the flat of the board and emptied it. The house vessel filled level.

Eru flushed.

“You settled it.”

“I filled it.”

“You shook it.”

“The grain falls into the corners. If you pour it from above like a man feeding birds, there is air under it.”

Adesuwa let them argue long enough for the winnowing woman to return. She took both vessels, performed the comparison without comment, and set them down beside Eru.

“There,” she said. “Now marry them.”

The house measurer smiled despite himself.

Adesuwa marked the result. The measures agreed. The original receiving practice, described by the man and demonstrated by two workers, agreed with Belaku’s. The repaired bottom of the district vessel held no hidden space. Its brass rim was sound. She had the scales brought out as well and tested the house weights against the set she had borrowed from the Ninth. Eru read each number aloud.

Men and women worked around them. Grain ran over wicker, dusty in the shafts of sun. A girl with a baby tied high on her back took filled bowls from one stack to another without changing her stride. The child slept with its mouth against the cloth. When the girl stopped to drink, the child woke and began to complain; she told it that there had been ample time to complain while they were working, and that this was hers.

Adesuwa found herself hungry again.

They ate at the edge of the court from food the house supplied to everyone on a full shift. Belaku accepted a bowl and sat apart, facing the water. Eru tried to follow him. Adesuwa touched the clerk’s sleeve.

“Let him eat.”

“I was going to apologize.”

“After he eats.”

The measurer sat beside her. His name was Soro. She had remembered it halfway through the tests and been irritated with herself for not remembering sooner.

“The load was wet,” he said.

“The record says so.”

“Not soaked. Harvest damp. They brought it under good mats. We dried it on the upper floor. Lost less than we thought we would.”

“Who made the allowance?”

“I did.”

“Under whose instruction?”

“The standing instruction.”

“Have you it?”

He swallowed before answering.

“Yes.”

She set her bowl down.

“Bring that one. And anything that replaced it.”

He looked at the doorway of the office. A cloth had been hung across it against the dust. Through a worn place in the weave she could see somebody moving.

“I told the allocation clerk the original figure stood.”

“What did they say?”

“That the original figure could stand where it was.”

“And?”

“That what it bought was not your business or mine.”

The replacement instruction was dated four months after Tembe’s delivery.

Adesuwa read it twice. It required all districts applying for current relief to have their latest contribution expressed in dry grain at the new standard allowance. The existing contribution remained entered. Beside it, a revised figure determined whether the district had met its share.

Tembe’s old allowance had recognized the dampness of a harvest brought in during a difficult season. The new allowance recognized less. Applied to grain that had already been received, dried and distributed, it made part of that contribution disappear from the claim without removing a grain from the floor.

“Who sent this?” she asked.

“The eastern allocations office. Vermilion authority.”

“Signed?”

“On the copy inside.”

The woman behind the cloth came out carrying a bundle secured in two places. She was young enough that Adesuwa had first mistaken her for Soro’s daughter, and dressed with considerably more care than anyone else in the court. A blue border showed at her wrist beneath a work sleeve. There was a smear of flour on her cheek.

“It came the day my future husband’s sisters arrived,” she said.

Soro shut his eyes briefly.

“Must the sisters come into everything?”

“If you had carried the instruction yourself they would not.”

She put the bundle down and untied it.

“They came to look at the courtyard,” she told Adesuwa. “For the wedding. There were four of them and the oldest was determined to find something too small. The courtyard was not too small, so she tried the oven. While she had her head inside it, this man sent me for the revised sheets.”

“You were working.”

“So was she. At finding fault.”

Eru grinned. The woman saw him and smiled back, then moved the wedding cloth away from the table edge before putting her ink-stained hand on it.

“Here. I copied it. I asked whether we were to alter the receiving roll. He said no. I asked whether the new figure went in the margin. He said no. A separate sheet, for allocation only. I remember because I had to cut paper that should have been used for the invitations.”

Belaku had come back without anyone calling him.

He listened while she named the man who brought the instruction. He asked whether that man was still at the office. She did not know. She could show the signature, which was not his. The signature belonged to Warden Yedru, directing eastern allocations on behalf of the Widow’s house.

Adesuwa knew that name too. Yedru had kept three districts fed through a broken lock two years before and made herself so unpopular in the process that the districts subsequently disagreed over which owed her the most.

Eru took the allocation sheet from Belaku’s box and laid it beside the office copy. Their figures matched.

Then he looked at Adesuwa.

She saw her stamp before he pointed to it.

The application of the revised allowance had passed through the Ninth. Eru had not been the clerk who checked it. Her deputy had not been the inspector. She remembered the evening now: a late boat, a queue of allocations bundled together, the water office asking whether the completed lot could go back before the lock closed. She had tested the addition and compared the authorized standard. Both had been right.

The clerk’s young face had gone still in an effort not to show her what he was thinking.

“Read it,” she said.

He read the wording beneath her stamp. Verification of measure and computation. No finding on the power to impose the allowance. No explicit finding on the district’s relief claim. The refusal had followed afterward, with her verified number attached.

Belaku put his hands flat on the table.

“So you knew.”

“I knew the calculation had been made under an instruction.”

“You saw our name.”

“Yes.”

“You saw the old contribution.”

“Yes.”

“And the new one.”

“Yes.”

He took his hands away. The tabletop had left dust in the lines of his palms. He brushed them against each other, carefully, as if he had touched something he did not wish to carry.

Soro began to speak. Adesuwa stopped him.

“The grain came in,” she said to Belaku. “It was dried. It went out. The vessels are right. The receiving count is right. I do not think anyone at this house stole from you.”

He looked at the six grains sewn into the record.

“Where did it go?”

“We can find the dispatches.”

“Will that bring it back?”

“No.”

The young woman had gathered the wedding cloth against her waist. Eru was holding a pen and doing nothing with it. Outside, the man on the roof had finally freed the last mat from the hatch. It came down with a scrape, and someone below applauded.

Belaku lifted the district measure from the table.

“Then we have spent the day proving we did what we said.”

“It needed proving.”

“To you.”

He set the vessel back in its box. Adesuwa did not stop him. She had accepted it into custody, but there was no reason to carry it any farther that day, and she did not want the first exercise of her authority after this discovery to be over his grandfather’s wood.

The clerk with the wedding cloth brought a clean wrapper for the papers. Belaku thanked her. It was the first time he had spoken gently since breakfast.

Adesuwa asked permission to copy the original instruction and the replacement. Soro gave it readily. She made Eru copy while she remained beside him and checked each line. Belaku waited at the water steps.

The sun had moved across the court. Dust on the damp part of the floor had become a skin under their feet. When Eru finished, the young woman offered him water and asked whether his beautiful handwriting extended to wedding invitations.

“It can,” he said.

“Then I will speak to your inspector.”

“You may speak to him,” Adesuwa said. “He has a voice again.”

For a moment the girl looked confused. Eru laughed, and Adesuwa was grateful to him for it.

They went back along the canal as the roofs began to give up their heat. Belaku carried his box on the side away from her. At the crossing a barge loaded with pale sacks passed under the footbridge. Its pilot ducked without looking up; he knew the height. A little boy on the stern lay on his belly and trailed a strip of reed in the water.

Adesuwa stopped him with a call.

“Pilot. Which store?”

“Upper east.”

“Taking in or out?”

“Out, unless somebody has moved the harbour.”

“Relief?”

“Ships.”

The barge moved beyond the bridge. The boy lifted his reed, looked at the empty end and put it back in the water.

Belaku watched until the sacks disappeared behind a bend.

“I am going there,” he said.

“The upper reserve?”

“Now.”

“It will be closed to visitors.”

“I am not visiting.”

She let him walk three paces before she followed.

“Belaku. The warden will not be there. If the duty crew turns you away, you will spend the night outside a wall. We need her at a desk with the instruction in front of her.”

He turned on her.

“You need that. I need a boat going the other way.”

She could have told him the reserve did not release grain to anyone who arrived angry at dusk. It would have been accurate. She looked down the canal instead, at the water beyond the bend.

“I know a pilot,” she said.

He waited.

“I don’t know whether he will help. Let me ask.”

1.3 The Pilot’s Landing

The pilot had taken his boat apart for the evening.

He sat under the stern awning with one foot in a bucket, repairing the edge of a sail that was too small to have belonged to the broad vessel behind him. A girl on the bank held the other end. Between them lay a length of cloth punctured by patches of several ages. The newest patch was green.

“You cannot see it from below,” he told the girl.

“You can see it from everywhere.”

“Then it will help people recognize us.”

“They recognize you already.”

“That is a difficulty, yes.”

Adesuwa stepped down onto the landing. The pilot looked up and left the needle where it was.

“I paid.”

“I have not asked you anything.”

“You are about to.”

She had known him for seventeen years. He had delivered grain to the Ninth when she was still allowed to sit at the short desk near the door, and had once watched her chase a beetle through a certified lot for half an afternoon. He referred to that beetle as her first prisoner. He was called Koba. Whether it was his first name, last name or the name of a boat he had lost, she had never established.

“This is Belaku, from Tembe Reach.”

“Then you came through the lower cut.”

“On foot from the ferry.”

“Ah. Sensible.”

“The ferry pilot said the cut was closed.”

“The ferry pilot has a boat too wide for it.”

“Is it closed?” Adesuwa asked.

“To him.”

The girl made a little sound that might have been agreement. Koba took his foot out of the bucket and wrapped it in a cloth. It was swollen around the ankle. Adesuwa looked at it.

“A rope,” he said.

“When?”

“Every time it can.”

“Can you work?”

“I am working. You have come to disturb me.”

He stood, favouring the foot more than she would have liked. The girl folded the sail and put a stone on it. Her hair was tied close to her head, and a length of thin cord ran around one wrist where other girls wore beads.

“What do you need?” Koba asked Belaku.

“Grain.”

“Then you have brought the wrong person. She examines it. She does not grow it.”

Adesuwa told him what they had found. He listened without interrupting, which she disliked almost as much as being interrupted. When she had finished, he looked at the girl.

“Bring the lamp.”

She went aboard. The boat shifted under her without much movement. It had been built to carry far more than the sacks visible under its middle awning.

Koba pointed toward them.

“Crew reserve. Mine, my brother’s, his wife’s people. Thirty measures in the forward bins.”

“Can you sell?” Adesuwa asked.

“Not all. They eat it when a lock closes and nobody pays for waiting.”

“How much can you release?”

“Eighteen.”

Belaku inhaled as if he had been struck. It was very little against five stores. It was enough to matter.

“I have authority to buy on our account,” he said.

“Then buy twelve. Six go to the Red Steps.”

“The Red Steps have grain.”

Koba watched him.

“They had grain when they refused the lower bank work.”

“They have eaten since then.”

“So have we.”

“Then you understand the difficulty.”

Adesuwa began to calculate. Eighteen measures would provide a modest number of household meals, even before the division. It could be delivered before dawn if the cut was passable. The reserve was sound if Koba had kept it as carefully as he kept his boats. She could examine it here, record the transfer and—

“No,” Belaku said.

She looked at him.

“Do not offer my agreement while you are thinking.”

“I said nothing.”

“You were about to.”

Koba bent to retrieve the bucket. The girl came back with the lamp.

“She often is,” he said.

Belaku went up the bank. He stood beside the folded sail and looked across the canal, where a woman was brushing dust from her doorway in broad, deliberate strokes. Every few strokes she straightened and called to someone inside. She was annoyed. Adesuwa could hear it in the effort she gave the broom.

The girl set down the lamp and came to Belaku.

“My aunt lives there,” she said.

“At the Red Steps?”

“Yes.”

“Does your aunt know they would not send people to cut the banks?”

“She was sick.”

“Everyone was not sick.”

“No.”

He looked down at her. She did not offer an answer for everyone else.

“What is your name?”

“Bese.”

“Do you live on this boat?”

“Sometimes.”

“Does he pay you?”

“Sometimes.”

“Bad arrangement.”

“I know where he keeps the good tea.”

Koba called from below that he was able to hear things spoken on land. Bese returned to him, and Belaku stayed beside the sail another moment.

When he came down he asked to see the grain.

They opened the forward bins. The millet was dry, stored above the bilge in lined compartments whose seams smelled of resin. Adesuwa put her hand deep into the centre and left it there long enough to test for warmth. Koba waited. He did not complain about the time she took now. Eru, who had followed them in the hope that the errand would end before supper, fetched the measures and began preparing the transfer paper.

“Twelve to Five Steps,” Belaku said. “Six to Red Steps. Not a loan.”

“Do you want that written?” Eru asked.

“I want it understood. Write it too.”

Koba nodded.

“You will come?”

Belaku looked at Adesuwa before answering.

“Yes.”

“Both of you?”

She had intended to send the clerk. The papers for Yedru were still to be put in order, and she had promised the western store that she would return to examine its floor supports at first light. Eru could write a distribution record. Koba knew the route.

Belaku lifted the empty box out of the way so Bese could pass with the first bowl.

“I will come,” Adesuwa said.

She sent Eru to the Ninth with copies and instructions. He was to notify her deputy, arrange another inspector for the floor and take a note to Dede. He opened his mouth at that final errand, thought better of it and closed it again.

“Tell her I am on the water,” Adesuwa said.

“Yes, Inspector.”

“And not to wait with food.”

“Yes, Inspector.”

“And Eru—”

“She will ask if you have a shawl. Do you have a shawl?”

Adesuwa looked at her bare forearms. Bese went under the awning and came back with one that smelled faintly of fish smoke.

“She has a shawl,” the girl told him.

They left on the ebb.

The crew was Koba, his brother’s grown son and Bese, who had the useful position of being expected to know everything and being able to claim she had not been told anything. She took the bow rope ashore when needed, carried messages, made tea, and twice corrected her cousin’s choice of pole without speaking. Adesuwa sat on a rolled mat near the stern with Belaku opposite her. The water slid past the hull close enough for her to touch.

At first there were lights on both banks. People called down from the landings. A man with a tray of fried cakes walked along beside them until a wall prevented it, still offering a price at which he claimed to be ruining himself. Koba bought six by holding the boat against a post while Bese climbed up. She returned with seven and a look that discouraged questions.

Belaku ate one. After a while he ate another.

The canal narrowed beyond the last brick landing. Here the banks were supported by roots and cut poles. Adesuwa could feel the change in the boat’s movement before she saw it. Koba steered more often, without appearing to do much. His nephew used a pole from the forward deck. Bese watched the bank and called quietly when they approached one of the low places where a towpath had slipped.

“The checkpoint is on the main water,” Koba said.

“I know.”

“I tell passengers. Some become offended when they discover where they have been.”

“Is this channel licensed?”

“For boats of this draught, in daylight.”

Adesuwa looked at the lamp.

“Then we are outside the licence.”

“Sun went down. It does that.”

Belaku made a noise in his throat. She could not decide if he was laughing.

“What prevents you using it after dark?”

“The fallen banks. People who think a pole is for leaning on. Men too proud to ask a girl what she can see.”

“And the law.”

“That also.”

She considered ordering him back. The main channel would take them past a duty crew who could require a transit authorization for grain removed from a registered reserve. Her stamp would establish the grain’s condition, not her power to divert its route. She had enough authority to hold them there until morning and none to make the sun rise earlier.

“Record the passage,” she said.

Koba glanced at her.

“In the boat book. With my name.”

He called to Bese, who said that she would do it when she had a dry hand.

They passed beneath a leaning tree. Its leaves brushed the awning with a sound like someone running their hands across loose paper. Beyond it a small flame burned low on a bank shrine. A boatman had left food there, or a family whose child worked the channel. Adesuwa could not tell from the boat. She had brought nothing appropriate and did not borrow an offering from somebody else’s freight. Koba touched the end of his pole to the water and lifted it without a word.

The Red Steps were red only where the lamp struck the old clay facing. Elsewhere water had darkened them almost black. A boy sleeping beside the landing woke as Bese jumped ashore and ran up toward the houses. Koba told his nephew to catch the lower post. The young man reached for it, hooked the rope and drew it tight.

The post moved.

“Let go,” Koba said.

The nephew held for another instant. It was enough for the whole rotten base to turn in the bank. Adesuwa put her hand out, absurdly, as if she might keep it upright from three paces away. The rope came loose. The stern swung. Bese caught the upper line against a second post and sat down hard on the steps with both feet braced.

“Pay out,” Koba told her.

She did, a little at a time. The boat turned until it lay crooked against the landing. Koba’s nephew stood with a coil in his hands, looking sick.

“I said let go,” Koba told him.

“I thought—”

“You thought wrong. Now bring the fender.”

The grain was unhurt. The boat had scraped the lowest step and caught under a projecting stone; they could not move it safely until the water changed or the obstruction was cleared. Koba examined the side with a lamp held low, swore at the landing, and asked whose beautiful idea it had been to neglect that post.

People were coming down from the houses. They had put on clothes hastily, wrapping cloth over sleeping shifts and fastening belts as they walked. An older woman carried a covered bowl because she had been eating when called and saw no reason to stop.

Belaku stepped ashore with the transfer paper.

“Six measures,” he said. “From Tembe Reach.”

Koba cleared his throat.

“Purchased from Koba’s crew reserve,” Belaku added.

The older woman uncovered her bowl, looked at it and gave it to the boy who had been sleeping.

“Who agreed to this?” she asked.

“I did.”

“For the five stores?”

“For the share I was empowered to obtain.”

“Then we will hear you in the morning.”

“You can hear me now.”

“We can receive grain now. I am not waking every household to decide whether to admire you.”

Bese turned her face away. Koba made no attempt to conceal his amusement.

The unloading nearly became a fight anyway. One man wanted the six measures kept together in the upper store; a woman beside him said the upper store was the reason she had no grain in her own house. Another argued that the largest households should receive first. Belaku began to name the conditions under which he would accept distribution and found himself interrupted by Bese, who was trying to get past him with a carrying bowl.

“Your feet,” she said.

He moved.

Koba’s nephew put the next bowl into the hands of the woman who had challenged the upper store. Bese took another to the man beside her. They began carrying side by side because there was only one clear way up the steps. A girl with a scarred chin fetched more bowls. Within a few minutes everyone who had been arguing was holding something that had to be carried somewhere before they could resume.

Adesuwa went up with them.

The upper store was a room attached to a dwelling, dry and swept, with a row of small containers already waiting. The woman with the bowl introduced herself as its keeper. She knew the number of people in each household. Adesuwa asked how it had been established, listened, and found nothing to contest. The woman then asked why a Ninth House inspector was here after dark with grain from Tembe when the district had been declared in default.

Adesuwa explained the two allowances.

A young man near the door said, “So the Widow has given the grain back.”

“No,” she said. “This grain was bought by Tembe.”

“Through her inspector.”

“Her inspector is here to verify the transfer.”

He nodded, not hearing the distinction she wanted him to hear. The keeper did hear it. She looked at Belaku, then at Adesuwa’s pouch.

“You should say that outside,” she said.

Adesuwa went back to the doorway and said it again, loudly enough for the people in the yard. She named the purchase, the division and her own limited part. A woman asked whether more would come tomorrow.

“I cannot promise it.”

“Then why have you come?”

“To see what this delivery does.”

The woman held up a bowl.

“It does this.”

She took it into her house.

They stayed until the water rose enough to free the boat.

Koba and his nephew worked at the projecting stone while two men from the landing replaced the rotten post with a temporary timber. Belaku helped, though no one thanked him with particular warmth. Adesuwa found herself seated with the storekeeper under an awning, listening to the history of a failed bank repair that differed from Belaku’s in almost every emphasis.

There had been sickness. There had also been a disagreement over where the cut would put the spoil. Men sent by the upper houses had arrived late and expected the Red Steps to make up the work. Someone had insulted someone else’s dead father. The keeper admitted that last part was foolish and became angry again while explaining why it was nevertheless understandable.

“You had food then,” Adesuwa said.

“We thought we had food.”

“Was the count wrong?”

“No. We thought the boat after that would come.”

The keeper’s grandson slept against her knee. She shifted him carefully while continuing to talk. His feet were dirty, the soles pale where he had curled them away from the ground. Adesuwa thought of Dede inspecting the plaster mixture. Before she had come here she had imagined the delay in grain as a number of days. She could now hear the boy breathing through his nose, a small whistle at every fourth or fifth breath.

She stayed until the keeper had finished.

At dawn they delivered the remaining twelve measures to Five Steps. Belaku’s porter was on the bank waiting. His wife had made a place inside the half-coated store and had brought bowls from three neighbouring houses. Nobody said that it was enough. They received it, took care with it and began measuring out breakfast for the workers.

Belaku gave his account standing in the doorway. When he came to the six measures left at Red Steps, his voice slowed. One man objected. Another asked whether their people had helped with the boat. Belaku said they had.

The porter’s wife listened to the end and turned to the nearest girl.

“More water in that pot,” she said. “There will be more hands here than I counted.”

Adesuwa had expected Belaku to look relieved. He looked tired and a little ashamed. She was tired herself, but could not identify what he believed he ought to have done differently, and did not ask in front of the others.

Koba took them back to Mbaro with empty forward bins. Bese slept curled around a rolled sail. The nephew held the steering pole. His uncle lay under the awning with the swollen foot raised on a board and corrected him without opening his eyes.

Adesuwa slept badly against the side of the boat. When she woke, her cheek bore the woven pattern of the borrowed shawl. Belaku looked at her and almost smiled.

“You can tell your sister you wore it,” he said.

1.4 The Reserve

Warden Yedru received them beside a stack of grain she had refused to release.

The reserve court was larger than Adesuwa remembered. New sheds had been built along the eastern wall, their roofs still pale. Between them men were moving sacks in files of six, each line waiting until the preceding line had cleared the raised doorway. A water boy passed among them. He carried two cups and washed each one between drinkers with an economical turn of the wrist.

Yedru stood in the shade of the new roof, listening to a man with a split thumbnail. She wore the Corps’ red at her shoulder rather than across her whole body. The cloth was faded along the fold. She was older than Adesuwa by ten years or more, tall and lean, with her hair wrapped close and her left hand held flat against her thigh as if it pained her to let it hang.

“Three crews,” she told the man. “If you bring two, the second waits for the first to come back. I cannot put your boat before every other boat because you did not hire enough people.”

“The men asked more.”

“Then you should have paid more.”

He began again. She let him reach the same point and said nothing. At last he went away.

Yedru watched him go before turning to them.

“Ninth House. You were expected yesterday.”

“I was not told.”

“You were expected somewhere yesterday, I am sure.”

Adesuwa had washed at the house and changed her cloth. She had not slept. The clean skin around her eyes felt too tight.

“Tembe Reach,” she said.

“I know.”

“This is Belaku, empowered by the five lower stores.”

“I know that too.”

Belaku bowed with the full courtesy owed an officer acting for the Widow. His voice, when he spoke, had none of the boat’s intimacy.

“May the granaries remain under the Widow’s keeping. We ask correction of the contribution entered against Tembe Reach, and release under the Covenant.”

Yedru returned the courtesy without haste.

“You ask release in the amount stated?”

“Yes.”

“And your present stock?”

“Less than when we made the request.”

“Be exact.”

He gave her the count taken at dawn. She listened, then turned and led them between the stacks.

Adesuwa had expected an office. Instead they went through a long shed where the grain smell was so strong that she could taste it at the back of her throat. Some lots had tags for shipping; others carried household or district marks. Yedru stopped beside a low enclosure that held sacks showing dark at the seam.

“Not fit to move yet,” she said. “Wet through on the road. We can save most.”

“From where?”

“The northern collecting houses.”

“Then the reserve is short.”

“The reserve is drying. The people who eat from it are not drying.”

She said it without emphasis, pointing to a chalked division on the floor. Workers were opening the wet sacks into shallow trays. Adesuwa knelt, took a little grain and crushed it between her fingers. It had not heated badly yet. Another day closed up would have changed that.

“How much floor?” she asked.

“All this. The shed beyond if the rain holds.”

“Labour?”

“Paid for six days. Borrowed for three after that. Then they go home to their own harvest.”

Belaku watched the trays being filled. A worker looked up and greeted him. He seemed surprised. They exchanged names of relatives; the man had married into a household above Five Steps and was here for wages because his wife’s brother needed a new boat plank.

Yedru waited until they finished.

They went on to the shipping stacks. The marks were for three northern ports and two along the southern coast. Yedru lifted a corner of one covering mat.

“Those sail tomorrow. Payment purchased the wet grain you have just seen. The crews have eaten our bread for eleven days while the boats waited. The first captain has begun charging for the delay. If I stop him, he will charge at every harbour where he speaks about us, which will be all of them.”

“The Covenant comes first,” Belaku said.

“Yes. And next month the Covenant must buy where this month it sells.”

“You have grain.”

“I have promises arriving from six directions. You are one of them.”

Adesuwa disliked the phrase and recognized its truth in Yedru’s face. She had once kept a receiving house open for two nights because the last boat carried enough for a district that would otherwise have waited a week. The men unloading had hated her by the second dawn. She had been too tired to care whether they understood why.

They finally entered a small office at the back of the shed. Its shutters opened toward the water. On the sill lay a clay toy, a bird with one wheel missing. Yedru set it upright as she passed.

“Sit.”

They sat. Adesuwa put the two instructions on the table.

“The change was applied to accepted contributions.”

“Yes.”

“Four months after receipt, in Tembe’s case.”

“Yes.”

“The wet-season allowance was part of the terms under which those contributions were made.”

“It was part of the receiving practice. Relief must be allocated on a common measure.”

“You can impose that measure on the next contribution.”

“And what do I do with this year’s applications?”

“Honour the accepted figure.”

Yedru leaned back. Her left hand remained flat, this time on the table.

“You can test a vessel without asking where the next vessel comes from. I cannot release a reserve that way.”

“Then say the reserve is short. Do not say Tembe failed to contribute.”

Belaku looked from one woman to the other.

The shutters rattled in a small gust. Yedru rose and fixed the latch herself. It required two hands. When she returned, the skin at the base of her left thumb was white with the pressure she had used.

“The districts which accepted an emergency wet allowance received an advantage over those which dried before bringing grain,” she said. “They now seek equal access to a limited reserve. I have made them comparable.”

“They were asked to bring it early.”

“Some were.”

“Tembe was.”

Yedru looked at Belaku.

“Have you the request?”

He opened his box. The paper lay below the measure, wrapped separately. Adesuwa had not seen it before.

“Why did you not show me that?” she asked.

“You asked what we delivered.”

He handed the paper to Yedru. She read it, read it again, and laid it beside the instruction.

The original request bore the sign of a canal-mother’s deputy. It asked Tembe’s contribution to arrive before the high-water closing of the upper route and explicitly permitted the seasonal allowance. The early delivery had supplied two districts cut off during the repair of the east lock.

Yedru took up her pen.

“I can except this claim.”

Adesuwa looked at the pen.

“On what ground?”

“Specific prior undertaking. I can authorize it now.”

“And the other districts?”

“They may produce their undertakings.”

“Some were given verbally at the collecting houses.”

“Then their witnesses may attend.”

“After three days in Mbaro, or six, or however long it takes your office to send them around?”

Yedru’s face tightened.

“Inspector, you have brought me a piece of evidence. I am prepared to act on it.”

“And leave the instruction standing.”

“Yes.”

The pen made a small movement between her fingers.

“Your verification of the computation will remain. The exception will be entered separately. I need your statement that the general standard is sound.”

“The measure is sound. Its use is not.”

“You may word it as narrowly as your office requires.”

“No.”

Adesuwa said it before she looked at Belaku.

He had gone very still. His hands were on the wooden box. Yedru waited, her eyes moving to him now. Adesuwa felt a sudden wish to take the word back and give it the explanation it had not waited for.

“I will not use Tembe’s release to approve the refusal of the others,” she said.

“You were not asked to approve their refusal.”

“You know what the statement will be used for.”

Yedru set down the pen.

“Then you have your answer for today.”

“We have a right of review.”

“You do. Submit to the canal-mothers’ receiving room. They sit in four days.”

Belaku spoke for the first time since the early-delivery request had been read.

“My people have less than that.”

“I have offered an exception.”

“To her.”

“To your claim, supported by her verification.”

He closed the box. The repaired bottom of the measure knocked against its side.

“Will you keep the offer open until tomorrow?”

Yedru considered him.

“Until noon. The shipping loads close then.”

He bowed. This time the gesture was shorter.

Adesuwa gathered the papers. Yedru kept a copy of the prior request and promised that the original would be returned with the refusal if Belaku chose not to proceed. They left through the drying shed, where a woman was calling for more empty trays. A worker stepped out of Adesuwa’s way, then immediately stepped back to catch a falling sack. Millet scattered at their feet.

Outside the court Belaku stopped.

“Why did you say no?”

“Because she would have made your case the exception that proved—”

“Why did you say it before I could?”

Adesuwa folded the top paper along an existing crease. She could feel how tired her hands were.

“I should have let you answer.”

“Yes.”

“She is relying on people being too hungry to challenge her.”

“They are hungry.”

“I know.”

“You went there last night. You stayed a few hours. You have breakfast waiting when you go home.”

She looked toward the lane, angry at the fairness of that and at the way he had chosen to say it. A goat had climbed onto a cart and was eating the corner of a mat. Nobody appeared to own either the goat or the cart.

“Would you have accepted?”

“I might have asked what the words were. I might have asked what I was agreeing to. I might have taken time to think of the people who sent me.”

“I was thinking of them.”

“You were thinking of districts. I know who gets the first bowl.”

He walked away before she had found anything she could say without making it worse.

She did not follow immediately. The goat pulled a strip free. A man emerged from a doorway and shouted at it with the weary recognition of a quarrel resumed. Adesuwa stood holding the papers until he looked at her as if she might belong to the goat too.

At the Ninth, Eru had prepared everything she had asked for and two things she had not. The delivery record was entered, the night passage noted, and the original certifications copied so there would be no dispute over which she had examined. He had also found the dates of three earlier appeals about the same recalculation.

“All refused?” she asked.

“Two waiting. One withdrew.”

“Why?”

“He went home.”

“Ask who knew him.”

Eru nodded. He had the look of someone who would willingly do another hour’s work if she did not ask him to sit beside her while she thought.

“Go and eat first.”

“I have eaten.”

“Then go and make wedding invitations.”

He tried to look surprised and failed.

Alone in the office, Adesuwa put the two instructions side by side. She could get Belaku his release by writing one sentence sufficiently narrow to defend later. She could insist, later, that verification of a general standard said nothing about the time at which it might be imposed. The distinction would be visible to anyone who took the trouble to read it.

She thought of the refusal on its narrow strip of paper.

Her deputy came in carrying a bowl covered with a cloth.

“Your sister.”

“Did she say anything?”

“Several things. Eat before I give you the rest.”

Adesuwa uncovered the bowl. Porridge, vegetables and a piece of the wrong fish. She laughed once and then began eating faster than she meant to.

The deputy sat in Eru’s place. He was a careful man, narrow through the shoulders, with ink permanently caught in the nail of one forefinger. His name was Ameto, and she had trusted his work for eleven years.

“You went to Yedru,” he said.

“Yes.”

“She cannot withdraw that instruction herself.”

“She signed it.”

“She can suspend it. Withdrawing it admits she had no power to issue it. That goes higher.”

“Have the canal-mothers challenged her?”

“Some have.”

“Then why does it stand?”

He put his ink-marked finger on the old instruction.

“Because the house that can make the challenge stick has a roof full of grain and no desire to feed everybody standing under it.”

Adesuwa stopped eating.

“Which house?”

He told her.

She knew it: the House of the Long Court, one of the four whose position on the Widow’s absence had been argued in every weighing room in Mbaro. Its canal-mother had died two years before. Since then the household had spoken of continuity, patience and the danger of precipitate claims. It had said all three so frequently that Adesuwa had begun to hear them as greetings.

“What do they have?”

“A testament.”

“Rumour?”

“I witnessed it.”

She stared at him.

“You never told me.”

“You never asked whether I had witnessed a canal-mother’s testament.”

“Do not be clever with me.”

“I am being exact. You usually prefer it.”

She put down the spoon.

Ameto waited. After a little while, when she asked him what he had witnessed, he told her.

1.5 The House That Kept It

The Long Court was full of people trying to leave.

A woman beside the entrance was distributing bundles of cloth from a basket. Men collected them, returned them, asked for different ones and were sent away with the first. Farther in, two children were disputing possession of a wooden horse whose legs had been repaired with sticks. A carpenter knelt beside an open door, shaving its lower edge while people stepped over him. The household’s water carrier had put down her jars across the narrowest part of the passage and was refusing to move them until somebody moved a trunk.

Adesuwa stood with Ameto in the entrance and waited for a gap.

“You said she would be receiving.”

“She is receiving her sister’s household.”

“That is not the same thing.”

“It is why I knew she would be here.”

The woman with the cloth bundles saw Ameto and called him by a name Adesuwa had never heard.

He took off his sandals.

“Must I know why?” Adesuwa asked.

“No.”

He went to embrace the woman. They spoke quietly. She touched his shoulder, looked at Adesuwa and gave the tiniest nod toward an inner doorway.

Adesuwa followed. The little passage beyond the court smelled of wet cloth and cool earth. At its end a room opened onto a second, smaller yard, where a tree had been trained sideways to shade a bench. A woman in a dark wrapper sat on the bench cutting a strip from a length of reed. The strip was thin and even. Beside her lay a child’s damaged basket.

She finished the cut before looking up.

“Adesuwa of the Ninth. You were angry with my nephew over a beam.”

“His weight was hung off centre.”

“You were still angry after he moved it.”

“He had moved it the wrong way.”

The woman smiled.

“I am Nare.”

Adesuwa knew who she was. Nare held the Long Court for the household that had produced the late canal-mother. She was neither that woman’s heir in office nor a candidate willing to be called one. Every time someone addressed her as if authority had descended with the house, she corrected them. Every time someone asked who now spoke for the house, she answered before anyone else could.

She set down the reed.

“Ameto. You have brought work.”

“He has brought me,” Adesuwa said.

“That is how I recognized it.”

They sat. A girl brought water, looked at the broken basket and asked whether she could have it back yet. Nare told her one side would fail if she filled it now. The girl asked how much she could put in without making it fail. Nare considered this as seriously as she might have considered a disputed appointment.

“Your cloth doll and nothing wet.”

The girl accepted the condition and went away.

Adesuwa waited until the sound of her feet had ended.

“May the Widow’s keeping endure,” she said. “I have come about powers exercised in her name.”

Nare inclined her head.

“People often do.”

“The emergency allowance imposed on prior contributions. You have heard of it.”

“Yes.”

“Do you approve?”

“No.”

“What have you done?”

“Asked for its withdrawal.”

“And when that was refused?”

Nare picked up the reed again and ran its end beneath the new strip already woven into the basket.

“You came quickly to that question. Usually there are compliments about the house first.”

“Ameto says you hold your aunt’s testament.”

“I hold several of her papers.”

“One bears on the Corps’ delegated authority.”

“Yes.”

“May I see it?”

Nare looked at Ameto. He returned her look without speaking. Adesuwa had seen him face down men trying to hurry a test. The expression was the same: patient, unpromising.

“After I have put my sister’s people on their way,” Nare said.

“When?”

“When they cease finding things they cannot travel without.”

Adesuwa glanced at the inner doorway.

“How long have they been here?”

“Eight days. The last three leaving.”

Ameto laughed. Nare rose with the basket and went into the larger court.

They waited through the departure.

It was longer than Adesuwa wished and more difficult to resent while she watched it. Nare’s sister had a sick husband, two children too young to carry useful things, an older daughter who had taken responsibility for everything and disliked being helped, and a servant whose mother lived in the house and who now had to leave her. The trunks contained bedding, utensils, gifts, work, and at least one object that everyone agreed belonged to someone absent. No one could decide where to leave it safely. The water carrier acquired a clear path at last. The carpenter finished the door, which closed beautifully until a child left a sandal under it.

Adesuwa took one end of a roll of bedding when the older daughter could not lift it. The girl thanked her as if she had expected an objection. Together they carried it to the front. Ameto went past with a jar balanced against his chest, followed by a man asking him not to tilt it.

In the lane, the departure became another problem. One of the hired carts had a loose pin. The driver said it had been loose when he arrived and therefore was not his responsibility. Nare looked at him for a long time, then sent for the carpenter. The driver became suddenly interested in assisting.

By the time the last child was found, kissed and installed on the correct cart, Adesuwa had learned the names of four members of the household, heard two incompatible accounts of a marriage quarrel, and accepted a piece of fruit from the sick man because refusing it seemed unkind. He had insisted on watching the loading from a chair brought into the lane. When they lifted him to the cart, he caught her sleeve.

“You are the inspector,” he said.

“Yes.”

“My wife says the house is quiet only when there is no one left worth listening to. Tell her she is wrong.”

“I have not inspected that claim.”

He laughed until it became a cough. His wife laid a hand on his back, waited, and then scolded him for talking to strangers when he could have saved his breath for thanking the people who had packed his things.

They left in a noise of farewells that continued long after the first cart had turned the corner.

Nare stood in the gate until the lane was clear.

“Now,” she said.

The testament was kept in a chest inside a room above the old store.

Two people came with them: the household’s senior woman of stores and a man who had served as witness to the canal-mother’s last declarations. Ameto had been the other witness. Nare asked Adesuwa whether she wished anyone else present. She did, and sent a household runner to find Belaku at the Ninth.

The wait was uncomfortable. Adesuwa had not seen him since the reserve. She did not know whether he had gone home, submitted the exception without her or decided to seek another inspector. Nare brought out other documents while they waited: the late canal-mother’s instructions for a store roof, an unpaid carpenter’s claim, a list of gifts to people who had served her. Her handwriting had become uneven near the end, the downstrokes heavy where she had rested her hand.

“She tried dictating,” Nare said. “Then she accused the scribe of making her sound kind.”

“Was she?”

“To some people. Briefly.”

The senior store-woman smiled at that.

Belaku arrived carrying his box. He had washed and changed his tunic. A dark band at one cuff had been sewn on slightly crooked. Adesuwa wondered who had done it and whether he knew. He acknowledged her, greeted Nare with proper courtesy and sat in the place offered.

Nare opened the final wrapper.

The document was longer than Adesuwa had expected. It began with provisions for the house and the stores under the canal-mother’s keeping, proceeded to instructions concerning obligations incurred in her office, and only then addressed the Corps. The contested passage described the limit placed on an emergency delegation made during the Widow’s withdrawal from public attendance. Renewal had required the canal-mothers’ assent after the named term. The late holder declared that no such assent had been sought from her and directed her house to challenge orders purporting to continue the delegation beyond its limit.

Attached was her retained copy of the original delegation.

Adesuwa read the limit. She read the renewal clause. She checked the marks of the parties and the dates on the attached copy. It was not a dying woman’s dislike of an officer. The missing assent could be tested against the thing those officers said had been granted to them.

Ameto identified the mark he had witnessed. The other witness did the same. Neither supplied an opinion on what should now happen.

“Has Yedru seen this?” Adesuwa asked.

“The passage was sent to her office.”

“With the attachment?”

“No.”

“Why?”

Nare’s expression did not change.

“Because sending the attachment would have put my aunt’s retained original in an office relying on there being no challenge.”

“A copy.”

“A copy has been disputed.”

“Then produce the original publicly.”

“And who governs tomorrow?”

“The canal-mothers retain their authority.”

“They retain several opinions about their authority. One wants all emergency assignments returned to the districts. One wants the Corps renewed under a different signature. One wants a declaration of vacancy before anything else. Another will agree to any arrangement that moves the northern shipping queue ahead of the eastern relief queue.”

Belaku spoke.

“You have grain.”

“Yes.”

“And floor.”

“Yes.”

“Then tomorrow is less frightening here.”

Nare looked at him without the courtesy she had given the inspector.

“Twenty-six households use our floor. Eleven bring grain; fifteen receive it. I can tell you the names. If I close the existing authorization and a new one does not follow, I feed them from the house. For a while. After that I can entertain them with accounts of my courage.”

“Tembe has already reached after that.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

The store-woman shifted in her chair. Adesuwa could see her preparing to intervene on Nare’s behalf. Nare stopped her by laying one finger on the wrapper.

“Tell me,” she said to Belaku.

He did.

He began with the early request, then the boats, then the people who had brought in grain before it was as dry as they would have liked because two districts were cut off. He gave the amount delivered and the accepted allowance. After that he spoke of the five stores separately. At one, the roof was open for work that could not stop halfway. At another, the keeper had begun issuing against the next day’s portion. A family in the third had sent a girl to relatives across the water because those relatives still had something to put in a bowl.

He did not raise his voice. He had spoken more angrily to Adesuwa about a clerk’s delay. Here he named people, and sometimes paused to remember a younger child’s name before going on.

Nare listened. The store-woman asked a question about the household with the open roof. Belaku answered. She knew the keeper’s sister. For a while they spoke across the room of a woman Adesuwa had never met, who had injured her hand while cutting reeds and would insist she could still do everything she had done before.

When they returned to the document, the light had moved off the chest.

“You do not need to declare the whole country ungoverned,” Adesuwa said. “You need to challenge an instruction issued under authority whose continuation you dispute.”

“It will be called the first.”

“Then name the second precisely. Keep the ordinary receiving practice. Keep the crews. Keep contributions recognized. Put the contested distribution under named canal-mothers until the broader claim is heard.”

“Named by whom?”

“Those districts willing to undertake the work.”

Nare looked at Belaku.

“And if yours receives first?”

“According to need.”

“It is easy to say.”

“Yes,” he said. “Last night it was harder to do.”

Adesuwa told her about Red Steps. Belaku did not interrupt to improve his part in the account. Nare asked where the grain had come from, how much remained with the crew, and whether the transit had been authorized.

“No,” Adesuwa said. “I certified sound grain and recorded the passage. I did not have authority to permit the night route.”

“Then you have made it easier to call the whole thing irregular.”

“Yes.”

“Are you asking me to join that?”

“I am asking you to help move the next load properly.”

A knock came at the door before Nare answered. The household runner stood outside with a man wearing a small Vermilion badge. He asked for Adesuwa by office and full name, then presented a notice requiring an account of grain removed from a registered crew reserve and carried through the lower cut after dark. The pilot’s licence was held pending inquiry. The boat was to remain at its berth.

Belaku stood.

“His ankle is hurt. He needs the boat to work.”

The messenger waited. He had not been sent to discuss an ankle.

Adesuwa accepted the notice.

“Tell the duty office I will attend before the second morning bell. The grain was sound. The delivery is recorded. The route was taken with my knowledge.”

“Your stamp is required until the inquiry concludes.”

She looked at the line he indicated. It authorized temporary custody of the instrument while the scope of its use was examined. Standard wording. She had seen it used after a stamp had been stolen, after a measurer had been caught accepting a payment, and once after an inspector’s sons used hers to certify a boatload of stones.

Belaku looked at her hand.

She undid the pouch. The brass was warm from being held against her body. She set it on the open paper while the messenger wrote the time and place of receipt, then asked him to read the description back. He did so. She inspected the impression he made on the receipt and signed.

He left with the pouch tucked inside his coat.

No one spoke until his feet had reached the stairs.

Nare folded the wrapper over the testament but did not put it away.

“You will need another inspector.”

“Ameto can verify the grain.”

Ameto nodded once. His face was grave.

“And you?” Nare asked.

Adesuwa rubbed her thumb against the place where the pouch’s cord had pressed it.

“I can still read.”

Belaku sat down again. After a moment he pushed his box toward the centre of the room, where everyone could see the old measure.

“Then read this with us,” he said. “And tell us what we can ask people to do before noon.”

1.6 Before the Water Falls

Adesuwa slept for two hours in a room that did not belong to her.

Nare’s household had made up a bed for the departed visitors and not yet taken it apart. The reed mat smelled of sun and the pillow faintly of hair oil. Adesuwa lay down intending to order the next morning in her mind. She woke with her mouth dry and somebody tapping at the door.

Belaku waited in the passage with a cup of water.

“It is nearly first bell.”

“Did you sleep?”

“Enough.”

She took the cup. It was too full; water ran over her wrist as she drank. He gave her a cloth without comment.

Downstairs the household was already awake. The store-woman, whose name was Sabe, had brought six slates and was attempting to explain to Nare why an empty floor was not the same thing as available drying space. A boy swept the courtyard around them, pushing the broom beneath their feet whenever they forgot to lift them. Ameto sat at the edge of the table with the testament copy and the old contribution terms.

“The floor is needed for the northern lots,” Sabe said.

“They have not arrived.”

“They are coming.”

“Then we put them in the west store.”

“The west store’s roof is being repaired.”

“Under cloth.”

“For sacks. Not open grain.”

Adesuwa took a bowl from a passing tray and sat beside Ameto. The porridge was thinner than Dede’s. Nare’s household had evidently fed more people than expected the night before.

“Is the west floor sound?” she asked.

Sabe turned to her.

“The floor, yes. The roof, no.”

“How much roof?”

“The edge toward the court. Rain comes in at the beam.”

“Can you hang a catch cloth below it and leave air over the grain?”

Sabe looked at Nare.

“That is what I said last month.”

“And I said we should repair the beam.”

“We did neither.”

Nare put down the slate.

“Bring the roofer.”

“He is at his mother’s house upriver.”

“The carpenter.”

“He will need cloth.”

“Take the cloth from the departure awning.”

Sabe nodded, satisfied at last, and sent the sweeping boy for two people Adesuwa did not know. The boy leaned the broom against a chair and ran. Ameto retrieved it before anyone could sit down.

They divided the morning.

Adesuwa would attend the inquiry with Koba. Ameto would compare the requested release against the old terms and prepare the actual grain inspection. Nare would call two canal-mothers who had opposed the retrospective instruction, asking them to assume this distribution rather than offering their support in the abstract. Belaku would take the proposed order to Five Steps and Red Steps. The people affected were to choose an independent witness, someone willing to insist that the Long Court’s commitments were as binding as the districts’.

“Not my mother’s brother,” Belaku said. “He would talk until the water went.”

“Someone from neither landing,” Nare said.

“Yes.”

“And not someone whom you can send away if you dislike the questions.”

Belaku put down his cup.

“Will you come to the landing yourself?”

“I have sent—”

“Will you?”

Nare looked at Sabe, who had returned with a roll of cloth so large that she had to manoeuvre sideways through the doorway.

“Yes,” Nare said. “After the floor is ready.”

Belaku accepted it. Adesuwa watched him fasten the lid of his box. He had not forgiven her yesterday’s refusal. He had also not excluded her from the morning’s work. She found she wanted to thank him for that and restrained herself, uncertain what he could reasonably be asked to say in reply.

Koba’s boat looked absurdly quiet with its ropes doubled and a notice fixed to the stern post.

He sat on the bank, swollen foot stretched out, drinking something Bese had made too strong. The nephew was nowhere to be seen.

“He has gone to ask his mother whether I can sell him,” Koba said.

“Can you?”

“I tried once. They returned him with a complaint about the cargo.”

Bese handed Adesuwa a cup. She declined it, then changed her mind after smelling the tea.

“I should have stopped the night passage,” she said.

“You would have been a long time getting home from the bank.”

“I have recorded that I accepted it.”

“I know. Your young clerk came at dawn. Very serious. Is he always like that?”

“No.”

“Good. It would ruin his face.”

At the duty office they were made to wait under an awning open toward the queue of working boats. Koba watched every departure as if noting a personal affront. Bese had come with them because she had made the boat-book entry. She held the book in a cloth, pressed against her chest. Adesuwa could hear her stomach. She gave the girl the cake she had taken from Nare’s breakfast tray and pretended to inspect the knot on her sandal while Bese ate it.

The officer hearing the inquiry was younger than Adesuwa had expected. He had a clean collar and a tired face. He asked the questions in order, giving each answer room to be entered. Who owned the grain? Who agreed to its release? Was the reserve below the required minimum for the crew’s current voyage? What was the transit authorization? Who had certified condition? Who had represented the passage as lawful?

“No one,” Adesuwa said to the last.

Koba looked at her.

“I asked him to record my presence and knowledge.”

“Your presence may have been understood as permission.”

“Yes.”

“Was it permission?”

“I lacked the authority to give it.”

The officer looked up from his page.

“You understand the question, Inspector.”

“I did not order him to turn back. He had reason to think I would accept the route. I did accept it.”

Koba began to say something. She shook her head, and he stopped.

They examined the boat book. Bese identified her entry and described the fallen post at Red Steps. The officer asked whether the delay had been caused by an unsafe channel. She said it had been caused by an unsafe landing. He asked again in a different form. She gave the same answer, becoming visibly annoyed.

“The boat was clear of the bank,” she said. “We were putting in. My cousin held after he was told not to. You can ask him, but he has gone to his mother.”

Koba covered his mouth.

The officer made another entry.

At the end he ordered the licence returned subject to a penalty for the night passage. The boat could sail in daylight. The reserve had been legally sold by its owners, and the retained minimum was adequate for the crew’s stated voyage. Adesuwa’s stamp would remain in custody until her superior had reviewed whether she had allowed its office to be used outside her remit.

“Superior?” she asked.

“The joint receiving bench.”

“They sit—”

“In four days.”

She stopped. He did not look pleased to have said it.

Outside, Koba counted the penalty twice.

“I will meet it,” Adesuwa said.

“No.”

“You carried the load because I came to you.”

“I carried it because Belaku bought grain and I wanted six measures at Red Steps. If you need to buy something from me, buy a seat. My boat is becoming very popular and I intend to behave badly about it.”

She smiled despite herself.

He put the licence carefully into the book and gave it to Bese.

“You can pay half,” he said. “Since you insist.”

By late morning the Long Court had become a drying house.

Workers were moving furniture from two ground-floor rooms, children carrying the small things and adults arguing about where the large ones could go without obstructing the next load. In the west store the carpenter had fitted a frame under the damaged roof edge. Cloth hung over it with a fall toward a jar. The temporary arrangement looked ugly and serviceable. Sabe was inspecting its underside from a ladder.

Adesuwa had brought Dede.

Her sister walked through the court with a face of professional disapproval that deepened whenever someone suggested the work was nearly finished.

“Nearly ready for what?” she asked at last.

“For grain.”

“The floor is ready for grain. Who is turning it?”

Sabe came down the ladder.

“We have twelve people until evening.”

“And after evening?”

“The house.”

“How many?”

“Those who remain.”

Dede put both hands on her hips.

“Then ask them before you say it.”

Adesuwa had heard that tone at home. She let her sister use it here.

The proposed release depended on grain in two conditions. Part was dry and ready. Part belonged to the newly received wet lots Yedru had shown them, grain that could be saved if spread soon and turned through the night. The reserve had neither enough drying floor nor enough spare workers to process it all before the next distribution. Moving those lots to the Long Court would free sound grain for immediate relief and put the remaining work where space had been found. But somebody would have to lose sleep. Nare’s offer of her house did not make her servants infinitely numerous.

They counted available hands.

Sabe could release six household workers without leaving meals, water and care unattended. Two neighbours offered three people between them. Dede could bring five after the store coating was done, but they would need food and a place to sleep afterward. Ameto knew four receiving-house workers willing to come for wages. Nare agreed to pay them. The total was still short if all the wet grain arrived at once.

“Send it in two loads,” Dede said.

“The channel—”

“Then take less.”

They fell silent.

Adesuwa looked at the estimate. Taking less would reduce the release. Tembe could still be fed for a limited period. Red Steps would receive less than Belaku had asked them to prepare for. The northern receiving houses would retain some wet grain and still need emergency labour.

Nare stood with her hand on the back of a chair that no one knew where to put.

“Could the districts send people?”

“Could they eat before they come?” Dede asked.

“Yes.”

“Then ask.”

They sent a boat.

The witness arrived with Belaku just before noon.

She was a middle-aged woman from a small district whose landing served the junction between the two cuts. Her name was Isata. She wore no badge, but the men who came with her waited until she had finished drinking before they began describing the channel. Her authority over them appeared to require very little display.

“I have not come to choose a Widow,” she said after the greetings.

“No one asks you to,” Nare replied.

“I have not come to attest to grain I have not seen.”

“Ameto will inspect it.”

“And I have not come because Belaku is especially pleasant.”

Belaku sighed.
