“She has said this twice on the boat.”

“It remains true at the landing.”

Adesuwa liked her at once, which made her cautious.

They met in the outer court. Nare had sent the testament and retained delegation, under guard and with both witnesses, to the canal-mothers’ receiving room. One canal-mother had agreed to come to the landing; the second would attend at the reserve and supervise release. Neither accepted responsibility for all eastern allocations. Their undertaking was for this disputed group of claims while the older authority was examined.

Yedru’s reply had come on a narrow sheet. She would attend the proposed comparison and bring the allocation records. She had not agreed to release the grain.

The exception offered to Tembe expired at noon.

Belaku put his box on the table.

“I could still go,” he said.

Nobody answered for him.

Adesuwa watched the shadow of the gatepost move across a crack in the paving. Somewhere beyond the wall a hawker was calling ripe fruit by the basket. Belaku took the old measure out of its box, looked at the repaired bottom and set it down again.

“What exactly is promised here?” he asked.

They went through it.

Nare named the floor, the paid workers and the meals. Belaku named the crews from Five Steps and the households that would send someone after receiving the first issue. Red Steps had sent its keeper’s nephew and two women who spoke for separate stores. They named their contribution to the work and insisted that the new landing post be paid for by all the boats that used it. Koba, arriving at the end of that demand, said he had suffered enough in the service of public timber. Isata made him state what he actually agreed to. He agreed to carry the second load at a reduced fare and help set a new post before it arrived.

Adesuwa gave her work without claiming the stamp she no longer held. She would present the old and new computation, identify her certification and answer questions. Ameto would inspect and certify the actual lots if they were released. A receiving-house clerk independent of the Ninth would copy the public findings. The young woman with the wedding cloth had volunteered for that work, provided nobody expected her to stay beyond the time she had promised her future household.

“Good,” Isata said. “She should be allowed to get married even if you have made a mistake.”

She asked who would feed a worker whose promised grain arrived spoiled. They revised the meal reserve. She asked what happened if the floor filled before the second load was dry. They reduced the wet quantity and brought forward a second night’s labour. She asked who would speak publicly if the canal-mothers refused the whole undertaking. Nare said that she would.

At last Isata laid both palms on the table.

“I can witness those things. I cannot make people brave enough to do them.”

“We know,” Nare said.

Isata looked around the court.

“Some of you do.”

They stood. Belaku left the box on the table for a moment and went to the gate. He looked toward the upper reserve. Adesuwa followed him only as far as the shade.

“Noon,” he said.

“Yes.”

He rubbed his face.

“I am not doing this because I am certain you are right.”

“I know.”

“My wife will ask me why I did not take the exception.”

“What will you tell her?”

“I do not know yet.”

He came back into the court and picked up the box.

Outside, the boats were waiting. There was enough water to get them to the landing. After that, every hour would make the loaded passage harder.

1.7 The Correction

By the time the first table was carried onto the landing, people were already arguing about where it should stand.

The low end was shaded. The high end could be heard from the steps. The middle left room for grain but none for the people who would have to receive it. A woman selling cooked beans said that wherever they put it, they were not putting it in front of her stall, because half the men proposing the positions owed her for yesterday.

Isata asked her which men. The woman named them. The table went at the high end.

Adesuwa set the old measure beside the new receiving-house vessel and placed the standards between them. Ameto arranged his inspection tools on a separate mat. He had brought another inspector from the Fourth House, a quiet woman with a narrow face whom Adesuwa respected and did not enjoy working beside. She had once found a discrepancy in a test Adesuwa had performed correctly and spent the rest of the morning demonstrating that the wording of the report permitted the discrepancy to reappear.

Today Adesuwa was pleased to see her.

Eru arrived with the receiving clerk and a boy carrying a stool. The clerk had left her wedding cloth at home. She wore an old working wrapper and a blue thread at one wrist.

“What should I call you in the finding?” Adesuwa asked.

“Reme. Daughter of the west kiln. And not Soro’s daughter, which he has been letting people say for years because he thinks it excuses not paying me enough.”

Eru put down the stool too quickly. One leg caught in a crack.

“Can you read her copy after she finishes?” Adesuwa asked him.

“Yes.”

“Then watch the words.”

Reme smiled without looking up.

Belaku was speaking with the Five Steps keeper and his wife. The porter had brought a group of workers who had left the store coating unfinished at a sound stopping point. Grey plaster dried on their arms. They listened to Belaku with expressions that gave Adesuwa no reassurance. He had evidently told them about the exception. One woman kept looking toward the reserve boats as if the lost opportunity might still appear there.

Nare came by water with the two witnesses and the wrapped papers. She had changed into a plain working cloth. Her arrival drew more attention than she seemed to want. People recognized the Long Court’s boat and passed the name up the steps. Someone began a formal greeting that would have become a speech if Isata had not directed him to carry a bench.

The canal-mother who had agreed to oversee the landing arrived on foot.

She was small and walked with a stick. Behind her came two assistants, neither carrying anything ornamental. One had the house’s records; the other had a rolled mat and a water flask. She greeted the public by naming the Widow’s office, the Covenant and the district keepers in an order everybody appeared to know. The crowd answered in several voices that settled, after the first line, into one.

Adesuwa had heard those words since childhood. She was usually checking a scale when they were said. Without her stamp at her waist, standing in the space before the tables, she found she was attending to the responses for the first time in years. Some people answered readily. Others came in late. One man said nothing and looked at the boats. A child repeated the last sound after the adults had stopped, and his mother laid a hand over his mouth too late to prevent laughter.

The canal-mother waited for it to end.

“I am Mother Selen of the lower junction,” she said. “I have come to hear the disputed measure and supervise any release lawfully made here. I have not come to settle the Widow’s absence. Speak to what we can establish.”

Yedru appeared as she finished.

The warden had brought two clerks and three members of the Corps. Their presence changed the spacing of the people on the steps. Nobody fled or fell silent altogether, but shoulders moved and a path opened where none had been requested.

Yedru bowed to Mother Selen. She greeted Isata, whom she evidently knew, and acknowledged Nare with a short inclination of her head. When she saw Adesuwa’s empty belt, her face showed no satisfaction.

“Have you a certifying inspector?”

“Ameto of the Ninth. Inspector Leshe of the Fourth will compare.”

Leshe nodded. Yedru accepted that and gave her clerk the instruction bundle.

Mother Selen struck the end of her stick once on the paving.

“Begin.”

Adesuwa began with the vessels because people had come expecting a false one.

She named the district measure and its history as Belaku had given it, then the receiving-house vessel and its retained counterpart. She explained that they were comparing capacities and practice, not attempting to recover grain that had already been distributed. The six grains sewn into the old record could identify the crop. They could not prove how damp a vanished barge load had been.

An older man on the steps asked why they had brought the vessels if the vessels could not prove everything.

“To show that this is not where the missing contribution went,” she said.

“Then show us.”

She had Belaku fill the district vessel. He did it as he had at the receiving house, letting the grain settle naturally into the corners and levelling it with the board. The contents filled the old house measure. A worker from the receiving crew repeated the comparison. Then Leshe took both vessels and performed it herself.

They agreed.

Eru read the result. Reme copied it. The man on the steps asked to look at the repaired bottom. Belaku handed the vessel to him through Isata, who kept it in sight while he examined it. He returned it without further objection.

Adesuwa placed the original receiving instruction beside the new one.

“Tembe delivered under a wet-season allowance explicitly permitted in the request for early delivery. The original entry accepted the contribution on that basis. The later instruction converted the same contribution under a smaller allowance and used the result to refuse relief.”

“Give us the numbers,” Yedru said.

Adesuwa did.

She used the district’s actual entry first, then a smaller example that could be followed without holding the full load in the head. Out of a hundred delivered units, the prior terms recognized ninety-six for contribution. The new calculation recognized ninety. A district asked to provide ninety-four and told that its ninety-six had been accepted later found itself four short. Nothing was removed from the store on the day of that change. The shortfall appeared when the accepted contribution was reconsidered.

“Because the reserve must be issued on a common basis,” Yedru said.

Mother Selen held up one hand.

“Your answer follows. Let the inspector finish.”

Adesuwa pointed to her mark.

“I verified the calculation under the later instruction. My stamp is here. The addition was correct. I did not ask whether the instruction could be applied to an already accepted contribution. The refusal used that verified calculation.”

There was movement on the steps. Someone said that the stamp should be struck out. Someone else wanted to know how many similar entries she had certified. A third asked whether the other inspectors had done the same.

Reme stopped writing and looked at Mother Selen.

“Enter the questions,” the canal-mother told her. “We will return to them.”

Adesuwa could feel heat along her neck. She had rehearsed the admission in Nare’s house. It had sounded clear there. Here it was being carried into arguments she could hear only in pieces.

Belaku did not come to her rescue. He stood beside the box, listening.

She finished the account by reading the original early-delivery request. When she named the two districts fed during the lock repair, a man in the crowd raised his hand. He was from one of them. He remembered the boats because his first child had been born while they were unloading and he had been told about it by three people before anyone would let him put down his sack.

There was laughter again. He waited through it, then said that the grain had arrived early and that they had needed it early. His account did not prove the allowance’s legality. It did establish why wet grain had been requested, in a voice that did not come from either desk.

Mother Selen asked him to give his name to Reme.

Yedru took her place at the table.

She did not dispute the vessels, the received quantity or the written request. She described the present reserve: the sound grain, the wet lots awaiting drying, the shipping contracts whose payment would purchase another season’s stock. She gave figures, named districts and dates, and asked whether those who wanted the old allowances honoured also wanted the next purchases cancelled.

A man shouted that foreigners could wait.

“Some can,” Yedru said. “The contract that feeds the northern collecting houses is paid by one of them. Which do you propose I stop?”

He shouted something less specific. She waited for him to repeat it and he did not.

“The instruction was intended to prevent claims outrunning what could be safely released. Tembe has produced a prior undertaking that permits an exception. I offered that exception. It was not accepted.”

Heads turned toward Belaku.

He stepped forward before Adesuwa could move.

“I was offered release with a statement supporting the general standard. I did not know what that statement would permit afterward. I asked for time. I have used it to bring people who will work if this grain is released.”

“You could have fed your own already,” a woman said.

He looked toward her. Adesuwa recognized one of the Five Steps workers.

“Yes,” he said.

The woman folded her arms. He did not defend himself further. The silence that followed was harder than an argument would have been.

Mother Selen turned to Nare.

“We have heard the act and the need claimed for it. You challenge the authority under which it was imposed.”

Nare came to the table with the wrapped documents.

She laid out the late canal-mother’s testament, the retained delegation and the copy against which the Corps’ clerks could compare it. Ameto and the second witness identified their marks. Reme read the pertinent passages aloud. Nare named the house’s previous private challenge and its refusal to surrender the original into an office whose exercise of authority was in dispute.

Yedru’s senior clerk asked for the attachment. He read it standing close enough to the table for his coat to brush the wood. Adesuwa watched his finger pause at the renewal requirement.

The warden read it after him.

“Continuation was assumed by necessity,” she said.

Mother Selen tapped the page with one fingernail.

“This requires assent.”

“The Widow’s office continued.”

“The Widow’s office is not disputed here. Your extension of these powers is.”

Yedru looked at Nare.

“You have held this while asking us to continue the work.”

“Yes,” Nare said.

“For two years.”

“Yes.”

A murmur moved up the steps.

Nare kept both hands on the table.

“I feared the interruption of distribution. I have come prepared to undertake part of it. The Long Court has opened its floors. The workers are named. The meals are provided. I ask that the retrospective refusal be suspended for these claims and that the accepted contributions stand pending full review.”

“Part of it,” Yedru repeated.

“Part I can perform.”

“The eastern office performs the rest.”

“Yes.”

Yedru looked toward the boats. Adesuwa thought she might leave. The three Corps members stood with their hands free, waiting for whatever she decided. A child somewhere behind the bean stall began to cry with the exhausted fury of someone kept too long in the sun.

Mother Selen asked for water to be passed along the steps. People shifted, helped older relatives into shade, sent children to the stall. Adesuwa found her own knees unsteady and sat on the edge of the spare bench.

Belaku brought her a cup.

“She was right about one thing,” he said.

“Which?”

“You could have taken the grain already.”

He shook his head.

“Nare held that paper while Yedru kept the boats moving.”

“Yes.”

“I want them both here when the first load goes.”

“So do I.”

He took the cup back and went to the water jar.

The grain nearly defeated them after the argument had been won as far as it could be won that day.

Mother Selen had authority, with the second canal-mother at the reserve, to oversee this temporary release. Yedru would suspend the retrospective refusal against the named claims without accepting that every emergency instruction she had issued was void. The receiving figures would stand while the wider delegation was reviewed. Reme entered the limitation twice, once in the finding and once in the release order, because Leshe insisted that anyone reading either document alone should understand it.

Then Ameto inspected the first wet lot.

It had been sent under a covering mat and lay close-packed in a shallow barge. From the bank it looked sound. When he drove his hand into the centre and withdrew it, grain clung to the sweat on his wrist.

“How long closed?” he asked.

The boatman named the hour of loading.

“Before that?”

“At the reserve.”

“Open or closed?”

The boatman did not know. Yedru came down to the boat. Leshe joined Ameto. Together they opened the central sacks and smelled the contents.

Adesuwa stood on the landing, unable to see past their shoulders.

“Bring it up,” Leshe said.

They emptied a portion into a tray. The smell reached Adesuwa then: heat and a faint sweetness that should not have been there. She crouched beside the tray.

“How much?”

“This centre group,” Ameto said. “The outer ones may be recoverable.”

“Do not mix them,” Leshe told the crew.

The boatman spread his hands.

“They were weighed together.”

“They will be separated here.”

It meant more work, less usable grain and another delay while the water continued falling.

The crowd had begun to hear. The woman from Five Steps pushed forward far enough to see the tray, then stopped with one hand against the table. Belaku went to her. She said something Adesuwa could not hear. His face tightened.

Yedru stood beside the spoiled grain.

“That was to be dried yesterday,” she said.

“Whose floor?” Adesuwa asked.

“The third shed. It was reassigned when the roof opened over the northern lot.”

They could trace the delay. It would not alter the smell.

Mother Selen came down the steps slowly, leaning on her stick.

“Can it be issued?”

“No,” Ameto said.

“Can any of it be issued?”

“After separation and drying, some. Not this.”

“Then enter it that way.”

The boatman protested that he had received a specified quantity and would be required to deliver it. Yedru told him she would sign the loss at origin. He looked at her, making sure, then called for more trays.

Adesuwa turned toward the people waiting at the tables. She had been about to tell them the loss was limited. She did not yet know its extent.

“We must revise the issue,” she said instead.

A man swore. She let him.

Belaku came back from the Five Steps woman.

“Use the dry grain for the first meals. The workers can stay if they eat. We will find more floor for the wet.”

“The planned dry issue was already divided,” Nare said.

“Divide it again.”

“People were told—”

“Tell them now.”

Isata called the store representatives together. They moved away from the public table to the edge of the landing, close enough to be seen, far enough not to be drowned in every objection. Adesuwa joined them only when they asked for the revised quantity.

The adjustment hurt. One store could wait half a day because its keeper had borrowed privately from a relative. Another could not. Red Steps gave up part of its first issue in exchange for first access to the next sound load. The Five Steps woman who had challenged Belaku demanded that the workers’ meals be taken from the same grain the rest of the households received, so nobody would be fed well for being useful while an old person waited.

They agreed.

Nare sent a runner ahead to Sabe, ordering an additional room opened and warning that less grain would arrive ready to eat. Dede, who had come to see what was keeping everyone, heard that message and took over its last half.

“Do not tell her to feed more people with less in a note,” she said. “Tell her I am coming.”

She went off with two women and the runner, all three now carrying things she had acquired from the landing.

Yedru ordered a dry reserve boat brought forward. It had been waiting behind the wet lot to clear the inspection point. Koba’s nephew and three men from Five Steps made a line along the side and guided it into the open berth. The first sacks came ashore.

No one cheered at first. They were watching the sacks.

Ameto tested the lot. Leshe compared. Eru read the certified amount, and Reme entered it in a hand so clear that people leaning over her shoulder could follow. Isata named the first destination. Belaku’s porter lifted the first load onto his frame.

Only then did the crowd make a sound.

It was less a cheer than many people beginning to speak at once. Someone called for room on the steps. Someone else asked where the empty baskets should go. The bean seller began selling rapidly to people who had remembered they were hungry. Mother Selen remained beside the table while the release order was read once more.

Yedru watched the first boat pull away toward the Long Court. Nare stood beside her.

“If your floor fails,” Yedru said, “it comes back to mine.”

“Yes.”

“Send word before the grain heats.”

“I will.”

Adesuwa heard them and said nothing. Across the landing, Belaku was helping the Five Steps keeper’s wife lift a vessel to the correct mark. The woman corrected his hand on the board. He changed his grip and did it again.

1.8 Who Watches Tomorrow

The Long Court’s best room smelled of opened grain.

Its rugs had been rolled and carried upstairs. The chairs stood along the passage, occupied whenever somebody had a moment to sit down. Under the temporary roof cloth a jar filled one drop at a time from water left in the damaged beam. Dede had put a second jar beside it and appointed a child to call when the first was nearly full. The child took this responsibility so seriously that she called every few minutes, requiring reassurance that the jar still had room.

Adesuwa worked in the west store until her back began to seize.

Turning grain looked easy from a doorway. After an hour bent over the long wooden rake, she understood why Sabe had refused to count every able person as a full night’s labour. Grain had to be spread evenly, moved without being driven into damp corners and checked where the layer thickened against an obstruction. The rake caught in a flaw in the floor every fourth pass. She began to anticipate it, lifted too early once and scattered a small ridge against her ankle.

Dede watched from the door.

“Your husband has never taught you that.”

“I have no husband.”

“I mean the weigh-house.”

“Come and do it, then.”

“I have forty people to feed. You have one rake.”

She went away before Adesuwa could answer.

The woman working beside her laughed quietly. She was from Red Steps. Adesuwa recognized the scarred chin of the girl who had fetched bowls in the night and realized she was older than she had first thought, with a child of her own asleep in the next room.

“You turn from the wall,” the woman said. “Then it does not catch.”

Adesuwa changed direction. It helped.

“Thank you.”

“My mother does the same thing. Tells you what is wrong while holding something she says is more important.”

“Is it?”

“Usually.”

They worked until the next pair relieved them. Adesuwa tried to straighten and discovered she could only do it in stages. The woman pretended to examine the rake head while she managed it.

In the court, Nare was washing bowls.

That should not have surprised Adesuwa. It did. The mistress of the Long Court had removed the rings from her hands and set them in a little cup beyond the splashing water. Two children dried what she passed them, becoming less careful as they grew tired. Nare corrected them without the authority she had used over departing relatives. At this hour everyone was liable to be sent to bed by someone else.

“Where is Sabe?” Adesuwa asked.

“Inspecting the second load.”

“It has come?”

“Koba brought it through before the water fell too far. He wants everyone to understand how inconvenient he found that.”

“He will tell them.”

“He has begun.”

Koba’s voice could be heard through the store doorway, describing a bend as if it had taken a personal interest in ruining his day. Bese corrected a distance. He said the distance had felt longer. She said distance was not measured by his feelings. Adesuwa closed her eyes and leaned against the cool wall.

“Sit,” Nare said.

“If I sit, I will stay.”

“You may stay.”

“I mean here.”

“So do I.”

A bowl slipped from one child’s hands and broke against the paving. Everyone looked. The child stood with her fingers spread, unable to decide whether to apologize or cry.

“Put your feet back,” Nare said. “There. Fetch the brush.”

The girl ran. Adesuwa crouched to collect the largest pieces and regretted it as soon as she tried to rise. Nare took the fragments from her and pointed to the chair. She sat.

For a while she watched the household work.

The workers had brought their own lives with them. A man waiting for his turn at the floor repaired a sandal. Two women argued about the best place to plant a particular crop and could not agree because each had a different kind of ground in mind. Someone had brought a stringed instrument and been persuaded to play only after everyone promised not to ask for the song he was known for. They asked for it before the first tune ended. He insulted them affectionately and began it anyway.

Adesuwa knew the refrain. She had not heard the second verse in years. Dede sang it from the cooking place, arriving at the last line before the musician did and accusing him of slowing down on purpose.

The voice from the next room startled her: Belaku singing too.

He had a lower voice than she would have guessed. It was not particularly good. He knew all the words and gave no sign that this ought to be an embarrassment. When the refrain came around, he carried a tray of bowls into the court and joined the last line with his mouth half open, uncertain whether he should finish the work or the song first.

Nare took the tray.

“You will spill them if you try both.”

He smiled. Adesuwa had seen him smile at a joke, but never like this, with his whole attention given to an unimportant pleasure. It made him look younger and, oddly, more formidable. There were people at home who knew this face. They had sent him here trusting he would come back with it.

He saw her watching and the smile diminished. She looked away first.

The spoiled grain was carried out before dawn.

Ameto had separated what could be dried safely from what could not. The rejected portion lay covered in marked baskets away from the usable stores. Yedru’s clerk came with two workers to witness its removal and make sure it was not quietly returned to a relief load. He was tired enough to misread a number. Reme corrected him. He checked her correction, thanked her and entered it.

Reme had missed the hour she had promised to go home.

Eru tried to persuade her to leave. She told him she could finish a page faster if he stopped explaining why she should not be finishing it. When she did stand, one of the older women put a food parcel in her hand and asked where she lived.

“My aunt is taking me.”

“Your aunt is asleep.”

“I can wake her.”

“Let the boy carry the lamp.”

Eru’s ears changed colour. Reme looked at him, considered the parcel, and said that he might carry that instead because she preferred to see where she was walking.

They left together. Adesuwa did not offer advice.

Ameto sat beside her with the inspection results.

“The second lot is better.”

“How much lost in all?”

He gave her the number. It was smaller than she had feared and larger than anyone here could comfortably afford.

“Entered at origin?”

“Yedru signed. The temporary release stands.”

“And the other claims?”

“Review in four days.”

“Three now.”

“Three.”

He leaned back and shut his eyes.

“Do you want to discuss my failure to tell you about the testament?”

“Not before breakfast.”

“I would prefer after a different breakfast. Next month.”

She laughed, and then stopped because laughing hurt her back.

Ameto opened one eye.

“You have been working.”

“Yes.”

“Unfortunate.”

He handed her the results. She read them while he slept.

The first household they visited after sunrise did not wish to discuss the release.

Belaku had asked Adesuwa to come to Five Steps while the early issue was being made. She went because she had promised the porter she would report, and because a part of her wanted to see the grain in bowls. She had brought the porter’s account. Twice on the walk from the landing she checked that it was still in her pouch.

The store had been coated as far as the workers could reach before leaving for Mbaro. New earth stood dark against the older surface. A ladder remained against the wall. The lizard from Dede’s straw heap was not there, but a child had arranged three beetle shells on a brick with the same grave attention to small discoveries.

The porter’s wife greeted them and showed the sound grain. She had divided it according to the revised issue, reserving workers’ meals from the same share. The households waiting outside knew their places and could see the vessel being filled. No one was being asked to trust a hidden room.

Adesuwa watched the first four portions. Then she stepped back.

Belaku took her to a house beyond the store. Its doorway had been hung with a cloth against flies. A woman inside was kneeling beside a boy whose arm lay on a folded mat. He had fallen while carrying water. The injury was not new, but the swelling had returned after he tried to use it.

“He lifted the jar,” the woman said when Belaku asked. “The full one. His sister could have done it, but apparently she is not a man.”

The boy stared at the floor.

“Does being a man hurt?” Belaku asked him.

The boy nodded reluctantly.

“I have found that too.”

Adesuwa sat where the woman directed and held the lamp while she examined the binding. She had expected thanks, or an account of the delay, and received instructions to move the light closer and not cast her hand’s shadow over the knot. She obeyed.

There was porridge on the hearth. A smaller child scraped the last dry pieces from the side of an old pot, though a full bowl waited for him. The woman told him to eat the fresh food. He ignored her until she reached for the pot, then surrendered it with a look of grievance.

Belaku asked whether they had received their portion.

“Yes,” the woman said. “Put that cup somewhere he cannot kick it.”

They stayed until the binding was secure. The boy wanted to know whether he would be able to help with the plastering. His mother said he would be able to help by remaining where she put him. Belaku promised to tell the others that the boy had been assigned important work guarding the household’s only good mat. The boy was old enough to know he was being managed and young enough to be pleased by the title.

Outside, Adesuwa breathed the morning air with an unexpected sense of relief.

“I thought she would ask about the review,” she said.

“She has an arm to worry about.”

“Yes.”

They walked back slowly. The district was waking around them, carrying water, moving fuel, sweeping the spaces where people would work. A man repaired the ladder outside the store. He had a mouthful of small wooden pegs and greeted Belaku through them. Belaku understood him and answered at length. Adesuwa could not make out a word.

At the landing, the temporary post had been replaced with new timber. Koba’s nephew was testing the rope around it while two men argued over whether it had been driven deep enough. Koba sat on an upturned basket and advised them as if his foot had conferred authority over all forms of wood.

Bese handed Adesuwa the borrowed shawl, washed and still damp at one edge.

“You left it.”

“I thought it was yours.”

“It is. You can return it when it is dry.”

Adesuwa took it.

Her stamp was returned on the second day after the release.

The joint receiving bench did not meet early on her account. Its senior member examined the duty officer’s report between two other matters and authorized return pending the full hearing. Adesuwa remained answerable for allowing her presence to be treated as permission on the night route. Koba’s penalty stood. The duty officer turned the grain certificate toward the light, checked the moon-visible mark and entered the impression against her name.

She held the brass for a moment before putting it away.

Ameto watched from the other side of the desk.

“Will you wear it to breakfast?”

“I usually do.”

“I know.”

She put the pouch in the locked drawer instead and asked him to hold the house for half an hour.

At Dede’s courtyard, the store wall had been finished. Its new surface caught the morning light softly, showing the sweep of hands that had smoothed it. The boys had moved on to another household’s wall and taken their disagreements with them. The recovered shutter hung straight. Somebody had left the lizard on the lintel, where Dede pretended not to see it.

Belaku sat at the breakfast table.

He had brought a small packet of dried fish, properly purchased from the blue-awning seller. Dede was examining it with the severity she usually reserved for gifts from relatives. He looked more frightened of her judgment than he had looked at Yedru’s table.

“It will do,” she said at last.

He exhaled.

Adesuwa sat opposite him.

“I am glad you stayed.”

“I sail after the morning issue.”

“You can leave the remaining papers at the Ninth. I will follow them.”

“I have appointed someone to attend.”

“Who?”

“The porter’s wife. She knows the store counts and will ask if they do not agree.”

“I can report to her.”

“Yes. She will watch the comparison herself.”

Adesuwa lifted her bowl, then set it down again.

“You do not trust my comparison?”

“I trusted the last one.”

Dede, mercifully, was at the hearth with her back turned.

Adesuwa looked at the fish. It was better than the kind she had bought. She could see that now, or had been told it often enough to see it.

“Tell her to come before first issue,” she said. “There is room beside the table if she comes early.”

“I will.”

He began eating. After a few mouthfuls he asked about the lizard on the lintel. Dede turned at once and said she had been waiting for a guest to notice, since no one who lived in the house seemed capable of removing it. Adesuwa said it had not been there when she left. Dede said a great many things happened while Adesuwa was out seeing whether other people kept their stores properly.

Belaku laughed, and this time did not stop when Adesuwa looked at him.

The fish passed around the table. In the lane, the first carrying frames went by empty. Dede called to one of the men to remind him that he still owed her a basket, and he called back that he remembered, and she told him remembering would not hold millet.

Adesuwa took another piece of fish before her sister returned to the table.

The Name in the Lantern

2.1 The Answer Before the Question

The lantern answered before Merasi had finished asking.

She stopped with her finger on the line. Around her the repair yard continued its morning work. A hammer struck the same small place three times. Somewhere behind the partition a man sneezed and was told, in two voices, to cover his mouth. The bread seller at the gate was arguing about whether yesterday’s order had included the boy who carried the ash away.

Merasi looked at the lantern.

It stood in the fourth place on the bench, brass ribs enclosed in cloudy glass. A little soot had gathered at the upper seam. Its handle leaned slightly to one side. Nothing distinguished it from the eleven others waiting to be tested except a new fastening where the old one had split.

She began the question again.

“Under whose keeping do you—”

“Under the keeping entered.”

The voice was low, male, without breath enough behind it to carry far. The answer was correct. It was meant to follow the question. The hesitation before the last word was too slight to mark as a fault.

Merasi put her pen against the paper and did not write.

“Again?” said the supervisor.

She looked up. He stood at the other end of the bench holding a cup in both hands. He had burned one thumb earlier and was keeping it off the pottery.

“The response overlaps.”

“Then wait longer before the next prompt.”

“It should wait for me.”

“Some of them learn.”

She waited. The supervisor drank. Behind him, a worker shook filings from a cloth into a bowl, careful to save the bright scraps. Brass was counted before it entered the room and again when it left. Nobody minded if a little skin went with it.

Merasi asked the second question. The lantern gave its required number. She checked it against the paper and found it right. The third established that the bound occupant would answer to the designated signal. The light strengthened briefly, washing pale yellow over her fingers, then withdrew until the dirty glass reflected the yard again.

She made the three marks.

The supervisor moved away.

Merasi remained at the bench while the next lantern was lifted into place. The man handling it was missing the end of one finger. He used the damaged hand beneath the base and the sound hand at the loop, carrying the weight with a delicacy that made each movement look slower than it was. He had been doing this since before she came to the yard.

“You are leaving ink,” he said.

She looked down. The pen had dripped onto the edge of the test sheet.

“Blot it,” he said. “Before it runs.”

She did.

The next voice was a woman’s. Merasi completed the questions without difficulty. Then a child’s, or a very small adult’s; she did not look up to distinguish. The answers were clear. The light held. No fault was entered.

At the meal break she walked to the water jar and stood behind three workers waiting to wash. She had forgotten to bring her cup. The youngest worker offered his. She said she would fetch her own, then remained where she was until he asked whether she had heard him.

“I heard.”

He moved aside. She washed the ink from her fingers and watched it run into the gravel beneath the jar. There was a crescent of old colour at the nail of her middle finger. It never quite came out.

The voice had not said her name. It had not asked for help. She had heard a familiar pause in the mouth of something trained to answer prescribed questions, and had supplied the rest herself.

She dried her hands and went to eat.

The bread was still warm at the centre. A woman from the wrapping room had brought lentils with onion and offered to share. Merasi accepted, regretted accepting because it obliged her to remain, and then ate with more appetite than she expected. The woman complained about a leak over her bed. Her landlord had sent a boy to inspect it from below. He had stood in the dry room at midday and said he could see nothing wrong.

“Put him in the bed when it rains,” one of the workers suggested.

“My husband is already there.”

“Then put the landlord there too. They can keep each other informed.”

The woman laughed, wiping oil from her chin. Merasi laughed a little late. Nobody remarked on it. The talk moved on to the bread seller’s son, who was growing too quickly for his clothes and had taken to standing very straight near the wrapping-room girls.

At the far end of the yard, the lanterns for export were being enclosed in padded wooden cases. Each case held one working unit, its paper and a repair fitting. The cases would travel from Galanina to the river warehouses, then down toward the island city that gave the country the name everybody used. Merasi had never seen that city. She knew the receiving marks of its offices more intimately than she knew the streets beyond her own neighbourhood.

The fourth lantern had not yet been packed.

She could see its crooked handle through the open door.

Her room was on the upper floor of a house divided among three families and two women who said they were cousins whenever anyone official asked. The stair was steep. A jar stood under the lowest step to catch the leak from a roof that could not be reached without crossing another roof whose owner objected to feet. The quarrel had survived two owners and was expected to survive the present one.

Merasi came home carrying bread, a small packet of washing powder and the thin fatigue that usually left her when she took off her shoes.

It stayed.

On the landing, the two cousins were quarrelling with the landlord over a broken latch. One had the latch in her hand. The other kept showing him where it had broken, though he could see the piece perfectly well. He insisted that repairs caused by use belonged to the tenant. She asked which repairs were caused by not using things. He said she was being difficult. The first cousin, who had barely spoken, asked him to put his refusal in writing so they could take it to the owner of the whole building.

That changed the argument.

Merasi normally enjoyed their quarrels. She knew where they would begin, where they would turn and which sister would find the word that made him leave. Today she stood with her bread under her arm and wished them all silent.

“You write,” the landlord said, seeing her. “Tell them use makes things break.”

“It does,” she said.

The cousins looked at her.

“So does rain.”

He made a gesture with his whole arm and went downstairs. The quiet cousin gave Merasi a look of thanks. Merasi felt ashamed of having wished them silent and more ashamed that the feeling irritated her.

Inside her room, she put the bread on the shelf and opened the shutter. Hot air moved through the space without cooling it. Across the narrow court someone had hung wet cloth between two windows. It dripped onto a lower awning, steadily, in one place.

She sat on the bed.

Dazhet had always answered before she finished when he knew what she was going to ask. It had annoyed her. She had once refused to continue a conversation until he let her reach the last word. He had waited with exaggerated patience, smiling slightly, and when she finished he had said that he was glad to discover he had understood her the first time.

She could recall the expression more clearly than the quarrel that caused it. His left eyebrow had risen a little. He had been sitting on the edge of this bed, repairing the sole of a shoe, and had put the awl down because she disliked him holding it while they argued. The room had belonged to them both then. Their bowls had sat on the same shelf. One remained there, a shallow blue one with a chip beneath the rim where he used to put his thumb.

She took it down.

There was dust inside. She washed it, dried it and set it back.

Dazhet had been taken from a household warehouse where he worked copying dispatches. The first explanation was an irregular authorization. The second was that he had known the irregular authorization was false. After that, the people she asked began telling her that they did not remember which office had received him. One man forgot a whole conversation while she was still referring to it. Another became frightened and asked her to leave before she could establish whether he had forgotten anything at all.

She had stopped asking in public first. Later she stopped asking people who might have answers.

His daughter had not lived with them. She had lived with her mother’s family, across the city, in a house Merasi had visited twice and never been welcome in either time. She would be grown now. Merasi had thought of finding her after the arrest and had not known what she could bring except trouble and an account of a father the girl’s household already considered unreliable.

Water continued falling from the cloth outside.

At sunset one of the cousins knocked. She brought a repaired latch to show that the landlord had returned with a worker, claiming the rain had damaged it after all. Merasi congratulated her. The woman lingered in the doorway, looking at the clean bowl.

“You have washed it.”

“It was dusty.”

“Yes.”

Merasi wished she would go. The woman went, kindly, which was worse.

She ate bread dipped in what remained of the midday lentils. Afterward she laid out the next day’s clothes, mended a small tear at the underarm and checked the purse in which she kept the room payment. She could not afford to miss work. She could not afford to be seen testing a lantern twice without a reason. She could still enter an observation against a faulty fastening; the unit had recently been repaired.

She put the mending away.

In bed, with the shutter open and the warm night entering, she heard the answer again. Not the words. The slight gathering of the voice before the end, as if the speaker had nearly said something less obedient and changed his mind.

Before dawn she rose, put on the clothes she had laid out, and took the blue bowl down from the shelf. She stood with it in both hands. Then she replaced it and went to the yard.

2.2 The Vacant Place

A lantern with a doubtful fastening had to remain under observation through one cooling.

Merasi knew the rule because she had entered it against three units in the previous month and been told not to be so thorough when the defect was merely cosmetic. She had asked the supervisor to sign that judgment. He had allowed the observations to stand.

Now she waited until the handling man put the fourth lantern on the inspection cloth. She touched the crooked loop without moving it.

“The replacement pulls against the upper seam.”

He looked.

“Only when lifted.”

“It is being shipped.”

He held it by the loop. A fine line of light appeared at the seam.

He set it down promptly.

“That is enough,” he said.

Merasi entered the fault.

Her handwriting was steadier than she expected. The defect was real. She had not invented it. The lantern would go back to the repair room and remain in Galanina at least another day.

The supervisor came when called. He inspected the seam, swore at the replacement work and asked how soon another unit could be prepared for the fourth place.

Merasi looked up.

“Another?”

“The lot is twelve.”

“We have twelve.”

“We have eleven and a fault. The river office bought twelve.”

The handling man went to the storage board. His damaged finger ran down the list without touching the ink. He stopped at an entry, frowned and called for the assistant who kept the unused fittings.

Merasi remained at the table. The paper beneath her hand named the twelve units by number. The repaired lantern’s number had been crossed through once and reinstated after its earlier repair. Below the lot, in a smaller hand, an unallocated binding awaited transfer from the lower holding rooms.

“Not cooled,” she said.

The supervisor turned.

“The replacement. It has not been held through the full interval after binding.”

“It has been held long enough.”

“Under the lower rooms’ conditions. Not in the export fitting.”

“We can test it.”

“Not through cooling before this load closes.”

He looked at her for longer than she liked.

“You are very interested in this place today.”

“The earlier repair was signed off yesterday. If the replacement fails as well, the river office will return the whole lot to us.”

That was possible. More important, it was the kind of possibility he would be expected to have considered. He took the sheet from her and read the names under the earlier repair.

“Bring the workman,” he said.

The repairer arrived carrying a roll of thin tools. He was older than Merasi. His coat hung loose, one shoulder higher than the other. He listened, examined the seam and said the replacement had been fitted before the brass cooled completely.

“Yours?”

“My bench. Not my hand.”

“Your signature.”

“Yes.”

The supervisor handed back the sheet.

“Repair this one. Hold the replacement. I am not signing two faults for the same place.”

The repairer nodded. He lifted the lantern by its base and took it away.

The supervisor put his finger beside Merasi’s entry.

“Your observation. If this delays the lot, the river office asks you first.”

She inclined her head.

After he left, the handling man looked at her with something like sympathy. She did not want it. She wanted to know what would happen to the unallocated captive now the replacement had been stopped. Nothing in the rule released that person. They would remain below until another place was required.

She waited until the yard settled before asking to carry the test sheet to the repair room.

The repairer worked behind a partition of wood and hanging cloth. Air passed through the upper opening, carrying the smell of heated metal into the yard. The bench below was covered with small fittings laid in order on a dark mat. A boy ground an edge on a stone at the far end, drawing the metal toward himself with his mouth open in concentration.

“Close the cloth,” the repairer said.

Merasi did. Her hand remained on it.

“I brought the observation.”

“Put it there.”

He pointed to a clean board. She placed the paper and watched him turn the lantern so its crooked handle faced away from the boy.

“Will it take long?”

“As long as it takes to cool.”

“I meant the repair.”

“So did I.”

He held the upper fitting in padded jaws and tested it with a thin blade. The light inside withdrew from the edge. Merasi had seen that happen before. She had never wondered whether the movement hurt.

The repairer looked at her.

“You should stand away.”

“I need to verify the response.”

“After.”

She stepped back. The boy looked up, saw the repairer’s expression and returned to the stone.

Work continued. Merasi could hear people moving in the yard, the soft collision of wooden cases, the bread seller laughing at something near the gate. In the room every small touch of metal seemed too loud. When the repairer loosened the loop, the light gave a brief shiver. The voice made a sound too low to be an answer.

Merasi pressed her nails into her palm.

“Is that expected?” she asked.

The repairer stopped.

“What?”

“The sound.”

He looked at the lantern, then at her.

“Yes.”

He completed the adjustment, drew the upper seam together and withdrew the blade. The light steadied. He told the boy to take a tray to the yard. The boy left, balancing it carefully. Merasi watched the cloth fall behind him.

“Now,” the repairer said.

She began the required questions. The answers came correctly. On the third, the voice hesitated again.

She set down the test sheet.

“Do you remember the room with the broken shutter?”

The repairer did not move.

There was no answer.

Merasi felt heat flood her face. She had done it. She had asked something outside the form, in a workplace, in front of a man whose livelihood depended on reporting faults. She reached for the paper as though she could put the question away with it.

“The shutter was not broken,” the lantern said.

Her hand stopped.

“The wall had moved.”

The voice was thinner than she remembered, almost rubbed away around the edges. Yet there was the same impatience with an explanation he had corrected before.

“Dazhet?”

Silence.

The repairer went to the doorway. He looked out, then shifted the cloth so it hung closer against the frame. When he came back he did not sit.

“The boy will return.”

Merasi’s mouth had gone dry.

“It is me.”

“I know,” the voice said.

She had imagined hearing that. She had imagined many things during the walk to work, though she had told herself she was merely checking a faulty response. In imagination his recognition had filled the room. Here the lantern stood on a worn mat with a repair tool beside it, and she could see the repairer’s hands waiting for her to do what she had come to do.

“How long have you been—”

“Find Teshi.”

She stared at the glass.

“Your daughter?”

“Yes.”

“I do not know where she is.”

“The blue court. South of the old dye stairs. House with two wells. Her mother’s people lost the house.”

The words came unevenly, as if each additional fact cost more than the preceding one. Merasi moved closer.

“How do you know?”

“Taken there. Before this.”

“Is she—”

“Alive. Then.”

The light diminished. The repairer touched the base with the back of one finger and withdrew it.

“Enough.”

Merasi did not hear him at first.

“What happened to you?”

“Merasi.”

The use of her name frightened her more than the recognition. She looked toward the cloth.

“Find her.”

She wanted to say that she had found him. She had spent years unable to ask after him, and now he was here, speaking, and had given her another errand before she had been able to tell him what had happened in the room he remembered. The resentment was small and ugly and she felt it clearly.

“I will try.”

“Do not tell her—”

The voice failed. The light flickered low.

“What?”

The repairer lifted a cover from the shelf and set it over the lantern. Darkness enclosed the brass.

“He needs the interval,” he said.

“Do not tell her what?”

No answer came from beneath the cover.

The repairer handed Merasi the test sheet.

“Enter that the response is delayed after strain. That is true.”

She took the pen. Her hand shook now, when the work required it to be steady.

“What will you say?” she asked him.

He looked tired.

“What I have said. The response is delayed.”

“Why?”

He turned to the tools.

“The boy is coming.”

She heard the tray before she heard the feet. She made the entry, blotted it and left through the cloth with the paper held flat in both hands.

The blue court was in a part of Galanina where the old buildings had been divided and joined so often that an outer wall might conceal two houses or six.

Merasi went after work, carrying a packet of washing powder so she had something to have come for. The old dye stairs were easy to find. Their lower stones had been stained beyond any natural colour of the river; blue showed in the cracks even where the exposed surface had worn grey. Women washed there in groups, lifting cloth, striking it, rinsing, pausing to speak. A boy carried fuel past them with a basket too large for his back. No one at the water steps paid Merasi much attention until she asked for the house with two wells.

“Which family?” a woman said.

“I have come about work.”

“Then the service gate.”

She pointed without looking up.

Merasi followed the wall. At the side entrance two people waited with baskets. One was selling greens. The other had brought mended sheets. Merasi joined them, feeling the washing powder in her hand become a very poor explanation.

A woman came to the gate and examined the sheets first. She counted them without hurry, turned one toward the light and asked whether the patch had been charged as new cloth. The seller objected. They argued quietly until the woman accepted the basket and refused the additional charge. The greens went in with less ceremony.

“What?” she asked Merasi.

“Washing powder.”

“We have it.”

“I was told—”

“Who told you?”

Merasi could not answer. The woman looked at her hands, then at the packet.

“You write.”

“Yes.”

“We have a clerk.”

“I am looking for Teshi.”

The woman made no visible response, but her hand tightened on the gate.

“Who asks?”

Merasi gave her own name, softly.

The woman considered it. She had a blue mark on her forearm, partly covered by her sleeve. A brass tag hung from a cord at her waist. She touched neither while thinking.

“Come at dawn,” she said.

“Here?”

“At the lower well. Buy water if somebody asks you.”

She closed the gate before Merasi could thank her.

The packet of washing powder had split at one corner. White dust marked Merasi’s fingers. She folded the loose paper over the tear and held it carefully all the way home.

2.3 At the Well

The woman at the well sang while she wound the rope.

It was too early for most of the courtyard to be awake. A boy had opened the gate and gone back to sleep on a folded mat, leaving one foot in the passage. Merasi stepped over it. The well stood beneath a low frame whose beams had been polished by hands and rope. A row of jars waited along the wall, each different enough for its owner to recognize without a mark. The singing woman had a small jar, chipped at the lip, and a larger one with a cloth pad wrapped around its neck.

She stopped when she saw Merasi.

“Have you a jar?”

“No.”

“Then you have come a long way to look at water.”

“I was told to buy some.”

“With what will you take it home?”

Merasi looked at the waiting jars. The woman followed her glance and shook her head.

“They belong to people.”

“I know.”

“Then sit. You look as though someone has sent you to ask a question.”

Merasi sat on the low wall. The woman continued winding. The rope creaked over the beam, stopped, creaked again. When the bucket appeared, she leaned back with practised use of her weight and drew it onto the coping. Merasi rose to help. The woman moved one elbow into her path.

“You will spill it. Hold the jar instead.”

She did. Water struck the bottom of the large jar with a hollow sound, rose, quieted. The woman stopped short of the lip.

“The house with two wells?” she asked.

Merasi looked at her.

“People who wait here before dawn either work there or wish they did not.”

“Do you?”

“Wash for it. When they have washing. They always have washing, but sometimes they decide it was already washed.”

The gate woman arrived carrying two smaller vessels. She acknowledged the singer, set down the jars and looked at Merasi.

“You came.”

“Yes.”

“Your name again.”

Merasi gave it.

“Who sent you?”

“Someone who knew Teshi.”

“That includes people we do not want.”

The singer had begun winding again, slower now. Merasi could hear the sleeping boy breathing near the gate. Above them, a shutter opened a crack and closed.

“Her father,” she said.

The gate woman picked up one of her vessels and turned it in her hands. The mouth was narrow; the handle had been repaired with wire.

“What does he look like?”

Merasi described him. The woman asked which hand he wrote with. Merasi answered. She asked whether he had lived here with the girl’s mother. Merasi said no.

“And how do you know him?”

“We lived together.”

The woman looked up. Her expression changed, not into welcome, but into a recognition of the particular difficulty Merasi had brought.

“Wait.”

She took the filled vessels and left.

The singer tied off the rope.

“You are not buying water,” she said.

“No.”

“Good. You would be bad at carrying it.”

She sat beside her smaller jar. Up close she was younger than Merasi had first thought. Her hair was threaded with grey at the temples, but the skin of her hands was still smooth except where the rope had thickened it. She rubbed the base of one finger, pressing a sore place.

“Do you know when the household receives inspectors?” Merasi asked.

“I know when it orders more water.”

“Is that the same?”

“Not always. Visitors wash. Inspectors make other people wash things twice.”

She gave two days of the ordinary week and a different hour for the next expected visit. Merasi listened. The details came without an offer or a bargain. Then the woman asked where Teshi’s father had been found.

Merasi hesitated.

“You need not say the house,” the woman said. “Was he alive?”

The question was so direct that Merasi found herself unable to choose a word.

The woman watched her for a moment and understood something of the answer.

“My youngest was taken,” she said.

She said it to the jar rather than to Merasi. After a while she added that the taking had followed an agreement. One name instead of the household. She did not explain who had offered it or whether she had been allowed to finish speaking before it was accepted.

“I hear things at dawn,” she said. “Sometimes names. They speak carelessly when they think nobody has slept enough to remember.”

Merasi looked toward the open gate.

“Do you want me to ask?”

“I want you to tell me if you hear. Do not make me a promise now because you are uncomfortable sitting here.”

Merasi lowered her eyes.

“I will tell you what I hear.”

The woman nodded. She began humming again, very quietly, with no words. Merasi did not ask whose song it was.

Teshi came through the service gate with a basket of vegetable ends balanced against her hip.

She was taller than Merasi remembered Dazhet’s daughter being, which was foolish; the girl Merasi had met would not have stayed a girl while everything else changed. She had a long face, a broad mouth and a small pale mark beside one nostril. Her hair was braided close for work. She looked at Merasi without surprise.

“You were in the room above the narrow court.”

“Yes.”

“He said your roof was better.”

“It still leaks.”

Teshi put down the basket. The gate woman remained inside the entrance, close enough to hear and far enough to be able to say she had been occupied with the latch.

“Where is he?”

Merasi told her.

The young woman’s eyes did not leave her face. Merasi had expected a sound, a movement, some visible injury at the description of the lantern. Teshi listened to the whole account, then asked how often he was allowed to speak.

“I do not know.”

“Can he hear when the cover is on?”

“I do not know.”

“Will they sell him?”

“The unit is marked for export.”

The word struck Teshi differently from the others. Merasi saw her bite the inside of her lip.

“He asked me to find you.”

“Why?”

“To get you out.”

The gate woman turned her head slightly. Teshi picked a leaf from the basket and folded it until it split.

“Can you?”

“I am trying.”

“That is not the same.”

“No.”

Teshi looked past her at the well, where the woman had lifted the cloth pad to her head. For a moment they were all occupied with the ordinary difficulty of getting the full jar safely up. The gate woman went to help. Merasi stood uselessly while they settled it. When the singer walked out, her steps were so measured that the water scarcely moved.

Teshi watched until she had gone.

“I would have to take someone,” she said.

“Who?”

“A girl in the kitchen.”

“How old?”

“Fourteen. Nearly fifteen, she says.”

“Family?”

“No.”

“Would her family—”

“There is no one here to ask.”

Merasi looked at the gate woman. She gave no help.

“Two people are harder to move.”

“Then it is harder.”

“Does she want to go?”

“Yes.”

“Does she know you are asking?”

“Not this morning. We have talked about leaving before.”

Merasi felt the plan she had scarcely begun acquire a second body, another mark, another set of movements she would have to arrange. She wanted to say that Dazhet had asked for his daughter. She did not quite allow herself to say it.

Teshi saw enough.

“She sleeps outside my door. If I disappear, she is the first person they ask. They ask as if she must know because they cannot bear to think they do not.”

The gate woman said, “They will ask me too.”

Teshi turned toward her.

“You could come.”

“No.”

The reply was so quick that Merasi looked up.

“My sons are in another house,” the woman said. “Their names can be found in the same place as mine. I will not make a hole for someone to look through at them.”

She pushed the gate a little wider.

“Take that basket. You have stood here long enough.”

Teshi lifted it. Before she went inside, she looked at Merasi again.

“Tell him I have not cut my hair.”

“Why?”

“He will know.”

Merasi nodded. Then, because she did not want to leave with nothing but instructions she did not understand, she asked whether Teshi had enough to eat.

The young woman almost smiled.

“I work in a kitchen.”

“That can mean either thing.”

This time she did smile, briefly.

“Yes,” she said. “It can.”

The gate woman’s name was Heshra.

Merasi learned it the following morning, when she brought a jar and bought water properly. Heshra watched her struggle with the full vessel, took some out, and said she would be less conspicuous carrying what she could manage. She had arranged another meeting at the service gate while the cook inspected a delivery of oil.

The younger girl was called Nemi. She had a narrow face and a way of keeping her elbows against her body as if she had learned to take up little room. She listened to Merasi’s questions, answered yes too quickly twice, and finally said that she wanted to leave but did not want to walk through the front court.

“No one proposes that,” Teshi said.

“You went there yesterday.”

“To take dishes.”

“They saw you.”

“They see me every day.”

Nemi looked at Merasi.

“He looks at who carries what. The one who writes. He says nothing, but he looks.”

Merasi recognized the kind of clerk she meant. Not necessarily cruel. Attentive, which might be worse for their purposes.

“Then we do not change what you carry until you are beyond him.”

Nemi considered this. It was the first answer that appeared to give her any comfort.

Heshra described the household routes. Hot food went through the central passage because the serving rooms lay above it. Dirty cloth and kitchen waste went through the rear yard and a covered walk to a smaller gate opening near the dye stairs. The clerk watched the central passage. The rear gate keeper checked what left in baskets, principally to prevent food being taken. Laundry was counted by bundle rather than opened at the gate because no one wanted to handle what came from the sick room.

“Are there sick people?” Merasi asked.

“An old uncle. Sometimes his complaints are worse than the sickness. The cloth is dirty either way.”

“Could they leave with the washerwomen?”

“Teshi takes bundles now. Nemi takes kitchen waste.”

“Together?”

“Sometimes. Do not make it always, suddenly.”

Heshra gave the information as if explaining an ordinary task to a slow new worker. She stopped when footsteps approached, took a bowl from a shelf and began complaining that the edge was cracked. Teshi answered sharply, defending the person who had washed it. Nemi ducked her head. Merasi stepped back against the wall.

A man went past carrying an empty tray. He glanced at them, heard the quarrel and continued. Heshra waited until the sound of the tray had ended before setting down the bowl.

“There is nothing wrong with it,” she said to Nemi, who was still examining the crack.

The girl put it down quickly. Teshi touched her wrist.

Afterward, Merasi asked Heshra what she could bring in return.

“News from Deremet,” the woman said. “It was a village west of the lower salt road. I do not know if it is a village now.”

“Family?”

“It was where I lived.”

“I can ask among the carriers.”

“Ask as though you want to buy something.”

Merasi nodded.

She had begun to understand why Heshra disliked promises. There were now four people whose questions she carried back to the repair yard, and only one of those questions concerned herself.

2.4 A Fitting Repaired Twice

Dazhet knew what Teshi meant about her hair.

For several moments after Merasi told him, no response came from the lantern. She was afraid the speaking interval had ended. The repairer had allowed her only the time needed to repeat a delayed test; the boy was outside grinding a strip under the supervisor’s eye.

Then the light moved against the glass.

“She wanted to cut it. When she was small. So no one could pull it.”

Merasi remembered a girl with thick braids looking at her from a doorway. She did not remember anyone pulling them.

“Who did?”

“Children. Her mother when it tangled. Me, once, trying to help.”

The pause was longer than before.

“She said she would grow it down to her feet and make us all carry it.”

Merasi could hear the pleasure in his voice, faint but unmistakable. She felt glad, then lonely, and was ashamed of the second feeling while the first still warmed her.

“She has a girl she wants to bring.”

“Bring her.”

“It is not as easy as saying it.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

The words came too sharply. The repairer, working at the other end of the bench, stopped moving a tool.

Merasi looked at the brass cage. Dazhet had no place to put his hands, no face with which to show her anger or fatigue. The light was all she could see. She had wanted him to understand how frightened she was and had spoken as if he were the one outside the danger.

“I am sorry,” she said.

“Do not leave her here to get me out.”

“I am trying to get all of you out.”

“If it is one—”

“Do not decide before I have tried.”

The light rose, then subsided. When he spoke again, the voice was quiet enough that she had to bend near the glass.

“I cannot see how to help you.”

She put her hand on the bench beside him. For the first time since recognizing him she was able to sit without asking another question.

The repairer worked. Outside, the boy coughed over his grinding. A cart crossed the yard with a loose wheel that knocked once per turn.

After a while Dazhet asked whether the shutter had been fixed.

“The owner says it is the wall.”

“It is.”

“I know.”

“Have you another room?”

“No.”

“Merasi.”

She waited.

“I was very tired of that room.”

She laughed before she could stop herself. It hurt. She had been keeping it for him in a way she had never admitted, and he had remembered its heat and the narrow bed and the water that came through after rain. She remembered those things too. She had been sleeping in them.

“So am I,” she said.

A little knock came from the glass. Merasi drew her hand back.

“Was that you?”

“No.”

The repairer looked up, listened, and went to the shutter. He lowered it a finger’s width. The noise stopped.

“Heat,” he said. “Keep your sleeve clear of the upper fitting.”

Merasi folded her arms in her lap. She had been leaning near it without feeling how warm the metal had become. Dazhet had endured every test she had entered that morning. She glanced at the work sheet, then turned it face down.

“Can you feel that?” she asked.

“The light?”

“The heat.”

There was no answer. She waited, afraid that he was considering how much she could bear to be told.

“I don’t know what you mean by heat,” he said at last.

She looked at the repairer. He had returned to his work. He did not help her.

“It is warm by my hand.”

“Then take your hand away.”

She did. He laughed, or something in the lantern made the beginning of the sound. It broke off so suddenly that she leaned forward again.

“Dazhet?”

“Here.”

The repairer’s tool moved along the bench. Merasi could hear the boy outside scraping his shoes together to rid them of filings.

She tried to think of something that would not require Dazhet to describe being where he was.

“The woman who sold the fried bread has moved her stall.”

“Which woman?”

“The one you said put yesterday’s pieces underneath.”

“She did.”

“She has a roof now. A proper one. The rain comes off the front and lands on the customer.”

“Then she has improved her own position.”

Merasi smiled. “Her daughter serves.”

“The little one?”

“She is taller than her mother.”

He was silent again. Merasi wished she had told him about someone who had not grown, married, moved, died. The stall itself had changed twice; its old position was occupied by a man selling cheap seals. Everywhere she tried to take him there would be someone else at the door.

“She still puts yesterday’s pieces underneath,” she said.

“That is reassuring.”

The humour in his voice was faint, but she heard it. She sat closer without touching the lantern.

For a while she told him about the cousins across the landing. He had known the loud one before her marriage. The quiet cousin was new to him. Merasi described their quarrel over a cooking pot: one had claimed the other could ruin any food merely by approaching it; the other had replied that it was difficult to improve food already burnt. Dazhet wanted to know whose pot had been used. Merasi could not remember. He said the account was incomplete without that information.

She had expected to weep when she heard him again. Instead she found herself explaining the arrangement of a room he had never entered, trying to make him understand why the cousin could not have seen what was happening from the doorway. She used a rag, a file and her folded work sheet to show him. Halfway through, she stopped.

“Can you see this?”

“Some.”

“What part?”

“Your hand when it moves.”

She left it still above the bench. The lantern’s light steadied.

“Not now?”

“No.”

She moved one finger.

“There,” he said.

Merasi moved the file slowly from one side of the rag to the other. She began again, describing where the doorway stood. This time he interrupted to ask questions. He could follow the quarrel well enough to take the wrong cousin’s side. She told him so. When she laughed, she heard the repairer set down his tool, then pick it up again without looking at her.

Afterward Dazhet asked for water.

She had already turned toward the jug when he said, “No. I forgot.”

Her hand stayed lifted.

“I do that,” he said.

Merasi brought the jug back anyway and drank. She was thirsty. She had been thirsty for hours without knowing it. A drop went down her chin, and she wiped it with the heel of her hand because the work cloth was dirty.

“Better?” he asked.

“Yes.”

She could not offer him anything. She set the cup where he might see her move it and told him that the loud cousin had eventually broken the pot herself.

The repairer put down his tool.

“Cover now.”

She rose. The lantern said nothing more.

The bent nail looked useless.

It was shorter than Merasi’s smallest finger, bent near the head and worn flat along one edge. The repairer set it on a square of dark cloth and placed a discarded brass fitting beside it.

“This construction,” he said. “Only where the tongue passes under the return.”

He inserted the flat edge into a slot she would not have noticed. The nail turned, stopped, then yielded a little farther under pressure. Something inside the fitting clicked.

“Once.”

“Why once?”

“The return breaks. You will not close it again.”

“But it opens.”

“This opens. You must still draw the seam without splitting the glass.”

He made her try on the discarded piece. Her first attempt did nothing. The second slipped and scratched the brass. He moved her hand slightly, not taking the work from her. On the third try she felt the little turn beneath her thumb.

“There,” he said.

The word was almost tender. She looked up and found his face as tired as before.

“Did you put it there?”

He took the broken fitting and turned it under the light.

“I make things that close. Sometimes I leave a way for them to open.”

“Do others know?”

“Fewer than should. More than is safe.”

“What happens when it opens?”

“That depends on who is inside.”

“He will be free?”

“Of this fitting.”

“And then?”

The repairer wrapped the damaged piece.

“I cannot give him back what was taken to put him there. I cannot tell you how long he will stay near, or what else will hear him when the glass no longer keeps him.”

Merasi stared at the nail.

“Can he follow someone?”

“Some do. Some go at once. You may call. He may answer.”

The repairer began clearing the bench. It was not dismissal so much as refusal to leave dangerous things lying where someone could see them.

“Why give this to me?” she asked.

He paused with a tool in his hand.

“I thought you might use it.”

She waited, expecting more. He put the tool away.

The boy returned with the strip he had been grinding. The repairer examined it and showed him a place that needed another pass. While they spoke, Merasi folded the dark cloth around the nail and put it inside the seam of her sleeve.

The departure order came at midday.

The lot would leave Galanina the following evening, a day earlier than the receiving office had first required. An Esoiti delegation was inspecting holdings along the river and wanted its assigned lighting delivered before it moved on. The supervisor read the instruction twice, then began assigning work in a voice that rose whenever someone made him repeat a name.

Merasi entered the change. Her pen did not pause. It frightened her afterward that she had managed to write so neatly while the time she needed disappeared beneath the words.

The defective lantern had to pass its observation by morning. If it failed, the supervisor would request a waiver for the substitute. He said this within her hearing, then told her to prepare both forms so neither decision would delay the lot.

She prepared the successful-test form. She prepared the substitute form as well, leaving the authorization blank.

At the meal break she went to the gate with the pretext of buying bread. The boy who carried the seller’s basket had grown into a tunic patched across the shoulders. He asked how many and held out his hand.

“Two. Do you know a washerwoman called Heshra?”

“Lots of women wash.”

“The house by the dye stairs. Two wells.”

“I deliver there.”

“Tell her the oil delivery is tomorrow evening.”

“Is it?”

“Tell her.”

He looked at the coin, then at her face.

“I will tell her what you said.”

She gave him the proper price for the bread and another small coin for the errand. Too much would be remembered. Too little would be repeated as a complaint. She did not know whether she had judged it correctly.

The boy went away. She stood with the bread and found she could not eat.

At home, she laid out what she could carry.

The room looked altered merely because she had begun choosing. A spare wrapper, the money for the next payment, a needle, thread, a small knife used for food, the washing powder in its repaired packet. Her work permits could explain her presence at some gates and betray her at others. She set them aside and came back to them three times.

The blue bowl was too large and breakable. She knew that immediately. She wrapped it anyway, took it out again, washed it though it was already clean, and set it on the floor beside the bed.

Someone knocked. The quiet cousin stood outside holding a little pot.

“Too much cooked,” she said.

Merasi let her in. They sat with the pot between them and ate without speaking for a while. The woman looked at the things on the bed. She did not ask where Merasi was going.

“The latch holds,” she said.

“Good.”

“He used the old screws. My sister made him replace one.”

“She would.”

A little smile. Then they ate again.

Merasi wanted to tell her everything. The urge was so strong that she had to put down her spoon. She wanted another person in the room to know what she was choosing, to see that she had not merely failed to pay for next month and slipped away. The woman opposite her had work, family, a repaired latch and no reason to be asked to carry this knowledge.

“Would you take the bowl?” Merasi asked.

The woman looked at the floor.

“The blue one?”

“Yes.”

“For how long?”

“As long as you wish.”

She considered the question beneath the answer and chose not to speak to it.

“It is a good bowl.”

“There is a chip.”

“Under the rim. I see it.”

Merasi gave it to her wrapped in a cloth. The woman took it with both hands.

When she had gone, Merasi put the permits inside her spare wrapper, stitched the bent nail into the cuff she would wear tomorrow, and lay down without undressing. She listened to the cousins talking across the landing. Their voices rose over the question of where the new bowl should stand, and for a few minutes she was able to sleep.

2.5 Permission

The inspectors arrived before the bread.

Merasi knew something was wrong when the gate remained shut after the first workers had entered. The boy with the basket waited outside in a patch of sun, changing the strap from one shoulder to the other. A member of the Black Hand stood inside the opening, dressed without a badge but with a stillness that kept people from asking him to move.

The supervisor had washed. His burnt thumb was wrapped in clean cloth. He spoke to an Esoiti clerk beside the testing bench, nodding more often than he needed to, while the repairers gathered at their workstations and attempted to look as though they had been busy for a long time.

Merasi set down her bag.

“Your observations,” the supervisor said.
