She brought the test sheets. The clerk took them, compared the lot number with his own list and handed them to the Black Hand man, who had left the gate without anyone seeing who replaced him.

He was ordinary-looking. That was the first thing Merasi noticed and the thing she later found hardest to describe. A man approaching middle age, with close-cut hair, a narrow mouth and a small fold in one eyelid. His cuffs were clean but the edges had been turned in to conceal wear. He read quickly, then slowed at the fourth unit’s entry.

“You found this?”

“Yes.”

“Before the change of departure?”

“Yes.”

“Who ordered the repair?”

“The supervisor.”

“Who identified the earlier work as faulty?”

“The repairer.”

“Which?”

She gave his bench designation. The man looked across the yard at him.

“Name.”

Merasi gave it. She had learned it from his signature years before and rarely used it aloud. Saying it now felt like placing something on the testing cloth.

The man made a mark.

The inspection continued. It was thorough in places that mattered and inattentive in others. The Esoiti clerk examined the response forms, the signal assignments and the seals on the lower holding records. A young attendant checked the cases for unauthorized marks. He found a workman’s tally scratched inside a lid and required it to be planed away. The bread boy was admitted at last, but nobody bought until the supervisor told them they could.

Merasi took her two loaves. The boy held the basket low and said, “She said she knows.”

“Who?”

“The oil.”

He looked frightened by her question. She accepted the bread and did not give him another message.

The Black Hand man was watching from the bench.

At midday they were taken to the upper receiving court.

Only the supervisor and clerks were required, with one representative from each repair room. Merasi went because her observation had interrupted the export lot. The rest of the yard remained at work under a guard who did not give instructions and did not need to. The repairer carried his tool roll unopened. Merasi walked beside him without speaking.

The court lay within an old building whose black stone retained the morning’s cool. Brass screens divided the waiting spaces. Through them she could see the shapes of people seated beyond, some straight-backed, some leaning toward a neighbour. No one spoke above the level at which their words would carry to the next enclosure. Servants brought water in small cups. The water tasted faintly metallic.

The supervisor kept rubbing the clean bandage on his thumb. Merasi wanted to tell him to stop. The small repetitive movement had become unbearable.

An Esoiti official called their lot. They went forward into a longer room with a high opening at one end. Light fell through it onto a strip of empty floor. Beyond that, in shadow, someone sat.

Merasi had seen a Sister once, years before, from the far end of a procession route. She had remembered the absence of ordinary response in the crowd nearest the figure: people lowering their faces without the bustle and adjustment that accompanied a human dignitary. Here she could see the veil distinctly. It hung motionless although air moved the cloth at the high opening.

She looked down.

The official described the export delays. He did not mention Merasi’s name. He described irregularities in workshop oversight, an insufficiently disciplined maintenance schedule and the possibility that deliberate lenience toward defective bindings had become habitual. He proposed a correction: review of the repairers’ guarantees, reassignment of two families attached to the workshops and retention of wages until the costs of delay were recovered.

The supervisor’s thumb stopped moving.

The official waited.

Somewhere behind Merasi a person swallowed. She heard it as clearly as a dropped cup. The strip of sun on the floor showed every pale mote passing through it. One settled against the toe of her shoe. She watched it because she did not know where else to put her eyes.

A voice from the shadow said, “You have prepared the names.”

It was not a question.

The official bowed.

“Yes.”

“Then proceed.”

No one touched Merasi. No one threatened her. She stood in the cool room while the official took his prepared paper from a sleeve, and she understood that he had arrived knowing what he wished permission to do.

The repairer beside her moved one finger against the outside of his tool roll.

They were dismissed.

In the waiting enclosure the supervisor sat down too abruptly and spilled his water. The servant brought a cloth. He apologized to her twice, then seemed to hear himself and stopped. The Black Hand accountant waited by the exit with Merasi’s observation sheet.

“A moment,” he said.

The others went on. The repairer did not look back.

The accountant took her into a small room that had probably once served as a store. A line on the wall showed where shelves had stood. He offered no chair, but there were two, and after a moment Merasi sat in one. He sat opposite.

“You were careful with the fault.”

“It was visible.”

“After lifting. Not before.”

“The handle was crooked.”

“Many handles are crooked.”

He laid the observation flat.

“You prevented an uncooled substitute from being installed.”

“It would not have met the interval.”

“The supervisor could have requested a waiver.”

“He is requesting one now.”

“Yes.”

The man studied her. She tried to find in his face what he wanted and found only attention.

“The repairer’s family is among those named for reassignment,” he said.

Merasi felt herself move before she could prevent it. A small straightening, no more.

“You know them?”

“No.”

“You know him.”

“I work beside his room.”

“Has he spoken to you about the bindings?”

“He explains faults when I ask.”

“Has he expressed reluctance to repair them?”

“He repairs them.”

The accountant tapped one corner of the page.

“You could be a witness to insufficient care at the earlier repair. That would distinguish your diligence from his failure. It would also explain why you were so interested in the unit.”

She looked at the old line on the wall. One end was darker, where the shelf had protected stone from smoke. She could imagine the former room full of ordinary things, jars or folded cloth, a person kneeling to reach the lower shelf. She wanted to be in that room, at that time, with a simple object to fetch.

“He identified the fault himself,” she said.

“After you noticed it.”

“Yes.”

“He signed the earlier work.”

“Yes.”

“Then you need not invent anything.”

She understood the offered version. All its parts were true. It would leave the repairer responsible for whatever larger suspicion the official wished to discover. It would leave her observant and obedient, perhaps even useful.

“The signature belongs to his bench,” she said. “He told me it was not his hand.”

“Did you verify that?”

“No.”

“Then you are repeating his account.”

“Yes.”

The man waited. She could feel the bent nail inside her cuff when she moved her wrist. She kept her hand still.

“I recorded what I observed,” she said. “I can repeat it. I cannot say why the earlier work passed without examining the fitting and speaking to the person who made it.”

“Do that.”

She looked at him.

“Before departure. Bring me the account. If the fault was ordinary, say so precisely. If you find something else, do not protect a bench because you eat in the same yard.”

He folded the sheet.

“You will remain until the lot is dispatched.”

She had gained time and lost the freedom to leave early. She could not tell whether he believed her or had decided to watch what she did next.

At the door he said, “The bread boy. What did he bring besides bread?”

Merasi stopped.

“An answer about a delivery of oil.”

“To you?”

“To a household where I had asked after work.”

“Do you intend to leave this employment?”

She turned. For the first time, the truth was useful without needing to be made smaller.

“Yes.”

He looked at her cuffs, her bag, the tiredness she could no longer conceal.

“After the dispatch,” he said.

The repairer heard the account without interrupting.

When she reached the proposed distinction between her diligence and his fault, he looked away. Merasi stopped.

“I did not agree.”

“You told him what I said about the hand.”

“Yes.”

“The boy’s hand.”

She had not thought of that. She felt sick.

“Was it?”

“The handle was his. The seam was mine. Now they will ask him how much he knows.”

“I was trying—”

“I know.”

He did not say it kindly. He resumed polishing a fitting, working the same patch too long before noticing and moving his cloth.

The boy sat at the far end, eating bread with his eyes on the floor. Merasi wanted to apologize to him and could not do it without telling him more than he knew. She put the formal inquiry on the bench.

“We have until departure.”

“For what?”

“For an account of ordinary failure.”

The repairer laughed once, without humour.

“I can give him that.”

“Can the lantern remain here until then?”

“It must cool again after the inspection. They strained it.”

“Then this evening.”

He looked at her cuff. She did not touch it.

“Do you know where you will go?”

“Toward the outer landing. There is a laundry route from the house.”

“And you?”

She had not solved that part. He saw it.

“The broken cases leave after dispatch,” he said. “Wood and cloth, under the rear awning. The man who carries them never takes as many as he agrees. The supervisor makes him come twice.”

“Will he help?”

“No. He will carry cases. Do not ask him to help.”

He pointed to the cloth wall behind the storage bench. Beyond it lay the narrow gap between the repair building and the yard’s outer wall. Merasi had seen workers pass through to clear ash from the small furnace. She had never used it herself.

“It opens near the disposal gate. You will be filthy.”

She nodded.

“I can be filthy.”

He looked at her work clothes as if doubtful, then set the polishing cloth aside.

The boy had finished his bread. He came to the bench carrying the handle the accountant would ask about.

“Do I say I made it?”

“Yes,” the repairer said.

“Do I say you watched?”

“I did watch.”

“You were outside for some of it.”

“I watched the finishing.”

The boy looked from him to Merasi. She had never seen his face so still. When the supervisor shouted at him he usually looked sullen; he had a way of making obedience look like a favour he was considering. That had disappeared.

Merasi pulled the stool out.

“Show me what you did.”

He did not sit.

“I know what I did.”

“I need to write it.”

“You wrote it before.”

The repairer began to speak. Merasi shook her head, then wished she had not. She had no right to manage him in front of the boy.

“I wrote what he told me,” she said. “Now they have asked for more. I should have asked you first.”

“Would that have changed it?”

She could not promise him that. She drew the sheet toward her and left the stool where it was.

The boy put the handle on the bench. He showed her the first cut, the part he had annealed, the point at which the repairer had sent him out because the metal was too hot for the next turn. Merasi wrote slowly. The boy watched each word form. When she used the term corrected, he pointed at it.

“I had not finished.”

She struck it out and put completed. He read the sentence again, moving his mouth.

Outside, someone called for the repairer. He ignored the first call. At the second he set his tools in a row and went, leaving the cloth wall moving behind him. Merasi and the boy stayed with the sheet between them.

“Could I have made the seam fail?” he asked.

“The handle could pull against it.”

“Did mine?”

“I did not see it do that.”

“Then write that.”

She did. He sat at last, on the edge of the stool, with his feet braced beneath it.

They spent the rest of the interval describing ordinary work. Merasi had to ask him to repeat several things. He began to speak more quickly when he found that she did not know how a handle was held during cooling, and used a loop of discarded wire to demonstrate. His hands were deft. There was a little thickened scar at the base of his thumb. He showed her how the work could look sound and still pull awry when lifted.

“Who checks it?” she asked.

“I do. Then he does.”

“And after it is fitted?”

“You do.”

She looked at the sheet. The boy waited.

“Yes,” she said, and wrote it.

When the repairer returned, the boy was arguing over whether a little meant anything useful in an account of the turn. He sounded almost like himself. The repairer listened, took up the wire and showed them both the limit with his thumbnail. Merasi marked the place in a small drawing. She made two copies of the account, read them aloud, and asked each of them whether she had left anything out.

The boy said no. The repairer added the time at which the lantern had been allowed to cool.

Merasi sanded the ink. Her wrist hurt. She had spent the whole afternoon keeping the bent nail pressed against the inside of her arm, as though someone could see it through the cloth if she relaxed.

The boy picked up his handle.

“Will you be here when they ask?”

“Until the dispatch.”

He nodded. She did not tell him she would try to leave before anyone asked again.

That evening Heshra sent word through a washerwoman who came to the yard for discarded wrapping cloth. The rear household route was still possible. The small kitchen cart would pass the lower gate after the inspection meal, but Nemi was too frightened to go with it. Teshi had refused to force her. They would make their decision at the last ordinary carrying time.

Merasi stood reading the message, which concerned laundry and said nothing that would have convicted anyone alone. She wanted to send back an order to be ready. Instead she sent the hour at which she would try to reach the landing and asked the washerwoman to repeat it exactly.

The woman charged for the second journey. Merasi paid.

2.6 Hot Dishes Out

By late afternoon the household had used nearly every clean dish it owned.

Merasi knew this because Heshra had told her to expect it, and because the washerwoman returned to the repair yard carrying a bundle that smelled of spilt food and hot water. The inspection meal had run long. The household clerk had kept his place near the central passage, watching the dishes go up and the dirty ones return. The kitchen cart had not come.

“Not come at all?” Merasi asked.

“Axle. The driver sent a boy to tell them. They hit the boy for bringing the news late.”

Merasi stared at the bundle.

The woman shifted it against her hip.

“They have another way. Your girl says do your own work.”

“Which girl?”

“The tall one. She was annoyed.”

Merasi almost laughed. It came out as a short breath that hurt her chest.

The washerwoman went to collect her payment from the wrapping room. Merasi remained beside the testing bench. The eleven sound lanterns stood in their cases, fitted and covered. The fourth place remained open. The faulty lantern was under observation in the repair room, its final test due before the lot left. The accountant had returned and sat at the supervisor’s desk with the inquiry papers before him.

She could see his hand through the doorway.

The repairer came to fetch her.

“Test.”

She took the form.

Inside, the boy was asleep on a folded sack. His cheek rested on one bare arm. The repairer had sent him for food and he had returned too tired to eat all of it. A piece of bread lay beside his knee.

“He knows?” Merasi whispered.

“No.”

“Will they ask him?”

“Yes.”

The repairer moved the lantern into the screened corner where damaged glass was usually examined. A cloth could be drawn around it to keep splinters from the rest of the room. He set a shallow tray below the base.

“If it breaks, leave the pieces there.”

Merasi took the nail from her cuff. The stitches resisted. She had made them too careful. She used the small food knife to cut the last thread and nearly dropped the nail into the floor dust.

The repairer caught it.

“Breathe.”

She realized she had not been.

He gave it back.

The first required question brought Dazhet’s voice, weak but clear. The second established the number. The third produced a flare at the seam. It was still faulty. The new adjustment had not held through strain.

The repairer looked toward the sleeping boy.

“They will have to accept a partial dispatch,” he said quietly. “Or a written waiver for tomorrow. Not another unit tonight.”

“Can you enter it?”

“I have.”

He showed her the repair finding. Fault remained after repeated adjustment; further strain risked breakage. It would keep the substitute in the lower rooms, at least through this departure. She did not mistake that for saving the substitute. It prevented her escape from demanding another body immediately in exchange.

Outside, the supervisor called for the forms.

The repairer took the legitimate fault finding and went out.

Merasi drew the screening cloth around the lantern. She could hear Dazhet inside, a low uneven sound, not words. She sat on the stool because her knees were trembling and placed the tray closer.

“I have found her,” she said.

“I know.”

“She is trying to leave. With the girl.”

“Now?”

“Now.”

The light moved.

“I can open this. I do not know what happens afterward.”

Dazhet was silent for so long that she thought he had failed to hear.

“Do it,” he said.

She slid the nail beneath the return.

Nothing happened. She adjusted her grip as the repairer had shown her and pressed. The nail scraped, caught. She could feel the small hidden resistance through the metal. Outside, voices rose. The supervisor was refusing the delay; the repairer was explaining that a broken fitting would give him a larger delay. The accountant asked a question in a voice too quiet for Merasi to hear.

She turned the nail.

Something gave inside the brass with a sound so slight that it might have been in her own hand. The light struck the glass. She nearly let go. The nail had twisted beneath the head and would not come out.

She left it.

The seam had to be drawn apart. She had watched the repairer do this with a thin blade. Her knife was too thick. She found the proper tool on the bench, brought it back and inserted the edge carefully. The glass trembled under her hand. A line of air moved against her fingers, cold enough to hurt.

“Wait,” Dazhet said.

She stopped at once.

Outside, someone approached the cloth wall. She heard a foot drag, then the scrape of the boy waking. The repairer said his name sharply and told him to fetch the discarded fitting. The footsteps turned away.

“I am afraid,” Dazhet said.

Merasi bent closer.

“So am I.”

She waited with the blade held still. Her wrist ached. A drop of sweat ran down the side of her nose and she could not spare a hand to wipe it.

The light withdrew from the seam, then returned.

“Now.”

She drew it open.

The room changed temperature. The cold moved across her face and into her open mouth. The light went out inside the lantern, leaving the dirty glass almost black. Somewhere beyond the screening cloth the boy made a startled sound.

Merasi sat with the broken fitting in her hands.

“Dazhet?”

There was no answer from the lantern.

She lowered it into the tray. One edge of the glass cracked against the brass and fell inward. She had expected release to have a visible direction. She could not tell whether he had gone past her, through the wall or nowhere at all.

Then a voice near her shoulder said, “Where?”

She turned too quickly and struck her knee against the bench.

“The lower dye stairs. Then the outer landing.”

The cold moved away.

“Wait,” she whispered.

It paused, or she imagined it did. She could feel her own breath against her lips now.

“I am coming.”

No answer. The air warmed by degrees.

The repairer had left the design record beneath a weighted board.

Merasi knew which sheet it was because he had shown her the serial mark. It recorded the construction of the repaired batch and the workers assigned to it. The flaw could be traced from the broken unit to the drawing, then to the bench. Destroying the sheet would not make the repairer innocent in the eyes of anyone determined to blame him. It would remove the diagram by which an inspector might find the same opening in every cage he had made.

She lifted the board.

The sheet lay beneath two ordinary inventories. She took it, folded it once and carried it to the small furnace. Its heat pressed against her knees. The draft would take loose paper readily, but not all at once. She tore the drawing into strips, feeding each into the hottest place, watching ink darken and curl. The smell was sharp.

The boy saw her.

He stood beside the far bench with the discarded fitting in his hand. For a moment neither moved.

“Go to him,” she said.

The boy looked toward the yard. He was old enough to know that something was wrong and young enough still to believe someone might explain it if he waited. Merasi wanted to give him an explanation he could repeat safely. She could think only of another instruction.

“He asked for that.”

The boy went.

The last strip burned. She put the inventories back under the board and left the empty space where the drawing had been. Her own copy register would show she had consulted it. She could not remove that without emptying a whole book. She left it.

At the rear cloth she paused to listen.

The disposal gate opened with a heavy scrape. Men were arguing about the number of broken cases loaded. One said the agreement was for dry wood, not wet cloth. The other said he could take the whole lot or leave it. This was the ordinary quarrel the repairer had described, occurring with an indifference to her need that made her suddenly grateful.

She went through the cloth into the ash passage.

It was narrower than she expected. The outer wall pressed against her shoulder. Ash had gathered ankle-deep near the furnace opening, light on top and clotted beneath. She bent to pass a low projection and caught her sleeve. The cloth tore. She stayed still until the voices outside covered the sound, then worked the sleeve free.

At the passage’s end she could see the disposal cart. Its driver stood with his back to her, refusing another bundle. Beyond him the gate remained open. She had to cross three exposed paces to reach its inner side.

She waited too long.

The driver accepted the bundle and turned. She pressed back into the ash. He looked toward the passage, saw her, and frowned.

“What are you doing there?”

“Clearing behind.”

“Then clear the cloth out too. They put it with the wood and expect me to burn it.”

He pulled a dirty wrapping from the cart and threw it toward her. She caught it badly, dust rising around her face.

The driver went to the front of the cart. She carried the dirty cloth into the open as if she had been sent to take it somewhere. The gate worker looked at the bundle, then at her ash-covered clothes, and moved aside to let the cart pass. She went with its rear wheel, close enough that the gate hid her for an instant as it swung.

Outside, evening light struck her eyes.

She walked. She did not run until she had turned the second corner.

At the dye stairs she found no one waiting.

Cloth hung over the lower rails. The water below was dark with reflections from the opposite bank. A woman kneeling at the last step looked up when Merasi approached and then looked away. Merasi’s torn sleeve and blackened clothes had made her somebody less worth noticing.

She went down three steps, stopped and looked along the bank.

Teshi had said to do her own work. Heshra had given the outer landing. The cart had failed to come. The two young women might still be inside the house; they might have reached the water and gone; they might have been stopped in the laundry walk. Merasi could return to the service gate and ask, bringing the repair yard’s pursuit behind her if pursuit had begun. She could wait here until the agreed hour passed. She could go to the landing.

She stood long enough for the kneeling woman to glance at her again.

“Are you washing?”

“No.”

“Then let me put the cloth there.”

Merasi moved. The woman spread a length of linen across the step where she had been standing. It bore a brown stain near one edge and the stitched mark of a household. Merasi recognized the mark from Heshra’s gate.

“Who brought that?”

The woman looked at her more carefully.

“Why?”

“I was to meet—”

“Then meet them elsewhere.”

“Have they passed?”

The woman wrung a corner of the linen. Water ran over her hands.

“Tall girl. Younger one holding a basket as if someone might steal it. They helped me carry. Went along the lower path.”

“When?”

“Before you.”

She returned to her work.

Merasi wanted to embrace her. She wanted to ask whether the girls had seemed frightened, whether a man had watched them, whether a cold movement had passed along the water. Instead she thanked her and went toward the outer landing.

Behind her the woman called, “Do not walk like that.”

Merasi stopped.

“Like what?”

“Like every doorway knows your name.”

She tried to change her pace. For the next ten steps she could think of nothing except where to put her feet.

2.7 The Outer Landing

The outer landing was still a city landing.

Merasi had imagined a dark bank, a boat waiting clear of the last houses, somewhere that would begin to feel outside. Instead she found a queue. Three carts waited under a patched awning. A man counted bundles beside a lamp. Two women sat on a rolled sail, arguing about whether the small boat at the steps could take all the people who intended to board it. Beyond them another boat lay against the far post, its crew hauling a covering over sacks.

Teshi stood beside the first cart with Nemi behind her.

Merasi almost called. Then she saw the man holding Teshi’s wrist.

He wore the landing keeper’s cloth tied at one shoulder. His grip was not violent, and that made the sight worse: he held her as an ordinary part of his work, keeping a person where a question required them to remain. Teshi stood very straight. Nemi clutched her basket with both hands.

Merasi approached slowly.

The man looked at her ash-covered clothes and back at the young women.

“Another one?”

“I am travelling with them.”

“Under whose authorization?”

She took out her work permit. It was folded inside the spare wrapper, which now had a dark smear along one edge. He read it, looking from the writing to her face.

“This is for the repair yard.”

“Yes. I have left.”

“It does not permit household transfers.”

“They are carrying laundry.”

“Then they should return by the laundry gate.”

Teshi spoke before Merasi could.

“We have delivered. We are going to the washer’s store.”

“Which washer?”

Teshi gave a name Merasi did not recognize.

“Her mark?”

Nemi fumbled inside the basket. The man tightened his grip on Teshi, expecting movement. Merasi made herself stay still. Nemi produced a cloth strip with a stitched sign and handed it over.

He examined it.

“Delivery, not passage.”

“The store is on the lower bank,” Teshi said.

“Then walk.”

“It closes at the second bell.”

“Then you should have come earlier.”

Behind them someone complained about the queue. The keeper ignored it. Merasi saw that he was not searching for fugitives; he was dealing with a form of request he disliked, a household sending marked workers into somebody else’s charge without paperwork clear enough to protect him. Every delay made the rest of the landing notice them more.

She looked at Teshi. The young woman did not look back. Her attention was fixed on the keeper’s hand.

“What is the fee for the laundry boat?” Merasi asked.

He told her.

She had enough to pay for three and little left afterward. That did not solve the authorization. She opened her purse anyway, giving his eyes another object to follow.

A woman came down the steps from the second boat.

“Those bundles are mine,” she said.

Merasi recognized her as the washerwoman who had carried the messages. Relief made her dizzy.

The keeper recognized her too, without pleasure.

“Then take them. These two return to their house.”

“They are bringing them to my store.”

“Your cloth gives delivery only.”

“Because I did not expect the kitchen cart to break its axle. Would you like the axle? I can fetch it.”

The people in the queue laughed. The keeper’s mouth tightened.

“Put the changed destination in writing.”

The washerwoman looked at Merasi.

“You write.”

Merasi understood. She could enter an ordinary change of delivery destination under the washerwoman’s own authority. It would not free the two workers. It would explain why they were on the boat. Her handwriting and work designation would remain at the landing, pointing directly away from Galanina if anyone came after them.

The keeper released Teshi’s wrist to fetch paper.

Merasi wrote.

She stated the washer’s store as destination, named the two workers by the household identifiers Heshra had supplied and added the reason for the changed route. The washerwoman made her mark. The keeper asked Merasi to identify herself beneath the text. She did so.

“Fee,” he said.

She paid for three.

When he returned her permit, he held it for a moment between his fingers.

“You should have had this ready.”

“Yes.”

He let go.

Teshi lifted the basket from Nemi’s arms. The younger girl held on until Teshi said her name, then released it. They went down the steps after the washerwoman. Merasi followed, feeling each stair through the thin soles of her shoes.

At the boat, the washerwoman put a hand against her chest to stop her.

“Not this one.”

Merasi stared.

“Your name is on the paper now. You go on the first boat. I take them to my store. If somebody looks, that is what happened.”

“Where is the store?”

“You wrote it.”

“And after?”

“After I have had them under a roof, I will decide which door they use.”

Teshi looked at Merasi then.

“I know her.”

“From the house?”

“Yes.”

“Do you trust—”

“I know her,” Teshi repeated.

The washerwoman’s face was hard with impatience. Merasi disliked her suddenly: the fees, the withheld information, the way she made herself necessary while refusing to account for the next step. She also knew she had no safer place to offer.

“Your father may come,” she told Teshi quietly.

The young woman looked past her at the water.

“Is he out?”

“Yes.”

“Where?”

“I heard him after. He asked where you were. I told him.”

Teshi pressed her lips together. She nodded once, then stepped into the boat with the basket. Nemi followed, taking the washerwoman’s hand for the last step.

Merasi remained on the landing until the first boat’s pilot called for her to move. She boarded it and sat where directed, between a man carrying a cage of chickens and a woman with a wrapped bundle she would not put down. The man apologized for the smell. Merasi told him it was all right and discovered she meant it. She knew what was making that smell. It required no interpretation.

The boat pushed away.

The second vessel remained at its post, waiting for another load. Teshi stood beneath the awning. Merasi could see the pale mark beside her nose, then only the shape of her face, then the whole figure became part of the landing.

A cold line moved along the side of the boat.

The chicken nearest Merasi woke and thrust its head against the wicker. The man covered the cage with a cloth.

“Quiet,” he said. “You have complained all day.”

Merasi looked over the side.

There was no face in the water. No light. The current made a small hollow against a projecting board, filled it, emptied it again.

“Dazhet?”

The woman beside her turned.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

Merasi looked back toward the other boat.

“Your hair,” a voice said, so near that she felt it at her ear.

She touched her head before she understood. Ash had caught in the loose strands. Once, years before, Dazhet had spent an evening picking plaster dust from her hair after the landlord tried to repair the shutter’s wall without moving their bed. He had laughed every time she said he was pulling, and then apologized more seriously when he found a piece caught near the scalp.

“Teshi is on the other boat,” she whispered.

The cold lingered.

“She knows. I told her.”

The chickens settled. The woman beside her shifted her bundle and said the air had turned strange. Merasi watched the space between the two boats, willing herself not to call again.

On the landing, Teshi lifted her face.

Merasi could not know what she heard. The young woman put down the basket and stepped closer to the water. The washerwoman said something, then stopped. Teshi stood with her hand at the end of one braid.

The first boat turned into the channel. The landing went behind a wall.

They reached the lower bank after dark.

The pilot wanted everyone ashore before he unloaded the heavier goods. Merasi helped the woman beside her lift the bundle, which turned out to contain two carefully wrapped lamps and a cooking pot. The woman had brought them from her mother’s room after a death. She said this without invitation while they waited for the gangplank to settle, perhaps because Merasi had been so silent all the way.

“I have nowhere to put the pot,” she said.

“Then why bring it?”

“My sister wanted to sell it.”

Merasi took the heavier end. They carried it to the bank together. The woman’s brother was waiting with a cart. He thanked Merasi and asked whether someone was meeting her.

“No.”

“There are rooms by the kiln.”

“How much?”

He told her. She had enough for one night if she did not eat. The washerwoman’s fee and the three passages had left her with coins whose inadequacy she could feel before counting them.

The woman looked at her torn sleeve.

“Have you had trouble?”

“Work.”

The woman accepted the word. She had her own bundles to account for and a brother waiting to argue about the pot. She pointed Merasi toward the rooms and went away.

Merasi followed the path uphill. Lamps burned behind screens in the houses. Somewhere bread was being taken from an oven; the smell made her stomach contract. She passed a group of men eating in a courtyard and turned her face away because she could not bear to look hungry in front of them. At the lodging house a boy asked for payment before showing her the room.

It held a mat, a shelf and a water jar. The shutter closed badly.

She sat on the mat and laughed until she began to cry.

There was no one to hear except perhaps the boy outside, who knocked once to ask whether she wanted a lamp. She said no. He went away. In the dark she took the permit from her wrapper, felt the fold where the keeper had held it, and put it on the shelf.

Her name was now at the outer landing, attached to Teshi and Nemi’s passage to the washer’s store. The damaged lantern would be found empty. The drawing was gone. The boy in the repair room had seen her at the furnace. The accountant knew she intended to leave.

She could not go back to the room above the narrow court.

She lay down and listened to footsteps outside. Each one seemed about to stop at her door. Some did, at other doors, followed by voices, the settling of bodies, an argument about water. Ordinary people arriving late. She tried to hear them as ordinary people and sometimes succeeded.

Near dawn she slept.

2.8 The Hours of a New Room

The lodging keeper would not extend the room on the strength of her handwriting.

Merasi offered copying, accounts, letters, anything for which a steady hand might be useful. The keeper said his cousin did all of those things. She asked whether the cousin needed assistance. He said his cousin needed paying and that he already had enough people wishing to be useful instead of bringing coin.

She stood in the doorway with her folded wrapper under her arm.

“Is there work nearby?”

“Kiln. Washing. Unloading when the boats come.”

She looked at her ink-stained fingers. He followed her glance and softened slightly.

“The kiln sometimes needs marks put on orders. Ask the woman at the side gate. Not the man in front. He will tell you no because he enjoys being asked.”

She thanked him and went.

At the kiln they did need marks put on orders, but only after the orders had been sorted and the delivery pots counted. The woman at the side gate asked whether Merasi could lift a tray. Merasi said yes. The woman gave her one. It was heavier than expected, and she carried it badly for the first twenty paces before finding a way to keep its edge from cutting into her hands.

The work smelled of warm clay, fuel and water spilled on hot ground. Finished pots stood in groups under a roof open at the sides. Some had flaws that only the workers could see. A man passed his nail along a rim and set a vessel apart. Merasi looked for the damage and found none.

“Listen,” he said, tapping it.

She heard a dullness in the note only after he struck a sound one beside it. He did not mock her for missing it the first time. Later she showed him that two order marks had been reversed on a delivery strip. He stared, swore, and went to move the pots before they were loaded.

At noon the woman who hired her gave her food.

“You can write after we finish carrying.”

Merasi nodded. Her palms were sore. She had not thought of ink as easy work before.

She stayed that night in a room behind the kiln, shared with two other workers. One snored. The other woke whenever the snoring stopped and asked whether he was still breathing. Merasi lay on a borrowed mat and found the question oddly reassuring. Somebody was paying attention to a sleeping person’s ordinary survival. Nobody called it an inspection.

On the second day she went to the washer’s store.

She had waited because she needed to earn enough for the journey and because the washerwoman had told her to let the two young women come under a roof before asking for more. Waiting had not improved her temper. By the time she reached the door, she had composed several accusations, each more reasonable in her own mind than the last.

The washerwoman opened with a bundle in her arms.

“You took your time.”

Merasi stared at her.

“I was told—”

“Yes. Come in. Do not stand in the doorway telling me.”

The store was crowded with drying cloth. Light came through it in soft panels, making the room seem full of walls that moved when someone passed. Nemi sat on a stool sorting strips by length. Her shoulders were lower than Merasi remembered. Teshi stood at a basin with her sleeves rolled up, rinsing a cloth over and over.

The blue mark on her forearm was still visible.

Merasi looked at it too quickly. Teshi saw.

“It does not wash off.”

“I know.”

“There is someone who can fade it,” the washerwoman said. “Not today. Not by scrubbing it raw. Tell her.”

Teshi put down the cloth.

“I was washing this.”

The older woman gave her a look and went out with the bundle, leaving them among the hanging sheets.

Merasi sat beside Nemi.

“How are you?”

The girl glanced at Teshi, then at the sorted strips.

“I slept.”

“Good.”

“Until morning.”

Merasi nodded again. She wanted to ask whether anyone had come after them, where they would go next, who knew they were here. Instead she asked what the strips were for. Nemi showed her how the longer ones would be used to tie bundles and the shorter ones saved for patching. The work required little explanation and enough attention to keep the silence comfortable.

After a while Teshi came and sat on the floor opposite them.

“He came,” she said.

Merasi held a strip between her fingers.

“At the landing?”

“Yes.”

“What did he say?”

Teshi looked down.

Merasi let the strip fall onto the proper pile.

“You need not tell me.”

“He said I had grown.”

A short laugh escaped her, more anger than amusement.

“What else was I supposed to do?”

Merasi smiled before the words hurt her. Dazhet had missed years. He had been given a few moments and used them to say something every absent father might say. Merasi wished she had heard him say it.

“He asked if I was afraid,” Teshi said.

“What did you say?”

“Yes.”

Nemi stopped sorting. Teshi reached across and took a strip from her lap, folded it and put it in the long pile.

“Did he stay?” Merasi asked.

“For part of the water. I could hear him. Then less. I do not know where he went.”

Merasi looked at the cloth walls moving around them.

“Neither do I.”

For a while no one spoke. Nemi resumed sorting. Outside, the washerwoman argued with a customer over whether a stain had been present when the cloth arrived. She described the stain with an intimacy that made Teshi cover her mouth. Nemi looked up, saw her laughing and began to laugh too.

Merasi laughed with them. Dazhet did not return to hear it. The laughter remained.

The washerwoman knew a midwife who could help with the marks.

It would take time. The women would need different work papers before they travelled through gates where arms were examined. The midwife would not perform the fading until she had spoken to them herself, and the person preparing the papers would not do it cheaply because cheap work had already killed someone she knew. Merasi offered what she had earned at the kiln. Teshi refused half, kept half after Nemi looked at her, and insisted it be entered as something they would return if they could.

“Do not make another obligation,” Merasi said.

Teshi’s mouth tightened.

“Then do not make us take it as children.”

Merasi accepted the condition.

The washerwoman said they would move separately when the time came. Nemi might remain with a household nearer the lower market, where a cousin of the washerwoman needed someone willing to learn stitching. Teshi intended to take work farther downriver. She had no wish to remain in sight of Galanina’s walls.

Merasi asked whether they wanted her to come.

Teshi looked surprised. That was answer enough, but she did not make Merasi supply it alone.

“We do not know you very well,” she said.

“No.”

“I would like to know where you are.”

Merasi gave the kiln’s name. She did not offer the room behind it as if it were a home she had already established for them.

At the door, Teshi caught her sleeve, carefully, where it had torn.

“Did he ask you to do all of it?”

“He asked me to find you.”

“Not this?”

“Not in those words.”

Teshi considered her for a moment.

“Thank you,” she said.

Merasi had wanted the words. When they came, she found she had no graceful way to receive them. She touched Teshi’s hand and left before her face could do something that asked for more.

On the rest afternoon at the kiln, Merasi went to see the two young women again.

She carried a small parcel of food that had seemed generous when she bought it and now looked like a payment for admission. She nearly left it outside. Nemi opened the door before she could decide.

“Are those the flat ones?”

“Yes.”

“Teshi hates them.”

“I don’t hate them,” Teshi called from inside. “I said they’re mostly air.”

Nemi took the parcel with both hands. “Then you can have two.”

They were working on a torn sheet spread across the floor. The washerwoman had left them a heap of narrow strips and instructions about how much of the sound cloth could be recovered. Teshi had marked a line through a stain. Nemi had marked another that would save an extra hand’s width. They had been arguing when Merasi knocked.

“Which?” Nemi asked her.

Merasi knelt. She examined the stain from both sides and asked whether the cloth would be sold as wrapping or made into something else. Nemi stared at her. Teshi began to laugh.

“We thought you might just choose.”

“I can choose.”

“Go on, then.”

Merasi chose Nemi’s line. The girl crowed. Teshi turned the cloth and showed them a little tear hidden beneath the stain.

“You could have told me.”

“You didn’t ask.”

For a moment Merasi heard the accountant’s voice. Her stomach tightened before she could help it. Teshi saw something change and laid the cloth down.

“I was teasing.”

“I know.”

But she had stopped smiling, and for a while they all worked carefully. Merasi held the cloth while Nemi cut along Teshi’s line. The scissors caught. Nemi muttered an insult she must have learned in the kitchen, so elaborate that Merasi lost the beginning of it before the girl reached the end.

“Who taught you that?”

“Nobody.”

“You were born saying it?”

Nemi grinned, bent over the scissors and said it again, more slowly. Teshi corrected a word. They disagreed about the anatomy involved. Merasi set the cloth down because she was laughing too hard to hold it straight.

They ate sitting on the floor. Nemi carefully divided the food into three portions, then took one piece from Teshi’s and added it to her own. Teshi watched her do it. Merasi waited for a rebuke that did not come.

Later Nemi lay on her stomach with her chin on her arms. She had finished eating but did not want to work again. Teshi pulled a needle through a stubborn fold and asked Merasi what Dazhet had looked like when he was young.

“Young when?”

“Before.”

Merasi put down the strip she was winding. There were several befores. She chose one in which he had been wearing a bright new wrapper he could not afford.

“He stood differently in it. As if he had become wider.”

“He used to do that.”

“He said it was good cloth.”

“Was it?”

“It caught on a nail the first time he sat down.”

Teshi smiled without looking up. “I remember a tear. He said someone had tried to rob him.”

“Someone had tried to make him pay for his food.”

Nemi rolled onto her back. “Did they get it?”

“Yes. After he borrowed from me.”

“Did you get yours?”

“Eventually.”

Teshi stopped sewing. “He was bad at coming back when he said he would.”

Merasi kept winding the strip.

“Yes.”

“He came for my mother’s funeral. He had to ask which house.”

Merasi had known about the funeral. She had helped him find a clean cloth for it. She had not asked where he was going until he had nearly reached the door.

“I didn’t know that part.”

“He stayed a long time once he got there.”

Teshi resumed sewing. Merasi wanted to defend him and could not find anything to say that Teshi had not already allowed him herself. Nemi closed her eyes. The afternoon light moved down the hanging sheet until it lay across the girl’s feet.

They spoke about him for a while, disagreeing about small things. Teshi remembered a song Merasi had never heard. Merasi tried to remember a joke and could not make it work; Teshi said that sounded like one of his. Once they both fell silent at the same time. Nemi was asleep by then. A fly settled on her ankle, and Teshi brushed it away with the end of the cloth.

Merasi stayed until the washerwoman returned. Nothing had been decided about the papers or the next place to go. She had a numb foot, a loose thread on her skirt, and half the joke still troubling her on the road back to the kiln.

News came from Galanina in pieces.

The export lot had left with eleven working units and one sealed case containing the failed fitting for examination. The uncooled substitute remained in the lower rooms. Merasi heard that from the handling man’s sister, who bought pots at the kiln and recognized her voice before her face. The woman stared, glanced around and lowered the pot she had been examining.

“You should not be here.”

“I work here.”

“You know what I mean.”

Merasi did.

The repairer had been questioned. His family had not been reassigned with the first group, because the missing design record had made his presence necessary to the inquiry. That was not safety. The boy had been moved to another bench. The supervisor claimed he had warned everyone repeatedly about careless documentation. The accountant had asked after the clerk whose departure occurred at the same time as the record’s destruction.

“He knows your name,” the woman said.

“Yes.”

“He asks it as if he does not.”

Merasi helped her choose two pots and did not reduce the price; the kiln owner would have asked why. At the gate, the woman said she would not bring another message. Merasi told her she should not.

She stood among the finished vessels afterward, unable to work for several minutes.

The repairer was alive. The boy was alive. She had left them beneath questions she had helped make. The fact that the original fitting had failed, the truthful defect she had used at the start, might give them a defensible account. The missing drawing might take it away. She could not arrange the words they would be asked to say.

At the next carrying shift she dropped a tray.

Only one pot broke, but the noise brought everyone. The woman at the side gate looked at the pieces, looked at Merasi and said that the cost would come from her next wages. Merasi nodded. She knelt to gather the fragments.

“Not your hands,” the woman said, bringing a brush. “If you cut them you cannot write either.”

That evening Merasi stayed after the others to finish the orders. She checked every mark twice. When the last one was done, she sat in the doorway of the cooling shed and watched the light leave the pots. They were plain vessels, intended for water, food, oil. She knew little about their making. Their surfaces held the marks of people who had known what they were doing, and sometimes a place where a hand had faltered and corrected itself while the clay was still soft.

She remained there until the kiln woman came to close the gate.

Heshra’s village still existed.

Merasi learned this from a carrier who had passed near Deremet during the previous season. She asked him first about buying a certain kind of basket, as Heshra had advised. He did not know the basket-maker she named because she had invented the name. He did know the road, the shallow well beside it and the two houses beyond the turn where his party had bought food. He described a grove Heshra had mentioned, smaller now, and a new wall around the water place.

Merasi wrote what he had seen, keeping his uncertainty where it belonged. She added the season and his direction of travel. She did not write that Heshra’s relatives were alive. She did not know their names.

The washerwoman took the message for a smaller fee than before. Merasi counted the change into her purse. “Thank you.” The woman was already wrapping the paper.

For the woman at the well there was less.

Merasi had heard no reliable account of the child. A name resembling the one given appeared in a list the handling man’s sister remembered, but the age did not fit and the destination was uncertain. Merasi almost included it. A little hope, she thought, something to carry back in exchange for the morning’s help. Then she remembered the woman asking not to be promised comfort because Merasi disliked sitting beside her.

She wrote that she had found no dependable news.

The sentence looked cruel on the page. She left it.

Weeks later the washerwoman returned a small reply. Heshra thanked her for the road and the two houses. She asked whether the carrier had seen the grove’s oldest tree. Merasi went to look for him again, carrying Heshra’s question about the tree.

The woman at the well sent no reply.

Merasi took a room of her own when she could afford the first payment.

It stood behind a house where people made cord. Fibres caught on everything. She found them in her food, her bedding, the hem of her wrapper. The woman who owned the room warned her that the work began early. Merasi said she began early too. Neither mentioned that the room had a sound shutter until the owner opened and closed it to demonstrate the latch.

She bought a bowl from the kiln.

It was plain brown, deeper than the blue one, with a small irregularity beneath the rim that had made it unsuitable for a matched order. The potter offered it cheaply. Merasi ran her thumb over the imperfect place and chose it without bargaining.

On the first evening she ate sitting in the doorway. The cord-makers had finished, but one remained winding a short length she meant to take home. A child sat beside her, asleep with his head tipped against her hip. The woman’s hands continued their practiced work above him. She asked Merasi whether the room needed a hook for clothes.

“Yes, if it is permitted.”

“It is your room while you pay for it. Put the hook where you can reach.”

Merasi smiled.

Inside, she placed the bowl on the shelf and hung her work cloth over the back of the chair. The permits she had used at the landing were wrapped beneath the bedding. The bent nail was gone with the broken fitting. Her torn sleeve had been mended, the new thread obvious where the old cloth was thin.

She sat for a while before lighting the lamp.

Voices came through the wall: the cord-maker waking the child, someone asking whether there was food left, a complaint about a missing sandal. Merasi listened without trying to find another voice among them. When the lamp caught, she trimmed the wick, moved it nearer the work she had brought home and began copying the next day’s orders.

The Weight Beneath Us

3.1 Where the Cups Tremble

Nia knew the city had begun to move because her mother’s cup was trembling on its shelf.

She caught it before it reached the edge and put it farther back. Her mother had always left it there, precisely where the morning vibration would find it, and had always blamed somebody else when it fell. Nobody used the cup now. It remained with its handle toward the room, waiting for a hand that was underneath the city.

Outside, the first hauling call rose from the southern deck. A second voice answered from a point farther along the line, then a third. The floor gave its familiar low shudder. Nia felt it through her heels and leaned into the movement without thinking. A little powder sifted from the lintel where the old plaster had never quite learned to flex with the stone beneath it.

Beruke slept through all of this.

He lay on the mat beside the inner wall with one arm across his face. His feet extended beyond the bedding. One heel was so close to the meal jar that Nia considered moving the jar, then decided to move his foot instead.

He woke angry.

“Get up,” she said.

“I was up.”

“You were horizontal.”

“I was thinking.”

“Of what?”

“How pleasant it was before you came.”

She dropped his foot. The heel struck the mat with a noise that satisfied neither of them. He sat, rubbing his face, while she divided the morning food. He was older than she was by enough years to have once been entrusted with her and few enough that he still resented the duties this had established. She remembered being carried on his shoulders to watch a new roller set beneath the western deck. He remembered that she had pulled his hair and been praised for waving.

“Are you going below?” he asked.

“Before the hall.”

“Take the sweet beer. She asked.”

“You could take it.”

“I have store work.”

“So do I.”

“Mason work. Different work.”

“Does it prevent you carrying a jar?”

He took his bowl and ate. Nia tied the beer jar into a sling. Its contents were weak enough for her mother to drink through a working day, sweet enough that she would complain if the attendants watered it further. Beside it Nia put a clean cloth, a little oil and the comb her mother had asked for twice. She had forgotten the comb the first time and brought the wrong one the second. The right one had three missing teeth and had been wrapped in cloth beneath her mother’s sleeping mat, where nobody practical would have looked for it.

Beruke watched her tie the bundle.

“What?” she asked.

“Nothing.”

“You have been looking.”

“Make the knot lower. It will rub against the jar.”

She lowered it. He rose and took his bowl to the washing basin. Through the doorway she could hear a neighbour telling a child to get off the moving seam between two blocks. The child argued that his foot fitted. The neighbour replied that so would his whole body after the stones shifted.

Beruke paused beside the door.

“Will she ask?”

“About the Naming?”

He did not answer.

“Yes,” Nia said. “Probably.”

“Tell her I was at work.”

“She knows where you work.”

He left without his belt knife and returned for it a moment later, making the departure less final than he had intended. Nia was kind enough not to smile until he had gone again.

Khazembo moved more slowly than a person could comfortably walk.

From the ground it was immense and almost motionless, a city of pale and dark stone travelling behind its own dust. From inside, the motion was everywhere. Water leaned in a jar. A door swung until somebody wedged it. Voices rose to cross the continuous grinding underfoot, and people standing on opposite sides of a moving joint waited for the next settling before passing a child or a hot pot across.

The lower streets had names from ground they had left years before. Nia passed the Old Reed Market, where no reeds grew, and the West Quarry Steps, which now faced south. A boy was chalking a new direction on a board while his grandfather told him the old direction had been perfectly adequate for fifty years. At a food stall two women had started an argument about a marriage and moved seamlessly into the price of sorghum because the bride’s aunt had approached. The aunt heard enough to begin again at the marriage.

Nia bought a fried cake for herself and another for her mother. The seller wrapped the second carefully, knowing where it was going.

“She ate yesterday?”

“Yes.”

“Properly?”

“She said they gave her too much.”

“Then she ate properly.”

The woman added a small broken piece to the wrapper. Nia paid the ordinary price. Her family’s raised standing had brought several privileges; she found the offered kindness of a cake easier to accept when it had not been entered among them.

The route down to the chamber passed through the oldest part of the city’s bearing structure. Here the stone was dark with touch and the air smelled of oil, dust and the sour sweetness of beer left in small dishes for those who listened in the fissures. An attendant sat beside the upper stair, mending a strap. He greeted Nia and called her name down before letting her pass.

Her mother answered with a complaint about the noise.

“You asked who it was,” the attendant called back.

“I heard who it was.”

“Then why ask?”

“To find out if you were awake.”

Nia smiled in the dark.

The chamber was low. Lamps stood in recessed places where the movement could not overturn them. At its centre, within the keystone’s fitted cradle, her mother lay with her arms resting beside her. She was awake. She was always awake when Nia came, and Nia had stopped asking whether she slept because the answer changed according to how much her mother wished to reassure or rebuke her.

The woman’s hair had been divided into small braids to keep it clear of her neck. Grey showed at the roots. It had shown before she went down, but less. Nia tried not to look too carefully and was caught doing it.

“Have I grown horns?”

“No.”

“Then bring the beer.”

Nia sat on the low stool. Her mother’s name was Maleba. People on the upper streets had begun speaking it with a reverence she would have found intolerable at home. Here she still asked for things sharply and expected them brought correctly.

Nia held the cup while she drank. A tremor passed through the stone beneath them. Maleba closed her eyes until it ended.

“Too fast?” Nia asked.

“The turn. They are bringing the left line in.”

“Can you tell?”

“Yes.”

“How?”

“Can you tell when somebody stands on your foot?”

Nia looked down. Her mother sighed.

“Do not look wounded. I am not angry with you.”

“You sound angry.”

“I have many people to choose from.”

She asked for the comb. Nia produced it. This earned a look of such exaggerated surprise that she laughed despite herself. She worked the comb through the loose hair near her mother’s temple while Maleba described the attendant she wanted changed.

“He sings the same verse.”

“You like that song.”

“I liked it before he sang the same verse for six days.”

“He knows the rest.”

“Then he is withholding it maliciously.”

“I will ask.”

“Do not ask him whether I want him changed. Tell him I have asked.”

Nia nodded. She had been about to make the request easier on the attendant by making her mother less definite.

Maleba looked at the food wrapper.

“What is that?”

“A cake.”

“Then why are we discussing songs?”

Nia broke it into pieces small enough to eat without lifting her head. Maleba accepted the first, chewed, and looked toward the stair.

“Your brother?”

“At the stores.”

“So he says.”

“He is working.”

“He is avoiding me while working. Men can do two things.”

Nia continued feeding her. The stone-song rose slightly, a low note felt in the cup more than heard. Her mother’s hand closed on the edge of the cradle.

“Is it worse?”

“No. Do not ask every time it changes.”

“I have to know.”

“You have to believe me when I answer.”

Nia put down the cake. She was angry suddenly: at the chamber, at the attendant upstairs, at her brother, at the people who came down to honour Maleba and went back up without having to feed her or see the new grey. Her mother watched the anger arrive.

“You could have said no,” Nia said.

“I could.”

“Then why—”

“We have done this.”

“Not with Beruke.”

“Bring him. We can all be unhappy together.”

Maleba turned her face away. Nia sat holding the wrapper, ashamed of having used the visit to ask again for a different past. After a while her mother said she would like the rest of the cake, if Nia had not decided to punish her by eating it herself.

Nia gave it to her. Before leaving, she told the attendant plainly that Maleba wanted someone else on the afternoon singing turn. He looked hurt. She let him be hurt and went upstairs.

At the upper stair she met two women carrying a cloth-wrapped bowl. One was her mother’s cousin. The other wore the narrow shoulder cloth of the people who prepared candidates for the Naming. They stopped when they saw Nia.

“Is she receiving?” the cousin asked.

“She is awake.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

Nia looked at the bowl. Its wrapping was new, dyed a deep colour that would survive ceremonial washing badly. “Has she asked you to come?”

The cousin frowned. The other woman waited until the frown had done all it could, then said Maleba had requested the bowl brought from her sister’s house. Nia remembered hearing something about it and being too occupied with the comb to listen.

She stood aside. The two women went down, and she followed because she wanted to know what they would say about Beruke.

Her mother looked past the visitors and saw her.

“Have you forgotten something?”

“No.”

“Then either sit or go. Don’t stand there like a guard.”

Nia sat beside the stair. The bowl was unwrapped. Maleba could not sit up to see it properly, so her cousin tilted it toward the lamp. A pattern of small incisions ran around its shoulder. Nia knew it: the marks represented the people who had eaten from it when the household was first recognized. Her own was the shallowest, made with a tool she had been too young to hold securely.

“There,” her mother said. “That is where you repair it. Not over the names.”

A crack ran down the side. Her cousin had suggested filling the whole band and cutting the pattern again. Maleba refused. They discussed the repair with an attention Nia found difficult to bear. The bowl would be used at the Naming, on a table where people would praise what Maleba had given and look at her children while doing so.

The visitor from the Naming house asked whether Maleba wanted the bowl carried by someone in particular.

“Beruke has a good pair of hands,” the cousin said.

Nia drew breath.

“Then he can use them at his work,” Maleba replied. “Ask Ule.”

The cousin looked embarrassed. The other woman nodded and began wrapping the bowl again.

They stayed to discuss a marriage in the western block. Maleba asked after a woman who had moved away with the timber workers, and the cousin produced three incompatible accounts of where she had gone. Nia ceased listening closely. She was watching her mother’s face, waiting for the effort of the visit to show.

At last the women left. Nia remained on the stool by the stair.

“You heard that,” Maleba said.

“Yes.”

“Then you can stop looking as though every person with a bowl has come to steal your brother.”

“They do ask him.”

“I know.”

“You ask him.”

“I ask him to visit.”

Nia rubbed her thumb along the stool’s edge. She had spent the morning defending him against questions he had not been there to hear. Now she could not remember precisely which questions her mother had asked and which had arrived from other people wearing her mother’s importance.

Maleba asked for the bowl to be brought back before the repair, so that she could see exactly where the filler would go. Nia promised to tell the cousin.

“And tell her without improving it,” Maleba added.

Nia went upstairs. In the sunlight she found the women discussing the same marriage with an attendant. She gave the message exactly and resisted the desire to add anything about Beruke.

Before she reached the fitting bench, Nia was sent to inspect a crack in a household’s doorway.

The householder had described it as wide enough to put his thumb in. It was not. He showed her anyway, pressing his thumb against the line until she feared he would injure himself proving the urgency.

“It moved last night.”

“This plaster has moved. The joint beneath it is meant to.”

“My wife says you will say that.”

His wife, sitting in the shade behind him, raised one hand without looking up from her work.

Nia scraped the loose surface away and showed him the fitted stones. He listened until she said there was no danger, then asked whether a senior mason could come.

She wanted to leave him with his crack. Instead she marked the ends, told him what change to watch for, and said she would ask her master to look when he passed.

“You are Maleba’s daughter,” the wife said.

Nia nodded.

The man’s manner changed. He asked her to sit, offered a drink, and began explaining that his concern had been for the whole block, not merely his own doorway. She remained standing. She had not become a better mason between his question and his recognition.

His wife looked at her face and smiled slightly.

“Go to your work,” she said. “I’ll keep his thumb out of it.”

Outside, Nia stopped at a stone-trimming yard where two apprentices were shaping blocks for a new household wall. One had dressed his piece too smoothly and was being told that a handsome face would not make the hidden join fit. The other had left the face rough and was enjoying his companion’s correction too openly to escape one himself.

Nia set down her tools and helped them turn the heavier block. Together they checked the bed, marked the high point, and argued about how much to remove. Here nobody offered her a seat because of her mother. They asked her to hold the rod straight.

She stayed longer than necessary. By the time she returned to the fitting bench, she was late and in a good mood, which made the damaged chisel seem a deliberate affront.

The argument with Beruke began over a chisel.

He had borrowed it from her tool roll and returned it with the edge turned. She found it while preparing work at the lower fitting bench. He said it had been turned already. She said she had dressed it the previous evening. He asked whether every mark on every tool in Khazembo would now be laid at his door.

“It is my tool.”

“It was a small job.”

“Then use a small tool.”

He put down the store tablet he was carrying.

“What did she say?”

“That she wants another singer.”

“About me.”

“That you are avoiding her.”

“I am avoiding being told where to lie down next season.”

“She has not told you.”

“Everyone else has.”

Nia looked toward the open passage. Two apprentices had stopped working near enough to hear. She lowered her voice.

“Then tell them you will not.”

“And stay here while they praise how willing she was?”

“You would rather go?”

“Yes.”

He said it without the anger she expected. That made it harder to answer.

“Where?”

“Somewhere the ground stays put.”

“Which somewhere?”

“I am allowed not to know yet.”

She picked up the chisel, put it down and reached for the hammer beside it. The fitting under repair needed a retaining lip tapped back into place. She struck too hard. The hammer glanced and hit the pale stone beside the socket with a sharp crack.

Both of them stopped.

A white mark showed on the surface.

Nia put the hammer down very carefully. Beruke looked at the mark, then away. One of the apprentices made a sympathetic sound and was silenced by the other.

“Go,” Nia said.

Her brother picked up the tablet. For a moment she thought he would apologize. He left instead.

She finished the fitting. Her hand shook with the effort of being precise. When the senior mason came to examine it, she showed him the white mark. He touched it, said she would dress the surface later and told her to stop trying to drive the whole city with one hammer.

By evening the note beneath the floor had changed.

Nia heard it while washing her hands. A shallow dish beside the basin began to tremble, stopped, then moved again against the grain of the usual vibration. Outside, a hauling call ended abruptly. Another voice called for a halt. The floor gave a slow sideways settling that made everyone in the room reach for something.

Then Khazembo stopped.

The silence after the last roller ceased was full of other sounds: crockery, feet, frightened oxen, somebody shouting for a child. Nia stood with wet hands over the basin and saw again the white mark where her hammer had struck.

3.2 The White Mark

The Mason-Kin Hall had begun to weep along a carved seam.

Water gathered beneath the name of a House that had supplied cutters for four generations. It ran through the low relief of a woman’s hand, dropped from the stone fingertips and darkened the floor. An elder set a bowl beneath it without comment. People arriving looked at the bowl before taking their places.

Nia stood near the entrance with her tool roll pressed to her chest.

The hall was crowded but did not sound crowded. Everyone was accustomed to raising their voice over the city’s movement. Now they began too loudly and lowered their voices halfway through a sentence. A child cried in the passage outside and was carried away. The sound remained audible much longer than it should have.

The senior mason was describing the settling at the western supports. Nia heard “by morning” and “if the soil takes it” and stepped forward before she could decide to wait.

“I struck it.”

Several people turned. The mason stopped.

“The keystone fitting. Beside the socket. My hammer slipped.”

“When?”

She gave the time.

“How hard?”

“Hard.”

“What were you doing?”

She described the repair. She did not mention Beruke. That omission made the rest of the account feel less true, though the work itself was accurately named.

The elder beside the dripping seam asked to see the hammer. Nia unwrapped it. He took it, weighed it in his hand and returned it without a judgment she could use.

“We will inspect.”

“I can show—”

“You will show us. Bring a lamp.”

She followed them down.

At the chamber entrance an Aeler bearing-caster waited with two workers and a frame of measuring rods. He had broad hands, darkened at the knuckles, and a leather apron so old that its repairs had become the thickest parts. Nia knew him by work rather than friendship. His name was Dovek. He had once spent an entire afternoon refusing to accept a bearing seat her master considered adequate and been proved right when the first cast cooled crookedly.

He looked at her lamp.

“Lower. I need the floor.”

She lowered it.

The white hammer mark showed plainly. Beside it, beneath the fitted lip, a deeper split had opened along a seam that should have remained under compression. Dovek did not touch it. He set a rod across two reference points and asked one of his workers to read the gap.

Nia waited for someone to tell her how much she had broken.

The senior mason crouched, put his thumb against the white scar and traced its shallow edge.

“That is your blow.”

He moved the lamp toward the open seam.

“This is underneath.”

“I could have—”

“No.”

He said it firmly enough to stop her. Dovek looked at the socket and asked who had last checked the load-stone.

The question changed the room.

One worker went for the store record. Another fetched a narrow inspection mirror. Nia held the lamp while the senior mason examined the piece seated within the fitting. From outside it looked correct: dressed stone, proper dimensions, the exposed face worked to match its neighbours. He scraped a hidden edge with a small tool. Dust fell into his palm.

“Granite,” he said.

Dovek leaned closer.

“Ordinary?”

“Yes.”

The bearing-caster closed his eyes briefly.

“Do not move the assembly.”

No one argued. He called his workers and placed them at the supports, then asked for timber, wedges and two more hands who could obey a direction without improving it.

Nia remained beside the lamp.

The senior mason looked at her.

“Who had access?”

She knew before the record arrived.

Beruke worked the replacement stores. He had carried a tablet that afternoon. He had borrowed her chisel, not for a small repair to wood or metal, but for something he had not wished to describe. She could see him looking at the white mark and turning away.

“No,” she said.

The mason waited.

She hated him for waiting. It gave her nowhere to put the answer except into words.

“My brother has the store key.”

“So do others.”

“Yes.”

“We will ask all of them.”

“Yes.”

The record named a load-stone removed as surplus material and ordinary dressing stone returned to the fitting store. Beruke had entered the removal. A second hand had witnessed the transaction. The receiving merchant’s mark stood beneath both.

Nia read it twice. The notation for the store category was old, carried over from an earlier arrangement in which the socket had held a reserve piece. That designation had never been changed after the city’s last repair. She seized on it.

“He thought it was spare.”

“Did he ask?” the senior mason said.

“He may have.”

“Whom?”

She had no name.

Dovek spoke from beneath the support frame.

“Find the stone. Decide what he thought after we can stand somewhere safe.”

Her mother had felt the change before anyone heard it.

When Nia entered the chamber, Maleba looked at her face and said, “You have been crying.”

“No.”

“Then you have found another way to make your eyes look foolish.”

Nia sat on the low stool. The lamp had been moved nearer the cradle, giving her mother’s skin a yellow cast. The new attendant was there, a woman who knew more than one verse and did not sing while Maleba was speaking. Nia was absurdly grateful for that small successful request.

“The load-stone is gone.”

“I know something is.”

“Beruke sold it.”

Maleba looked at the ceiling.

For a long time she said nothing. Nia had expected anger, denial, a question she could answer. She sat with her hands between her knees and waited.

“When?” her mother asked at last.

“We do not know exactly. The entry is from three days ago.”

“The second morning,” Maleba said. “After the long turn. I told the attendant it was pulling differently.”

“Which attendant?”

“The one you changed.”

“Did he tell the masons?”

“He said the song was right.”

Nia stood.

“Sit.”

“He should have—”

“Sit down, Nia.”

She sat.

“I need to know whether I can come out.”

“Not until they brace it.”

“How long?”

“They are working.”

“How long do they think?”

Nia looked at the attendant. The woman did not rescue her.

“By morning for the first bracing. If the lower bearing has not moved farther. They cannot replace the stone until they have it.”

Maleba nodded. She closed her eyes and opened them again, taking time with the movement.

“Then bring me clean water. Tell them when I felt it. And do not stand above me saying what your brother ought to have done. I can hear the passage.”

Nia bent to kiss her. Her mother turned her face enough to receive it, then said the lamp was too close and making her hot.

Outside, Dovek listened to the account of the changed load and sent one of his workers to check the lower bearing again. The senior mason went to speak with the original attendant. Nobody thanked Nia for the information. It entered the work and changed what happened next.

She returned upstairs to find Beruke’s sleeping mat empty.

His spare clothes were gone. The meal jar remained where it had stood that morning. Beside it lay the knife he had returned for, forgotten again or deliberately left. Nia picked it up, gripped it hard enough to hurt her palm and put it down.

Outside the room, neighbours were moving bedding toward the upper court. A block on the western side had settled enough to jam two doors. People were being asked to leave before the joints shifted further. A child carried a cage with nothing in it. He said his bird had flown when the floor stopped. His grandmother told him to bring the cage anyway.

Nia went to help with the first stuck door.

3.3 A Place to Stand

The Root-binder came while they were carrying an old man out through his window.

The door had jammed at an angle its frame could not accommodate. The old man refused to leave without the chest beneath his bed, then refused to leave before the chest, then discovered the chest would not fit through the window. By the time Nia reached the house, six people were shouting at him and he was shouting back with more breath than he had shown in months.

The stranger stood below the window holding a length of green branch.

“Turn him sideways,” she said.

“We are turning him.”

“You are turning his shoulders. His knee will not bend.”

Nia climbed onto the lower sill, saw the old man’s leg caught against the inner wall and moved the folded bedding that had been put there to protect him. The leg came free. They lifted him through, lowered him into four waiting arms and carried him toward the court while he demanded to know what would happen to the chest.

“Bring the contents,” Nia told the people inside.

“The key is with him.”

She ran after the old man. He had put it inside his cheek to prevent anyone mislaying it. Getting him to surrender it took longer than freeing his leg.

When she returned, the Root-binder was kneeling beside the gap beneath the western deck.

Nia knew what she was from the hands. Bark ran along the backs of the fingers, splitting where they bent. The nails looked ordinary and dirty. The green branch lay across her knees. She was listening to something below the stone.

“You cannot plant here,” Nia said.

“I can. That is why I came.”

“The wardens will take you.”

“They can take me after they stop the block sinking.”

She rose. She was shorter than Nia, broad through the shoulders, with a tired mouth and a strip of cloth tied around one wrist where the bark had cracked. She had no look of being pleased by the city’s distress. Nia had heard Root-binders described as waiting eagerly for precisely this failure. The woman looked at the people carrying bedding and said they should move the water jars before the next settling.

“Who are you?”

“Ruzha. I worked the lower timber before I went to ground.”

“Which city?”

“This one.”

Nia looked more closely. She could not remember her.

“You were little,” Ruzha said. “Do you have somebody who will listen?”

“Dovek might.”

“Then fetch him. Before a man with a stick decides he is more useful than I am.”

A man with a stick arrived while Nia was fetching Dovek.

The confrontation had not yet become violent when they returned. Ruzha stood with her green branch across both palms. The warden stood a few paces away, ordering her to leave the deck.
