Two neighbours watched from the house window; they had resumed emptying the chest, but slowly enough not to miss anything.

Dovek went past the warden and crouched where Ruzha had been listening.

“What can you hold?”

“This corner. Perhaps to the next post. Not the whole side.”

“How long?”

“Until it asks more than the ground can bear.”

“That is not a time.”

“It is what I can tell you.”

He nodded once, accepting the limit without liking it.

The warden said that Root-binding beneath a city was forbidden.

“So is losing the city,” Dovek replied. “Stand there and keep people back.”

“You do not give my orders.”

“No. If you need somebody else to tell you the ground is sinking, fetch them quickly.”

Nia went herself. By the time she returned with the senior mason, Ruzha had marked a small area below the deck where living roots could be encouraged to spread without entering the bearings. Dovek had set rods to show any change in settling. They intended to test a single span first.

The senior mason listened. His face made clear what he thought of a heretic proposing to put living wood into a system that depended on controlled movement. He asked Ruzha whether she could withdraw the roots.

“If you let me stay to do it.”

“You will remain under watch.”

“I expected no feast.”

“And afterward?”

“A hearing for the settlement ground I showed your predecessor. With people who have walked it.”

“That ground cannot hold this city.”

“No. It can hold some houses. You may come and tell me how many after you have looked.”

Nia watched the senior mason hesitate. He had been asked for a future concession at a moment when refusal might cost them the deck. She had seen him refuse less urgent bargains because he disliked the way they were offered. Ruzha saw the hesitation too and put the branch down.

“I can begin while you decide whether to be gracious,” she said.

He gave the permission.

The roots did not rise in a magnificent column.

At first the only visible change was a darkening in the soil beneath the deck. Ruzha knelt with one hand flat and the other holding the green branch against a seam. Her lips moved. Nia could not hear words. The oxen tethered nearby began shifting and snorting. Someone took them farther along the line.

A thin root appeared where there had been bare soil. It divided, disappeared beneath a stone, emerged thicker on the other side. More followed. They moved slowly enough that Nia could not see the change while staring directly at one place; when she looked away and back, a gap had narrowed. Dovek’s worker called the readings from the rods.

The settling slowed.

Ruzha’s bandaged wrist began bleeding through the cloth. She did not remove her hand.

Nia brought water. The woman shook her head, then changed her mind and let Nia hold the cup while she drank. Her mouth trembled against the rim.

“Will it hold?” Nia asked.

“For now.”

“What should I watch?”

“The earth beside the outer root. If it lifts there, call me. If it splits, move everyone back.”

Nia repeated the instructions to the warden. He looked offended at being instructed by an apprentice, then looked at the rooted mass beneath the stone and repeated them correctly.

During the night Nia took one watch beside the measuring rods.

The warden remained nearby, his stick across his knees. At first he corrected every person who came too close. Later, as the cold deepened, he wrapped himself in a cloth and left Nia to call them back. The roots had become difficult to distinguish from the shadows beneath the deck. She kept looking at the outer patch of earth Ruzha had named.

A family brought bedding past. Their youngest child was asleep against his father’s shoulder, mouth open, one hand tangled in the man’s collar. The older child carried a cooking pot as large as his chest. He stopped to ask whether the city had broken.

“Part of it,” Nia said.

“Will it walk again?”

“We’re working on it.”

He considered the answer, then asked whether he could leave the pot beside her until his father came back. Nia made a place for it beyond the barrier. He set it down with immense relief and ran after his family.

The warden watched him go.

“My son carried that size once,” he said.

Nia waited, but he did not continue. He rubbed his hands together and went to fetch more water.

Ruzha slept for a short time beside a low fire. Her wrists lay on her knees, palms upward. Nia could see bark along the edges of her fingers, not a covering she had put on but part of what the work had made of her. When the woman woke, she checked the earth before accepting anything to drink.

“Do they hurt?” Nia asked, looking at her hands.

“Yes.”

“Always?”

“Not always this much.”

Nia wished she had found a less foolish question. Ruzha warmed the cup between her palms and looked toward the dark bulk of Khazembo.

“I used to know every squeak in that block,” she said. “Now I keep hearing sounds I don’t know.”

“How long since you lived here?”

“Long enough for people to say I never understood it.”

The warden returned with water and a piece of food. He gave both to Ruzha without speaking of her right to be there. She ate, then went to the outer root. Nia followed, carrying the lamp.

Near dawn the boy’s father came back for the pot. He had forgotten where his son said he left it and had searched two other barriers first. Nia handed it over. He thanked her with such tired sincerity that she had to look at the rods until he went away.

By morning the western block had stopped sinking quickly enough for the evacuation to become orderly. The old man’s chest was emptied, its contents carried to him in baskets. He complained that the baskets smelled of onions. His granddaughter told him he was welcome to return through the window and supervise the chest personally. He declined.

Nia went below to tell her mother.

Maleba listened to the description of roots beneath the deck and asked whether they would be gone before movement resumed.

“That is the agreement.”

“With whom?”

“The woman who made them.”

“Then keep her fed.”

Nia nodded.

Her mother looked at her more closely.

“You have not slept.”

“Neither have you.”

“I have a reason.”

“So do I.”

Maleba shut her eyes. Nia thought she had offended her. Then her mother opened them again and said, “Bring me something I can ring.”

“A bell?”

“A pan. A cup. Something they cannot call a change in the song.”

Nia brought a small metal bowl and a striker, tested that her mother could reach them without lifting her shoulder, and told the attendants to answer it at once. Maleba struck it twice to make sure they did.

Only then did Nia leave to follow the stone.

3.4 The Stone Sold Onward

The merchant had already sent it to a worksite.

His tent stood beside a caravan camp on firmer ground beyond Khazembo’s intended path. He was supervising the loading of salt when Nia arrived with two city haulers and a small sledge. He listened to her account, asked to see the sale entry and fetched his own copy before answering.

“I bought a reserve piece.”

“You bought the load-stone in use.”

“That is not what he sold me.”

He was Pereshi, with careful speech in the city’s trade tongue and a beard he kept tied back from the work. His name was Faruz. The name on the sale mark was his, and the quantity paid matched the city record. Nia had hoped for something easier to hate: a furtive buyer, a false mark, a man waiting to profit from the failure he had caused. Faruz looked angry that a purchase properly made had brought strangers into his camp demanding he undo work already begun.

“Where is it?”

“Under a loading frame at the quarry spur.”

“How far?”

“Half a day’s walk. Less with a sound animal.”

“We have a sledge team.”

“Then take them. You will need the frame held before removing the stone.”

“You will come?”

“I will send the man who set it. I have a caravan to load.”

She almost accused him of valuing freight over her city. Then she looked at the workers, the animals, the food being prepared under the next awning. Their departure had a time and a cost too.

“Who is the man?”

Faruz called a name. A worker came out from behind the salt stacks, rubbing his hands on his trousers. Behind him, carrying a rolled blanket, was Beruke.

For a moment Nia could not move.

Her brother saw her and stopped. He looked at the sledge, the haulers, the city dust on her clothes. His face changed before she spoke.

“It stopped,” she said.

He lowered the blanket.

Faruz looked from one to the other.

“Your seller?”

Nia nodded.

Beruke set the blanket on the ground.

“I thought it was spare.”

“The designation was old.”

“I did not know.”

“You knew enough to put another piece in its place.”

He looked away.

Faruz took his copy of the sale entry from Nia and folded it.

“You are coming to the worksite,” he told Beruke. “Whatever you thought, you can help make the ground safe before my people move it.”

Beruke nodded.

Nia wanted to strike him. The wish was so immediate that she put both hands behind her back, where he could not mistake them. He saw anyway. She wanted him to say something that would make the wish unnecessary. He picked up the blanket instead and carried it to the sledge.

They left without eating.

The walk to the spur took them across ground whose apparent firmness deceived Nia at every change of colour. She knew stone, fitted joints, the movement of a deck beneath a street. Open plateau soil was another craft. The quarry worker led them around a shallow depression she would have crossed and showed a place where water remained beneath the crust.

“Your city could not stand here,” he said.

“It could not stand where it is either.”

He looked toward the dust on the horizon.

“No.”

Beruke walked beside the sledge. The haulers had given him a rope end and he kept it clear without being told. Nia watched him do useful, ordinary things and found each one unbearable. If he had been clumsy she could have shouted. He knew how to move a load. He had known that when he took the stone.

At a rest stop he offered her water. She took the skin because refusing would be childish, drank too little because accepting felt like forgiveness, and hated herself for making even thirst part of the quarrel.

“She asked for you,” she said.

“I know.”

“You do not know. You were not there.”

He sat on a low stone with his hands between his knees.

“Is she alive?”

“Yes.”

He bent forward until his forehead almost touched his hands. Nia had been keeping that answer from him, not deliberately in words but deliberately in everything around the words. She saw his relief and felt a sudden, unpleasant satisfaction before she could stop herself.

“She cannot come out until we put it back.”

“I will help.”

“You should have asked.”

“Yes.”

“Someone who knew.”

“Yes.”

“Not a clerk who read the same old store mark.”

“I did not ask the clerk.”

She stared.

“He would have asked why I wanted to sell it. I did not want to answer.”

The quarry worker stood a little way off, giving them the privacy of pretending to examine a harness knot. The haulers watered the animals. Beruke looked at the ground.

“I dressed the granite to keep the socket clean,” he said. “That is what I told myself. And so nobody would ask before I left.”

“Were you coming back?”

“I do not know.”

That hurt in a different place. She had expected him to deny it, or promise that he would have returned with money. His uncertainty left the abandoned room exactly as she had found it.

The quarry worker called them onward. Nia stood, gave Beruke the water skin and returned to the front of the sledge.

At the worksite, the load-stone sat beneath a frame holding a partly raised platform.

The quarry crew had used it to steady an awkward lift. It was not bearing the city’s impossible burden here, merely lending its extraordinary steadiness to a task ordinary timber could have managed less conveniently. Men worked beneath the raised edge fitting supports. One waved at the quarry worker and asked whether he had brought the last payment for the stone.

“He has brought its city,” the worker said.

The man came out from beneath the frame.

They explained. The foreman listened and began swearing at Faruz, Beruke, the old store designation and the habit of city people arriving with a crisis as if no one else had built anything that morning. Nia let him finish. When he asked whether she intended to pull the stone while his men were under the platform, she said no.

“Good. You are already more reasonable than your brother.”

They spent the afternoon transferring the load.

It should not have taken that long. A timber split where a previous worker had cut too close to a knot. The spare had been used elsewhere. The foreman sent two men to fetch it and made everyone stop until it arrived. Nia looked at the sun, at the city dust far away and at Beruke, who would not meet her eyes.

“We cannot hurry this,” the foreman told her.

“I know.”

“Then stop looking as if knowing is beneath you.”

She went to help dress the replacement timber. Her skill had a use here. The foreman examined the bearing face she prepared, accepted it and gave her the next piece without apology. She found that easier than sympathy.

Beruke worked beneath the frame when it was safe to do so. Nia watched him crawl into the narrow space. For an instant she wanted the foreman to call him back. Then she heard her mother’s bowl ringing in memory and looked away.

The foreman would not let Beruke work under the raised end alone. He sent a young quarry hand in beside him and gave Nia the outer wedge.

“Touch it when I say. Not when you think I meant to.”

She nodded. The wedge was ordinary wood. Its broad end had been struck so often that the fibres were opening. Nia turned it, found the sounder face and put the mallet beside it.

From beneath the platform her brother asked for a shorter block.

The quarry hand passed one. Beruke sent it back. There was a loose flake on the bearing face. Nia heard him explain where it lay and how to turn the block to see it. The quarry hand swore, scraped the surface and tried again.

“Now?” the foreman asked.

“Not yet.”

Beruke spoke so calmly that Nia looked under the frame to make certain it was him. He had his cheek near the ground and one arm extended to hold a guide in place. She could see the pressure whitening his fingers. He had always been good at this sort of work. As children they had stolen offcuts from the dressing yard and built cities across the kitchen floor; his had stayed up, hers had looked more like the real ones. Their mother had made them dismantle both before supper. Nia had usually taken the walls down too fast. Beruke had collected the pieces for her after she left the room.

“Now,” he said.

She struck the wedge. It entered a little way and stopped.

“Again.”

The second blow sounded different. Dust fell from the underside of the platform onto Beruke’s face. He closed his eyes and did not move his hand.

The foreman crouched to check. He sent everyone out, walked round the lift, and called the workers back into position. Twice more they shifted a small portion of the weight. Nia’s own urgency had become useless to her. The work advanced by movements too small to satisfy it.

When the new support held, Beruke crawled out backward. His shirt had caught beneath a splinter. He tugged at it, irritated, and Nia put a hand on his shoulder.

“Stay.”

She worked the cloth free. He waited with his face turned away from her. The seam had torn but not his skin. She pulled the loose thread through so it would not catch again.

The quarry hand sat on the ground and began coughing. The foreman brought him water, told him to wash his eyes before rubbing them, and asked Beruke whether he intended to buy the man’s mother a new son if this one choked to death while a city took back its property.

“I didn’t ask him to go in.”

“No. I did. Drink.”

He gave Beruke the cup next.

For a little while they sat among the offcuts while the frame settled onto its new footing. Someone brought a pot of food from the work shelter. There were more bowls than spoons. Nia shared a spoon with her brother because it was offered before she remembered being angry with him.

The quarry hand asked what Khazembo was like at night.

“Depends where you sleep,” Beruke said.

“Does it always sound like that?”

He pointed toward the distant city. From here they could hear only the occasional deep note of work beneath it.

“Not like that.”

“What does it sound like?”

Beruke tapped two fingers against his bowl, lightly, then paused and tapped once more. Nia recognized the rhythm before she recognized the sound he was imitating: the old upper water pipe, just outside their first room. He had complained of it for years.

“Not everywhere,” she said.

“In our house.”

“The pipe was repaired.”

“Badly.”

The quarry hand grinned. His own roof had a beam that clicked in hot weather. He described it at length, while Beruke ate and Nia watched the platform for movement. By the time the foreman called them back, the city’s dust had turned red in the sinking light.

They freed the stone near sunset.

It came out more quietly than Nia expected, a shaped piece of dark material whose surface held the light differently from the ordinary granite around it. The haulers placed it on the sledge with an attention that made the quarry crew cease talking. Beruke wrapped it. He knew how its corners needed padding. Nia could not bear that either, and made herself help rather than stand apart judging him.

The foreman required an undertaking concerning the cost of the replacement work. Nia signed for the city subject to the proper review. Beruke signed for the money he had received. He emptied the remaining fare onto a cloth. It was not enough. The foreman counted it, entered it and returned the few coins needed for food on the way back.

“I am not giving them to you,” he said. “I am paying to keep you moving.”

Beruke accepted them without pride.

They began the return under the first stars. The animals moved slowly with the load. Nia walked near the sledge until her legs shook, then accepted a place on its rear board for part of the way. Beruke walked beside her, holding the rope clear of the runners. Neither spoke for a long time.

At last he said, “I wanted one choice nobody praised.”

Nia looked at him.

“You have it.”

He flinched. She was glad for half a breath, then wished she had said nothing.

The sledge went on. Ahead, Khazembo’s lamps stood where the city should not still have been.

3.5 Under the floor

They brought the stone through the eating court because the store passage had narrowed during the night. A cook stood beside her overturned hearth, holding a ladle. She watched the haulers lift the sledge over the hearthstones and asked whether anyone intended to put them back.

Nia said she would.

“You’ll have forgotten by then.”

“Tell Beruke.”

The cook looked at him. News had arrived ahead of the sledge. He bent to the rear rail without answering her.

Under the Mason-Kin Hall, the lamps had burned down to blunt tongues. Dovek lay on a board with his head beneath the lower bearing. Someone had chalked three marks on the wall beside his feet. Nia looked at those before looking at the city caster. The lowest mark was new.

“Don’t put it down there,” he said.

She stopped the haulers. “It’s where you said.”

“I said that before the floor moved.”

They waited while he wriggled out. His beard was full of pale filings, and a welt ran across his forehead where the space beneath the bearing had diminished. He made them carry the stone another six paces to a mat behind the braces. Beruke reached for the wrapping. The senior mason caught his wrist.

“Leave that.”

“I know which way it came out.”

“You can show us with your mouth.”

Nia watched her brother pull his hand free. He kept his arms at his sides while they uncovered the stone.

It looked too small. After the journey, the arguments, the hours spent watching the quarry frame descend onto its new supports, she had expected to feel something when it returned. It lay on the mat with its rounded side uppermost and a seam of old dust in its groove.

Dovek sat beside it. He brushed the groove clean with a stiff hair brush, then asked for a lamp held lower. Nia crouched with it. The senior mason knelt opposite, and for a little while the three of them examined what the stone had suffered elsewhere.

A corner was chipped. Dovek touched it and looked at Nia.

“Quarry?”

“I didn’t see it break.”

“Then we don’t know.”

He marked the corner with a dab of wet chalk. Beyond the braces, a bowl sounded twice.

Nia put down the lamp.

Her mother was awake. The attendant beside her had gone to sleep sitting up, his head against a timber. Maleba had struck the bowl with the blunt end of the striker until he woke, then waited for Nia instead of explaining to him.

“Did he come?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Where?”

“Outside.”

“Eating?”

“No.”

“Send him to eat.”

“He should come down.”

Maleba turned her face toward her. Something had changed around her eyes. In the uncertain light Nia could not tell how much was exhaustion.

“Must I do that now?”

Nia felt the heat climb her neck.

“No.”

“Then send him to eat. And you. You’ve started answering questions I haven’t asked.”

Nia went back to the hall. Beruke was carrying empty water jars toward the stairs. She told him what their mother had said, and he looked past her toward the chamber.

“She doesn’t want me.”

“She wants you fed.”

He set the jars down too hard. One rocked against the other.

“Don’t make her kind for me.”

Nia had no answer that would improve matters. She picked up a jar and took it upstairs herself.

By morning there were two plans laid out on the hall floor. Dovek’s required them to lift the whole damaged bearing clear. The senior mason’s would have driven the stone back through the existing opening. Neither man liked the other’s plan. Nia stood behind the mason with a slate, writing down dimensions that changed each time someone reached under the assembly and found another damaged surface.

At last Dovek pushed the slate away.

“If I melt a replacement without knowing the seat, you’ll put it back under a crooked load.”

“The seat is cut to receive it.”

“It was cut to receive the old one.”

“And you know the old one?”

“I cast its upper ring.”

The mason looked up.

“When?”

“Before you had that belt.”

The mason’s belt bore three ranks of bronze bosses. Nia had never seen him without it.

“Then tell us what you put inside.”

Dovek rubbed a thumb over the welt on his head. He looked toward the open door, where apprentices and householders were trying to hear without appearing to listen.

“Shut that.”

Nia went to close it.

“Not her,” Dovek said when the mason motioned her out. “She can fit beneath it. You’re going to need her to recognize what she sees.”

For the next hour they spoke with an irritation so sustained that Nia almost missed the trust being placed in her. Dovek drew the hidden channels in the upper ring. The mason scratched out the usual outline of a load socket and showed the unequal shoulders beneath it. The stone had to sit with its chipped corner toward an empty relief, not toward the pressure face. Dovek’s casting would contract as it cooled; the mason’s shoulders would hold it differently once the city began to move.

Twice they called someone back from an errand because the instruction had already become wrong.

When the door opened again, a broad woman in a carving apron was waiting with a basket of hot food. Her name was Senga. Nia knew her as the carver who could make a continuous vine turn around three sides of a lintel without anyone seeing where the pattern changed. Senga put the basket between the plans.

“Move your feet. The broth goes there.”

“We’re working,” said the senior mason.

“I can see how well.”

She handed Nia a bowl first. Behind her came a younger man carrying bread and a packet of clean cloths. He kept glancing at the doorway to the carrier’s chamber.

Senga noticed. “If you want to ask her something, ask her. Don’t wear out the door with looking.”

He shook his head.

After he left, Nia learned that Senga had put her name forward for the coming Naming. Her husband had brought the bread. He wanted her to withdraw.

“Now?” Nia asked.

Senga tore a piece from a loaf. “It was before this.”

“And now?”

The carver chewed. She had been generous enough with the food and seemed disinclined to offer anything else.

Nia finished her broth.

They tried the first lift shortly after noon. Ruzha had spent the morning outside, barefoot beside the roots. She came down to hear the order herself rather than accept another apprentice’s version. The binders on her wrists had soaked through. When Dovek asked how long the cradle would hold, she made him come upstairs and look at the soil.

A fissure had opened beyond the third root. Water collected in it, black with disturbed earth. Ruzha pressed the end of a stick into the gap. Half the stick vanished.

“The trees that would hold this soil aren’t here,” she said. “I can make roots. I can’t make last year’s rain fall somewhere else.”

Dovek stood over the fissure for a while, then returned to the hall and abandoned the full lift.

They worked through a smaller opening. Nia went underneath with a lamp and a wedge, her cheek pressed against the cold floor. The load-stone waited on a low cradle immediately behind her. Dovek called for a quarter-turn on the brace screw. She heard the metal complain. The assembly rose no more than the thickness of her thumbnail.

“Again,” the senior mason said.

The bowl rang.

Nobody moved.

“Wait,” Nia called.

They waited. The bowl rang again, three quick blows. The attendant came from the chamber, pushing past the men at the brace.

“She says the left side is pulling.”

“The gauge is even,” said the mason.

Nia could see the gauge from where she lay. It was even. She could also see, through the gap beside the bearing, the thin line of light on the upper shoulder disappearing.

“Bring it back.”

“We’ve barely moved it.”

“Bring it back.”

The mason began to answer. Dovek put his hand over the man’s on the screw.

They eased the lift. Something inside the seat made a noise like a tooth breaking between stones. Nia shut her eyes. Dust lay across her tongue.

When she opened them, the line of light had returned.

Dovek took the lamp from her. He crawled beneath the assembly farther than she had thought his body would fit, and was a long time coming out.

“One of the inner keys has turned,” he said.

The senior mason stared at the floor. Then he called for a thinner chisel.

Nia went to her mother. Maleba’s fingers were still around the striker. The attendant offered to take it, and she tightened her grip.

“You heard?” she asked.

“Yes.”

Maleba closed her eyes.

The food sent for the afternoon attendants was cold by the time anyone fetched it. Nia found the bowls on a stair where a runner had set them down to help carry timber. She took them into the chamber. The man who had slept against the post tried to refuse his portion, embarrassed to eat while Maleba could not sit up.

“Eat,” Maleba said. “If you fall on me, I’ll have to carry you as well.”

He laughed once, too sharply, and began to eat.

Nia stayed while her mother drank. There were voices above them, arguing over the size of a chisel. Beruke’s voice appeared among them, repeating an instruction with unnecessary precision. Maleba heard it. She kept drinking and did not ask him to come down.

The turned key could not be reached from the opening where they had placed the stone. Dovek drew its position on the floor, scratched the drawing out, and went beneath the assembly to look again.

When he returned, he called for the rear inspection panel to be lifted.

The panel had not been opened in Nia’s working life. A store shelf stood over it, packed with small fittings arranged in trays. Beruke knew every tray. He began moving them before anyone asked and stopped only when the senior mason told him to leave the numbered pieces together.

“They are together.”

“They need to remain so.”

Beruke put down the tray he was carrying. He could have answered. Nia saw him decide not to, and the restraint made her more anxious than an argument would have.

They cleared the floor. The panel came up under two bars, releasing a smell of old oil and wet dust. A narrow passage ran beneath the rear of the bearing. Nia held a lamp over it. Something bright lay against the far wall: the edge of the turned key, just visible beyond a dark obstruction.

Dovek lay down to examine the gap. His shoulders would not pass.

“I can,” Nia said.

He looked at her, then at the opening. “You’d have to work left-handed.”

“I can do that.”

“Not well enough to strike blind.”

“I won’t be blind.”

“You’ll be holding the lamp.”

They tried lowering it beside her. The opening admitted her shoulder and the lamp separately, but not both at an angle that would let her see the key. The senior mason proposed removing another panel. Dovek pointed out the brace standing on it.

Beruke had been holding the emptied shelf against the wall. Now he said, “There’s a slot from the store side.”

Dovek turned.

“For checking the lower stock. You can see under the shelf from there.”

“Show me.”

Beruke led him to a small covered opening beside the store stair. It had been blocked with a folded sack to keep mice out. Removing the sack released a draught that made the lamp flare. From below, Nia could see the flame move across the wall behind the key.

“That,” she called. “Keep it there.”

The lamp disappeared. Dovek was testing whether the angle could be held from outside. Nia waited, lying with one arm beneath her. In the dark she heard Maleba cough through the stones. The sound seemed to come from everywhere.

The light returned.

Beruke said, “Can you see?”

“Yes.”

Dovek came back to the panel. They discussed the key, how far it needed to turn, which surface could be struck without damaging the seat. Nia repeated the instructions. She knew the tools. It was the confined space that made them unfamiliar: the chisel would have to be held against a face she could not reach with her stronger hand, the mallet swung through only a few inches.

They braced the upper ring again before she went in. The senior mason checked the screw twice. Then Nia lowered herself through the opening.

The air was warmer below. She could feel the bearing’s stored heat against her cheek before touching it. She set the chisel, found a position for her elbow, and asked Beruke to move the lamp a little to his right.

Her shadow covered the key.

“Back.”

He moved it.

“Not all the way.”

“I can’t see where your head is.”

Nia shut her eyes briefly. “Move it slowly. I’ll say.”

The light travelled across the stone. She waited until the bright edge of the key appeared.

“There.”

She struck. The sound was unexpectedly small, swallowed by the assembly. Nothing moved.

Again. Again. On the fifth blow the chisel slipped, and her knuckle hit the lower shoulder. She swore. Beruke asked whether she was hurt, bringing the lamp closer and blinding her.

“Stay where you were.”

He went back. Her hand throbbed. Above the panel, Dovek asked her to come out so they could inspect the tool.

“I have it.”

“Come out.”

She wanted to finish before anyone decided she had never been fit for the job. She also wanted her mother’s cough to stop coming through the wall. She handed up the chisel and backed out with difficulty, angry enough that the first breath of cooler air hurt.

The chisel’s edge had turned. Dovek set it aside.

“That key is harder than we thought.”

“Then a harder tool,” the senior mason said.

“And a smaller face.”

While they fetched one, Senga wrapped Nia’s knuckle. The carver had seen her strike and asked whether she was using the side of her wrist to compensate for the weak swing. Nia admitted it.

“Don’t. Bring your elbow in. Let the tool sit.”

“I can’t get my elbow in.”

Senga crouched at the opening. She examined the space, then asked for two thin boards and some folded cloth. They made a support that would hold Nia’s shoulder higher, giving her elbow room beneath it. It was uncomfortable in a different way, but when Nia tried the new position, the chisel stayed where she put it.

Beruke held the lamp for the second attempt. They had stopped apologizing to each other. She asked for a change; he made it. When his arm grew tired, he said so before the light began to wander, and they paused while he shifted his grip.

The key moved on the ninth small blow of the second attempt. Nia felt it give before she saw the thin gap open behind it.

“Moved,” she said.

Dovek asked how much. She told him. They eased one brace, tested, restored it, and let her strike again. The key turned by degrees. Each pause required her to lie still while the people above checked what the small change had done elsewhere.

Once Maleba rang the bowl, and everyone stopped. She wanted water. The attendant brought it, then came to tell them they could continue. Nia laughed once under the bearing, breathlessly, from a relief too quick to name.

At last the key came free. She caught it against the cloth before it could drop into the deeper space. It was smaller than the mark it had left on her hand.

She passed it up. Then she lay there for another moment because coming out required more effort than remaining still.

Beruke moved the lamp toward the exit. She followed its light.

Outside, the senior mason took the damaged key away to compare it with the replacements. Dovek went to inspect the newly cleared seat. Nobody made a ceremony of what Nia had done. Senga handed her water and asked whether she could still close her hand.

Beruke sat beside the empty shelf. His arm was shaking from holding the lamp.

Nia sat next to him.

“You knew that slot was there,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Why didn’t you say sooner?”

“You were all drawing.”

She leaned her head against the wall. After a little while he began laughing, quietly and without much pleasure. She laughed too. They stopped when Dovek came back, and both rose to see the next part.

3.6 Four hundred yards

The replacement bearing was cast in the eating court. By then the cook had given up asking for her hearth and was using the neighbours’ fire, which smoked more and was apparently arranged by a fool.

Dovek made her the first person to stand inside the barrier and inspect the cleared ground. She pointed out where spilled water ran beneath the paving. They moved the mould.

Nia carried charcoal until her arms shook. At the fourth load Senga took the basket from her.

“You’re the one who goes underneath. Keep your fingers.”

“I can carry charcoal.”

“So can I.”

Nia let go.

The pouring was done before sunrise. Dovek had shut everyone out except the two people he required to help. Through the barrier Nia saw the changing light on their faces and smelled the hot sand. Beruke waited near her with a fresh jar of water. He had carried messages all night. Once she had heard him tell a runner that he knew the way to the lower store, thank you, having worked there for four years before suddenly becoming incapable of finding a door.

Now he was silent. Nia leaned against the wall beside him.

“Have you slept?” he asked.

“A little.”

“You haven’t.”

“Neither have you.”

He gave her the water jar. They drank, passing it between them without cups.

Beyond the court the city’s edge stood at an angle that made the hanging cloths look crooked. The cloths were straight. Nia knew that and still found herself wanting to adjust them.

Senga came out of the barrier carrying empty tongs. Her husband followed with a bowl of sand. He was speaking to her very quietly. She stopped, listened, and set the tongs down.

“I said I would discuss it at home.”

“We haven’t been home.”

“Then wait until we have.”

He looked around at the court. There was nowhere to put the bowl that would not be in someone’s way. Beruke went over and took it from him.

The bearing needed time to cool. Time had become a thing people asked Dovek to shorten, as though it belonged to his craft and he was withholding a little out of professional pride. He would not. Nia slept for an hour on Senga’s folded apron, woke to the smell of broth, and found the carver sitting beside her with a small piece of wood on her knee. Senga was cutting a handle for the carrier’s striker. The existing one had raised a blister on Maleba’s palm.

“She asked for a wider grip,” Senga said.

Nia watched the curl come away beneath her blade.

“Did you ask her about the Naming?”

“Yes.”

“What did she say?”

“That my husband would keep asking if I had thought about it until I agreed with him.”

Nia almost smiled.

“Anything else?”

Senga turned the handle over. “Quite a lot.”

While the metal cooled, Nia went upstairs to retrieve a clean cloth and found the eating court full of people waiting for news.

They had been told not to stand near the bearing hall. They had obeyed by gathering at the nearest place where someone might emerge. The cook was feeding them if they paid and rebuking them if they blocked the fire. An old man had begun explaining the repair to people who had not asked, using a loaf and three stones. Every few minutes someone corrected him from an equally uncertain source.

Nia tried to pass unnoticed. The loaf was held out to her.

“Is this where the new piece goes?”

“No.”

“Then show us.”

She set down her bundle. She moved one stone, turned another, and explained enough of the arrangement to make clear why the work could not be hurried. The old man listened with the affronted attention of someone whose demonstration had been improved in public.

A woman asked whether Maleba would come out when the metal was fitted.

“After the movement.”

“How far?”

“We haven’t agreed yet.”

“Then what are they waiting for?”

Nia felt anger rising. It was an ordinary question, asked by someone frightened for her own room, but she heard the impatient assumption that her mother was one more part awaiting installation.

“They’re waiting because the metal is still hot.”

The cook pushed a bowl into her hand.

“Sit. You look ready to bite someone who can’t help it.”

Nia sat. The demonstration continued without her, somewhat more accurate than before. She ate while the cook told her about a nephew who had tried to mend a cracked pot with a substance recommended by a travelling seller. The pot had held beautifully until it was heated. Then it had released the entire dinner over the hearth.

“Did he ask for his money back?” Nia said.

“He said the seller had explained it was unsuitable for cooking.”

“What was the pot for?”

“That was my question.”

Nia laughed. It felt disloyal until she realized that Maleba would have wanted every detail of the nephew’s defence.

She took a second bowl down for Beruke. He was asleep sitting against the store wall, head forward, one hand still curled around the message tablet. She woke him carefully and gave him the food.

He stared at it as though she had brought an unfamiliar tool.

“Eat,” she said.

He obeyed. She sat beside him and repeated the story of the pot, making the nephew sound worse than the cook had. Beruke smiled without lifting his eyes from the bowl.

By late morning they could dress the casting. It did not fit at first. Dovek had expected that. The senior mason tapped the high edge with his nail until Dovek told him they had all heard it. Nia spent two hours helping them remove high spots. She rubbed soot across the bearing face, fitted it, drew it out, and watched the places where the black had rubbed away. Again. Again. Her shoulders ached from lifting her arms in the narrow space.

The missing inner key was replaced with one shaved to fit. They braced the socket from both sides. When the load-stone finally went in, Beruke guided its rear edge while Nia watched the chipped corner approach the relief.

“Stop.”

He stopped.

“Your side down.”

“How much?”

“Until I say.”

They moved it together, very slowly. The stone settled into its seat with a low note Nia felt through the heel of her hand. It kept sounding after they had taken their hands away.

Dovek asked for everyone to leave the lower hall except the carrier’s attendants. He listened from four places, one after another. The senior mason followed, unwilling to let the caster have the whole judgment. At the fourth place he put his palm flat against the wall.

The note had changed. It was less beautiful than Nia remembered, rougher around its lower edge. Neither man seemed troubled by that.

They tested the new assembly without movement first. A screw was eased, then another. The city settled onto its restored bearing. From outside came Ruzha’s voice, calling for a pause. One of the roots had taken more pressure than expected. They brought the screw back a little, waited, and tried again.

Maleba struck the bowl once.

Nia was already halfway to the chamber.

Her mother lay with her mouth open. The attendant held water where she could reach it. Maleba drank, swallowed twice, and looked at Nia.

“Tell me before you move.”

“I will.”

“All of you keep telling each other.”

Nia knelt beside her. “The stone is in. The new bearing has taken the load. Ruzha is releasing the roots a little at a time. When they’re clear, Dovek wants to move us to the hard ground past the old ditch.”

“How far?”

“Four hundred yards.”

Maleba looked at the low ceiling. She had never been able to see where Khazembo was going from this chamber.

“And then?”

“Then you come out.”

Her mother turned back to her. “Before the tests?”

Nia had not asked that. She went and asked it now.

Dovek wanted a full circuit of the new bearing under movement. The senior mason wanted a return over the same ground to compare the song. Nia made them come to the chamber and explain it to Maleba.

“No,” her mother said.

The mason drew breath.

“Four hundred yards. You can make your little circles another day.”

Dovek looked at the attendant, then at the bowl. “We stop on the first signal.”

“I know how to stop you,” Maleba said. “I want you to know when to stop yourselves.”

They left with an agreement none of them seemed quite satisfied with. Nia stayed long enough to wet a cloth and wipe her mother’s face.

Outside, households were being brought clear of the path. People who had refused to leave their rooms now carried possessions into the open, having discovered that remaining in a tilted house was one kind of courage and trusting it to walk another. Senga’s husband helped lower an old woman from the western block. He kept his eyes on the ladder until she was down.

Ruzha knelt beside the last root. Her hair was wet against her neck. She laid both palms on the bark and waited while the caster eased the final brace. The root creaked. A little soil rose around it, then sank. A long strand, thick as Nia’s wrist, withdrew beneath the earth with a dragging sound.

Ruzha fell forward on her hands.

Senga caught her before her face struck the ground. The two women remained there while the city took its first movement.

Nia ran to the hall.

At first she could not tell whether Khazembo had moved or whether the repaired assembly had merely taken up its slack. A jar shifted on a ledge. Dust fell from a carved mouth. Then the daylight at the upper stair changed, and beyond it a post went slowly backward.

The city was moving.

Nobody cheered in the lower hall. Dovek watched the bearing. The mason watched the stone. Nia watched the bowl in her mother’s hand.

Twenty yards. A pause. A test of the temperature where the metal bore against its seat. Another movement, longer this time. Somewhere above, people began to shout. A man’s shout turned into laughter and kept going until someone told him to shut up because they were trying to hear.

Maleba’s lips had drawn back from her teeth.

Nia knelt close enough for her mother to see her. “We’re past the ditch.”

Her mother nodded once.

The striker trembled against the bowl’s rim. Nia wanted to take it and could not. She held out her open hand instead. Maleba gripped two of her fingers.

The last movement seemed longer than all the others. Nia heard the bearing scrape and looked toward the hall. Dovek was on his knees, listening. He did not call a stop.

Her mother’s grip tightened. Then released.

“We’re there,” someone said.

Nia did not know whose voice it was. She waited until the city had ceased moving, then called for the attendants.

They had rehearsed lifting Maleba out. The rehearsal had used a folded mat weighted with stones. Nia had thought, watching them, that at least there would be one part of the work they understood completely.

Her mother cried out when they lifted her.

They stopped. They changed the angle. The second attempt was worse. Maleba swore at them, breathlessly, then told them exactly where to put their hands. Nia went behind her shoulders. Beruke appeared at the foot of the pallet, and this time nobody told him to leave it.

They carried her through the hall into daylight.

Nia had known there would be a change. She had watched the lines gather during the repair, had seen her mother become tired in ways sleep would not relieve. In daylight the change was no longer something the lamps might have done.

Maleba’s hair had thinned at the temples. Her hands lay on the blanket, narrow and mottled. The skin at her throat had loosened. Fifteen years had gone through her while they lifted and fitted and moved the city, and now she squinted angrily at the sun as though someone had chosen an inconvenient hour to bring her out.

“My shoes,” she said.

Nia bent close. “What?”

“Where are my shoes?”

No one had brought them.

Maleba shut her eyes. Beruke started laughing, a short helpless sound, and put his hands over his face.

3.7 Her own clothes

The first night at home, Maleba could not sleep because the house was quiet.

Below the city, the song had passed through her without stopping. Now she lay in her own bed and listened to Beruke trying not to make a noise in the next room. Every restrained movement became distinct: the jar set down carefully, the bench lifted instead of dragged, his bare foot touching a loose board.

“At least be clumsy naturally,” she called.

The bench came down with a bang.

Nia sat at the foot of the bed mending a torn hem. She had brought three dresses from the chest, and Maleba had rejected all three. The first pulled beneath her arms. The second had been chosen by Nia because it was easy to put on, which her mother had immediately recognized. The third was a good dress Maleba had kept for ceremonies and had no intention of wearing to sit in her own house while people came to see how badly she looked.

“Your blue one?” Nia asked.

“It needs washing.”

“I’ll wash it.”

“Tomorrow. Put that down.”

Nia kept sewing for another two stitches before obeying.

“Are they still outside?”

“Some.”

“How many?”

Nia went to look. Four women sat beneath the awning. One had brought a pot, one a parcel, and one nothing at all. The fourth had evidently joined them for the company and was telling a story that made the other three lean forward.

“Four.”

“Send the one with the story in.”

Her name was Ule. She had once shared a workshop with Maleba and began their reunion by telling her that the man in the next house had taken a second wife who could not bear his first wife’s cooking but had no objection to eating it.

Maleba moved a little higher on the pillows.

“Which man?”

Ule told her. Maleba’s laugh caught in her chest and became a cough. Nia reached for the water, but her mother waved her away and asked whether the new wife had discovered where he kept his good cloth.

The visit lasted too long. Nia knew that because her mother’s answers grew shorter, not because either woman would admit it. When Ule finally left, Maleba lay still for some time, smiling at the ceiling.

“That’s better,” she said.

In the dark hours before dawn, Nia woke to the sound of her mother trying to stand.

She was beside the bed before she knew she had moved. Maleba had both feet on the floor and one hand against the wall. Her face was set in the expression Nia remembered from childhood, the one that usually preceded an instruction to leave her alone.

“Where?” Nia asked.

“You know where.”

“I’ll get—”

“No.”

They crossed the room together. Maleba took Nia’s arm but refused to lean fully until her leg shook. Nia felt the weight arrive and adjusted without comment. In the narrow passage her mother stopped to breathe.

“Don’t tell your brother.”

“He’ll hear us.”

“Then tell him something else.”

Nia almost laughed. Her mother gave her a look, then smiled a little herself.

Afterward they sat on the bed’s edge. Maleba had begun to tremble from the effort. Nia brought a cover and put it around her shoulders. Neither lay down immediately.

“I thought I’d be stronger once I was out,” Maleba said.

“You’ve barely been out a day.”

“I know how long.”

Nia looked at the floor.

Her mother touched her wrist. “I’m frightened. You may let me say that without correcting the day.”

Nia nodded. She wanted to promise improvement and could not tell how much she knew from how much she needed.

They remained sitting until the trembling eased. From the next room came the sound of Beruke turning over with an exaggerated snore. Maleba looked toward the doorway.

“He heard.”

“Yes.”

“Terrible liar.”

Nia helped her lie down. This time, when she drew the cover up, Maleba took her hand and kept it for a little while.

The next morning Beruke brought breakfast to bed. He arranged the bowls on a tray, then began cutting the bread into small pieces.

Maleba watched until he had cut a second piece.

“Have my teeth gone?”

“No.”

“Then leave it.”

He put down the knife. He had been trying so hard since the repair that Nia found herself wanting to quarrel with him simply to get him out of the way. Instead she went to fetch more water and found the jar already filled.

Ruzha’s hearing took place two days later. Nia accompanied her. She had brought Ruzha before the masons and heard the promise made. The binder wore clean wrist cloths. Beneath them her hands shook when she tried to tie her hair.

Nia offered to do it. Ruzha turned her back without ceremony.

The hearing concerned the patch of settlement ground she had requested. Several people thanked her for the cradle before explaining why that particular ground could not be given. It was used for timber seasoning. It lay too close to a drainage run. The roots might interfere with the city’s departure. A man whose block had been evacuated said the residents would feel more secure if she stayed at a greater distance.

Ruzha listened to him. “How great?”

He named a place beyond the old quarry.

“Have you walked there?”

He had not.

“There is no water.”

The discussion continued. Nia waited for one of the masons to mention that the city’s own movements had damaged three nearby wells over the years. No one did. She mentioned it herself and was told that this was not the matter before them.

Ruzha pressed two fingers against Nia’s knee beneath the bench.

They left with permission to remain temporarily near the timber yards, where the existing households would discuss a longer arrangement. It was less than Ruzha had asked for and more than several speakers had wanted. Outside, Nia began an angry account of the hearing.

Ruzha stopped beneath the shade of a wall.

“I was there.”

Nia closed her mouth.

After a while the binder said, “If you know someone who will carry water this afternoon, send them. I can’t lift a full jar yet.”

Nia sent Beruke. He went without asking why she had chosen him.

At home she found Maleba sitting in a chair, dressed in blue. The washing had not quite dried. Dark patches showed along the hem, and her mother had tucked a cloth beneath herself to protect the seat.

Senga was with her. The carver’s husband stood by the door, holding his cap in both hands.

They stopped speaking when Nia entered.

“Don’t,” Maleba said to them. “She’s been stopping conversations all week.”

Senga looked at Nia, then back at her mother. “He says I can withdraw.”

“You can.”

“He means I should.”

Her husband twisted the cap. “I mean I’d rather have you.”

Senga looked away.

Maleba let the silence last. Nia had expected her to speak about the House, the honour, what they had earned. Those were the things her mother had always said when people praised her service. Instead she asked Senga what she feared most.

The carver rubbed a knuckle against her lower lip.

“Not coming out myself.”

“Dying?”

“No. Coming out and having everyone else decide which part of me they saved.”

Maleba looked at the cut bread on the table. Beruke had prepared enough for two meals before she caught him.

“Yes,” she said.

Senga’s husband sat down.

Later, when they had gone, Nia told her mother she would give back their household’s new standing if that would make the next Naming different. She had composed the offer while carrying water, and it sounded smaller in the room.

Maleba stared at her.

“Give back what?”

“The recognition. The places. If they say Beruke must—”

“They have not said he must.”

“They ask him every time he enters the hall.”

“Then make them stop asking. Don’t give away what I worked for.”

Nia stood beside the table with a bowl in her hand. “I didn’t mean—”

“I know what you meant. You keep making it sound as though I was tricked.”

“You didn’t know this would happen.”

“No. I did not. Now put the bowl down before you break it.”

Nia put it down.

Her mother reached for her cup. The movement was slow, and Nia stayed where she was, watching it, until Maleba had the cup safely in her hand.

The following morning Maleba asked to visit the cloth-workers’ room.

Nia said the room was across two joints and up a stair. Maleba asked whether the stair had been removed. When Nia said it had not, her mother sent her for the good blue cloth.

They went slowly. At the first joint Maleba waited for a movement that did not come; the city was still stopped. She looked down, annoyed at herself, and crossed with Nia beside her. The stair was harder. Halfway up, she sat on a low ledge and pretended to inspect a damaged corner while recovering her breath.

In the workroom, the conversation stopped when she entered.

“Don’t,” Maleba said. “I came to hear it.”

A woman moved a stool into a patch of good light. Another asked whether she would like tea. Maleba said she would like the strip of binding she had left unfinished before taking the chamber. Nobody knew where it was. They searched two baskets, then a shelf, then the place where someone had put it so it would be safe.

Nia sat against the wall and watched her mother take up the work.

The first stitches were slow. Maleba held the needle at the wrong angle, withdrew it, and tried again. Her fingers were not as strong as she expected. The strip slipped against her knee. She caught it before it fell and glared at anyone who might have offered to catch it for her.

The women resumed talking. They discussed the altered route, a son who had stayed too long with a trading party, a wall whose mason had blamed the householder’s cooking for a crack. Nia recognized the last complaint and kept quiet.

After a while Maleba began contributing. Her hands steadied when she stopped watching them so fiercely. A small length of binding grew beneath the needle.

Then she pricked her finger.

It was an ordinary accident. She had done it before. Nia nevertheless started forward, and Maleba’s expression stopped her halfway from the wall.

Her mother put the finger in her mouth, waited, and continued.

By the time they left, she had completed less than she would once have done before the first pot was hot. She showed the work to Nia on the stair.

“Uneven here.”

“A little.”

“You can say so.”

Nia touched the place where the stitches widened. “You pulled less on this turn.”

“Yes.”

They sat on the ledge again. Below them, people crossed the court carrying timber for the damaged block. Maleba watched them until her breathing eased.

“I want to go back tomorrow.”

“You’ll be tired.”

“Yes.”

Nia looked at the small completed length. Her mother folded it and put it in her sleeve.

Beruke was waiting at home. He had brought a pair of soft shoes from a maker who had been willing to let them try before paying. Maleba examined the stitching, criticized the fastening, and tried them on.

While she walked across the room, Beruke told her he had gone to the officers about repayment.

“How long?” she asked.

“They haven’t agreed. I can do part in work.”

“You can do good work.”

He stared at the floor.

“I wanted to go before they put my name with yours.”

Maleba sat down carefully. Nia moved toward the door, then stopped when her brother looked up. He wanted her there. She remained.

“They put it there when you were born,” Maleba said.

“I know what you mean.”

“Do you?”

He rubbed his palms against his knees. “Every time they asked, I could hear you saying yes. Even if you weren’t there.”

His mother watched him. She had taken off one shoe and held it in her lap.

“I wanted you to see what I had made possible.”

“I did.”

“And you wanted something else.”

“Yes.”

Nia expected the next answer to settle the matter, or wound him. Maleba seemed to have expected one from herself too. She turned the shoe over, examining the sole.

“I don’t like the place you chose to begin,” she said at last.

“I know.”

“I don’t know whether you do.”

He did not defend himself. The silence lasted until Nia could hear the cloth-worker’s song from the upper room. Her mother had returned with its tune, humming on the last part of the walk.

“Will you still be here at the Naming?” Maleba asked.

“Yes.”

“Then we can talk after it.”

Beruke nodded. He took the shoe she held and set it beside its mate. When he rose, she asked him to bring her the unfinished binding from her sleeve. He found it carefully, as though it might break.

Before they went out again, Maleba asked for the box above the sleeping shelf.

Beruke reached it more easily. He brought it down with a shower of dust, apologized when his mother coughed, and carried it into the light. The lid had warped. Nia held the box while he eased it free.

Inside were folded clothes, two small packets of letters, a child’s damaged shoe and things their mother had never thought worth throwing away. Maleba wanted the long cloth at the bottom. Beruke took the objects out carefully, making piles on the table.

Nia recognized the shoe.

“You kept that?”

“It was a good shoe.”

“I put my foot through it.”

“After you had worn it a year.”

Beruke looked at the split toe and began to smile. Nia knew what he was remembering and told him not to.

“You said the street had bitten you.”

“I was small.”

“You made us go back and look.”

“There was something sharp.”

“There was a step.”

Maleba reached for the shoe. Her fingers fitted over it awkwardly. Nia could see the seam of an old repair along one side, smaller stitches than her mother had managed that morning.

“Your father mended this part,” Maleba said.

Neither of them answered. They had been ready for her own memory of the quarrel, not his.

“He made a poor job of it. I told him the thread would wear through.”

“Did it?” Beruke asked.

“No. You grew.”

She handed it back to Nia, who put it beside the letters. Beruke lifted the final cloth. It was longer than he had expected and unfolded across his knees. A narrow border ran along it, faded where it had been exposed to light. Maleba touched the pattern and told him where to hold it while she examined the centre.

“I was going to finish this before the first time.”

Nia had never seen it. She had known there were unfinished things in the box, but had imagined the household sewing that accumulated whenever someone went away. The border repeated a design she associated with her mother’s older relatives. One end stopped halfway through a turn. The needle was still wrapped in a scrap and pinned to the cloth.

“For whom?”

“For me.”

Maleba said it with a little impatience. Beruke spread the cloth over the table. It covered the child’s shoe and half the letters. Their mother asked Nia to find the old thread, then rejected it after trying its strength between her hands. It broke with almost no pressure.

“We’ll buy some.”

“Not the bright one they sell near the steps.”

“I know.”

“You don’t. They changed it.”

“Then come and choose.”

Maleba looked at her. Nia waited, holding the broken thread. She had meant tomorrow, or the day after; she could already see her mother preparing to stand.

“After the Naming,” Maleba said. “I have enough to be stared at for this week.”

They folded the cloth together. Beruke tried to return everything else to the box, but she made him leave it open. There were letters she wanted to read, and she could not remember which packet held them.

Nia stayed while her mother untied the first bundle. Beruke went to fetch food. For several minutes Maleba read in silence, frowning at a hand Nia did not recognize. Then she laughed.

“What?”

“Nothing for you.”

She turned the sheet over. Nia sat back, caught between curiosity and the unfamiliar relief of being excluded from something that plainly pleased her mother.

When Beruke returned, Maleba hid the letter beneath the cloth. He asked what they had been doing. Nia said they had been waiting for him, and Maleba laughed again.

That afternoon Nia went to the naming officers with a smaller request. A person who declined should not cost an earlier carrier’s descendants the standing already earned. The officer said that had never been the formal rule. Nia asked him to say so where the candidates could hear.

It took three visits. On the second, Beruke came with her and was recognized before they had reached the door. The officer began to speak of restitution. Beruke said they would discuss that afterward, and Nia felt a sharp, unexpected pride in him.

On the third visit the officer agreed to read the words before the count.

He asked Nia to write them down exactly as they had agreed, so that nobody could accuse him of promising anything wider.

3.8 The Naming goes on

Beruke’s restitution began with the cook’s hearth.

She had remembered Nia’s promise and found it easier to collect from the brother. He lifted the paving, repaired the wet patch beneath it, and put the hearthstones back under close instruction. By midday the cook had revised her opinion of his usefulness sufficiently to let him eat beside the fire.

“You can go when it’s level,” she told him.

“It is level.”

“The oil says otherwise.”

He crouched to look. She had poured a little into the shallow pan and was watching it collect at one edge.

Nia left him there. In the hall, the load-stone had its own new entry. The word surplus had been struck through in the store record. Beside it the clerk had written who could authorize removal and where the assembly plan was kept. The clerk sanded the ink carefully. Nia waited to see where he filed the sheet.

The day of the Naming came bright and hot. Khazembo stood on the hard ground beyond the ditch, its western block still roped off for repair. Banners hung from sound posts. Children had been told not to climb the braces and were examining them with the attention the prohibition deserved.

Maleba wanted to walk to the ceremony.

Nia fetched the carrying chair anyway and left it near the door without comment. Her mother saw it and said nothing. She was occupied with her shoes.

They had fitted before she went beneath the city. Now one pressed across the swelling at the base of her toes, and the other slipped at the heel. Beruke knelt to adjust the tie. Maleba let him try, then told him he was pulling in the wrong direction.

“Bring the soft pair,” she said.

“The ones you don’t like?” Nia asked.

“The ones I don’t like.”

By the time they had changed them, the first drums sounded in the court.

Beruke looked toward the door. Maleba caught his eye.

“Go.”

“I can stay.”

“I know. Go and listen.”

He went.

Nia helped her mother stand. They crossed the room slowly. At the threshold Maleba stopped and looked at the chair.

“Halfway,” she said.

They took her halfway. Then she insisted on walking the remaining stretch with Nia’s arm beneath hers. People made room. Too much room, her mother complained, and then was irritated when someone failed to move quickly enough.

The naming officer had begun the preliminary words. Nia heard her own careful sentence read in his clear public voice. Declining would not revoke standing earned through a former carrier. The statement was narrow, just as he had insisted. Several people in the crowd turned to look for Beruke.

He stood near the cook, who had brought a covered bowl and was eating from it with complete disregard for the occasion.

The count followed.

Names, Houses, years. The old sequence passed from the officer’s voice into the answering voices around him. Nia knew when to respond and found herself late twice because her mother was shifting beside her. Maleba had brought a folded cloth for the chair and would not admit that it needed rearranging.

Senga stood with the other candidates. Her husband was several paces behind her. They were not touching. When the call came, she stepped forward.

A young man from a Sledge-Born household stepped back.

The small movement was plain enough. The officer paused, looked at his page, and continued. Nobody seized the man. Nobody announced that he had failed. A woman near Nia muttered something about children who ate what their mothers earned. Maleba turned her head.

“Louder,” she said.

The woman looked away.

Senga was answering a question. Nia could not hear all of it. Her mother had bent forward and was tugging at her shoe.

“It’s rubbing.”

Nia knelt. “Here?”

“No, the heel.”

She loosened the tie, slid a finger beneath the edge, and found the skin already reddened. She took out the small cloth she had brought, folded it twice, and eased it behind her mother’s heel.

Above them the crowd answered again. Senga’s husband had moved closer to the front. He held his cap under one arm, and his free hand was pressed flat against his thigh.

“Is that better?” Nia asked.

“Stand me up.”

She did. Maleba tried her weight, frowned, and tried it again.

“Yes.”

They stayed until the formal speaking ended. There would be songs afterward, and food, and the long circuit by which the new carrier was shown the city she would help move. Maleba wanted to go home before the crowd began following.

On the way they met Beruke. He had a piece of bread in his hand and dust down the front of his shirt. Someone had asked him to help shift a bench before the ceremony, and he had done it.

“I’ll finish the store work,” he said abruptly.

Maleba waited.

“And the merchant’s claim. Whatever they decide is mine.”

Nia looked at him. “Then?”

“I haven’t decided.”

Their mother held out her hand for the bread. He gave it to her. She tore it in half, returned one piece, and began eating the other while they walked.

At the house, Ule was waiting with the rest of the story about the new wife. She had learned where the good cloth had gone.

Maleba sat down without taking off her shoes.

“Tell me.”

The Second Cup

4.1 A good house for a horse

The mare had eaten the corner of the sale agreement.

Tsion took the remaining sheet from Senali, examined the damp crescent where the price had been, and asked whether his uncle habitually read contracts aloud to his animals.

“Only the parts he likes.”

“Then she has excellent judgment.”

The mare put her nose into Tsion’s sleeve. Tsion pushed it away, caught the lower lip, and looked at the teeth. Senali waited beside the stable door. He was a tall young man who had not yet learned how to occupy a doorway without appearing to block it. When his uncle called from the house, he moved aside with such speed that the mare lifted her head.

“Quiet,” Tsion told her.

The mare settled.

Tsion ran a hand down the near foreleg. No heat, no swelling. She lifted the foot, scraped away a packed bit of earth with her thumbnail, and found the sole sound. Behind her, the uncle was explaining that he had never asked more than the animal was worth.

“You’ve asked three prices since I came through the gate,” Tsion said. “If I stay until evening, we may discover what she is worth to you.”

He laughed. His name was Wosen, and Tsion knew him from two earlier journeys. On the first he had sold her a grey gelding with the disposition of an offended magistrate. On the second he had tried to sell her the same gelding, having bought it back from a man who needed his stable for a less expensive form of suffering.

This mare was better. Tsion walked her across the yard, turned her in a tight circle, then asked Senali to trot her to the fig tree and back. He obeyed without looking at his uncle. The mare moved freely. Dust rose behind her, pale in the afternoon sun.

“Who rides her?” Tsion asked.

“I do,” said Senali.

“And will you be sorry to see her go?”

He glanced at Wosen. “I’ll be going with her.”

That was the first Tsion had heard of it.

Inside, Wosen’s wife, Yalem, had begun roasting coffee. The smell reached Tsion before she sat down, and she abandoned the speech she had prepared about the price of fodder. Yalem rolled the beans in the shallow pan while a niece fanned the coals. She greeted Tsion without interrupting the motion.

“You’ve come at the right hour.”

“For whom?”

“For me. He’s been talking about that mare since morning.”

Wosen protested. Yalem tipped the beans and let Tsion smell them. The niece laughed into her shoulder.

They sat while the beans cooled. A neighbour arrived to borrow a sieve and remained to hear about the journey. Someone sent a boy for more cups. Senali went to fetch the sieve himself and returned with a small wooden wheel tucked under his arm. He put it beneath his seat when he saw Tsion looking.

Yalem ground the beans. The neighbour told Tsion that a man on the lower road had been claiming she once crossed the high pass in a storm with six horses tied to her waist.

“Five,” Tsion said. “The sixth had more sense than I did.”

Wosen asked whether the story was true.

“Which part would you pay to hear?”

“None of it.”

“Then the sixth horse was yours.”

They enjoyed that. Even Senali smiled, though he seemed to be waiting for a turn in the conversation that would never arrive if he remained polite. Yalem noticed and gave him the first full cup after the elders had been served.

“Now tell her.”

Senali set the cup down on his knee.

“I mean to go to the Monsoon Wharf. To the house of Mirem, where my mother’s cousin lived. There’s a hauling master there who takes learners.”

“Does he know you’re coming?” Tsion asked.

“He said he would consider me if I arrived before the northern ship.”

“What did he say he would pay?”

Senali’s ears reddened. “I haven’t asked.”

“Good. Then he can tell you anything.”

Yalem made a small disapproving noise at Tsion. She had earned the rebuke. The young man was making an effort to speak plainly, and Tsion had enjoyed herself at his expense.

She asked about the work. That was easier for him. He brought the little wheel from beneath his seat and showed her a model of a lifting drum. There were two grooves, a removable pin, and a crooked wooden hook. The hook was crooked because, he explained, he had shaped it for a different arrangement before discovering why that arrangement would break the rope.

Tsion turned the wheel. The hook climbed.

“How much can it lift?”

“That one? A spoon.”

“A useful beginning. Some houses make very heavy soup.”

He smiled properly then. She liked him.

Wosen said the coast would be good experience for a season. Senali said the hauling master took learners for longer than a season. Wosen reached for a small dish of roasted grain and asked whether Tsion’s buyer was still near the wharf.

“He was when he wrote.”

“Then you can take the boy.”

“The boy can ask,” Yalem said.

Senali did. He asked Tsion to travel with him to Mirem’s house, to show him how to manage the lower road, and to bring word if they became separated. He did not ask her to find him employment or persuade his uncle.

Tsion drank her second cup. It was an agreeable house: good shade, good coffee, a mare she could sell at a profit, and a young man who might make the journey less tiresome. She named a modest payment for the escort. Wosen began to bargain it down.

Yalem held the pot above his cup without pouring.

He stopped.

Before the third serving, Yalem sent the niece to fetch a packet from the inner room. The girl returned with two letters, one folded around the other. Senali reached for them. His uncle reached too. Yalem put her hand between theirs and gave the packet to Tsion.

“Read what has actually been asked.”

The first letter was from Mirem. It offered a place for Senali to sleep while he made inquiries, provided he came willing to help in the house. The second was the hauling master’s answer to a description of the little drum. It praised the young man’s interest and contained several questions about the way he had made the axle. At the bottom, in a different ink, was the invitation to come before the northern ship if he wished to be considered.

Tsion read the last part aloud.

Wosen said, “Considered.”

“Yes,” Senali said.

“That is not employment.”

“I know.”

“Then why have you told people—”

“I told them what he wrote.”

The neighbour who had come for the sieve became deeply interested in its rim. Tsion could see two old arguments beginning to occupy the room, both with paths worn into them. She handed the letters back to Senali before either could ask her to judge.

“How did you send the drawing?” she asked.

“A cloth carrier took it. Mirem asked someone at the wharf.”

“And the first letter?”

“I wrote that before the rains.”

He had been arranging this longer than Tsion had assumed. Wosen looked away at the mention of the date. The young man had not awakened one morning with the sea in his head.

Yalem took the empty cups. When she rose, Senali rose too, but she sent him for water before he could follow her. Tsion went to help carry the tray.

In the workroom, the niece was cutting small pieces from a loaf while pretending not to listen through the open doorway. Yalem gave her a task outside. The girl left slowly enough to make her feelings clear.

“Does he know what it costs?” Tsion asked.

“To go? Some of it.”

“And to stay?”

Yalem scraped the pan. “We know what he does here. He has made sure of that.”

Tsion leaned against the wall. The room held the warmth of the fire and the sharper smell of recently ground beans. A basket of mending stood beside the door. One sleeve had been turned out, the stitches patiently opened before a proper repair could begin.

“Wosen thinks it will be a season.”

“Wosen thinks a thing becomes agreed if he says it at every meal.”

“What do you think?”

Yalem stopped scraping.

“I think the boy put the seasoned wood under his bed because every time he put it in the common store, someone burned a useful piece. I think Dawet cannot learn from a person who takes the tool out of his hand whenever he works too slowly. I think we will have a difficult month.”

She resumed her work.

“I also think you will enjoy his company until he asks you the same question a fourth time.”

Tsion smiled. “I can answer it differently the fourth time.”

“That is what he will be checking.”

Senali came in with the water. They moved aside for him. He filled the vessel without spilling, then stood waiting to know whether he was needed for anything else. Yalem told him to sit with his uncle. He obeyed, though his face had closed again.

Tsion watched him leave.

“You call him a boy,” she said.

“So do you.”

“I’ve known him for an afternoon.”

Yalem set the pan down. “Then perhaps you will find it easier to stop.”

At the third serving, they spoke the undertaking again. Tsion would bring Senali to the coastal household he had named, keeping company with him and providing the ordinary guidance she had offered. He would travel under his own name and answer for his own purchases. If injury or closed roads delayed them, she would send word when she could. The warmth of the cup passed into Tsion’s fingers and remained there after she put it down. Senali watched her do it before drinking his own.

Then they discussed the mare’s price.

By dusk Tsion owned her. The damaged agreement had been replaced, and the mare had been moved beyond reach of the new one. Senali went to pack. Wosen walked with Tsion to the yard.

“He’ll be back before the long rains,” he said.

Tsion looked toward the room where Senali was folding his blankets.

“You heard what he asked.”

“He’s young. A little salt air will make him miss good water.”

She might have asked what work the young man was leaving behind, or whether the letter from the hauling master said anything about returning. Instead she rubbed the mare’s forehead and remarked that young people generally came home hungry.

Wosen laughed and seemed reassured.

That evening Yalem gave Tsion a place to sleep beside the inner wall. The household talked late. She heard Senali and his uncle through the partition, their voices lowered, then Senali alone moving things in his room. The mare stamped once in the yard. Tsion listened for a second stamp, heard none, and went to sleep.

4.2 The wheel he leaves

The broken piece arrived before breakfast, carried by two neighbours who had not come to say farewell.

It was an axle collar from the water wheel below the house. A tooth had split along the grain. Senali took it from them, turned it, and looked toward the yard where the mare stood saddled.

“I told you that one was going.”

One of the neighbours, a woman with flour on her forearms, folded her arms over it. “You told your cousin.”

“Where is he?”

“Looking for you.”

The cousin arrived while they were speaking. His name was Dawet. He was older than Senali and had the broad, patient face of a man who had spent a lifetime being told that patience would compensate for whatever he did not know. He carried a replacement piece cut from green wood.

Senali stared at it.

“That will swell.”

“It was what I had.”

“You should have used the seasoned block.”

“Which one?”

“The one under—”

He stopped. The seasoned block was under his own bed, beside the model drum and the tools he had packed.

Tsion unsaddled the mare.

At the wheel she found a queue of people who had expected the morning’s work to be done before the heat. Two sacks of grain lay beneath a cloth. A boy guarded them from chickens with an attention he had never given his lessons. The wheel’s slow knocking had become a hard irregular blow.

“Stop it,” Senali said.

The flour-marked woman, whose name was Lemlem, had already pulled the feed clear. “We have.”

“Before it reaches the collar again.”

Dawet ran to the sluice. By the time the wheel stopped, Senali was knee-deep beside it, feeling the split with both hands.

Wosen came down after them. He did not say that Senali ought to remain. He asked whether the repair would last until the next rains, whether Dawet understood the alignment, whether the second collar was sound, and who would answer if it failed while people were waiting to grind.

Each question was reasonable. Tsion grew irritated with him anyway.

“Sell me the grey as well,” she said. “Put part of the price toward someone who can tend this.”

Senali looked up from the water.

“The grey?” Wosen said.

“Yes, that expensive grievance in your stable. I’ll find him a priest to argue with.”
