“We haven’t discussed—” Senali began.

“You’ll need someone paid,” Tsion told Wosen. “A neighbour who does this between his own work won’t become a wheelwright because you wish him one.”

Senali climbed onto the bank. Water ran from his clothes.

“Stop.”

They both looked at him.

“I’m here.”

“I can see that,” Tsion said.

“Then speak to me before you buy another horse to settle my life.”

Lemlem turned away to hide her smile. Tsion felt it even without seeing.

“I was trying to help.”

“You could hold the collar while I cut it.”

So she did. They carried the damaged piece to a patch of shade. Senali fetched the seasoned block from his room, then the adze he had meant to take. Dawet sat beside him, asking questions that made Senali impatient until Tsion began asking them too.

“How will he tell which side faces the water?”

“It only goes one way.”

Dawet held up the old piece. “I put that one on backward.”

Senali looked at it. After a while he cut a mark into the new block.

The repair consumed the morning. Tsion watered the mare, brought food, and returned to find the cousins arguing over a wedge. Dawet wanted to know why the wedge should be left proud. Senali said because they might have to take it out. Dawet asked why he had not said so the first time.

They ate under the wheel-house awning. Lemlem had made enough for everyone, though her own work had been delayed. She told Senali exactly how many people depended on the wheel and then asked whether he would write to describe the sea.

“You want me to go?” he said, surprised.

“I want my grain ground. I can want you to write as well.”

She took the empty bowls away before he could answer.

In the afternoon Senali made Dawet fit the new collar himself. It went badly twice. On the second attempt Senali reached to take the mallet, then withdrew his hand with visible effort. Dawet struck the wedge and listened.

“Again?”

“No. That’s enough.”

They let the water through slowly. The wheel turned. The collar passed its first pressure point without knocking. Dawet’s face changed. He watched the second turn as closely as the first.

Wosen asked whether Senali could leave the adze.

The young man said nothing.

Tsion took the mare uphill. She did not wait to hear the answer.

They departed the following morning. The adze remained at the house. Yalem had packed food into a basket too large for Senali to carry comfortably, and he had repacked it twice while she added things he had removed. At the gate she pulled his face down and kissed his forehead.

“Ask about your wages before you work a month.”

“I will.”

“And if the man is a fool, don’t make him your uncle because he has a roof.”

Wosen stood behind her. He heard, which was surely intended.

He embraced Senali stiffly. Tsion saw the young man’s hands hover before settling on his back. Neither promised a return date.

On the road, Senali walked beside the mare for nearly an hour before speaking.

“You told him I’d come home.”

Tsion looked ahead.

“I said young people come home hungry.”

“What did you mean?”

She had enjoyed that sentence when she said it. She disliked having to examine it now.

“I meant he’d stop worrying if I made him laugh.”

Senali adjusted the basket on his shoulder.

“He didn’t stop. He thought you agreed.”

Tsion took the basket from him and secured it behind the saddle. She had no joke ready that she wanted him to hear.

4.3 Warm stone

The pass-stone was warm in Senali’s palm when the sky was still clear.

He had picked it from the hollow where travellers left it and stood looking at it as though someone had played a trick on him. Tsion took it, held it against the inside of her wrist, and put it back.

“We’ll stop at the upper shelter.”

“Before noon?”

“Yes.”

“The lower road is quicker.”

“It is.”

“Then—”

“The stone doesn’t tell us where the rain will fall. If it falls on the lower road, we’ll be beneath it.”

He looked down the valley. Far below them the track curved around a dark slope and disappeared into trees. It looked sheltered. Tsion remembered bringing a pack animal up from that curve with mud in its ears and no load left on its back.

She led the mare toward the shelter.

They arrived before the keeper had finished sweeping. He examined the sky, then Tsion, and asked whether her horse had made a reservation. His name was Gebru. His beard was white except for the part beneath his mouth, which bore evidence of breakfast.

“She’s prepared to speak well of your establishment,” Tsion said.

“Let her speak to the fodder.”

There was less fodder than she wanted. There were also two muleteers, a woman travelling with a sick sister, a cloth seller whose bundles occupied three people’s space, and a young singer who had accepted a ride uphill and now regretted the direction. Gebru put Tsion near the door and gave the mare a place beneath the outer roof.

The rain came in the afternoon, hard enough to throw droplets through the woven wall. Senali watched it for a while, then went to help Gebru deepen a runnel beyond the threshold. Tsion took off the mare’s saddle and found heat along the near foreleg.

The animal had shortened her stride during the last climb. Tsion had noticed and told herself it was the slope. Now the mare shifted away when she pressed above the fetlock.

She asked for cold water. Gebru brought a bucket and looked at the leg.

“Stone?”

“Perhaps.”

“Long way to the sea.”

“I know where the sea is.”

He left her with the bucket.

Inside, the cloth seller was arguing that his bundles could not be moved because one contained a commission for a person whose rank should concern them all. The woman with the sick sister asked whether the rank would keep the cloth dry if she threw it into the rain. He shifted one bundle.

Senali came back muddy to the knees. Tsion asked him to hold the mare while she changed the wet cloth around the leg. He did it gently, speaking to her in the private voice he had used at home.

“You knew she was sore,” he said.

“Just now.”

“On the climb.”

Tsion pulled the cloth tighter than she intended. The mare lifted her foot.

“Hold.”

Senali steadied her.

After a while he said, “We can rest tomorrow.”

“We may have to.”

“You sound angry with her.”

“I’m angry with the weather.”

He looked through the roof at the rain, which continued without apparent embarrassment.

That night Gebru served a thick stew and portions of flat bread. The singer attempted to earn a larger portion by singing about a guest who had died of hunger in a wealthy man’s house. Gebru joined in from the second line, using a version in which the guest had brought nothing, eaten everything, and stolen the bowl.

By the end the sick woman was laughing into her blanket. Her sister asked them to stop because laughter hurt her chest, then demanded the last verse when they did.

Tsion felt her temper ease. She told a story about the grey gelding. Senali corrected the part where she claimed it had refused to cross a bridge because it disliked the view.

“He refused because you were carrying a red cloth.”

“It was an ugly view.”

“You waved it at him.”

“To improve it.”

He laughed. It was the first time since they left the house that he had laughed without remembering the quarrel immediately afterward.

Later, when the others slept, he sat beside her near the banked fire. His little model drum lay between his knees. He was replacing its string with a finer one begged from the cloth seller.

“What does your buyer need her for?” he asked.

“His daughter’s riding.”

“Does she ride well?”

“I haven’t met her.”

“Then how do you know this is the right mare?”

“The father described her.”

Senali looked at her until she added, “I’ll watch the girl ride before I sell.”

“If we arrive in time.”

Tsion turned a stick in the ash.

“The price is good,” she said. “Better than I’ll get inland. I planned the rest of my buying around it.”

“So did I. Around getting there.”

“Yes.”

He tightened the string. The little hook rose unevenly, caught, and fell. He began again.

Before dawn the sick woman’s sister woke Tsion.

The woman had begun to shake. The fever had risen during the night, and the sister wanted to go down at once to the healer in the next settlement. She had already packed their things. Their bundle stood beside the door, tied so tightly that the cloth strained around its contents.

Tsion sat up. The shelter was dark except for the fire. She could hear rain beyond the roof, lighter than the previous evening but steady.

“How far is the healer?”

“Half a day.”

“On a good road?”

The sister looked at her.

Gebru came from his sleeping place and knelt beside the patient. He knew the healer and the lower track. He also knew a woman at a farm above the shelter who might have something useful and, more importantly, would know whether the patient could be moved. He sent the singer, who was light on his feet and had complained most loudly about being idle.

The singer stared at him. “In the rain?”

“Bring her in the rain too.”

He went.

Tsion helped the sister loosen the patient’s clothes. The woman was trying to apologize for occupying so much space. Her sister told her to stop, with a fierceness that silenced the whole room. Senali brought water and was sent for a clean cloth. The cloth seller offered a strip from a damaged piece, then became embarrassed when everyone thanked him as though he had given away his entire trade.

While they waited, the sister explained that she had delayed leaving home because the illness had seemed to improve each morning. She kept repeating the sequence: better at first light, worse after noon, a little food accepted, then none. Tsion listened until she realized the woman was trying to find the exact hour at which her decision had become wrong.

“She’s here now,” Tsion said.

The sister rounded on her. “I know where she is.”

Tsion shut her mouth and held the basin.

The woman from the farm arrived before sunrise with the singer behind her, wet through and offended by the hills. She asked everyone except the sister to move away. She examined the patient, asked what she had eaten and drunk, and smelled the cup in which a travelling remedy had been mixed. Then she asked who had sold it.

Nobody knew the seller’s name.

She did not promise to cure the fever. She stopped the remedy, gave the patient small amounts of water, and stayed until her breathing eased. When the sister again asked about going downhill, she told her they would decide after the rain stopped.

The shelter settled around this new waiting. Gebru put more fuel on the fire. The muleteers took their harness outside to leave the patient room. Senali repaired a split cup handle while listening for another request. Tsion went to see the mare, feeling foolishly grateful that the animal’s problem would at least permit examination without being asked whether she had waited too long.

When she returned, the sick woman was asleep. Her sister sat beside her, holding the bundle she had packed for departure. She had loosened its knots but had not put anything away.

Tsion sat nearby. Neither spoke for a while.

“I’m sorry,” the sister said at last.

“For what?”

The woman gave her a tired, irritated look. Tsion accepted the correction and nodded.

They remained there until the patient woke and asked why the singer was staring at her as though he had composed the ending already.

In the morning the mare was worse. Tsion watched her take four steps, stopped her, and removed everything from her back.

4.4 On foot

They stayed at the shelter two more nights.

On the second day Senali repaired Gebru’s hauling strap. A buckle had worn through its backing, and the keeper had been tying it shut with a piece of cord. Senali took it apart, asked for scrap leather, and spent most of the afternoon discovering why the original maker had cut the backing in an awkward shape.

He wanted to explain the discovery to Tsion. She was kneeling beside the mare with another cold cloth and answered without listening. After two attempts he went to tell the cloth seller, who knew enough about stitching to argue with him.

Tsion heard them through the wall. Their voices rose, then lowered over the strap. She could have joined them. Instead she remained beside the animal until her knees hurt.

On the third morning a small pack train arrived from the upper road. Tsion bought space for their bundles and the heavy basket of food. The owner named a price appropriate to a person who knew she had no good alternative.

She paid it. Senali opened his purse.

“Put that away.”

“Half the basket is mine.”

“And the mare is mine. We are walking because of her.”

He paid for the basket anyway, quietly, while Tsion was checking the lead rope. She found out at the first halt and let it stand.

The descent took days. Rain had left the path slick in places and broken its edge in others. Tsion led the mare on the outside of the track where the ground was kinder, walking close enough to see every change in her step. Senali went ahead when the footing narrowed and came back to report it. He became good at reporting only what mattered: loose stones after the bend, a fallen branch they could pass beneath, water over the track but no depth beneath it.

At a loading stage above the lower settlements, the train stopped. One of the crosspieces had shifted, leaving the planks unsupported along the outer edge. The muleteers wanted to move the animals across one at a time and hope the inner side held.

Senali knelt beside it. He could see how to spread the load with a spare spar, but the spar belonged to a carrier whose bundle required it.

“Then we unload,” said the train owner.

Nobody thanked Senali. They spent an hour carrying goods across by hand. Tsion waited with the mare while a muleteer complained that a young man with a clever eye was more expensive than a blind one.

Senali heard and flushed. Tsion was about to answer when the muleteer grinned at him.

“You can carry mine first.”

He did, and by the time the animals crossed, they were arguing about the best way to secure the new spar. The argument was friendly enough to make Tsion feel briefly left out.

That evening they ate in a lower settlement where the air was warmer and the cooking smelled different from the pass. A woman serving them recognized Senali’s household name. Her sister had been at the wheel-house the day of the repair.

“How is Dawet managing?” Senali asked.

“He’s telling everyone he fixed it.”

Senali put down his spoon.

“Did he?”

“He put it on, didn’t he?”

“Yes.”

“Then he’ll have to keep it going.”

The woman sat with them long enough to hear about the road. She said Wosen had been speaking of an escort who encouraged young men to abandon their people. Tsion felt herself stiffen.

“What else does he say?”

“That she paid too little for the mare.”

Tsion laughed despite herself. Senali did not.

Later he walked with her to water the animal. The mare was improving. The heat had diminished, though Tsion would not yet put a load on her.

“He’s making it your fault,” Senali said.

“He sold me a horse. It usually takes a month before a man regrets that much.”

“I’m serious.”

“So am I.”

He watched the mare drink.

“I didn’t tell Dawet everything. About the wheel. I thought if I left enough written down, he’d manage.”

“Can he read your drawings?”

“Some.”

Tsion rinsed the bucket.

“Then write again from the coast. Ask which part he can’t.”

Senali picked up the empty bucket before she could. “You make it sound easy.”

“The writing? No. I was going to offer to dictate. You may still refuse.”

He smiled a little.

At the lower settlement, a carrier offered Senali a place on a fast cart.

The carrier had stopped to water his animals and recognized Yalem’s household name. He had taken sacks from the wheel-house before. He was going to the coast with spare room beside the driver, no heavy load, and an impatience to finish the road before another rain. He could bring Senali to the wharf well ahead of a woman leading a recovering mare.

Senali looked at Tsion before answering.

She was examining the mare’s foot. She had heard every word and wished he had not looked, because now any answer she gave would appear to release or retain him.

“The road is good ahead,” the carrier said. “He’ll be there tomorrow evening if the axle behaves.”

Senali asked whether he knew Mirem’s house.

“Everyone knows Mirem’s house. If you owe her, she makes certain of it.”

The offer was genuine. Tsion knew the man well enough to trust him with an animal, which she considered a stronger recommendation than trusting him with an anecdote. He had spare room and wanted company. No one was proposing a deception.

She lowered the mare’s foot.

“You could go,” she said.

“And you?”

“I’ll come when she can.”

The carrier went to bargain for fodder, leaving them beside the trough. Senali watched him go, then looked at the road downhill. He had packed his model carefully that morning. It would be easy to put his small bundle on the cart.

“What happens to the cup?” he asked.

“We can agree to change the way we finish. I would place you with him in front of witnesses, and he would take the road I’ve promised to see you through. Or we can release each other here.”

“Would it hurt you?”

“Only if I pretended I had done what I hadn’t.”

She had been ready with that answer. She was less ready for his next question.

“Do you want me to go?”

“I want you to get there.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

Tsion rubbed the mare’s neck. The animal had begun nudging the trough with her lip, discovering whether another drink could be persuaded out of an empty place.

“I would miss the company.”

Senali smiled, then lost the smile as he looked toward the cart again.

“If I stay, I miss more time.”

“Yes.”

“You’ll tell me to go.”

“No.”

“You just did.”

“I said you could.”

He gave her an exasperated look, so like his aunt’s that she had to stop herself commenting on it. He was trying to make a decision. He did not need to be turned into a charming recollection of home.

She went to settle the fodder account and left him alone.

The keeper of the yard had charged for the mare’s feed by the bundle, then counted two partly used bundles as whole. Tsion argued mildly, lost, and paid. During the argument she kept seeing the empty place beside her on the road. She had complained about Senali’s questions, his impatience, the way he wanted every piece of advice justified. Alone, she could walk at the mare’s pace without explaining herself. She found she did not much want to.

When she returned, Senali was helping the carrier secure a wheel guard. The man’s cart had a loose bracket. Senali had spotted it and was fitting a wooden spacer before it worked free.

Tsion waited until they finished.

“Have you decided?”

Senali tightened the last fastening. “I’m staying.”

She felt relief and made her face less pleased before turning fully toward him.

“Why?”

He stood, wiping his hands. “Because I don’t want to arrive tomorrow with nobody I know. Because I’ve spent two days thinking I should be faster and I don’t know what I’ll do when I get there. Because I like the mare. Choose one.”

“I’ll take the mare. She has no manners and needs encouragement.”

He laughed, then became serious again.

“I may be sorry later.”

“You may.”

The carrier shrugged when told. He had wanted company, not a passenger badly enough to insist. Tsion gave him a message for Mirem saying they were delayed and still coming. Senali added his name in his own hand. Watching the cart leave with that little piece of paper made the destination feel closer and more real.

For an hour afterward, Senali was irritable. He walked too far ahead, came back, asked why Tsion stopped where there was no shade, and complained that the basket strap had twisted. Tsion let the first two complaints pass. At the third she stopped him, straightened the strap, and told him he was allowed to regret his decision without making her carry all of it.

He said nothing for some time.

Then, at the next halt, he took the mare’s lead while Tsion sat down. He offered no apology and no further explanation. He stood with the animal while she drank, looking at the road the cart had taken.

The following day they left the pack train for a slower road along the cultivated slopes. The mare no longer needed the gentlest ground, but Tsion wanted another day before testing her under a rider. Senali asked whether she was being too cautious.

“Yes,” she said. “Perhaps.”

He seemed surprised by the answer.

They stopped near a stream at noon. Tsion took off her boots and put her feet in the water while the mare grazed above them. Senali unpacked the last of Yalem’s food. Some of it had become hard enough to discourage ordinary teeth. He softened it and divided the pieces.

“You could have sold her uphill,” he said.

“I could have sold her before I bought her, if I had known who wanted her.”

“You know what I mean.”

She did. He wanted to know whether the journey had become an obligation she resented. She had not decided how much of that to tell him.

“I liked the idea of this buyer,” she said. “A girl who needed a steady mare, a good price, a road I knew. I thought I’d be clever about three things at once.”

“And me?”

“You were the fourth.”

He looked at the softened bread in his hand. “I don’t like being one of your bargains.”

“You aren’t. You were company. I thought that would be easy too.”

The stream moved around her ankles. She flexed her toes against a smooth stone and watched a small insect fight its way across the surface.

Senali said, “I used to come here with my mother.”

“To this place?”

“Near it. Before she died. We went down to see her cousin once. I remember the stream more than the road.”

Tsion had known his mother was dead. She had not asked when. She asked now.

He told her, then began describing an argument his mother had had with a man over a basket. He could not remember what the basket contained or why either of them wanted it. What he remembered was her standing ankle-deep in water, holding it above her head while the man shouted from the bank.

“Was she stealing it?” Tsion asked.

“I don’t think so.”

“You sound unsure.”

“I was small. I thought she was winning.”

Tsion laughed, and he laughed with her. He tried to recall the end of the argument and could not. They spent some time inventing possible endings, each less likely than the last.

Afterward he washed the bowls. Tsion stayed with her feet in the stream until he told her the mare had begun examining their food basket. She got up quickly, swore at the cold water in her boots, and found him grinning.

“She hadn’t touched it.”

“She was thinking.”

“Then let her finish. She may improve on us.”

They went on rested. For the rest of the afternoon, whenever one of them fell quiet, the other proposed another ending to the story of the basket.

They first saw the sea from a bend in the road where Tsion had intended to stop and make an occasion of it. She remembered her own first view: the impossible expanse, the distant sails that appeared too small to hold people, the light spread flat across moving water.

Senali was looking at the mare’s foot when they came around the bend.

“There,” Tsion said.

He looked up.

For a while he said nothing. Then he asked where the other side was.

She pointed vaguely toward the horizon.

“That is not an answer.”

“No. It isn’t.”

He stood a little longer. A pack train passed behind them. The mare tugged toward a clump of grass, and Tsion let her have it before they continued down.

4.5 Three weeks late

Mirem’s house had a carved door, three businesses, and a smell of fish that no amount of washing had persuaded to leave.

Tsion liked it immediately. Senali stood beneath the lintel holding his model drum and looking as though he had come to apologize for it.

Mirem received them in a shaded court behind the front shop. She was sorting cloves from a shipment that had taken damp. Her fingers moved while Tsion introduced them. At the name of Senali’s mother, she stopped.

“She used to steal the crisp edges from the pan.”

Senali smiled uncertainly.

“You don’t remember me,” Mirem said.

“No.”

“You were small enough to carry and heavy enough to make me stop.”

She rose and embraced him. He put the little drum behind his back to keep it from catching in her clothes.

The warmth that had remained in Tsion’s hands since the third cup faded as Mirem led him inside. Tsion flexed her fingers. She had kept the undertaking. It surprised her that the sensation should end so quietly, between a greeting and a request to bring the packs.

Mirem found the mare a place in a neighbour’s yard. She found Senali a mat in a room shared by two men waiting for passage. For Tsion she offered a small room whose roof leaked only on one side. That evening they ate rice with a fish sauce sharp enough to make Senali cough. Mirem’s youngest granddaughter watched him drink water and asked whether people in the highlands seasoned food with dust.

“With good manners,” Tsion said.

The child considered this. “Does it taste like anything?”

Mirem laughed so hard that she had to put down her bowl.

The hauling master came the next morning. His name was Bakiri. He had one shoulder higher than the other and a habit of examining every rope he passed, even in someone else’s courtyard. Senali brought out the model drum before Bakiri had finished his greeting.

The master took it, worked the little hook up and down, then turned it over.

“You cut this?”

“Yes.”

“What did you use for the pin?”

“A nail.”

“What sort?”

Senali hesitated. “One that fitted.”

Bakiri nodded and handed it back. “The ship’s late.”

“How late?”

“Three weeks already.”

“When will it come?”

“When the wind has finished somewhere else.”

Senali waited for more. Bakiri explained that the new hauling frames were aboard the absent ship, together with their maker, who would be responsible for installing them. Until then he could take no learner for that work. He could offer day labour moving sacks if there were sacks to move.

Senali’s mouth tightened.

Tsion saw it and looked away, giving him time to choose his answer. He asked about the wages. Mirem, passing with a basket, stopped long enough to hear the question and then continued with a pleased expression.

The wages were poor. Senali accepted two days’ work.

Senali’s first morning at the wharf began with the wrong basket.

Tsion had gone with him because she needed to pass that way on her business. She meant to leave him at the upper yard. Instead she paused to watch him approach Bakiri, introduce himself again though the man clearly remembered him, and ask where he was needed.

Bakiri pointed to a stack beside the lower shelter. Senali brought the nearest basket. It held packing cloth. Bakiri had wanted the carrying pads beneath it. He corrected him without anger and turned to another worker.

Senali put down the basket too quickly. A folded cloth slid out. He picked it up, found the pads, and brought them. Tsion moved behind a post before he could see her watching.

The next error was one of vocabulary. A hauler asked him to take the return through. Senali looked toward the path. The hauler meant the loose end of the rope. He repeated the instruction louder, which did not help. A young hauler called Nasor leaned past him and put the rope in Senali’s hand.

“This.”

Senali flushed. He fed it through, copied what the other men did, and got the next part right.

Tsion had once learned coastal work words by being laughed at. She remembered the heat of it long after the words became ordinary. She wanted to tell him that no one was judging his whole worth by the rope. Then a man laughed at something near the frame, and Senali looked down as though he believed precisely that.

She left before he caught her.

At midday she returned with food she had bought for herself and enough extra to make the intention obvious. He was sitting on an upturned basket with his sleeves rolled. A red mark crossed one shoulder. He looked pleased to see her and then wary of being pleased.

“How is it?” she asked.

“He doesn’t teach. He points.”

“Does anyone else understand?”

“They’ve been here.”

She passed him the food. He ate quickly, then noticed and slowed.

“I asked about the frame. He told me to move sacks.”

“He told you that yesterday.”

“I know.”

His disappointment was too raw to bear much correction. Tsion watched a line of ants trying to carry a crumb larger than all of them together.

“Do you want to leave?”

“No.”

“Then what do you need?”

He looked toward the frame. “Someone to say the words before I am already doing the wrong thing.”

That afternoon Tsion asked Bakiri whether there was a person Senali could work beside while he learned the calls. She intended the request to sound practical. It came out too much like an aunt arranging protection.

Bakiri looked at her, then past her. Senali stood by the stacked pads, close enough to have heard.

“He can ask me,” the master said.

Tsion stepped aside. Senali approached with a face she could not read.

“The calls,” he said. “I know the work at home. I don’t know what you call parts of it here.”

Bakiri picked up a rope and named its parts. Senali repeated them. The lesson took less than a minute. Then the master put him beside Nasor and told the younger hauler to make sure the instructions reached him before the load did.

Senali did not speak to Tsion when he left the yard that evening. She walked beside him for a while before saying she had made the request badly.

“Yes.”

“I thought—”

“I know what you thought.”

They went past a shop where a woman was beating dust from a mat. The cloud forced them apart. On the other side Tsion found Senali waiting for her.

“Thank you for the food,” he said.

She nodded. They walked home together, leaving the rest of it alone.

The next morning Tsion learned that her buyer had already found another mare.

She heard this from the buyer’s steward at a gate behind which she could see a well-kept yard and a girl riding a chestnut in slow circles. The girl sat easily. The chestnut looked sound. Tsion could find nothing to dislike except the circumstance.

The steward regretted the delay. He regretted that she had come so far. He did not regret purchasing another animal when the first had failed to arrive.

Tsion agreed with him so pleasantly that he offered her water.

She drank it, thanked him, and walked back to Mirem’s house with a headache.

Senali was there, washing his shirt in a basin. Salt marked the shoulders. His second day at the wharf had involved moving sacks three times because the space assigned to them belonged to someone whose ship had unexpectedly arrived.

“Did he buy her?”

“No.”

He looked down at the shirt. “I’m sorry.”

“Don’t be. It’s a handsome gate. I enjoyed standing outside it.”

He laughed once, then looked at her face and stopped.

She sat beside the basin. He offered to wash her scarf while he was at it. She gave it to him.

They stayed.

The delay acquired a shape. In the mornings Senali went to the wharf if Bakiri had work. Tsion walked the mare on firm ground, watched her turn, and kept returning with less to worry about. Mirem’s neighbour charged for fodder and asked Tsion to advise on a donkey that bit whenever someone tightened its girth. The girth had been rubbing over an old sore. Tsion adjusted it, showed the man how to pad it, and refused his attempt to pay with a basket of fish. She had nowhere to keep fish. He brought the basket anyway, and Mirem cooked it for everyone.

The fish arrived in a covered basket while Mirem was arguing with a supplier. He and the donkey’s owner immediately began discussing the price it should have fetched. Mirem let them reach agreement before telling them that neither would be eating any if they remained in the way.

She took the basket through to the cooking place. Tsion followed, intending to explain that she had refused it. Mirem handed her a knife.

“Then help.”

“I didn’t accept the fish.”

“You’ve accepted the knife.”

Tsion sat. Mirem showed her where to begin. By the third fish she had scales on her sleeves, beneath one nail and inexplicably above her eyebrow. The granddaughter came to inspect, announced that Tsion had missed a bit, and brought her a smaller blade. Her mother sent her away before the advice became intolerable.

The supplier stayed for supper. So did the donkey’s owner, having remembered an errand nearby at precisely the hour when the cooking smelled best. Senali returned with Nasor and stopped in the doorway, uncertain whether the young man could be added to a household he had not yet paid in full.

Mirem counted the places.

“Fetch the bench. Both of you. If it falls apart, neither of you sits.”

They brought it from the store. Nasor tested it with an elaborate caution which made the child insist on sitting between them. Tsion was given the end nearest the water because Mirem had seen her taste the sauce and reach for a cup.

The food came in dishes that moved down the bench and sometimes failed to move back. There was a dispute over who had been served the head. The supplier claimed it was a mark of distinction. The donkey’s owner offered him the other one. Mirem asked whether they had come to eat or to make the fish wish it had remained in the sea.

Senali was quiet at first. He watched which dish Nasor chose and followed him. Then Mirem’s daughter put something on his plate herself and told him how she preferred it. He tried it, looked surprised and took more. Tsion saw him reach for the water without embarrassment after the next mouthful.

The talk moved to a messenger who had ridden through the lower market the previous week shouting for room where there had been none. The donkey’s owner claimed to have stopped him with a single word. His neighbour supplied the word. It was a woman’s name. The rider had mistaken him for someone else and dismounted to ask for directions.

“He stopped,” the man insisted.

“He did,” Mirem said. “A victory for your voice.”

They began offering other occasions on which he had mistaken an accident for an achievement. He defended himself with vigour and eventually accused the supplier of selling damp goods. Mirem put another piece of fish on his plate before the accusation could grow serious.

Nasor asked Tsion whether horses recognized people by their voices. She said they recognized the people who fed them with extraordinary clarity. Senali began to tell the story of the mare eating the contract. He used Tsion’s question about reading it aloud, but gave himself her reply. Tsion let him get to the laugh before correcting him.

“You weren’t listening closely enough,” he said.

“I was buying the horse.”

“And paying for the story.”

Mirem approved of this answer. Tsion pointed out that it came from a man whose first wages had barely covered his sandals. Senali held up one foot. Nasor examined the worn sole and said the merchant had cheated him.

By the time the dishes were cleared, the supplier had promised to send a sound basket to replace the one whose bottom had given way that morning. Mirem made him say which morning. He looked aggrieved. The child fell asleep against Senali, who continued talking with his arm held at an uncomfortable angle rather than wake her.

Tsion carried the dishes inside. Mirem stood by the washing bowl rubbing a stiff place in her wrist. Tsion took the heavy pot before she could ask.

“Does it always hurt?”

“When there are more people than spoons.”

Tsion washed the pot. Behind them, Nasor was trying to lift the child without waking her. She woke anyway and protested that she had not been asleep. Senali supported her feet while Mirem’s daughter took her through to bed.

The household grew quiet by degrees. The men at the gate prolonged their leave-taking with another argument about the fish. Mirem leaned against the wall, listening until she could bear it no longer.

“Go home,” she called. “Both of you know the way.”

Tsion laughed so hard that she had to put down the pot.

In the afternoons Tsion visited possible buyers. One wanted a mare that would carry a heavy man up steep tracks and still look delicate when he arrived. Another believed that a horse delayed by lameness should cost less than its feed. A third asked whether she could guarantee the animal would bear a spotted foal. Tsion described the services she could provide and those requiring assistance from a deity.

On the fourth day Mirem took Tsion to the store she shared with two other households. The lock had begun sticking, and the man who usually repaired it was away. Tsion protested that she knew more about a horse’s teeth than a lock’s. Mirem said she had seen her open a jammed stable door with a spoon and considered the distinction academic.

The store stood behind a row of shops. Its lower courses were cool to the touch. Inside were sacks, jars, wrapped bundles, and the concentrated smell of goods waiting to be wanted elsewhere. Mirem opened the shutters while Tsion examined the lock.

It was not the lock. The door had swollen against its frame. The key worked if she pulled the lower corner toward her, which Mirem had been trying to do from the wrong side.

“Humidity,” Tsion said with the satisfaction of having found a problem that belonged to the place rather than to herself.

“Yes,” Mirem said. “We have heard of it.”

They planed the catching edge with a borrowed tool. Afterward Tsion helped move jars from a damp patch. One jar belonged to a man who had died aboard a ship the previous season. His brother had written twice about collecting it and had not come. Mirem knew the contents would keep, so she left it where it could be found.

“What is in it?” Tsion asked.

“Resin. He thought it would sell better here.”

“Does it?”

“Not while it’s in the jar.”

Mirem had no interest in making the story sadder than it was. She asked Tsion to lift the next one. Outside, a porter argued with a customer about whether his price included carrying the load through a second doorway. The customer said the doorways were part of the same house. The porter said his legs experienced them separately.

Tsion began to laugh. Mirem listened, identified the porter by his voice, and called that he still owed her for a broken bowl. He replied that the bowl had been weak before he met it.

They ate in the shop’s shade. A woman selling cooked food brought them a dish and remained to tell Mirem about a marriage proposal made from a ship whose owner had already promised passage to someone else’s daughter. The details grew tangled. Tsion asked which person was actually getting married.

“That,” the woman said, “is why everyone is interested.”

The conversation ranged through three households and two ports. Tsion understood only half the relationships and enjoyed herself more because nobody paused to explain the whole town for her benefit. When she asked about one unfamiliar practice, Mirem told her it belonged to that particular family and that the neighbours found it exhausting too.

On their return they passed a shaded place where sailors were waiting outside a small house. One carried a repaired lamp. Another held a bowl of water with leaves floating in it. Mirem lowered her voice while they went by, then resumed her story on the next street. Tsion did not ask for an explanation. The men were occupied with someone inside who might recover or might not, and the street had made room for them without turning their waiting into an attraction.

At the house, Senali was asleep on his mat. His shirt lay beside him. The skin across his shoulder blades had rubbed raw under the carrying pad.

Tsion stood in the doorway. She had seen him move sacks that morning with the determined speed of someone hoping not to be noticed as new. He had refused the older hauler’s advice to change sides more often. Now he slept on his face, one arm hanging over the edge of the mat.

Mirem set down the food they had brought back.

“Let him sleep.”

“He’ll miss the evening work.”

“There isn’t any.”

“He said there might be.”

“He wanted there to be.”

Tsion left a clean piece of cloth beside his shirt and went to the mare.

The next morning he asked her how to stop the pad rubbing. She showed him without mentioning that she had already seen the injury. He tried the new arrangement, walked around the yard, and adjusted it himself.

“I thought if I carried more, Bakiri would notice.”

“He noticed.”

“Not in the way I wanted.”

Tsion tightened a strap on the mare’s temporary saddle. “He told you to change sides.”

Senali looked embarrassed. “Yes.”

“Then he was speaking to you.”

The young man took the pad and went out. Tsion was pleased with the exchange until she realized she had managed, again, to say the last useful thing. She spent the morning wanting to find him and add something less instructive. By the time he returned, he had spent the morning with Nasor and no longer needed her to repair the conversation.

Nasor, the young hauler Bakiri had paired him with, had a narrow face, an astonishing capacity for food, and two shirts, one of which he called his visiting shirt because he never worked in it. He showed Senali which food sellers would allow credit and which would remember the debt in front of every person passing. He also taught him a board game played with counters in shallow hollows.

Tsion claimed to know the game. She knew a related game, played uphill with different rules. By the time the dispute was settled, Nasor had won twice.

They played in the court after supper. Mirem sorted goods beside them. Her granddaughter appointed herself keeper of the captured counters and had to be removed when it became clear that she was supplementing her uncle’s side.

“He needs them,” the child said.

“He needs to learn,” said Mirem.

“He’s old.”

“Then he has had longer.”

Senali laughed until he could not make his move. Tsion looked at him across the board. He had begun to tan differently, the lower half of his face darkening beneath the line of his headcloth. His hands were blistered. He still went quiet when someone spoke of a ship coming in, but he had learned three people’s names that had nothing to do with his promised employment.

On a day without work, Nasor took them to see the boatbuilders.

Tsion had expected Senali to spend an hour examining a pulley and another explaining why it interested him. He did both, but she found enough to occupy herself. A hull lay tipped above the water, its ribs exposed, men working along its curved side. The smallest apprentice carried tools and insults between them. He returned the insults better than the tools.

Nasor knew one of the builders and obtained permission for Senali to stand close. The builder showed him a joint, then asked him to find the reason for its shape. Senali offered two answers. Both were wrong. The man told him to look at the next joint before trying again.

Tsion wandered to the shade with Nasor. A woman there was sorting lengths of cord. She knew horses only as expensive animals that ate money and occasionally kicked it back out of someone. Tsion defended them with a warmth she rarely felt while paying for fodder.

“Boats eat money too,” she said.

“Yes, but they wait outside.”

Nasor bought them a cool drink from a passing seller. He gave Tsion the fuller cup, and she suspected him of trying to make a favourable impression on the person who might pay for supper. She approved of his method enough to pay later.

Senali remained at the hull until the workers stopped. When he returned, his hands were black and his face had the absorbed expression Tsion remembered from the wheel-house before Wosen arrived. He had discovered why the joint was cut as it was and wanted to tell someone.

Nasor listened for a few sentences, then asked whether the builder had mentioned wages. Senali said they were not discussing employment. Nasor looked at him with affectionate disbelief.

They walked back along the water. The tide had left a broad band of wet ground where children searched for things to sell, eat, or show one another. Senali removed his sandals and went down to it. Tsion remained on the path until Nasor asked whether highland traders feared wet feet.

She took off her boots and followed. The mud was warm at the surface and cool beneath. A small creature vanished into a hole under her toes. She made a sound that both young men would remember longer than anything she had taught them about horses.

For an hour they did nothing useful. Nasor showed them how to look for movement in the shallow pools. Senali found a shell and declared it perfect, then found a better one and gave the first to Tsion. She put it in her pocket, where it promptly cut the lining.

A group of children challenged them to cross a run of slippery stones without touching the water. Nasor made it halfway. Senali went farther and fell more impressively. Tsion declined until the children began discussing whether her age explained her caution. She crossed two stones, decided the children were correct, and stepped down deliberately before they could enjoy a fall.

By the time they returned, Mirem wanted to know why three grown people had brought half the shore into her court. Nasor explained that Tsion had required an introduction to the sea.

“I know the sea,” she said.

“From a respectful distance,” Senali added.

Mirem sent them to wash and laughed all the way to the cooking room.

That evening Senali put the perfect shell beside his model drum. He did not send it home. Tsion noticed and was glad he had kept something merely because he liked it.

After the others went to bed, he and Tsion played one more round. The court smelled of cooling rice and damp rope. Somewhere beyond the wall, sailors sang a song whose answering line came in several languages. Senali tried to count the voices.

“Do you miss home?” Tsion asked.

He moved a counter.

“The bread.”

“Only the bread?”

“The way my aunt knows when I’m hungry before I do.”

“Mirem knows.”

“Mirem asks whether I’ve paid.”

Tsion grinned. “A useful variation.”

He looked at the board for a long time.

“Your turn,” she said.

“I know.”

He took three of her counters with a move Nasor had shown him. Tsion demanded to see it again. He showed her slowly, enjoying her indignation.

4.6 The letter inland

The letter came in a packet brought down with dried fruit. It had been folded around a smaller sheet of drawings, and the outer edge smelled faintly of the fruit seller’s oil.

Senali read it at breakfast. His expression changed before he reached the end.

Tsion waited until he set it down.

“Bad news?”

“The wheel stopped again.”

“What failed?”

“He doesn’t say.”

“Dawet?”

“My uncle.”

He pushed the letter toward her. Tsion read slowly. Wosen’s hand was cramped, as though he disliked giving each word enough space to stand independently. He described the interruption, the neighbours waiting, the cost of hiring a man from the next valley, and the concern that Senali’s departure had been encouraged by someone who would not remain to see its consequences.

Tsion read that sentence twice.

Yalem had added a line at the bottom asking whether he had found a place to sleep and whether the food agreed with him.

The small sheet was Dawet’s. It showed the collar, the axle, and a mark beside the retaining wedge. There were three questions. One had been crossed out and written again more plainly.

Tsion held it up. “This tells you what failed.”

“He says the wedge worked loose.”

“Can you answer?”

“Yes.”

“Then answer him.”

Senali took the sheet back. “And the rest?”

She was already composing a reply to Wosen. She would begin with his own account of the third cup, remind him that an adult had requested an escort, state the precise limits of her undertaking, and observe that the wheel had required attention long before she bought the mare. It would be a splendid letter. She could hear herself reading it aloud.

Senali folded Wosen’s page sharply.

“Don’t,” she said.

“I wasn’t going to burn it.”

“What were you going to do?”

“Make it smaller.”

He folded it again.

Mirem entered carrying a basket of damaged cloves. She asked Senali whether he could spend the afternoon helping turn the drying mats. He said Bakiri might have work. Mirem asked whether he had gone to find out.

“Not yet.”

“Go now. If he has none, I have plenty.”

After he left, she sat down to sort the cloves.

Tsion said, “His uncle thinks I’ve stolen him.”

“Have you?”

“No.”

“Then why are you holding his letter?”

Tsion put it on the table.

That afternoon Senali came back early. Nasor had been offered a place on a repair crew. It was the kind of work Senali had hoped to do: fitting a lifting frame, checking its pins, learning from men who made something rather than moving what belonged to others.

“Nasor knows the foreman,” he said.

“Yes,” said Mirem. “He has worked here for three years.”

Senali looked ashamed of himself. Mirem did not comfort him. She gave him a mat to turn.

Tsion helped. The cloves had to be spread thinly enough to dry and brought in before the evening damp. They worked across the court on their knees. Mirem’s granddaughter sorted out sticks, stones, and anything else she deemed unsuitable, including several perfectly good cloves whose shapes offended her. Senali showed her how to distinguish a split one from a twig. She immediately began correcting Tsion.

By evening the work had taken the worst edge off his disappointment. When Nasor arrived in his visiting shirt, Senali congratulated him. The first attempt sounded false. He tried again when Nasor described the frame, and soon they were drawing its arrangement in the dust.

Nasor stayed after the drawing was finished. He had a question about Senali’s little lifting drum, though he pretended it was something the foreman had asked him to find out.

Senali brought it. They sat at the edge of the court, where the last dry patch remained, and tried to arrange the model as the larger frame would stand. Nasor put a stone beneath one side. The model tipped.

“That won’t work,” Senali said.

“It stands like that.”

“Then you’ve drawn it wrong.”

Nasor looked at him. Senali’s face changed before Tsion could intervene.

“Show me again.”

The young hauler fetched a straight piece of wood. He put it across the drawing, shifted the stone and showed where the frame joined the wall behind it. Senali took the model apart. A small pin fell into the dust. Both men stopped and began looking for it.

Tsion found it under her heel. She handed it over without offering an opinion.

They worked until Mirem wanted the place swept. Nasor understood how the full-sized frame was used; Senali understood what his own device would do under a pull. Neither could quite explain the awkward part to the other. At one point Senali reached across and moved Nasor’s hand. Nasor moved it back. Then he stood, found a length of cord and asked Senali to pull while he held the other end at shoulder height.

The cord turned in Senali’s grip. He looked up.

“Oh.”

“Yes,” Nasor said. “That.”

They tried again, slowly. Tsion moved her feet out of the way. The model had begun to resemble something Senali had not brought from home.

When Nasor left, Senali walked with him to the corner. He returned carrying the model and sat down with Dawet’s letter. Tsion watched him draw the wedge twice, once from above and once from the side. He crossed out an instruction, tried it with the loose pin, and wrote a different one.

“Does Dawet have cord like yours?” she asked.

“He has better.”

“Does he know which bit you mean?”

Senali frowned at the page. Then he added a small mark beside the part.

He worked on it while she went to see the mare.

The animal walked without shortening her stride now. Tsion turned her both ways, then let a stable girl ride her at a walk around the yard. There was no heat afterward. She could sell her honestly, though she would disclose the delay and accept what it did to the price.

The buyer she settled on was a woman named Rukiya who supplied mounts for messengers between the inland road and the port. She examined the mare without asking foolish questions. She also offered less than Tsion wanted.

Rukiya made the first offer while the mare was walking away from them.

Tsion let the number pass. She asked the rider to turn toward the gate, where the ground rose slightly. The mare climbed without shortening. Coming down she shook her head at a fly.

“You can see how she carries herself,” Tsion said.

“I can see her today.”

“Which is the day you’re buying her.”

“And I intend to own her tomorrow.”

Rukiya called the rider in. She put a hand beneath the girth, checked the fastening and asked him to take the saddle off. Tsion liked her less as the examination continued, not because it was careless but because it gave her so little to dispute.

The buyer asked exactly where the soreness had begun. Tsion described the path, the first shortened step, the rest and the slower descent. She stopped herself from improving the account by making the delay sound wholly voluntary. Rukiya listened without looking at her.

“How many days since you last felt heat?”

Tsion told her.

“Ridden?”

“Only at a walk since she came sound.”

“Then that is what I’m buying.”

She offered the same number again. Tsion named the price she had expected before the journey. Rukiya shook her head and handed the reins back to the rider.

For a moment Tsion thought the woman was ending the discussion. She felt a sharp urge to recover it by lowering her figure at once. Instead she watched the mare reach toward a plant over the yard wall. The rider moved her away without jerking her mouth.

“Who will ride her?” Tsion asked.

“Two messengers, chiefly. One young. One who will tell you the other is too young.”

“How heavy?”

Rukiya described them. Neither was too heavy. The inland stages were shorter than Tsion had feared; the woman already kept animals at the farther post. There would be rest between journeys, provided the mare did the work expected of her.

Tsion asked for another sum. Rukiya refused as politely as before.

They sat in the shade. A worker brought water and remained to report a broken stone near the rear gate. Rukiya asked him to mark it so no one led a horse over it in the dark. When he had gone, she told Tsion that a sound animal was worth waiting for but a trader’s expenses did not make one sound.

“I haven’t offered you my expenses.”

“You look as though you intend to.”

Tsion looked down at her dusty boots. One had been repaired badly on the road. She had caught the leather herself on a corner of the shelter bench and blamed the repair for an hour before remembering it.

“I’ve had an expensive month,” she admitted.

“I’ve had three.”

They considered each other. Then Rukiya made a different proposal. She would take the mare after another six days’ sound work. Tsion would remain responsible for fodder until then. If the soreness returned, they would begin again.

Rukiya asked why she had brought the mare so far on foot.

“She was sore.”

“Others would have ridden her.”

“Others may sell you their next horse.”

Rukiya smiled and made a small addition to the offer. Tsion accepted before the woman thought better of it.

On the way back she bought a sheet for her reply to Wosen. She sat in Mirem’s court with it until the light failed. The splendid letter grew shorter each time she imagined Senali reading it.

He came to sit beside her. His own letter was finished. He had answered Dawet’s questions, added drawings of the wedge and its retaining tie, and asked him to write back about the second collar. He had not answered his uncle’s accusation.

“I don’t know what to say to him,” he admitted.

“Neither do I.”

“You always know what to say.”

Tsion laughed. “I always have something to say. You’ve travelled with me long enough to notice the difference.”

He leaned back against the wall. For a while they listened to the house settling into its evening tasks.

“Will you go when you sell her?” he asked.

“Yes. I need to get uphill before the roads worsen.”

He nodded. She had expected relief, perhaps even impatience. His silence made her wish she had another journey to offer.

4.7 Water over the steps

The late ship arrived on a tide that made everyone hurry.

By then the wharf had been waiting so long that its ordinary preparations had become a collection of things people intended to do when there was a ship. Spare ropes were elsewhere. A storage room held someone else’s cargo. Two experienced haulers had taken inland work. Bakiri spent the morning finding men and the afternoon telling them to stop doing what the men beside them were doing.

Tsion brought the mare down because Rukiya’s messenger stables lay beyond the wharf, and the buyer wanted to see her pass among carts and noisy work. Senali walked with them. He had been given a place on the unloading crew for the day. He kept touching the new leather guard he wore around one palm.

“You’ll wear it out before you lift anything,” Tsion said.

He dropped his hand.

The ship stood offshore beyond the shoal, sending cargo in smaller boats. Men moved bundles up the wet steps, past the hauling frame, and into the waiting yard. The air smelled of salt, timber, fish, unfamiliar spices, and the sour warmth of too many people carrying too much at once.

Rukiya was waiting near the upper yard. She watched the mare pass a squealing cart axle without shying and approved. Tsion was less pleased with the angle of a wagon left across the animal lane.

“That ought to move.”

“It belongs to a man who thinks the tide will wait for his porter,” Rukiya said.

She sent someone to find him.

Senali had gone to the frame. Nasor stood on its working side with a rope over one shoulder. He greeted his friend with a lifted chin and immediately told him to get out of the place where a descending bundle would strike him. Senali moved.

For an hour the unloading went well enough. The frame lifted heavier goods over the worst of the steps. Hand carriers brought smaller parcels up between loads. Bakiri watched the feet of the frame and ordered more packing beneath one side when the tide began washing across the lower stones.

Tsion stayed with the mare in the upper yard, talking to Rukiya about the route north. They heard the first argument from below and gave it little attention. Arguments accompanied unloading as predictably as wet rope.

Then Senali shouted Bakiri’s name.

Tsion turned.

A large bundle hung from the lifting line. Its outer wrapping had shifted, revealing the corners of several packed cases. One end dragged lower than the other. The frame’s nearer foot had moved a finger’s width toward the step below it.

“Down,” Bakiri called.

The men at the drum began lowering. The bundle caught against the side of a boat that rose on the next wash. For an instant the load was no longer wholly theirs, then it dropped back onto the line with a shock that made the frame jump.

“Leave it!” someone shouted.

Another man pulled harder.

Senali pointed at the foot. Nasor saw it and dropped the rope from his shoulder. He ran to clear the carriers beneath the frame while Bakiri ordered the drum stopped.

The wagon across the upper lane chose that moment to begin moving. Its driver shouted at a donkey. The donkey backed into the mare.

Tsion caught the mare’s head before she could swing her hindquarters into a line of people. Rukiya took the donkey by its halter. Neither had room to turn the animals freely. The wagon pressed forward again.

“Stop the cart,” Tsion said.

The driver could not hear her over the wharf. She struck the sideboard with the flat of her hand and shouted in a voice she usually reserved for horses about to do something expensive.

He stopped.

Below, the frame tilted farther. Senali had his hands on a spare length of timber and was trying to get it beneath the moving foot. Bakiri caught his shoulder and dragged him back.

“No hands there.”

The timber fell. A wave came over the lower step and carried it sideways.

Tsion backed the mare one slow pace at a time. There was a wall behind them, a cart to one side, and the donkey rolling its eyes at the shouting. She needed the cart’s rear wheel moved without the front advancing into the crowd.

“Turn the shaft toward me,” she told the driver.

“I can’t.”

“Unhitch him.”

“That will take—”

Rukiya was already unfastening the near trace. The driver saw her and climbed down to do the other. Tsion held the mare while two women from the storage yard pushed at the wagon’s rear. It moved enough to open a narrow space.

A case burst below them. Something pale scattered over the water. A man yelled that the cargo was sugar. Another yelled that the frame was going.

It went slowly at first, turning around the foot that still held. The lifting line drew taut at an angle none of the men at the drum could control. Bakiri cut the retaining lash on the caught bundle. The cases fell against the boat, broke free, and slid into the water.

Freed of the load, the frame struck the steps instead of the people beside it.

The noise stopped conversation across the yard.

Then everyone spoke at once.

Tsion got the mare through the space beside the cart. The animal trembled, and her hind foot slipped on a wet patch. Tsion did not pull her forward. She waited until the hoof found ground, touched her neck, and asked again.

They reached the dry lane beyond the yard. Rukiya followed with the donkey. Behind them the cart driver was shouting about the missing cargo as though volume might make him its owner.

Tsion tied the mare where she could not be struck by another vehicle and went back.

Senali was sitting on the upper step with his hand in his lap. There was blood between two fingers. Bakiri crouched beside a man whose ankle had been caught beneath a falling spar. Nasor was in the water with two boatmen, pushing loose cases clear of the hull before the next wash drove them against it.

Tsion knelt beside Senali.

“Show me.”

“It’s only a cut.”

“Then showing me will be quick.”

He opened his hand. The cut was long but not deep. She wrapped it with a clean strip from her scarf, pulled the knot tight, and made him lift his hand above his knee.

“I saw it move,” he said.

“Yes.”

“I thought I could brace it.”

“Bakiri saw where your fingers would be.”

Senali watched the men in the water. He was shaking enough to make the bandage tremble.

Tsion stayed beside him until he stopped apologizing for it.

They worked until dark. The ship still had cargo to land, but Bakiri refused to use the damaged frame. Hand loads only, on the safe steps, with the lower approach cleared. The cargo owner accused him of losing money. Bakiri named the man with the injured ankle and asked where he should send the cost of feeding his household.

Rukiya took the mare to her own stable, with Tsion’s permission. The sale would wait until the animal had rested after the fright. Tsion agreed, though the delay meant another expense she had hoped to avoid.

Mirem fed half the unloading crew that evening. She charged those who could pay and made a very visible record of the others, assuring them they would be reminded. Senali ate with his injured hand tucked against his chest. Nasor sat beside him, smelling of seawater and broken sugar.

“The timber was a good thought,” Nasor said.

“Bakiri says it would have crushed my hand.”

“It was a badly placed good thought.”

Senali began laughing. He could not seem to stop. Tsion looked toward Mirem, who shook her head slightly and passed him more food.

Later, in the quiet court, Tsion found him examining the little model drum. He turned it over, took out the pin, and laid the pieces separately on the table.

The next day Senali wanted to return to work before the dressing on his hand had dried.

Mirem stopped him at the door. She had already sent someone to ask what work was available. There would be no hauling for him until he could grip properly. Bakiri would let him help sort fittings if he could keep the injured hand clear.

Senali objected that he could decide what he could hold. Mirem pointed to the place where he had tied his sandal with his teeth and told him to make a better argument.

Tsion found him in the court afterward, using his sound hand to take apart the model drum. He had laid every piece in a line. The little hook looked smaller than she remembered.

“What are you changing?”

“The way it stops.”

“Will that stop it?”

“I don’t know yet.”

She sat beside him without touching the pieces. He tested a wedge, removed it, tested another. The drum held under a small pull and slipped under a harder one. He swore.

“Good,” Tsion said.

He looked at her.

“You’d rather know now.”

“I’d rather have done it right.”

She let him work. From the cooking room came the sounds of Mirem and her daughter disagreeing about a delivery. The granddaughter sat on the step, plucking the frayed end of a cord and watching Senali’s bad temper with interest.

After a while he said, “When Bakiri pulled me back, I thought he was stopping me because he didn’t trust me.”

Tsion kept her eyes on the small drum.

“I was angry. Even then.”

“You’d seen what needed doing.”

“I’d seen one thing.”

He put the wedge down.

Tsion asked whether he wanted to visit the injured hauler. Senali had been avoiding the suggestion since the previous evening. The man’s ankle had been caught beneath the spar. He had a household, children, and work he would not do for some time. Senali seemed to believe that walking into his room would require him to decide how much of the accident had been his fault.

“He may not want us,” he said.

“Then we’ll leave.”

They took food. The man received them in a room full of relatives who had all brought advice about the ankle. He looked exhausted by the advice and delighted by the food. His name was Abdari. Before either visitor could offer sympathy, he asked Tsion whether the mare had found a buyer.

She told him. He knew Rukiya and approved of the sale, then disputed the price so vigorously that his wife told him he could bargain over his own horse when he had one.

Senali sat near the doorway. Abdari asked how his hand was. Senali showed the dressing and said it was minor.

“Still yours, then.”

“Yes.”

“Keep it that way. I won’t carry your share as well when I get back.”

Senali smiled uncertainly. Later, when the relatives went to fetch more water, he began to ask about the falling frame. Abdari interrupted to say he had been looking at the boat and remembered very little of what happened above him.

“But you heard me?”

“I heard shouting. There was plenty.”

Senali looked disappointed before he could hide it. Tsion recognized what he had wanted: a clear account in which his warning belonged somewhere definite, doing good rather than merely preceding injury.

Abdari’s wife asked him to shift a basket out of the passage. He did it with his sound hand. She thanked him and asked whether he could return the following morning to carry water, since the younger relatives had work and the older ones found giving instructions less tiring.

He agreed.

On the way back, Tsion expected him to discuss the frame. Instead he asked where Mirem kept the large carrying jar. The next morning he fetched it before anyone reminded him.

Tsion went to the wharf alone. Bakiri was examining the broken support with the woman who had brought the new frames. Their conversation was heated and exact. She heard enough to learn that the packing under the foot had compressed and that the wave had not been an extraordinary one. Men who had worked the tide for years were angry with themselves as well as with the cargo owner who had pressed them to hurry.

No one was waiting for Tsion to pronounce on it. She watched until Bakiri noticed her, then asked whether he wanted the story Senali had told her about the first movement.

“Yes. Better from him.”

“He’ll come after carrying water.”

“Good.”

The answer pleased her. She had been preparing to defend his absence. She went back through the yard, where the dangerous wagon had finally been moved, and found she could see the gap it had blocked without imagining herself trapped there again.

At Mirem’s house, Senali had left the disassembled drum on a high shelf beyond the granddaughter’s reach. Beside it lay a drawing with several crossings-out. Tsion did not examine it. She put her market parcel on the table and went to help with the meal.

4.8 A cup before going

The sale went through four days later.

The mare had come through the wharf without renewed soreness. Rukiya rode her, dismounted, watched her walk, and paid the agreed lower price without attempting to profit from Tsion’s need to leave. Tsion counted the money twice. She had made very little on the journey. She had also spent three weeks telling herself that the next buyer would improve the account.

Rukiya asked whether she would be coming down again.

“If you pay more next time.”

“If you bring the horse on time.”

They parted on good terms.

Tsion used part of the money to buy a small, steady pack horse from a carrier returning by sea. Its coat was ugly and its feet excellent. She would take it inland and leave it with Wosen’s household, where it could haul grain while the wheel was being tended. It was not enough to make good every day of interruption, and she said so in her letter.

She wrote the letter herself, with Senali nearby to help with one place-name. She acknowledged that she had made light of his plans when Wosen had plainly been trying to hear something else. She should have asked the household to speak openly before accepting the escort. She had not promised his return and could not do so now. The horse was for the work her carelessness had helped leave unsettled, not payment for the young man’s choice.

Senali read the page when she offered it.

“He may be angry anyway.”

“He can be angry while using the horse.”

The young man gave her a look that was so like Yalem’s that she laughed.

“I know. It isn’t a cure.”

He wrote his own letter that afternoon. This time he answered his uncle. Tsion did not ask to read it. He enclosed part of his earnings and a new drawing for Dawet, then spent longer writing to his aunt than to anyone else. He asked Mirem how to describe the fish sauce she had served on the first night. Mirem said he should tell the truth: it had made him cry, and he was a better man afterward.

Before she bought the pack horse, Tsion went once more to see the mare at Rukiya’s yard.

The mare had already learned where the feed was kept. She greeted Tsion, then turned her head toward the store. Tsion pretended to be offended.

“Three weeks on foot, and that is what you remember.”

Rukiya’s stable girl laughed. She was called Bahira and had a quiet way with animals that Tsion trusted more than confidence. The mare stood while she lifted each foot. When Bahira found a small stone, she showed Tsion before removing it.

“Good eye.”

“She was favouring it after the yard.”

“Which yard?”

“The broken paving behind the messenger room. I’ve told them.”

Tsion walked over to inspect it and found Rukiya there with a man carrying replacement stones. The girl had been heard. Tsion felt herself relax.

She stayed to watch Bahira ride. The mare moved at an easy pace, responsive without being hurried. Rukiya had chosen a light saddle and a bridle that fitted. There was nothing for Tsion to correct, which left her briefly without a reason to remain.

Rukiya offered water.

“You’ll have a good journey uphill,” she said.

“If the road agrees.”

“If it doesn’t, you’ll tell everyone the story.”

Tsion smiled. “Only if I look sensible in it.”

“Then I hope I hear no story at all.”

At the gate Tsion looked back. The mare had lowered her head toward Bahira’s sleeve. Bahira pushed the nose aside with exactly the firmness required. Tsion left before the animal could make her feel she ought to be needed longer.

She took the long way through the market afterward. She bought needles for Yalem, a small bag of seasoning she thought Wosen would dislike until he had eaten enough of it, and a strip of leather for Dawet’s tool grip. Senali had asked her to take the drawing, not gifts, but the leather was from her. She had watched the man’s old grip turn in his hand at the wheel.

At Mirem’s house she found Senali teaching the granddaughter the game. He was allowing her to win until she accused him of it. Then he beat her properly, and she threw one of the counters at his knee. Mirem called from the next room that she would take the board away if either of them behaved like her sons.

Tsion sat beside them. The child looked at her purchases and asked which was hers.

“The privilege of asking politely.”

The child considered the possibilities, then asked so extravagantly politely that Senali choked with laughter. Tsion gave her a small wooden animal she had bought for precisely this encounter and claimed to have intended it for someone much better behaved.

That night, after the household quieted, Tsion unpacked and repacked her own things. The room seemed larger with her bundle closed. She had spent weeks wishing the room were cheaper, drier, closer to the mare, farther from the early cooking. Now she knew which floorboard would complain and where the first light entered. She could find her boots in the dark.

Senali knocked at the open door.

“Do you need help?”

“No.”

He stood there a moment longer.

“Come in anyway,” she said.

He sat on the floor while she checked the purse. They talked about the inland horse, which had an unpromising face and an excellent walk. He asked whether his uncle would accept it. She said Wosen would object at length before accepting anything that useful.

“He’ll ask what it cost.”

“I’ll tell him.”

“The true price?”

“He’ll know if I lie. That’s why we enjoy bargaining.”

Senali leaned his head against the wall. “Tell my aunt I like Mirem.”

“Write it.”

“I did. Tell her too.”

Tsion nodded.

“And the food. I like the food now.”

“I’ll tell her that you cried only once.”

“Twice.”

“Your aunt needn’t know everything.”

He smiled and stayed until she had finished packing. When he left, she did not immediately put out the lamp. She sat on the mat with the closed bundle beside her and listened to him cross the court.

Bakiri’s new frames had finally been landed. Their maker was a quiet woman from another port who took one look at the damaged wharf and asked for the tide marks before discussing installation. Senali watched her work for two days before she allowed him to hold a tool.

His apprenticeship began with cleaning fittings and carrying them to the correct places. The wages were less than he had imagined and more certain than the day work. He would learn to cut and fit parts when she trusted him not to put his hands beneath a shifting load.

“She keeps saying that,” he complained to Tsion.

“You keep having hands.”

He flexed the bandaged one.

Nasor had gone aboard a coastal vessel for six weeks’ work. He left his visiting shirt with Mirem and instructions that Senali was not to borrow it. Senali put a fish bone in the pocket and told the granddaughter to make sure it remained there. She demanded a counter from his game set as payment. He refused on principle, then gave her two when Mirem was not looking.

On Tsion’s last morning, the household prepared coffee.

Mirem’s daughter roasted the beans in the court while the granddaughter swept carefully around everyone’s feet. There was no hurry. The pack horse stood in the neighbour’s yard, already loaded, but the inland caravan would not leave until the heat had begun to rise. Tsion sat where she had first seen Mirem sorting the damp cloves. The table had been scrubbed. A darker patch remained where the wet baskets had stood.

They talked about Nasor’s shirt, the cargo owner’s account of the wrecked frame, and the astounding number of people who now claimed to have shouted a warning before Senali did. Mirem said the wharf would soon be unable to contain all the heroes of that afternoon.

Senali said very little. He kept glancing toward the door as though her departure might occur while he was not looking.

At the second cup, Mirem thanked Tsion for bringing him safely.

“He did some of it,” Tsion said.

“He can thank himself on his own time.”

Senali smiled at his cup.

Tsion accepted the welcome. When the pot was set aside, they remained talking until a messenger from the caravan came to say the others were ready.

At the gate, Senali handed her a packet for Yalem and a small wooden hook.

“From the model?”

“I’ve made a better one.”

“This one held a spoon.”

“It can still hold a spoon.”

She turned it in her hand. The curve was awkward, recognizably his first attempt. She put it in the inner pocket where she kept small things that would otherwise be lost.

He embraced her with none of the hesitation he had shown his uncle. She was surprised by how tightly she held him back.

“Write,” she said.

“You too.”

“I’ll send you the price of every horse I sell.”

“Then I’ll never know whether you’re well.”

She laughed against his shoulder and let him go.

From the street she could see Mirem calling him back to help move a basket. He answered, took the heavy end, and nearly caught his bandaged hand beneath the handle. He changed his grip before anyone corrected him.

Tsion led the pack horse toward the inland road. At the first turn she reached into her pocket to make sure the hook was there. It caught in the lining. She freed it carefully and walked on.

The Honey Owed

5.1 The song begun badly

Mbala began on the wrong note and remained there through the first verse.

Nkiru waited beneath the ladder with the gathering pot between her feet. She had tied her face covering carefully, tucked her sleeves, checked the rope, and risen before daylight to be ready for a man who now seemed determined to insult every bee in the orchard.

At the answering line she sang his name instead of the words.

He looked down.

“You’ll frighten them.”

“They’ve heard you.”

Above him, the hive hummed in the hollow of the tree. He waited, one hand against the bark, until the hum lowered. The pause belonged to the work. Nkiru kept her own voice low when she began the next verse, and this time Mbala found the note.

The smoke rose thinly. They did not want a choking cloud. They wanted the bees to move far enough from the working edge for him to take what the orchard had yielded without tearing the whole comb free. He loosened the first piece, inspected it, and lowered it in the basket. Nkiru steadied the rope and tipped the comb into the pot.

Honey slid over her wrist. She licked it before Kelemi saw.

Her aunt saw.

“The first portion,” Kelemi said.

“Was on my arm.”

“Your arm can wait.”

A woman at the next tree laughed. Kelemi went to inspect her pot, and the woman became busy with her covering.

They worked through the cool hours. The orchard lay between their gardens and the deeper green where the path bent out of sight. Flowering trees had once stood along that path on both sides. Now there were planted beds, a retaining wall, and a drying platform where a household spread its profitable crop. Everyone knew which trees had gone. Nkiru had helped carry one of the trunks to the carpenter. She remembered because it had rained and the bark had stained her best work cloth.

The bees remembered the blossoms with less accommodation.
