At the third tree Mbala called for a different knife. Nkiru fetched it. At the fourth he refused to climb until the ladder’s upper lashing was replaced. Someone accused him of delaying because breakfast smelled good. He invited the accuser to climb first. They changed the lashing.

At the oldest tree they changed places. Nkiru climbed, and Mbala held the rope below.

The bark was cool where the sun had not reached it. She tested each foothold before trusting it, feeling through the soles of her feet for loose edges. Above her, a branch carried the old marks of previous harvests: healed cuts, a loop left from a former support, the dark trace where smoke had been held too close one season. She had been the one holding it. Kelemi had made her return afterward to watch the bees until she understood what her hurry had disturbed.

Today she worked more slowly. The face covering narrowed the world. She could hear her own breathing and the hum beyond it. Mbala sang below, well enough now that she could use the rhythm without wanting to correct him.

She took the outer comb first. It came free easily. The second had grown around a rough knot, and she had to change the angle of her knife. A bee walked across the back of her hand. She waited until it moved before pressing again.

From below, Kelemi called that the cloud was changing.

Nkiru looked through the leaves. A patch of brightness had gone dull. The bees would feel the coming weather before anyone at the meal decided whether to carry the dishes under cover. She checked the remaining comb and chose to leave it.

Mbala pulled the empty basket up for another load.

“Nothing more,” she called.

“There is room.”

“Leave it.”

He lowered the basket. When she came down, he asked why. She showed him the shape with her hands and pointed to the hive. He listened, then nodded. They disagreed about enough ordinary things that Nkiru appreciated the ease with which he accepted her judgment above a tree.

At the basin she found a bee caught inside the fold at her wrist. It had not stung. She opened the cloth carefully and let it crawl onto a leaf. Mbala’s little son watched with intense disappointment when there was no drama.

“Will it know you?” he asked.

“It knows I am in the way.”

“Father says they know our names.”

“Your father tells them his name often enough.”

The boy ran to repeat this. Mbala bowed toward the tree as though introducing himself to an honoured guest. Several workers laughed. Kelemi told them to wash before they put honey on every surface in the village.

Nkiru carried the full pots into shade. They were warm against her legs. She remembered doing this with her mother, being allowed to hold one handle while her mother bore nearly all the weight. She had believed herself indispensable. Later, when she became strong enough to carry a pot alone, she had been annoyed that nobody made an occasion of it.

She set down the last one and called the little boy to help move the cover over it. He took the far corner gravely. She let him pull it into place without correcting the uneven edge.

By midmorning the pots stood beneath shade, covered against insects and dust. The workers washed at the basin. Smoke clung to their hair and clothes. A little boy had been given the important task of keeping the chickens from the food and had interpreted it as permission to chase them throughout the orchard.

Kelemi called him back. She made him sit beside the covered dishes and told him that if a chicken escaped his attention from that position, she would personally congratulate it.

They ate with their hands sticky despite washing. Mbala had a sting above one eye and was pretending it improved his appearance. His sister said it was the first improvement she had noticed in years. He tried to retaliate and was stopped by Nkiru, who knew exactly which man the sister had been visiting and preferred not to have the whole orchard hear it.

Kelemi did not sit until the first portions were set aside.

There should have been one full pot from their household. She placed two by the marked root.

Nkiru looked at them.

“That is ours for the lower houses.”

“It is ours for the grove.”

“Both?”

Kelemi adjusted the leaf over the second pot. “Both.”

The conversation around the food thinned. Mbala looked at the pots, then at Kelemi. His sister stopped scraping the last paste from a bowl.

Nkiru had known the yield would be poor. She had walked the dry edges of the orchard, counted the new beds where blossoms used to stand, and heard the bees working farther away. She had prepared herself to take less home. She had not prepared to see the household’s offering doubled without a word.

“When did you agree to that?”

“Not here.”

“Then where?”

Kelemi looked at her until Nkiru felt the old impulse to lower her eyes. She kept looking instead.

“The upper household’s portion is ours this season,” her aunt said. “I said I would bring it.”

“The upper household has people in it.”

“It does.”

“People who eat.”

“Yes.”

“Then why—”

“Because I said I would.”

No one laughed now. Nkiru could hear the little boy chewing. A bee settled on the edge of an empty bowl and walked around it.

Kelemi covered the pots and called for the carrying poles. The offering would not be taken into the inner grove until the appointed evening four days later, but it had to be separated now. Once the first portion was named, nobody could quietly treat it as household surplus.

Nkiru went to fetch the poles. She could feel people watching her back. Mbala caught up at the shed.

“You knew?” she asked.

“Not about two.”

“What did you know?”

“That the upper house couldn’t pay.”

“And everyone decided I would discover it when the pots were full?”

He took one end of a pole. “I didn’t decide anything.”

That was true enough to make her angrier. They carried the pots to Kelemi’s store, where the offering would wait. The store smelled of dry leaves, smoke, and the old sweetness of honey caught in cracks no washing could reach.

Kelemi tied the door cord herself.

Afterward she sat beneath the awning and took off her work cloth. Her hair was flattened on one side. She looked tired, and Nkiru resented that too. It left so little room to be angry without feeling cruel.

“We promised the lower houses,” Nkiru said.

“I know what we promised.”

“Then tell me what to take them.”

Kelemi rubbed her jaw.

“I’ll find something.”

Nkiru waited. Her aunt did not look up.

From the orchard came the sound of Mbala attempting the song again, alone this time, correcting the note he had missed. He got it right, then sang it wrong deliberately. Someone threw something at him. His startled shout reached the store, and Kelemi smiled before remembering that Nkiru was still there.

5.2 The pots put aside

The next morning, Nkiru began with Kelemi’s aunt, who had lost most of her teeth and none of her ability to complain about food.

Nkiru brought a small bowl instead of the pot they had promised. Her great-aunt examined it and asked whether they had begun harvesting from ants.

“The grove takes more this time.”

“The grove has no teeth either. It can wait.”

Nkiru smiled despite herself and sat to soften the woman’s meal. Honey made it easier to swallow and more likely that she would finish. Without it she pushed the bowl away halfway through and announced that she had lived long enough to know when someone was feeding her paste.

Today she ate slowly, conserving the sweetness. Nkiru pretended not to notice. She washed the bowl, swept the patch beside the sleeping mat, and listened to an account of a neighbour’s son’s marriage that grew less flattering with every detail.

The old woman asked her to stay until the afternoon shade reached the doorway.

Nkiru had other visits to make. She said so, then saw the disappointment pass across her great-aunt’s face before the woman could turn it into a complaint. She sat down again.

They sorted a basket of old ties. Some were useful, some had become too brittle, and some the old woman refused to discard because she remembered where they had come from. Nkiru could see no difference between two cracked pieces of fibre. Her great-aunt identified one as part of a carrying sling made by a woman dead for twenty years and the other as something nobody had ever bothered to make properly.

“That one can go.”

“Both will break.”

“I know which one I want to look at.”

Nkiru put the remembered piece back.

A neighbour came to borrow a small pot. She remained to hear the marriage story and disputed its least flattering detail. The old woman grew animated. Nkiru watched her forget the ache in her jaw while arguing about whether a groom’s mother had truly said something disgraceful in front of the whole gathering or only intended to say it later.

The visitor left with the pot and a warning not to chip it. Nkiru rose to go too.

“Does Kelemi still have the pain?” her great-aunt asked.

“Yes.”

“She always thinks pain can be outwaited. Her mother was the same.”

“Was mine?”

The old woman looked at her.

“Your mother complained immediately. It was one of her better qualities.”

Nkiru laughed. She knew so few accounts of her mother that did not arrive dressed for remembrance. This one pleased her enough to make her linger at the door.

“What else?”

“She disliked washing the big pot. Always found another task when it needed doing. Then she would tell you how much she’d finished while you were still at the pot.”

“I do that.”

“Yes.”

The old woman seemed delighted to have caught her.

Nkiru left smiling, then felt the weight of the two smaller vessels she carried and remembered why she had come. At the next house she stayed long enough to help repair a torn sleeping mat. By the time she reached Kelemi’s yard, her anger had become less satisfying.

Lenyo was leaving with an empty carrying basket.

He greeted her pleasantly. He was a prosperous man, careful with his clothes and generous in ways he preferred people to witness. His household owned the drying platform that occupied the old flowering strip. He had also paid for a new roof on the public shelter the year the wind took it.

“What did you bring?” Nkiru asked.

“Ask your aunt.”

He did not say it sharply. That made it worse.

Kelemi had put his pot beside their two. It was smaller and had a blue mark near the rim. Nkiru knew it. Lenyo used those pots for honey intended for exchange rather than local sharing.

“He can spare one,” she said.

“He has spared one.”

“After taking the ground that fed five.”

Kelemi pressed a thumb against her aching jaw. “Go and look at the ground before you count his life so easily.”

Nkiru did. She went in the afternoon when the workers were turning the drying crop. Lenyo’s wife, Orema, stood beneath a shade cloth with a baby asleep against her back. Two older children worked beside hired hands. The crop had to be turned before damp gathered beneath it; a careless heap would spoil from the centre outward.

Orema saw Nkiru watching and handed her a rake.

“If you’re here to be angry, you can do it from that side.”

Nkiru took the rake.

They worked until the platform lay in even rows. The baby woke, complained, and was passed to the oldest child. Orema checked a handful of the crop by breaking it between her fingers.

“The lower bed feeds my sister’s household,” she said. “The upper one pays two people’s wages. The platform keeps the whole harvest out of the rain.”

“The trees fed the bees.”

“I know.”

“Then why did you take them?”

Orema looked toward the wall. “Because the first bed did well. Because my brother needed work. Because the council said the passage was wide enough if we left the marked tree. Pick whichever answer makes you least tired.”

Nkiru drove the rake too hard and caught a board. Orema pointed at it until she eased the teeth free.

At the far end of the platform a young woman was speaking with Lenyo. She wore a loose cloth around one wrist, dark where it touched her skin. Nkiru recognized her as Tembi, a Waker who had visited the orchard after the last poor flowering. Kelemi had sent her away with food and no permission to work.

Tembi waited until Nkiru came down from the platform.

“I could bring the trees into flower.”

Nkiru leaned the rake against the wall. “Now?”

“Some. Not all.”

“For how long?”

“Long enough for this harvest’s lack.”

She spoke without flourish, which made the offer harder to dismiss. Nkiru thought of her great-aunt scraping the bowl to gather the last sweetness.

“What would you need?”

“A place to work. Blood from willing hands. Permission to wake what is sleeping beneath the roots.”

“And afterward?”

Tembi looked toward the surviving trees. “They would need tending.”

“They need tending now.”

“More.”

Orema came to stand beside them. “How much more?”

Tembi described a flowering drawn out of season: water carried daily, dead growth cut quickly, fevers watched among those who worked closest. The next ordinary flowering might be weaker. She could not say by how much. She had done the work on a smaller stand, and two trees had died after the third forced bloom.

Nkiru was grateful for her honesty and wished she had lied. A liar could have been sent away easily.

Lenyo asked whether one bloom would be safe.

“No working is safe because you do it once,” Tembi said. “I can tell you where I would begin.”

They walked together to the old path. Nkiru had not meant to join them, but she went. The air beneath the trees was cooler. A branch had grown low over the passage since her last visit, and she had to duck. Tembi placed her hand on a trunk and stood still.

The leaves trembled though the air did not move.

Nkiru felt a sudden longing to see blossoms there, thick and pale, the bees crowding them, the pots full. She could almost smell it.

Then the baby began crying on the platform, and Orema turned back toward the sound.

“I won’t decide without the others,” she said.

Tembi took her hand from the trunk. A little green sap had appeared beneath her palm.

5.3 Called to witness

The next morning’s gathering went badly before anyone mentioned the offering.

Mbala’s sister had brought a different covering for her face. The old one had rubbed the skin raw beneath her jaw. She asked Kelemi to examine the new stitching before she went up the ladder. Kelemi did, pulling each edge, then sent her to wet the lining. She had no patience for a person choosing discomfort merely because it was familiar.

Nkiru carried the smoking bowl. It was too full. She had packed it in a hurry while counting how much honey might still be taken from the lower trees. At the first hive it smothered itself, producing a thick bitter cloud. The bees lifted in a disturbed sheet.

“Down,” Kelemi said.

Nkiru lowered the bowl. Mbala’s sister stayed on the ladder with one arm across her face. For a few moments all the ordinary sounds of the orchard vanished beneath the hum. Nkiru heard a child inhale to cry and Kelemi tell him, very softly, to close his mouth.

They waited. One bee found the opening at Nkiru’s cuff. She felt it moving against her wrist and did not shake her hand. When the first sting came, her fingers clenched around the bowl.

Kelemi took it from her.

“Walk away.”

Nkiru walked to the basin. She wanted to run. She could hear the woman descending behind her, one careful rung at a time. At the basin Kelemi set down the smoking bowl, drew Nkiru’s sleeve back, and dealt with the sting. It hurt far more than such a small wound ought to have hurt.

“I made it wrong,” Nkiru said.

“Yes.”

She waited for the rest. Kelemi gave none. She emptied part of the fuel, loosened what remained, and passed the bowl to Mbala.

Nkiru sat beside the water while the others returned to work. The child came to stand near her. He had been told not to ask whether a person was hurt when he could see blood or swelling. He observed the rule by asking whether she would climb again.

“Later.”

He nodded and went to find something less worrying to watch.

By midday her wrist had swollen enough to make the covering tight. Kelemi sent her to carry empty pots rather than climb. Nkiru objected until Mbala’s sister held up her own reddened jaw and asked whether she would like a companion in stupid work.

They took the pots together. Away from the others, the sister finally said what Nkiru had been waiting to hear.

“You keep looking at the full ones before you finish the tree.”

“We need them.”

“Yes. We need you to look at the tree too.”

The words were ordinary, and their ordinariness hurt. Nkiru had wanted the danger to explain how much she cared. Instead she had made someone else stay still above a disturbed hive.

She went to Kelemi after the meal and asked to prepare the next smoke under her eye. Her aunt said she could do it herself. Nkiru insisted. Kelemi watched without correcting until the bowl was ready, then nodded once.

The afternoon gathering yielded less than they hoped. They left two combs untouched because the colony needed them. Nobody proposed taking them after Nkiru made the decision aloud. She was relieved and then ashamed of the relief, as though she had expected her neighbours to become greedy because she had become frightened.

They carried the pots home in the long light. Her wrist throbbed with every step. She stopped at the store to put away a lid and found someone already inside.

Ovani stood just beyond the doorway.

The door cord hung loose. He had not broken it; Kelemi had left it untied while fetching a vessel from the house. Ovani stood with the blue-marked pot open and a small bowl in his hand.

He looked at Nkiru and did not pretend to be doing something else.

“My mother hasn’t eaten.”

“That’s the offering.”

“I know.”

“Put it back.”

“I’ve mixed it already.”

She looked at the bowl. He had brought softened meal and added honey to it. The mixture clung to the spoon.

Ovani lived with a mother whose hands had curled until she could no longer hold a cup. His sister had married into a household farther north and sent food when she could. He did work for anyone who would give it, but the orchard had needed fewer carriers this season and the road crew had not paid him yet.

Nkiru knew all of that. She also knew the blue-marked pot had been named among the first portions. It could not be taken quietly because its owner was rich or because the bowl was small.

Footsteps sounded behind her.

The Leopard Society hunter wore his mask low over his face. Nkiru knew him without seeing it. Konda had a slight drag in his left foot from an injury he disliked discussing. He had come to witness the offering count, not to catch a theft. The timing was so unfortunate that for a moment she hated him simply for being there.

He stopped at the doorway.

“Who opened the pot?”

Ovani lifted the bowl.

Konda asked Nkiru what she had seen. His public voice was slower than the one he used when buying fruit from Mbala’s sister. Each question left room for an exact answer.

She told him. She did not say Ovani had intended to replace it because he had not said so. She did not call it a household portion because she had argued about its designation all morning and knew precisely what it was.

Kelemi returned while they were speaking. She looked at the open pot, then at Ovani’s bowl. Her mouth tightened.

“Let him take that to his mother.”

Konda did not move aside. “The portion must be answered.”

“I said let him take it.”

“You can carry the bowl.”

Kelemi took it from Ovani. Nkiru saw his hands remain in the shape of holding it after it was gone.

A child appeared behind Konda, saw the mask, and backed away. The hunter moved his staff out of the doorway so she could pass toward the house. She went quickly, watching him over her shoulder.

Nkiru knew what his mask allowed him to ask. She also knew the restrictions he carried while wearing it. He could not take a household’s food as a fee for passing over an offence. He could not name a person guilty without witnessing the act or hearing proper testimony to it. Those limits had protected people here. They did not make this encounter less frightening.

“Was the pot named before you took it?” he asked Ovani.

“Yes.”

“Did anyone give you leave?”

“No.”

“Was the store opened by you?”

“The door was open.”

Konda turned to Nkiru for that point. She said she had found it open and could not say who had untied the cord. She saw relief flicker across Ovani’s face when she did not add a suspicion to what she knew.

His shoulders had begun to sag. He was a narrow man, quick on his feet when carrying loads, but he looked old in the doorway. Nkiru remembered him teasing her about her first attempt to climb the orchard tree. He had tied the ladder himself afterward, pretending to inspect Mbala’s work so she would not know he was making it safer for her.

She had not thought about that in years.

Konda asked whether there was anything else he should hear before stating what would follow. Ovani shook his head.

“You can tell him about the road crew,” Nkiru said.

“They still owe me.”

Konda waited.

Ovani looked at the empty space where the bowl had been. “That doesn’t put the honey back.”

“No,” the hunter said. “It may tell the witnesses whom else to ask.”

Ovani named the foreman, the number of days, and the payment promised. Konda repeated the name until he had it correctly. Nkiru felt a little of her anger shift away from the man in the mask and hated how easily that could happen, merely because he had done one part of his work properly.

The hunter named the consequence if the offering remained short: Ovani would be held from the grove paths and brought before the local witnesses to answer for what he had taken. Until then someone would have to stand for his appearance, or he would go with Konda now.

His mother was alone in the house. Kelemi had taken the bowl but would not stay all night. Nkiru knew how long the woman waited when she needed turning.

“I’ll bring the honey,” she said.

Konda turned toward her.

“By the offering evening,” she added. “And I’ll bring him if the witnesses still require it.”

Ovani began to object. She spoke over him, angry now in several directions at once.

“You can go home.”

Konda asked her to repeat the promise without the anger. She resented that most of all, but she did it. He took out a short bark cord, named her, named Ovani, and wound it once around the store post where the existing offering marks were kept. Nkiru felt a tightening across the base of her thumb, brief enough to be doubted if she had not known the rite.

Two evenings remained.

Konda asked whether Nkiru understood what it meant to stand for Ovani’s appearance as well as the honey. She said she did. He made her say where she could be found. She gave her own house, then added the orchard, the lower households, and the platform where she had worked the previous afternoon.

“You needn’t name every place you’ve stood,” he said.

She heard the familiar impatience beneath the public voice and was unexpectedly close to tears. She could have borne solemnity more easily. She looked at the bark cord until the feeling passed.

Kelemi returned before the hunter departed. She had fed Ovani’s mother and left water within reach. She told him this without looking at Konda. Then she asked the hunter whether he required anything else from the store.

“No.”

“Then close the door behind you.”

He did.

After the hunter left, Ovani stood outside the store.

“I didn’t ask you.”

“No.”

“I would have gone.”

“And your mother?”

He looked toward his house. “Kelemi would have found someone.”

Nkiru wanted him to be grateful. She had put herself in trouble and wanted one person to make it easy to feel good about it. His stubbornness deprived her of that.

“Go,” she said.

He went.

After dark, Kelemi set the washed bowl on the table and sat down carefully. Nkiru could see that her jaw was hurting again.

“How much do we have left?” Nkiru asked.

“Enough to replace what he took.”

“And the lower houses?”

Kelemi said nothing.

Nkiru went to the shelf where the household stores stood. There were smaller jars behind the grain, including one her aunt had intended to exchange for cloth. She took them down, one after another. The last was nearly empty.

Mbala arrived while she was measuring. He had heard. People always heard quickly when there was something to make an evening less dull.

“How much?” he asked.

She told him.

He whistled, then stopped when he saw Kelemi’s face.

“I have a little.”

“You have children,” Kelemi said.

“They eat other things when made to.”

He put his small jar on the table. Nkiru had been ready to refuse an offer too small to change anything. Instead she found herself staring at it until he nudged it toward her.

“Don’t look like that. My sister will say I stole it from her.”

“Did you?”

“No. She knows where it is. That makes it borrowing.”

Kelemi laughed despite the pain in her jaw. Mbala sat down and stayed while they counted.

5.4 Where the thread goes

The Conservator came in the morning carrying a basket of roots for Kelemi’s jaw.

Her name was Raseme. She was younger than Kelemi and had been treating the tooth for several weeks, each visit ending with the same recommendation that it should be drawn. Kelemi preferred a succession of temporary remedies to one permanent indignity.

Raseme listened to the story while preparing a rinse. She asked to see the store post afterward.

Nkiru expected her to look at the new cord. Instead she crouched before the older ones and held a palm a little way from them. Her fingers moved as though testing warmth above a fire.

“There are too many returns laid across this house,” she said.

“Can you release them?” Nkiru asked.

“I can see where they pull.”

She followed one cord to a knot hidden beneath another. Its fibres had bitten so deeply into the post that the wood had grown around them. Kelemi looked away.

Raseme did not announce a motive. She asked who had witnessed the substitution.

“Essua,” Kelemi said.

They went to the Earth Society elder’s house after the morning work. Essua received them in a room whose shelves held wrapped bark records, ordinary baskets, and a broken cup she had been promising to mend for years. She was sewing when they entered. She kept the needle between her fingers while Kelemi spoke.

Nkiru remained by the door until Essua told her to sit. The elder had known her since childhood and still made her feel she might be asked whether her feet were clean.

The record was not hidden. Essua found it in the second bundle, unwrapped it, and read the first agreement aloud. The upper household had been allowed to settle near the grove after leaving a dangerous road. Its first offering had been carried by Kelemi’s household for one season, until its gardens could feed it.

“Then the second,” Raseme said.

Essua read that too. A poor harvest had extended the arrangement. The next extension had been spoken before three witnesses, of whom Kelemi and Essua were two. It had not been brought before the full gathering.

“Why?” Nkiru asked.

Essua put the bark down.

“Because we thought it could be managed.”

“By us.”

“By your aunt, who agreed.”

Kelemi’s fingers closed over her knee. Nkiru could see the knuckles paling.

“The upper house is full now,” Nkiru said. “They have gardens. Why do we still—”

“They have people in the rear rooms who cannot yet work openly,” Kelemi said.

The room became very quiet.

Nkiru knew there were travellers in the upper house. She had seen strangers carry water at odd hours and had been told not to make a curiosity of them. Sekogo forbade the taking of people, but roads beyond its protection did not. Families arrived with claims still following them, and sometimes with relatives left behind who could be hurt if their new place became known.

“How many?” she asked.

“No.”

“I only—”

“No.”

Kelemi looked older with that single word than she had while carrying the harvest pots. Nkiru sat back.

Essua admitted that the flowering strip had been narrowed during the same years. Each household had obtained permission for its own small alteration. She had recorded those too. No single permission said that the whole passage should become a platform and two beds, but that was what anyone walking it could see.

Raseme set the loosened cords on the mat between them.

“The grove still receives honey,” she said. “It receives less flowering ground. The household portions are being carried farther and farther from the people who cleared it.”

“So we put it back,” Nkiru said.

Essua looked at her. “With whose hands?”

Nkiru thought of Orema turning the crop, the sleeping baby, the workers who would lose wages. She disliked the question because she now had an answer complicated enough to delay her anger.

“Mine,” she said. “For a start.”

Kelemi gave a short laugh without amusement. “You have promised honey by tomorrow evening.”

“I know.”

“And trees won’t flower because you dig quickly.”

“I know that too.”

Essua made a copy to bring to the gathering, where she would tell what she had allowed. She would not let the entire account be carried by Kelemi.

Outside, Nkiru walked beside her aunt. She wanted to ask about the upper household, about how long Kelemi had been taking food there, about whether Nkiru herself had carried baskets without knowing whom they were for.

Kelemi stopped to rest in the shade. Nkiru waited with her.

After a while her aunt said, “Your mother stayed there once.”

Nkiru turned.

“Before you remember. Before she could come here safely.”

“Why didn’t you tell me?”

“She asked me not to make it the first thing people knew about her.”

Nkiru looked down the path. There were children chasing one another beyond the next house, raising dust with their heels. She had thought she knew the ways her aunt could make her feel small. This was a new one, and she did not think Kelemi had intended it.

They left Essua slowly. Nkiru took the basket without asking. Kelemi let her carry it.

On the way back from Essua’s house, Kelemi asked Nkiru to wait while she stopped at the upper household.

Nkiru said she could carry the basket in. Her aunt considered, then nodded toward the doorway, telling her to leave it with the woman at the front. Nkiru found that permission more disconcerting than being refused. She had been preparing to argue that she knew how to keep quiet.

The house was larger inside than it looked from the path. Several rooms had been joined around an old cooking court. Newer screens divided spaces that had once been open. A woman sat in the shade scraping a fibrous stem across a flat piece of wood. The stripped fibres lay in a pale pile beside her.

Nkiru set down the basket and named her aunt.

“I know who she is.”

The woman went on scraping. She had a low voice and a scar running through one eyebrow. Nkiru wondered whether to say what she knew, whether ignorance would now be a kind of pretence. The woman looked up and saw the effort on her face.

“If you have brought questions, take them back.”

“Only the basket.”

“Good.”

A child came from behind a screen carrying a cup. The woman took it, drank, and gave it back with a brief instruction. The child answered in a language Nkiru did not know. The woman repeated the instruction in the local speech, patiently, and the child tried the words before going away.

Nkiru turned to leave. A noise came from the workbench: the scraping edge had split. The woman swore, not quietly, and looked at the broken piece with an anger out of proportion to its size.

“There is another at our house,” Nkiru said.

The woman ignored her. She tested the remaining edge, which tore the fibres instead of stripping them.

“I could bring it.”

“Would that be another thing your aunt has done for us?”

Nkiru looked at the basket. “No. It would be me bringing a tool.”

“And then you would want it back.”

“Yes.”

The woman laughed once. “At least you know that much.”

Her name was Lurebe. She gave it while showing Nkiru the width of the edge she needed. The tool at Kelemi’s house was narrower, but it might serve if the handle could be packed. Nkiru said she would fetch it. She returned to find her aunt resting by the path and told her what had happened.

Kelemi listened. “Bring the good one. The other will break.”

“Then we’ll be without it.”

“For the afternoon.”

Nkiru fetched it. When she came back, Lurebe had arranged two seats and a small pile of unstripped stems. She intended to use the visitor’s hands while she had them. Nkiru sat down before she could reconsider the errand.

The work was tedious at first. Then it became demanding as she tried to keep the strips even without losing too much of the fibre. Lurebe showed her once, made her repeat the movement, and returned to her own pile. From the rooms came the sounds of a meal being prepared, a child protesting sleep, someone singing a tune without the words. It was easy to forget why people had come here until a stranger called from the outer gate and every voice stopped.

Lurebe laid the scraper down.

Nkiru waited with a stem in her hands. The call came again: a delivery of fuel. A woman answered, went out, and returned. Conversation resumed, unevenly at first. Lurebe picked up the scraper and had to change her grip before she could use it.

Nkiru looked down at her own work.

“What are these for?” she asked.

“Matting. Screens. Whatever people will buy.”

“Do you sell them here?”

“Someone takes them.”

She did not say who, and Nkiru did not ask. The finished work was good. One rolled mat near the bench had a pattern that shifted from dark to light depending on which way it lay. Nkiru admired it. Lurebe said the pattern had been an attempt to hide mismatched fibres, then unrolled it to show the place where it had failed.

“I can’t see it.”

“I can.”

They worked until Kelemi came to fetch Nkiru. Her aunt examined the new strips, picked out two weak ones, and said nothing about the time taken. Lurebe asked to keep the scraper until the next morning. Kelemi looked at Nkiru, leaving the answer to its borrower.

“All right,” Nkiru said.

At the door Lurebe gave her a narrow woven band.

“For the basket handle. It’s cutting your hand.”

Nkiru began to say it wasn’t necessary, then remembered the question about another thing done for the house. She accepted the band and fitted it before leaving.

Kelemi walked beside her in the shade along the path. She did not ask what Nkiru had learned. At home, when Nkiru reached for a pot, her aunt pointed out the strip of fibre caught in her sleeve and told her not to put it in the food.

That afternoon Nkiru went to Ovani’s house with a bundle of dry fuel. She told herself she was checking that he had not decided to leave before the witnesses required him. The fuel made the visit look less insulting.

He opened the door with a cloth over one shoulder. The room smelled of warm water and cooked meal. His mother lay on a raised bed near the window. She turned her head when Nkiru entered and asked whether the orchard had sent someone to count her spoonfuls.

“No,” Nkiru said.

“Good. I lose count myself.”

Ovani took the fuel and put it by the fire. He did not invite Nkiru to sit until his mother asked why their visitor was standing like a person waiting to be paid.

Her name was Sewa. Nkiru had known her when she could carry two baskets and still have breath to shout at children. Now she needed Ovani to lift her cup. He held it at an angle she disliked, and she corrected him before taking the first sip.

“You’ve been doing that for months,” she said.

“And you’ve been telling me for months.”

“Then one of us ought to have improved.”

He smiled despite himself. Nkiru sat on a stool whose seat had been repaired in three different kinds of fibre.

Sewa asked about the harvest. Nkiru described the oldest hive, Mbala’s singing, the pot the little boy had helped cover. She did not mention the store. Sewa listened until Nkiru began praising the boy’s seriousness, then asked whether his father had finally learned the correct words to the second verse.

“He knows them. He changes them to annoy people.”

“His mother did the same. She once made a whole song out of a man’s badly tied cloth. He wouldn’t come to a gathering for a season.”

Ovani stirred the food. “You sang it too.”

“It was a good song.”

Nkiru found herself laughing. Sewa looked pleased and asked for another drink. This time Ovani held the cup correctly.

He had a small piece of work to finish outside: a carrying frame promised to a neighbour in exchange for grain. He glanced at the door twice before asking whether Nkiru could stay long enough for him to fit the last binding. She agreed. He explained where everything was, then began to explain how his mother preferred the cup.

“I am still here,” Sewa said.

He stopped and went outside.

Nkiru moved the stool closer to the bed. Sewa asked her to open the window a little more. A moth struck the outer cloth and remained there, a dark shape against the lamplight.

For a while they listened to Ovani working. Nkiru could hear the binding scrape over wood, pause, begin again. He sang a few words under his breath, stopped, and tried a different knot.

“He should sleep,” Sewa said.

“Does he?”

“When I don’t need something. Then when I do, he doesn’t hear because he’s too tired. Afterward he is angry with himself, and I have to listen to that.”

Nkiru did not know what to say.

Sewa turned her head toward her. “You think I am complaining about him.”

“No.”

“I am. He’s here. I have nobody else to complain about nearly so often.”

She looked toward the window again.

“Could someone come at night?” Nkiru asked.

“Sometimes someone does.”

“More often?”

“Would you like to?”

The question caught her. She thought of the orchard, the deadline, Kelemi’s tooth, the tasks that had already filled tomorrow before it arrived.

“After the offering,” she said.

“The evening meal will need warming,” Sewa said. “He leaves it covered by the fire.”

The old woman asked her to shift the cloth beneath one shoulder. Nkiru did it clumsily. Sewa told her to stop pulling from the edge and put a hand beneath her first. Nkiru followed the instruction. It worked better.

When Ovani returned, the frame was finished. He showed it to his mother. She examined the join and said the neighbour would find something to object to because he enjoyed the shape of a bargain more than its ending.

“I know,” Ovani said.

“Then don’t let him pay you in promises.”

He set the frame by the door. Nkiru rose to leave. Outside, he walked with her to the path.

“I’ll be there,” he said.

“At the offering?”

“Wherever Konda says. You needn’t keep checking.”

She looked at the fuel through the open doorway. “I brought that because you needed it.”

“Yes. And you looked at my pack before you sat down.”

She had. It lay beneath the stool, tied for a journey he took whenever work required him elsewhere. She had noticed the knot and wondered whether it had been freshly made.

“I’m sorry.”

He nodded. For a moment they stood in the awkwardness she had created. Then Sewa called from inside, asking whether he intended to serve the evening meal after she had gone to sleep.

Ovani rolled his eyes toward the doorway. Nkiru smiled, and he went in.

On the way home, she tried to decide which evening after the offering she could actually keep free. She chose one before reaching her own door.

5.5 The path shut

Nkiru carried the last household pot to the store and stopped with it against her hip.

The offering pots stood ready. Mbala’s jar and the smaller contributions would make good what Ovani had taken, but only if they surrendered everything meant for the lower houses. Kelemi had arranged the vessels as though the decision had already been made.

Nkiru set her pot outside the door.

“No.”

Her aunt turned.

“The first portions are there,” Nkiru said. “The second promise is yours. I won’t take the food we set aside for them.”

Kelemi looked past her toward the post. The bark cord had tightened. Nkiru felt the pull beneath her thumb again, stronger this time, and had to resist rubbing it.

“You gave your word.”

“I’ll find another way.”

“By tomorrow.”

“Yes.”

She picked up the household pot and took it down the path.

At her great-aunt’s house she distributed the honey into three smaller vessels, leaving one and taking the others to the next two households. The work steadied her. An old man asked whether she could move his water jar before she left. A woman wanted help lifting her sleeping child from a mat that had become wet. No one asked for an account of her courage.

On her way home she met Mbala running.

“The orchard path.”

“What?”

“Come.”

The path had been there that morning. Now a tangle of branches crossed the place where it entered the inner grove. They were not broken branches. Each grew from a tree Nkiru knew, though the trunks had never stood close enough for them to meet. Beyond the tangle, the white patch on a familiar stone appeared farther away than it should.

Mbala threw a fallen twig through a gap. It dropped from another gap to his left.

People gathered. Someone blamed the missing honey. Someone blamed the Waker’s hand on the tree. Tembi, standing at the edge of the crowd, said she had awakened nothing and would answer that before any witness they chose.

A woman Nkiru knew only by sight arrived from the farther gardens with a basket of cut leaves. She had heard that the way was closing and tried to reach the outer path before it changed. Now she stood at the tangle, looking from one opening to another.

“My sister is behind there.”

“Two gatherers are coming the long way,” Mbala said.

“She isn’t gathering. She keeps the small bed beyond the bend.”

The woman named her sister. Several people recognized the name. Nobody had seen her return.

Nkiru looked through the gap. The white stone appeared again, then vanished behind a branch she would have sworn had not moved. The new arrival took a step toward it.

Mbala caught her elbow. She struck his hand away.

“Don’t hold me.”

He let go but stayed between her and the opening. Nkiru went to fetch a lamp though daylight remained. She did not know what else to bring. By the time she returned, the woman was calling her sister’s name.

A voice answered.

It was faint, somewhere beyond the trees. The woman turned at once, certain of its direction. Nkiru heard it again and thought it came from farther left. Two people began talking at once. The voice stopped.

“Quiet,” Kelemi called as she approached.

Everyone fell silent. They waited. A bird called high above them. There was no answer from the path.

The woman began to cry in a silent, furious way, her face tightening while she stared through the leaves. Nkiru stood beside her with the unused lamp. She wanted to say they would find another route, but the farther path would take hours and the sister might already be walking somewhere else.

Kelemi took the basket of leaves and set it safely away from people’s feet. She asked the woman what her sister had carried that morning and which bed she meant to tend. The questions steadied her enough to answer. There was a water gourd, a hoe, and a cloth of a particular colour.

Mbala sent two people to the side path. They would go only to the first clear marker and call, then return if the way altered. Nkiru wanted to go with them. Kelemi told her to remain where the woman could see someone listening.

She remained. The woman asked whether the grove took people when honey was withheld. Nkiru knew accounts in which it had, and accounts in which it had merely let them wander until someone fulfilled a promise. She could not tell which kind of account this would become while standing in it.

“I don’t know where she is,” she said.

The woman turned away from her.

Raseme reached them a few moments later. The Conservator examined the branches without touching them. A line of ants crossed one twig, disappeared beneath a leaf, and emerged on the underside of a branch several feet away.

“Don’t send anyone through,” she said.

The missing gardener came out behind the store half an hour later, carrying her hoe and demanding to know why someone had moved the basket she left by the path.

Her sister ran to her. The two women embraced awkwardly around the tools. Then the sister began scolding. The gardener had heard no one calling. She had followed the usual way home and found the last stretch longer than it should have been. She was angry about the lost time and remained angry while people tried to make her frightened.

Nkiru set down the lamp. Her hands were shaking. The gardener’s clothes were damp on one side, and a pale dust clung to the back of her hoe. Nothing on the ordinary path produced that dust. Raseme would inspect it later. For now Kelemi brought the women water and told everyone else to give them room.

Two gatherers were still beyond the orchard. They knew another way back, but it would bring them home after dark and over ground where they had expected daylight. A boy was sent to meet them at the longer path with a lamp and water.

Mbala’s son tried to find another way around the tangle. His father caught him by the back of his cloth before he had taken three steps into the undergrowth.

“I can see the other side,” the boy protested.

“So can I.”

“Then why—”

Mbala pointed at the twig that had fallen from the wrong gap. The boy stared at it. His face changed as he understood that his father was not merely invoking a rule made for children.

Nkiru took him back toward the houses. He walked beside her very quietly until they were out of sight of the branches.

“Will the trees come here?”

“I don’t know.”

He looked up at her, frightened by the answer.

“Raseme is there,” she added. “She knows more than I do. Your father is listening to her.”

“Father doesn’t listen.”

“He does when a ladder’s bad.”

The boy considered this and accepted it. At the yard, his aunt gave him a bowl to wash and told him she needed every bit of dirt gone. He set to work with the fierce concentration of someone grateful to have a thing that could be made right.

Nkiru went back to the path. On the way she passed two women turning toward the longer road with empty baskets. They had intended to gather leaves for a meal. Neither blamed her aloud. One asked how long the closure would last, and Nkiru had to say again that she did not know.

By the time she reached the others, the path had acquired more branches. Or perhaps she was noticing the ones she had missed. She could not establish even that much with confidence.

Nkiru stood beside the closed way, feeling everyone’s attention return to her whenever there was nothing else to watch.

Lenyo arrived in the late afternoon. He had heard that the next harvest could not be reached. His own workers used the path. So did people carrying garden produce to the road. He looked at the branches, then at the gathering.

“What is being done?”

“People are cooking,” Kelemi said.

He stared at her.

“They’ll be hungry while we argue.”

The cooking took place in Nkiru’s yard because it was nearest the store. Women arrived with pots, men with fuel, children with instructions they forgot halfway across the court. Mbala’s sister brought peppers and an opinion about every person she passed. Ovani came after settling his mother and offered to cut wood. Nobody sent him away, but several people watched him longer than they needed to.

Tembi spoke again about the forced bloom. This time people listened with less suspicion. Nkiru could hardly bear that she understood why.

“We need honey now,” Lenyo said. “You are speaking of years of planting.”

“The bloom won’t make honey by tomorrow,” Tembi said.

“No. But it might keep the next path open.”

Orema sat on a low stool nursing the baby. “And the trees afterward?”

“We would tend them.”

“Who?”

Tembi looked around the yard. She did not answer with a general promise. She named the daily work and asked who would do it.

Two people offered. Both already owed time elsewhere. Their relatives objected at once.

Nkiru went to the store and brought out the remaining household grain. She set it beside the cooking fire.

“If we work the old strip tomorrow, this feeds us.”

“It doesn’t replace a harvest,” Orema said.

“No.”

“It doesn’t pay the two men whose crop is there.”

“No.”

“Then stop setting things down as though they answer.”

Nkiru’s face burned. She wanted to say that at least she had brought something. She did not say it because Orema had come with a pot of beans and was feeding her baby while others argued about the land beneath her feet.

Essua arrived before sunset. She repeated the account of the altered corridor and the substituted portions. Some people had known most of it. Others knew only the permissions they had asked for. The full shape sounded ugly when spoken together.

A man asked why she had not objected earlier.

“I accepted the first alteration,” she said. “It was easier to accept the next than to say I should have questioned the first.”

Kelemi looked at her sharply. The elder went on to name the upper household’s exemption without naming those sheltered there. She asked everyone present to leave the protected people out of the argument. One man began to ask how many mouths there were. Mbala’s sister told him he could count his own if he had nothing better to do.

Before dark, Raseme attempted to reopen the path enough for the return of the gatherers. She asked only that much. Nkiru and Kelemi brought the first portion to the outer root. Essua stood witness. The Conservator laid her hands against the bark and spoke quietly.

For a moment the branches lifted.

Nkiru saw the white stone where it belonged. She stepped forward with relief so sudden that she did not notice Raseme raise a hand.

The path bent away. The stone was gone again. A branch struck Nkiru across the cheek and pushed her backward into Kelemi.

They fell together. The offering pot remained upright by a luck Nkiru would later be unable to explain.

Raseme took her hands from the tree. Blood showed beneath two fingernails.

“No more tonight.”

The gatherers returned by the long way after everyone had begun to worry aloud. One had twisted an ankle. They ate in the yard, furious at the cold food, the unnecessary distance, the boy whose lamp had smoked, and the fact that the entire village appeared to have saved its arguments for their arrival.

Nkiru sat with a wet cloth against her cheek. Kelemi’s shoulder had stiffened after the fall. Neither apologized. When the food reached them, they ate from the same bowl because someone had forgotten to bring another.

After the yard emptied, Nkiru went to Orema’s house.

She had not intended to go. She had washed the bowls, covered what remained of the food, and tried to sleep. Each time she shut her eyes she saw the path fold away from the white stone. At last she got up, found the jar she had borrowed from Orema for water, and carried it back as an excuse to knock.

The house was still awake. The baby cried inside. Orema opened the door with her hair loose and looked at the jar.

“That could have waited.”

“Yes.”

She let Nkiru enter.

Lenyo sat beside a low table, counting small packets of seed. He put a cover over them when he saw the visitor, not to hide them but to stop himself continuing. Nkiru had expected him to be asleep. She had imagined his household as a place where the day’s argument could be put aside because there was food enough behind every door.

Orema took the jar and went to the baby. Lenyo offered Nkiru a stool.

“Have you come to ask again?”

“Yes.”

“The ground is wet. If we take the wall down carelessly, it will damage Hedi’s terrace.”

“Then carefully.”

“You make a word do a great deal.”

Nkiru looked at the covered seeds. “Tell me what you’d need.”

He did. Not all of it was reasonable. He wanted the crop kept whole, his workers paid, a replacement platform, and assurance that the grove would reopen the path when the work was done. She could offer none of those alone. The assurance she could not offer at all.

Orema came back carrying the baby. “He knows that.”

Lenyo looked at his wife.

“You know nobody can promise the grove,” she said. “Ask for what people can do.”

He rubbed his face. The confident man who had brought a pot to Kelemi’s yard looked tired and older with his hand over his eyes. Nkiru tried not to make too much of that brief alteration. He could be tired and still intend to keep the useful ground.

Orema sat beside her.

“Where can the drying crop go?”

They named yards. Lenyo rejected some for good reasons. Orema rejected others. Nkiru found herself defending a poor choice merely because it was hers, then stopped and crossed it from the small list she was making on a scrap of bark.

The baby reached for the scrap. Nkiru moved it away. He objected loudly enough to end the discussion until Orema gave him an empty spoon.

“The upper bed,” Orema said when they could hear again. “If we lift what can still be used and pay the men for the rest, that ground can open first.”

Lenyo shook his head.

“It was the last bed we put in.”

“It is also the best drained.”

“Because we moved the water.”

He looked at her, then toward the dark beyond the doorway. Hedi’s terrace lay in that direction. Nkiru thought he might answer angrily. Instead he asked who would help with the crop before dismantling began.

“I will,” she said.

“You will be trying to find honey.”

“Mbala. His sister. Ovani, if his mother has someone.”

“Have you asked them?”

“Not yet.”

“Then ask.”

She rose. Orema stopped her and gave her a little food wrapped in a leaf.

“I ate.”

“I saw what you ate.”

Nkiru accepted it. At the door she turned back, wanting to secure the whole agreement while the room was warm and the conversation had not failed.

Orema met her eyes.

“Morning,” she said.

Nkiru left.

Mbala was asleep when she reached his house. His sister was not. She listened at the door, took the list, and added her own yard with a warning that anybody who damaged her cooking shelter would repair it before going home.

“Wake him?” Nkiru asked.

“I’ll tell him. He’ll think it was his idea by breakfast.”

At Ovani’s house she did not knock. The light was out, and she remembered the night she had promised after the offering. She went home instead.

Kelemi sat awake beneath the awning. She had made a warm rinse for her jaw and was holding the cup between both hands.

“Where?”

Nkiru told her.

Her aunt listened, then asked for the list. She read it slowly. At the name of a household that would not have enough hands, she made a small mark and named someone who might help. Nkiru had not thought of them because they lived farther from the orchard and rarely came to the harvest meal.

“They owe me nothing,” Kelemi said before Nkiru could ask.

“Then why would they come?”

“Ask them.”

Nkiru sat beside her. They finished the food Orema had sent, breaking the softer parts for Kelemi. The night insects were loud beyond the yard. Neither woman tried to say that the morning would go well.

5.6 A wall taken down

Orema agreed at first light to remove the upper bed and half the drying platform.

Lenyo objected to the half. He wanted the platform retained until the crop was finished. The flowering strip could be reopened around it, he said. Raseme walked the proposed route with him and asked where the new trees would put their roots. He pointed. She pointed at the foundation stones.

They stood in silence long enough for Nkiru to hope the matter had been settled. Then he proposed another arrangement that moved the loss onto a poorer household’s garden.

Orema called him back.

“The upper bed,” she said. “Half the platform. The rest after the crop is moved.”

“And where does it go?”

“Into people’s yards. If they mean what they’ve been saying.”

People began offering space. Some offers were useless: too damp, too shaded, too far from a roof when rain came. Orema refused those without gratitude. The useful ones she inspected herself. Nkiru accompanied her, carrying pegs and twine to mark what each household could take.

It took most of the morning. The deadline remained that evening. Nkiru kept feeling the cord pull when she flexed her hand.

Mbala found her measuring a patch beside the public shelter.

“Konda is on the road.”

“Now?”

“He said he’d come before the offering. He didn’t say how much before.”

Nkiru drove the peg into the ground.

“Then he’ll see us work.”

The two hired men whose crop stood in the upper bed had names, which Nkiru realized she had failed to ask until the matter concerned their payment. They were Beto and Sumbi, cousins with different households and no desire to be treated as a single claim. Beto would accept shared labour on his own garden in exchange for the lost crop. Sumbi needed food now because his youngest child was ill and he had spent what he had set aside.

Kelemi brought out a cloth she had intended to keep for the next gathering. It was a good cloth, carefully stored. Sumbi did not want cloth. Orema offered food for it, examined its edge, and named a fair amount. Kelemi agreed before Lenyo could begin improving the exchange in his own favour.

The wall came down under a sun that made tempers short.

Nkiru worked with Ovani, passing stones from the top course. They were heavier than they looked. The wall had been well built, which was inconvenient for everyone now blaming it. They stacked the stones where they could be used to repair the public shelter’s foundation. Lenyo protested that the stones were his. Essua asked him to mark the stack if he wished to keep them separate. He went to fetch paint, then returned without it.

Halfway through the work, a lower stone shifted. Water seeped through the gap behind it.

“Stop,” Orema called.

They stopped too late. A section of the bed slumped, carrying soil against the neighbouring terrace. Its outer lip cracked. Nkiru heard someone swear her name as though she had personally removed the wrong stone.

The neighbour, a small woman named Hedi, climbed down into the wet soil and began directing them. She had warned Lenyo years earlier that his wall was carrying part of her drainage. He had apparently considered the warning an expression of temperament.

Now she put him beside the cracked lip and told him to hold a board while they cut a new run for the water.

“Not there. There.”

Lenyo moved the board.

“You see where it’s coming from?”

“Yes.”

“Then let it come.”

Nkiru dug until her back burned. Ovani carried the wet soil away in baskets. Tembi, who had remained after her proposal failed to find enough hands, worked beside Hedi without invoking anything. The Waker knew roots and drainage well enough to argue about both. Once Hedi accepted a suggestion from her, the rest of the workers began treating her as someone who might carry a useful answer rather than merely a danger.

Kelemi brought water. She tried to lift a full basket of soil and could not. Nkiru took one handle, and her aunt snapped that she had not asked for help.

“I need to get past you,” Nkiru said.

That allowed them to carry it together.

At noon they ate standing or sitting on the dismantled wall. Nobody had enough shade. Mbala’s sister announced that if the grove preferred flowers to beans, it could come and explain itself to her children. Raseme said the grove had never asked them to plant beans in its flowering passage. The sister offered her a bowl anyway.

Konda arrived while they were eating. He wore his mask and carried no visible weapon beyond his long staff. Nkiru rose before she meant to.

He looked at the wall, the opened ground, and the pots beneath the shelter.

“By evening,” he said.

“I know.”

He went to sit where he could watch the path. Someone offered food. He accepted after loosening the lower edge of the mask without removing it.

His presence changed the afternoon. People worked harder for a while, then became angry at having done so. Nkiru found herself hurrying whenever she noticed him and making mistakes she would not otherwise have made. Hedi sent her away from the drainage cut after she stepped on a newly packed edge.

“Carry stones. They don’t care how frightened you are.”

Nkiru carried stones.

By late afternoon the corridor was visibly open, though it looked wounded rather than restored. Stumps stood among raw earth. The remaining trees had space around them they had not had for years. Orema’s drying crop lay in several borrowed yards, requiring more people to turn and cover it. Lenyo had gone to inspect every one and came back with complaints that were mostly justified.

Before they finished clearing the strip, they had to decide what to leave standing.

Nkiru had assumed the answer would be the old flowering trees and nothing else. Hedi disagreed. A small shrub had rooted against the wall and now held the wet edge of her terrace. Removing it at the same time as the stones would undo the repair they had just made. Tembi wanted to keep another plant because it shaded the base of a surviving tree whose roots had been exposed for years. Lenyo wanted to keep nearly everything that produced something he could sell.

Raseme walked among them and marked no plants until she had heard the people who tended them. The Conservator did not like the speed of the work. She said so whenever someone called it a restoration. Nkiru finally asked whether she would rather they put the wall back.

Raseme turned to her.

“I would rather you stop using that question whenever someone asks you to slow down.”

The workers near them became conspicuously busy.

Nkiru gripped the handle of her basket. She had been carrying stones since morning. Her cheek hurt. The promise around her hand hurt. She wanted one person to acknowledge that speed mattered too.

Raseme crouched beside the exposed root.

“Come here.”

Nkiru came reluctantly.

The root had grown around a buried shard. Its bark was thin on the upper side, almost translucent where the soil had been scraped away. Beneath it, small pale growths trembled. Nkiru thought at first that they were insects. Then one unfolded into a shape like a closed flower and folded again.

“The grove has been making do under the wall,” Raseme said. “We don’t know everything it used.”

Nkiru lowered the basket. The little growths withdrew when her shadow crossed them.

“What do we do?”

“Cover this part loosely. Leave the shrub until we have a better bank. Plant farther out.”

Tembi brought damp leaves. Together they covered the root without pressing the soil hard around it. Nkiru worked with two fingers, slowly enough not to tear the pale tips. When she finished, Raseme asked her to fetch another basket of loose earth.

She did. It was work she could complete without announcing anything, and she was relieved to have it.

At the platform, the last of Orema’s crop was being carried away. The hired men lifted the trays carefully. A child ran between them and was caught by Orema before he could strike a corner. She handed him the empty cloths and gave him a route where he would not be in the way.

Lenyo watched the space where his platform had stood. Nkiru had expected to enjoy his discomfort. Instead she recognized the look from her own face at the offering pots: something planned, worked for, and relied upon had been moved by an argument other people considered settled.

He caught her watching.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

He looked back at the cleared space. “I could have taken it down after the harvest.”

“Would you?”

He opened his mouth, then shut it. They both knew he might have, and might have found another good reason to wait. Neither answer would return the afternoon.

Nkiru picked up a fallen tray. Its edge had split during the move. She carried it to the shelter to be repaired before the crop needed covering.

The honey was still short.

Nkiru knew the amount without counting again. She had counted at dawn and at midday. The grain and labour agreements had saved the crop owners from bearing the whole loss, but they had not filled the missing part of the offering. She stood in the store with the measuring bowl in her hand and tried to think of a person she had not already asked.

Ovani came to the door carrying a small jar.

“From my sister.”

“When?”

“This afternoon. She came by the longer road.”

Nkiru took the jar, then hesitated. “Your mother—”

“Has food. My sister brought that too.”

He looked exhausted and annoyed. Nkiru had spent two days treating his household as a problem for her to solve and had not once asked whether he was trying anything himself.

She set the jar beside the others.

“Thank you.”

He nodded and went to wash.

There was enough.

Nkiru sat on the store step for a moment before telling anyone. Her hand had begun to cramp around the measuring bowl. She opened it slowly, finger by finger.

5.7 What the bees choose

They carried the offering at sunset.

The path remained shut. Beyond the new clearing, the branches crossed one another with the patient strength of things that could afford to wait. Nkiru could no longer see the white stone at all.

Raseme led. Essua walked beside Kelemi, carrying the record of the altered responsibilities. Konda followed Ovani. Nkiru carried the last portion, made up from several households, in a pot whose lid did not fit properly. She held it level with both hands.

The people who had worked followed at a distance. Orema brought the baby. Lenyo had soil on his clothes and looked as though he had been wronged by the entire afternoon. Hedi walked with a stick because she had twisted her knee in the drainage cut and refused to let anyone carry her.

At the outer root they set the pots down.

Konda asked Nkiru whether the missing portion had been made good. She answered. He asked Ovani whether she had compelled him to leave his dependent without food. Ovani answered sharply that his sister had come, and Konda accepted the answer without rebuke.

The hunter touched the new cord on the carried witness-post. Its fibres loosened. The pressure beneath Nkiru’s thumb eased so abruptly that she nearly dropped the pot.

There remained the grove.

Raseme spoke the offering. She named what had been taken, what was returned, and the ground opened for future flowering. She did not promise that new trees would blossom by the next harvest. She did not call the dismantled platform a gift. Essua spoke after her, acknowledging the permissions she had witnessed and the changes made without a full gathering.

Kelemi named the household portion she would still carry for the refuge. Nkiru turned toward her.

“One part,” her aunt said, looking directly at the root. “The rest will be shared by the houses that have agreed. Those names are here.”

She held up the bark strip. Nkiru had not known the agreement was finished. While she carried stones, Kelemi and Essua had been making their own rounds.

The leaves moved.

Not all at once. One branch lifted enough for a moth to pass beneath it. Another withdrew with a scraping sound. Nkiru saw a dark gap where no path had been, then the familiar white stone beyond it.

Nobody moved forward.

Raseme waited. A root rose beside the first pot and pressed against its side. The pot tilted. Honey spilled slowly over the lip and into the rough bark. Nkiru had an absurd impulse to save the lid, which had taken Kelemi half a day to fit properly.

The second pot cracked.

Lenyo made a sound of protest. Orema put a hand on his arm. The honey spread into the soil. Bees came down from somewhere above, fewer than Nkiru expected, and settled along the wet edge.

The grove did not touch the third pot.

Raseme looked at Kelemi. The aunt stood very still.

“Set it farther in,” the Conservator said.

Nkiru lifted it. The gap between the branches was barely wide enough for her shoulders. She could hear the breathing of the people behind her. She stepped through sideways, put the pot beside the white stone, and backed out without turning.

Nothing happened.

The light was failing. Hedi shifted her weight on the stick. The baby began to fuss, then quieted when Orema moved him. Somewhere behind Nkiru, Mbala started the harvest song very softly.

He began on the right note.

Other voices joined, uncertain at first. Nkiru knew the answering line and could not sing it. Her throat had tightened. Kelemi’s voice came beside hers, rougher than usual because of the tooth.

A bee landed on the third pot’s rim.

Then another.

The branches moved far enough apart for Raseme to pass. She walked only to the stone, stopped, and looked back. The way behind her remained where it should.

They crossed in pairs, without carrying tools, and came back. That was all they attempted. The deeper harvest would wait until daylight. Konda removed the loosened cord from the witness-post and gave it to Nkiru. It had begun to soften at the knot.

“You answered,” he said.

She put it in her pocket, too tired to know what she felt.

On the way home, Mbala’s sister asked whether the bees had approved of his corrected song. He said they had waited years to hear it properly. She replied that she sympathized with them.

Nkiru laughed. It hurt the scratched place on her cheek.

The opened strip looked raw in the last light. It would need planting, weeding, water in the dry weeks, and protection from the convenient encroachments everyone would eventually begin proposing again. Hedi stopped beside the repaired drainage run and made Lenyo promise to inspect it after the next rain.

“Not from up there,” she said when he glanced toward his house. “Standing here.”

He agreed.

Raseme remained by one of the surviving trees. A few bees had found a late cluster of blossoms high in its branches. She watched them until Nkiru came back to ask whether she needed help walking.

“In a moment,” the Conservator said.

They stood together. The bees worked without attending to either of them.

They left two people by the reopened way until the last offering vessels could be brought back.

Nkiru volunteered. Mbala stayed with her. The others went ahead carrying tools, empty jars, sleeping children. Their voices diminished along the path. For the first time since the closure, Nkiru could hear the grove without people trying to explain it over one another.

The broken pot lay beside the root. She gathered its larger pieces into a cloth. Some were held too firmly by the new growth to remove without cutting. She left them. The honey had drawn insects of several kinds, not all bees. They moved around her fingers with an intent disregard that made her work slowly.

Mbala sat on the white stone, which was now where it had always been. He had taken off his head covering. Sweat had flattened his hair into a shape Nkiru would have mocked on any other evening.

“Do you think it was her sister calling?” he asked.

“No.”

“You sound certain.”

“She didn’t hear us. She said so.”

“That doesn’t mean we didn’t hear her.”

Nkiru looked toward the trees. She did not want to argue herself into a more frightening answer. She tied the cloth around the potsherds and set it beside the emptied vessel.

After a while Mbala began humming the song. Very softly, without any performance in it. The sound steadied the space around them. Nkiru leaned against a root and let her shoulders drop.

“I was going to take the last comb when we harvested,” she said.

He stopped humming.

“Before I saw it properly. I had already put it in the pot in my head.”

“You left it.”

“Yes.”

He picked a piece of leaf from his hair. “I thought about going back for it after you left.”

She turned toward him.

“I didn’t.”

“I know.”

“You didn’t know until I said.”

Nkiru smiled tiredly. “I don’t want to fight.”

“Good. My eye still hurts.”

They waited until Raseme returned to check the path. She examined the third pot, the place the branches had opened, and a root neither worker had noticed crossing beneath the white stone. She did not seem pleased or displeased. She asked them to carry the intact vessel and leave the broken pieces until morning.

“The cloth too?” Nkiru asked.

“The cloth can stay.”

It was her good carrying cloth. She almost protested, then decided she had enough left for one evening. She could fetch it in daylight.

On the walk home, they passed the reopened strip. A firefly hung over one of the raw patches and went dark. Nkiru stumbled on a stone she had helped put there. Mbala caught her arm without speaking, and they continued at a slower pace.

5.8 After the work

Kelemi finally allowed the tooth to be drawn the next morning.

She made more noise about it than she had about the closed path, the extra offering, or falling on her shoulder. Raseme told her this was because the tooth was in her own mouth. Nkiru, fetching water, made the mistake of laughing.

Her aunt refused to speak to her for an hour, which Raseme considered useful to the healing.

The reopened path did not bring an immediate rich harvest. Two hives had moved farther in, and one hollow held less than they expected. Mbala returned with a sting on his other eyebrow and accused the bees of arranging his face symmetrically. The gathering pots came home partly filled. Nobody pretended otherwise.

Later that morning, Orema asked Nkiru to help collect the trays from the borrowed yards.

The crop had been sheltered during the night, but some of it had taken damp while being moved. They inspected it in the shade. Orema lifted a corner, smelled it and put it down again. She said nothing for so long that Nkiru asked whether it could still be dried.

“Some.”

“How much?”

“We’ll know when we separate it.”

They worked on a clean mat. Nkiru had handled honey since childhood and knew the small differences that mattered there; here she had to be shown twice. Orema did not make that difficult. She put a good piece and a spoiled one beside each other and let Nkiru feel them before beginning again.

Lenyo arrived carrying the last two trays. He stood at the edge of the mat.

“I said after the harvest.”

Orema did not look up. “Put them in the shade.”

He did. Nkiru continued sorting. There was nothing she could say that would change the damaged crop. She wanted him to go away before she tried to say it anyway.

He stayed, kneeling on the other side of the mat. For a while the three of them worked without speaking. Then he found a patch that Orema had discarded and insisted it could be saved. She broke it open and showed him the centre. He put it back.

The baby woke in the adjoining room. Orema went to him, leaving Nkiru with Lenyo and the smell of the spoiled crop.

“How long have you worked this bed?” she asked.

“Which one? The one that is no longer there?”

“Yes.”

He told her. He had improved the soil each year. The first crop had barely paid for the work; the next had been good. He described the water as it had run before the wall, a thin unreliable trickle that reached the far rows too late. Nkiru listened. She knew why the flowering strip mattered. She had not spent those seasons kneeling here.

Orema came back with the child on her hip. She heard the end of the account and added that they had quarrelled over the first load of stone because he had bought it before asking who would carry it.

“You carried none.”

“I was carrying him.”

Lenyo glanced at the baby. Nkiru thought he might smile. He did not, but he took the child when Orema handed him over. The boy reached for a handful from the mat. His father caught the small wrist and turned him toward the doorway instead.

They finished before the heat became oppressive. The loss was less than Orema had feared and more than Nkiru had hoped. Orema set aside the sound crop and asked Nkiru to carry a portion to the household that had sheltered the most trays. Nkiru picked it up.

At the gate, Lenyo called her back. He gave her the better basket, the one with a firm base.

“That one will spill.”

She changed baskets and took the load. When she returned later, he was already measuring the space left beside the smaller platform. He did not ask her to admire it.

The work on the corridor continued. Young trees arrived from three households. Some had been grown for other places and were given reluctantly. Orema insisted that the planting leave access for tending, and Tembi argued for a wider spacing around the oldest roots. Hedi supervised the water run from a stool, enjoying the authority of an injured person whose earlier warnings had proved correct.

Nkiru spent most of the day carrying mulch. It was warm, damp work. By afternoon she smelled of leaves and soil, and the scratched place on her cheek itched whenever she sweated. Ovani worked beside her until he had to go home to turn his mother. He returned later with food from his sister.

They ate without discussing the store.

The refuge household remained occupied. Nkiru carried one basket there and left it with the woman who answered. She did not ask to see the people in the rear rooms. As she turned away, someone inside laughed at a remark she could not hear. The sound made the house seem briefly ordinary, which she suspected was what its inhabitants wanted more than her careful interest.

Three evenings later, Nkiru kept the night she had promised Sewa.

Ovani looked surprised when she arrived with a sleeping cloth. He had apparently expected the offer to become one of the things postponed by the work on the grove. He began explaining the water, the cup, the cloth beneath his mother’s shoulder. Nkiru told him Sewa had already taught her one of those things and could teach the others.

He left slowly. At the door he came back for a tool he did not need. Sewa ordered him out.

They spent the first hour talking. Sewa wanted the account of Kelemi’s tooth, which Nkiru gave without mercy. Then the old woman became tired and asked for the lamp to be lowered.

Nkiru lowered the lamp and lay on the sleeping cloth beside the bed. The room was warmer than her own. Sewa disliked the draught from the lower opening, but without it the air hardly moved. Nkiru loosened her collar and folded one arm beneath her head.

For a while she listened to the household sounds dying away. Ovani’s sister moved a vessel outside. Someone passed on the path and called a greeting to a person farther up the slope. In the roof a small creature went about its business with an apparent disregard for the size of its feet.

“Do you hear that?” Nkiru whispered.

“I have heard it for three nights.”

“Shall I—”

“No. You’ll make it run.”

Nkiru lay still. The creature scratched, paused, scratched again. Sewa sighed.

“It is more active than I am. I dislike it.”

Nkiru began to laugh. She pressed her mouth against her arm to quiet herself, which made the bed shake when Sewa joined her. It took several minutes to stop. Afterward the room seemed less hot.

Sewa wanted to know whether Lenyo had actually carried the boards. Nkiru said he had, and described the mud on his knees. The old woman was disappointed that he had worked without making a more memorable objection.

“He did enough of that before.”

“I wasn’t there before. You could make it better in the telling.”

Nkiru considered the temptation. Then she gave Hedi an exaggerated gesture and Lenyo an answer he had not quite made. Sewa interrupted to improve it. Between them they produced a quarrel which would have taken half a day and ended in several people falling into the drainage cut.

“You mustn’t repeat that,” Nkiru said.

“To whom? The roof?”

Eventually Sewa tired. Nkiru drew the cover higher and lay down again. The laughter had left her pleasantly heavy. She was almost asleep when Sewa asked to be turned.

Nkiru rose too quickly. She caught the edge of the cup with her heel and had to steady it before kneeling beside the bed.
