She remembered Ovani’s instructions in the wrong order: the cloth first, then the shoulder, unless Sewa wanted to move the leg herself. The old woman told her where to put her hand.

“Lower. No, not all the way down. There.”

Nkiru moved her. Sewa caught her breath.

“Did I hurt you?”

“Stop asking while you’re still holding me.”

She stopped asking and finished. The cloth had wrinkled beneath the hip. They had to do part of it again. By the time Sewa was settled, Nkiru was sweating and the cover had fallen to the floor.

She picked it up, shook it outside the doorway and brought it back. Sewa watched her without speaking. Nkiru wanted praise with an intensity that shamed her. Ovani had been doing this without an audience for months.

“Water?” she asked.

“Yes.”

She held the cup at the angle Sewa preferred. A little escaped at the corner of the woman’s mouth. Nkiru wiped it with the clean end of the cloth, and Sewa asked her to leave the lamp where she could see its light.

“Is it too dark?”

“I know where I am. I like to see it.”

Nkiru moved the lamp.

She woke later to coughing and did not know where she was. The roof creature had gone quiet. The coughing stopped, then began again before Nkiru had fully sat up. She reached for the cup. Sewa pushed it away and pointed to the bolster.

They raised her a little. Nkiru sat behind the shoulder until the breathing eased. Her own breath had grown shallow from listening. She made herself breathe properly, and Sewa looked round as if annoyed by the demonstration.

“I’m here,” Nkiru said, then wished she had not. It was obvious.

“I can feel your knee.”

She shifted it. Sewa closed her eyes. Nkiru stayed where she was until a cramp began in her calf, then eased herself away inch by inch.

The next time she slept, she slept deeply enough to miss the first call. At the second she woke ashamed, tangled in the cloth and certain something dreadful had happened. Sewa wanted the cover moved from her feet. Nkiru moved it. The old woman asked for another turn before Nkiru could begin apologizing.

Near dawn they were both awake. Nkiru sat on the floor with her back against the bed. Sewa’s hand lay over its edge, close to her shoulder. Outside, someone had begun stirring a cooking fire; the smell came through the opening Sewa disliked.

“Open it a little,” she said.

Nkiru did. Cooler air entered. A bird called from the cleared strip, and another answered farther away. Sewa listened with her eyes open.

“That’s the one that comes to the old fence.”

Nkiru knew the call without knowing the bird. She said so.

“It steals the threads when I leave them outside. Blue ones, chiefly.”

“Perhaps you leave more blue.”

“Perhaps I know my own thief.”

They waited for it to call again. When it did, Sewa smiled. Nkiru sat back against the bed and let her head rest there until the sound of Ovani at the door made her open her eyes.

At dawn Ovani returned carrying bread. He had slept at his sister’s house. Nkiru envied him his rested face.

“Did you sleep?” she asked.

“After the game.”

“You stayed up?”

“My sister’s husband owed me a rematch.” He set the bread down and went to open the shutter. “Still does.”

He took over quietly, letting her drink before asking how the night had gone.

“Your mother says you put too much water in the meal.”

“She says that to everyone who feeds her.”

“It was better thicker.”

“Then you can come again and prove it.”

She said she would come after two evenings, which was soon enough to help and late enough that she could sleep properly first. Sewa, pretending not to listen, asked whether either of them intended to pass the bread.

On the way home Nkiru went through the orchard. The path held its place. There were still fewer bees than there should have been, and the new planting looked small beside the raw strip. She checked the water at one root, lifted a cover that had fallen, and left the rest for the people whose morning work it was.

Kelemi was awake when she reached the house. A bowl waited near the fire.

“How was she?” her aunt asked.

“Demanding.”

“Good.”

Nkiru ate, washed, and lay down. Through the open wall she heard Kelemi turning the planting list over with someone at the door. Her aunt’s voice was still muffled by the healing jaw. She was refusing a suggestion with enough force to reassure Nkiru that the tooth had not taken anything essential with it.

The first hard rain after the reopening came while half the new ground was still uncovered.

Nkiru woke to water striking the outer wall. She lay still for one breath, enjoying the coolness, then remembered the bare strip above Hedi’s terrace. By the time she reached the door, Kelemi was tying her work cloth.

“You stay,” Nkiru said.

Her aunt looked at her.

“Your jaw.”

“My jaw will be wet whether I sit here or there.”

They went together. The path held, though in the dark Nkiru distrusted every familiar turn. Water shone on leaves and ran along the newly disturbed ground. At the strip, two of the small trees had tilted. The loose covering around another had washed into the drainage run.

Hedi was already there, sheltered beneath a piece of matting held by Lenyo. She was giving directions in a voice that allowed no possibility of mishearing. Lenyo looked miserable. Nkiru took one corner of the matting so he could help clear the blocked run.

The work was cold and clumsy. Mud pulled at their feet. A basket slipped from someone’s hands and went downhill, scattering the soil it was meant to preserve. Tembi arrived carrying stakes. She put them beside the most exposed plants and called for ties.

Nkiru had brought none. Kelemi took a length from her own carrying strap and cut it without hesitation.

They braced the little trees. One had lost too much root soil and would not stand properly. Tembi dug around it with her hands, lifted it carefully, and carried it under shelter. Nkiru asked whether she could make the roots hold.

The Waker looked at her across the rain.

“Do you want me to?”

Nkiru hesitated. The possibility had arrived before she had decided what she was asking. They were all wet, tired, and watching the work of several days move downhill. A little strength in a root would be useful. She knew enough now not to call it a little thing merely because it would be useful.

“How much would it take?”

Tembi examined the seedling. “More from this than I want to ask of it tonight.”

She put it in a pot instead. They would keep it sheltered until there was a better place for it. Nkiru helped carry the pot to the house nearest the strip, where a woman who had objected to the whole plan made room beside her own door and wrapped the exposed root ball.

They worked until the first light. The drainage run held. One planted tree was lost, two would need to be reset, and the platform space had become a shallow pool. Lenyo stood looking at it without speaking.

Orema brought hot food. She handed him a bowl, then another to Hedi. The two women began arguing about whether the upper edge needed stone or planted reinforcement. Neither had enough strength left to do the work immediately, which did not prevent the argument.

Nkiru sat beneath the shelter with mud drying on her legs. Kelemi’s cut strap lay across her lap. She tried to draw the ends together; her fingers kept slipping.

“I’ll mend it tomorrow,” she said.

“After you sleep,” Kelemi replied.

For once they agreed without effort.

Later that day Ovani came for her. Sewa had asked whether Nkiru meant to keep the second night. She had forgotten the date while listening to the rain.

“I said I would.”

“You look terrible.”

“I can still sit in a room.”

“And fall asleep in it.”

Nkiru flared at him. He waited. She had failed to wake at Sewa’s first call even before the night in the rain.

“Tomorrow?” she asked.

He thought. “My sister can come tonight if I fetch her before she begins her own work.”

“Will that cost her?”

“Yes.”

He did not soften the answer. Nkiru offered to do the sister’s carrying the next morning. Ovani said she could arrange that herself and left before the whole exchange became a quarrel.

She found his sister, explained, and took the task the woman actually needed done. It was washing two large cloths after the mud from the previous night, not the carrying Nkiru had imagined. She could do it after sleeping. The arrangement was made in a few tired sentences.

At home, Kelemi had put a cover over the sleeping mat. Nkiru lay down still intending to remove the mud from her heel. When she woke, someone had washed her feet and left the basin beside the door.

The meal after the planting took place in the public shelter. Lenyo’s stones had been used to repair the foundation, with his eventual consent. He mentioned this three times before Mbala’s sister thanked the stones directly.

Long dishes were set along the floor. Children ate early and returned pretending they had not. The workers sat wherever there was room. Tembi came late, having stayed to check the new trees, and someone moved a bowl toward her without an argument about what else she might have offered.

Kelemi sat beside Nkiru. Her jaw was swollen. She inspected the roasted food and looked away from it with an expression of offended hunger.

Nkiru found the softer beans and put them within reach. Her aunt gave her a brief glance and began eating.

Essua asked Kelemi whether she was pleased with how the matter had ended.

“It has not ended,” Kelemi said through the tender side of her mouth. “There are trees to water.”

“You know what I mean.”

“Yes.”

She took another spoonful.

Nkiru had imagined, several times, that there would be a moment when her aunt acknowledged she had been right to refuse. She had also imagined telling Kelemi that she understood the refuge, and receiving some private admission about the years of work Nkiru had not seen. Neither speech arrived. Kelemi asked her to pass the soft food again.

Across the shelter, Mbala began the harvest song.

This time the first verse concerned a wall that had been so carefully built it required half the village to persuade it to fall. Lenyo stared at him, then at the people beginning to laugh. Orema leaned over and supplied a rhyme that improved the verse at her husband’s expense.

The second verse was about a young woman who promised honey before looking in her pots.

Nkiru threw a leaf at Mbala. He caught it against his chest with exaggerated injury and continued.

Kelemi laughed, put a hand to her jaw, and laughed again more carefully. Nkiru watched her for a moment, then joined the answering line. She sang loudly enough to carry it when Mbala wandered off the note.

What Was Returned

6.1 The returned face

Tavere recognized the voice and almost missed the woman who had used it.

She was standing beside the lower landing with a long wrapped burden across her shoulders. Two carriers waited behind her, both human, both visibly relieved to have reached a place where someone else might take responsibility for it. The woman had a narrow face, a pale line along one cheek, and eyes that rested on Tavere with courteous interest.

“Tavere?”

The old voice had caught on the second syllable when she was tired. This voice did not. It had the same low warmth, but the name came cleanly.

“Yes.”

“I was told you would know where to take this.”

She lowered the burden with the carriers’ help. Beneath the wrapping, metal touched stone with a deep, brief note.

Tavere looked at her hands. They were longer than she remembered. One thumbnail had split and grown back with a ridge. She had known every small hurt on those hands once, had watched one heal and another arrive before they were old enough to make their oaths.

“Orasha.”

“Yes.”

The woman smiled. Tavere saw the smile reach muscles that had not held it before.

Around them the landing continued its work. A boy carried a basket past, stopped at the wrapped object, and was pulled onward by someone who needed the basket. Water sounded below the planks. The carriers shifted their weight, waiting to be paid.

Tavere took out her purse.

Orasha stopped her. “I have their due.”

She counted coins into the first carrier’s hand and added a little beyond the agreed amount. The man thanked her. His companion received the same slight excess. Neither looked surprised. They took the coins and went uphill toward food.

Tavere watched them leave, then turned back.

“The anchor?”

Orasha unwrapped one end. The metal was dark, worked into a broad curved shoulder with a deep channel for the bridge binding. Tavere had seen the drawing years before. The finished thing looked heavier, uglier, more likely to hold.

“You brought it.”

“That was the work.”

Tavere wanted to ask where she had been. Instead she called for a lifting frame.

People came. They came because the landing needed hands, then because they recognized the name spoken among them. Recognition moved through the court without a single announcement. One person fetched water. Another went to clear a room. A third asked whether Orasha had eaten.

Within minutes Tavere found herself standing beside a woman whose arrival had become everyone’s business.

Orasha accepted water, drank, and offered the vessel back with both hands. The woman who had brought it belonged to Tavere’s web; her face registered the fraction too much in the gesture, then smoothed. She took the vessel and went to fetch food.

Tavere had known what Orasha would become. They had stood together before their adulthood oaths and spoken of it, badly. Tavere had sworn into the people among whom she would live and work. Orasha had sworn away, following the broken crossing’s work beyond the valley, carrying the undertaking in a body that would continue even when memory ceased to accompany it.

At her departure they had used the rite for the dead. Tavere had hated the singers for knowing all the verses.

Now Orasha stood in the afternoon light, alive, turning her head at sounds Tavere no longer noticed. Her skin had darkened along the jaw. Her shoulders sat differently beneath the travelling cloth. She looked at the houses as a visitor would, interested in where water was kept and which walkway led to the upper rooms.

“You can stay with us,” Tavere said.

“For the work?”

“Yes.”

It was not the whole answer she had meant to give. It was the answer Orasha could accept.

They carried the anchor to a store near the bridge road. Tavere checked the supports before letting the frame take its weight. Orasha watched closely. She asked two questions about the floor, both good. Tavere answered the first and stumbled over the second because it was a question Orasha once would have answered for her.

Later, in the house, someone had already laid out a sleeping mat and a folded cover. There was food, a basin, and a place for the traveller’s few possessions. Tavere saw her own influence in every arrangement without remembering having asked for any of them.

Orasha set down her pack.

“This is generous.”

Tavere disliked the word. Inside the web, no one called an ordinary place to sleep generous. Orasha was outside it now. The distinction sat between them as plainly as the pack.

“You used to sleep there,” Tavere said.

Orasha looked at the mat. “Did I?”

There was no distress in the question, only a willingness to receive what Tavere wished to tell her.

“Before the room was changed.”

“What was changed?”

Tavere pointed to the shelf, the doorway, the place where a wall had been taken down so that two rooms could share the evening air. As she spoke, she realized she was describing a decade of household decisions to someone who could see only a pleasant room with a place prepared for her.

Orasha listened. When Tavere finished, she asked where she should put her boots.

Before the meal, Tavere went to the store alone.

The anchor lay on its supports. Its wrapping had been folded beside it, and a small packet of instructions rested in the curve of its shoulder. Orasha had left the packet open. Tavere read the first page, recognized measurements she had helped take before the old span was finally closed, and put it down.

Outside, a child was practising a knot with a length of discarded fibre. He had made the same error three times. Tavere stopped to show him, then found herself demonstrating with an impatience that made his hands less certain. She apologized and let him undo it without help.

When he had gone, she remained on the store step.

The last evening before Orasha’s oath returned to her in pieces. A bowl of unripe fruit they had insisted was ready. A cloth spread on the warm stone. Orasha pulling a loose thread from Tavere’s sleeve and winding it around one finger while they spoke. Neither had wanted to begin with what the next morning would do.

They had climbed to that ledge to avoid the household, which was preparing for the morning with a thoroughness Tavere found offensive. Someone had washed the travelling cover. Someone had counted the food. The same people who had told them to be careful on every childhood errand were now helping Orasha leave with clean clothes and a sound pack.

On the ledge, Orasha bit into the fruit and refused to admit it was sour.

“Your eyes are watering,” Tavere said.

“The wind.”

“There isn’t any.”

“There is where I’m sitting.”

She moved closer as if to demonstrate. Their shoulders touched. Tavere leaned against her, then moved away because she was angry and wanted the anger understood without having to speak it.

Below them, people crossed the lower bridge with the evening’s last burdens. The household lamps were being lit. They could identify several of the people from the way they carried things: the woman who held every basket as if it were empty, the man who stopped halfway whenever somebody called to him, even if he had no interest in the answer.

Orasha began making up what they were saying. Tavere refused to laugh at the first attempt and failed at the second. They had played this game for years. Orasha gave the basket woman an absurd complaint about the moon, then made the man on the bridge offer to mend it with the wrong tools.

“You always make him foolish.”

“He always stops on the bridge.”

“He’s kind.”

“He can be kind on the landing.”

Tavere could remember the warmth of Orasha’s shoulder more clearly than the invented conversation. The old face came less easily now that she had seen the new one. She had not expected that. She had believed, until the afternoon, that she had kept it whole.

That last evening, Orasha had eventually set the fruit aside and asked whether Tavere would come down before the singing.

“I don’t want to hear it.”

“I know.”

“They’ll sing as if you’re gone while you’re standing there.”

“Then look at me.”

Tavere had looked. There had been a mark by Orasha’s mouth where the fruit had caught her skin. Tavere brushed it away with her thumb, more roughly than she meant. Orasha caught her hand and held it between both of hers.

For a few moments neither spoke. Tavere had been certain that if she remained still enough the evening would continue. She could hear a loose fastening tapping against a post below, a small persistent sound that had gone on through everything they said.

At last someone called them to eat. Orasha answered that they were coming. Tavere pulled her hand away.

Tavere had asked whether she could carry the anchor’s task without swearing away. Someone else could travel, or the metalworkers could be persuaded to come, or the bridge could remain closed another season. She had produced alternatives quickly, each one more reasonable than the last.

Orasha had listened until Tavere heard herself and stopped.

“I want to go,” she had said.

The words had seemed an injury then. Tavere had looked at the fruit between them and thought of all the things she had assumed would continue simply because they had been good.

“Will you remember?”

“I don’t know.”

“Me?”

Orasha had taken the thread from her finger and laid it across Tavere’s palm. She had not made a promise. At the time Tavere had hated her for withholding the one answer that might have made the night bearable.

She had kept the thread for several years. Then it had broken while she moved her things from one room to another, and she had thrown it away in anger. For days afterward she had searched the sweepings whenever nobody watched.

At the store, Tavere rubbed her empty palm against her knee. She had forgotten the thread until this moment. Orasha’s missing years had not made Tavere’s own recollection complete.

A voice called from the court. She went back to the house.

Orasha was helping a boy bring water up the steps. He had given her the fuller vessel. She had accepted it and then offered to carry his as well. The boy, who possessed no reason to refuse an advantageous arrangement, had let her.

Tavere took one vessel from Orasha at the top.

“He can carry his own.”

“He offered to show me the cistern.”

“He lives here.”

Orasha looked toward the boy, who had vanished into the house.

“I see.”

There was amusement in her voice. Tavere let herself smile.

“He’ll try it again.”

“Then next time I’ll know the cistern.”

They carried the water inside together.

That night the household ate together on the open floor above the court. Children moved between adults without consulting their parents. Dishes passed. Someone began a dispute about a new weaving frame and received three answers from three directions. Orasha sat beside Tavere and learned the names she was offered. She remembered them when she used them again.

Tavere watched for a failure that might make the missing years easier to understand. There was none. Orasha could follow a conversation, notice a joke, ask after an injured hand. She simply did not turn toward Tavere at the moments when the old Orasha would have turned.

After the meal, Tavere took her to the walkway overlooking the gorge. The red stone held the day’s warmth. Across the darkening space, bridge lamps marked the routes between households. Far above, the forest rim lay black against the sky.

Orasha rested her forearms on the rail.

“It’s beautiful.”

“You said that the first time.”

She glanced at Tavere. “Then I seem consistent.”

Tavere laughed before she could help it. The laugh brought tears close enough to frighten her. She looked down into the gorge until they passed.

Orasha waited beside her, not asking what was wrong.

6.2 More than was offered

In the morning Tavere brought a cup to Orasha’s room.

The drink was the one she had preferred before leaving: bitter at first, sweeter as it cooled. Tavere had made it without thinking and realized, at the door, that she was about to test something she had no right to test before breakfast.

Orasha took it with pleasure.

“Thank you.”

“You may not like it.”

“I like it.”

She drank again, proving the statement without being asked.

Later Tavere found her own work basket repaired. A loose handle had been reinforced and the frayed edge bound neatly. Orasha had used material from her pack, a finer thread than the basket required.

“I noticed it while you were fetching water,” she said.

Tavere ran her thumb over the repair. The old Orasha had mended things quietly too. She felt a warmth so sharp that she had to set the basket down.

Then she went to the court and found a child’s toy repaired with the same fine thread. Beside it lay a new loop for the basin handle belonging to the woman who had brought Orasha water at the landing. The loop was slightly better than needed, carefully finished at both ends.

Tavere stood over it until the woman came to fetch the basin.

“She does lovely work,” the woman said.

“Yes.”

They were preparing a long stretch of communal cord that day, enough to replace the worn side rail along three walkways. Tavere took her place among the workers. Orasha asked to help and was given a task that could be explained without assuming years of shared work. She learned quickly. Her fingers were patient, her tension even. When someone offered a stool, she later brought back a better cushion for it.

Nobody reproached her. The excess was small, smooth, almost invisible to the humans passing through the court. Among Tavere’s people it showed whenever she accepted a kindness and answered. Orasha seemed aware of their awareness. She remained courteous and continued working.

The work required a long even pull, and by midmorning they had begun arguing about the song used to keep it.

One group preferred a slow count with a rest after every fourth turn. Another said the rest encouraged people to loosen too much. Seluve proposed the song her own teachers had used, which prompted an account of how badly those teachers had tied a famous winter rail. Tavere remembered the rail. It had held, but its curve had offended every person who crossed it for years.

Orasha listened without joining in. When the next pull began, she matched the hands beside her rather than the song. Her timing was good. She still had to be told when they changed the work because someone farther down the line had found a flaw.

Tavere felt the change before the call reached her. The people around her had adjusted, their shared attention passing along the work. She looked at Orasha and saw her continue for a fraction longer, then stop as the spoken instruction arrived.

No harm was done. They eased the tension and began again. Tavere had nevertheless seen something she had been refusing to imagine: the woman beside her could not enter the common work by merely wishing to be helpful.

During the next pause, Orasha asked for the changes to be called before the hands moved.

A worker said they always called them.

“After you know,” Orasha replied. “I need to hear before you do it.”

The worker looked at Tavere, perhaps expecting her to explain. Tavere asked him to give the call earlier. He did so for the rest of the morning, occasionally too loudly, which made the children imitate him from the steps.

Orasha thanked him at the end of the pull. He accepted with a small awkward nod. Then they discovered he had left a loop twisted and began correcting it together, the ordinary irritation of the task relieving them both.

At the break, Tavere took her cup to the edge of the court. Orasha followed and sat with one leg folded beneath her. The posture was unfamiliar. Tavere remembered the old habit of stretching both legs straight and crossing the ankles.

“Do you like this work?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“You can stop if you’re tired.”

“I know.”

Tavere drank. She had offered the same permission to nobody else that morning.

Orasha watched a child trying to carry three cups at once. “I like the part where everyone argues about doing something they can all do.”

“That is most of the morning.”

“I noticed.”

They laughed. The child dropped one cup, looked around to see who had heard, and found so many people already moving to help that he began to cry before anyone could say it was only a cup.

Orasha stayed seated. She had started to rise, then seen that she was not needed. Tavere remained beside her until the crying eased.

At midday a little girl asked why Orasha’s face was different from the carving in Tavere’s room.

Tavere’s hands stopped.

“What carving?” Orasha asked.

The girl’s mother began to call her away. Tavere shook her head. Everyone was now trying not to listen.

“An old one,” Tavere said.

“Of me?”

“Yes.”

“I would like to see it.”

She said it without coyness. Tavere could not tell whether the request was another return for the cup, another small generosity, or actual curiosity. She hated having to wonder.

They looked at it in the afternoon.

It was a crude little figure cut from a piece of discarded bridge timber. Tavere had made it when they were young, before her hands had become useful at anything more demanding than basic cordwork. The face was scarcely a face. Orasha had recognized herself then because Tavere told her who it was and because love had made her generous about the nose.

The present Orasha turned it over.

“You made this?”

“Yes.”

“The grain fights the cut.”

“It fought me.”

“I can see that.”

Tavere laughed uneasily. “You used to say it looked exactly like you.”

Orasha examined the face again. “I may have been kind.”

The remark was light, offered with a smile. Tavere felt herself recoil from it before she could pretend otherwise.

Orasha lowered the figure.

“I’m sorry.”

“No. You were. I knew that.”

She took it back too quickly. One sharp corner caught her palm. She looked at the small red mark and was absurdly grateful for somewhere to put her eyes.

“What is it for now?” Orasha asked.

Tavere did not answer immediately. The question had been practical. The object belonged to a life Orasha did not possess. She could admire the effort, regret the hurt, and still not know why it should travel farther with them.

“I keep it,” Tavere said.

Orasha nodded. “Then keep it.”

Outside, the cordworkers called for Tavere to check a join. She put the carving back on its shelf and went to them.

Before evening, the children persuaded Orasha to show them the folded paper birds she made from scraps of the packing around her tools.

Tavere came into the court carrying a basket and found four children staring at a square of paper as if it might hatch. Orasha folded it, turned it, folded again. One child interrupted to say that the wing should be on the other side. Orasha gave him a square of his own.

He worked in silence for nearly a minute before asking for help.

The first bird flew badly. The second struck Seluve in the shoulder. The third sailed through the open doorway and landed in a bowl of water, where it began to unfold. This was judged the best result by everyone except the person who had filled the bowl.

Tavere set down her basket and asked where Orasha had learned the folds.

“A town near a salt lake. Or perhaps on the road before it. I have the sequence, but not the person.”

She made another bird while speaking. Her fingers remembered the turns without hesitation. Tavere watched the new thing emerge from hands she had once assumed she knew completely.

“Could you teach me?”

Orasha moved over.

They sat among the children. Tavere folded the first corner too sharply and could not reverse it cleanly. The child who had been corrected earlier offered advice with immense satisfaction. Orasha let him explain. Tavere tried again, more carefully.

Her bird came out heavy on one side. She threw it anyway. It turned in a slow circle and dropped into the basket she had set down.

“A useful bird,” Seluve said. “It knows where things belong.”

The children demanded more paper. Orasha had no more clean scraps. Someone proposed using an old account brought by a human trader, and Tavere had to intervene before the children decided that everything written on paper was equally finished with.

The game became one of making the existing birds perform more ambitious flights. A line was stretched between two posts. A cup was set beneath it. A small argument arose over whether touching the line counted as crossing it. Orasha took no side until asked, then proposed a rule that pleased no one and was therefore tested immediately.

For a while Tavere forgot the carving in her room. She watched Orasha laugh when a bird struck the back of its maker’s head. She laughed too. When the laughter ended, she found the earlier hurt still there, but she did not leave the court because of it.

Later she gathered the wet birds and spread them to dry. One had come apart enough to reveal a fragment of an instruction beneath the folds. It concerned the anchor’s wrapping, nothing private. She turned it over carefully anyway.

Orasha stood beside her.

“Keep the fold loose there,” she said, touching the edge of Tavere’s bird. “It will turn better.”

Tavere adjusted it. They tried the bird once more before going inside. It crossed the line and missed the cup by a generous distance. Orasha applauded softly, with a smile that did not require Tavere to have succeeded completely.

That evening she avoided sitting beside Orasha. She told herself the workers needed her judgment on the next day’s distribution of tasks. They did, briefly. Afterward she remained among them while Orasha spoke with a human trader about a route along the eastern lowlands.

The trader had brought honey from Sekogo. One jar stood open, and people took a little with the ends of clean sticks. Orasha asked about the coming rains, the condition of a crossing, the price of passage for one person without much cargo. Tavere listened from across the room.

When the trader left, Orasha came to her.

“You left your tool beside the frame.”

She handed it over. She had cleaned the resin from the grip.

Tavere accepted it and thanked her with the ordinary exactness due a kindness across the boundary of the web. Orasha smiled, evidently relieved that the exchange had gone well.

Tavere lay awake afterward, angry with herself for having made her relieved.

The following evening, the household held a meal for the returning workers from a northern crossing, and Tavere was asked whether Orasha would join it.

She answered before asking. Of course she would. Half the people coming had known her before she went away.

By the time Tavere found her at the store, a place had been arranged and someone had brought out the old song-board used when that group gathered. Orasha listened to the invitation, then asked whether anything would be expected of her.

“Only to eat.”

The answer was insufficient. Tavere knew it and let it stand.

That afternoon, before the meal, people arrived with reasons to look into the store. One asked about the anchor, another about the road, a third about a person she believed Orasha had met in a distant town. The last question could be answered. Orasha knew the woman, or knew enough of her recent life to identify her, and gave a precise account of where she was working. The visitor left satisfied.

Tavere felt a brief, ugly envy of her.

At the meal, Orasha sat where Tavere placed her. The food was good and the company noisy. A man who had once helped them repair a broken rail began recalling the day they had both fallen into a shallow pool. He addressed the account to Orasha. She smiled and listened. When he asked whether she remembered what Tavere had shouted, she said she did not.

He supplied the words, louder. Several people laughed.

“I don’t remember the day,” Orasha said.

The man’s smile faltered. “Yes. Of course.”

He began speaking to someone else too quickly, leaving her outside the new conversation. Tavere tried to bring her back by asking about the town she had mentioned earlier. Orasha answered, but the account interested only two people. The others were still talking about the old pool.

Then the song-board came out.

The first song was one Tavere knew well enough that her mouth began the answer before she had decided to join. The response moved around the room. Orasha listened, watching the faces turn toward her at the place where she had once taken a line.

Tavere sang it for her.

The room accepted the substitution and went on. That was almost worse than a pause. Tavere could feel Orasha beside her, holding the cup in both hands. When the song ended, she put it down and asked where she could wash.

Tavere showed her the basin. Outside the room, the air was cool.

“Would you like to go?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“I can come.”

“You should stay. They are your friends.”

“They’re yours too.”

Orasha looked at her then. She did not seem angry. The absence of anger gave Tavere no way to defend the mistake as merely a quarrel.

“They knew me,” Orasha said. “I can meet them. It is harder when they keep waiting for someone to answer from where I am sitting.”

Tavere put a hand against the basin’s edge. From inside came the beginning of another song, followed by a correction and laughter.

“I should have asked.”

“Yes.”

“Do you want me to explain?”

“Not tonight.”

Orasha washed her hands, dried them, and went back to the room only long enough to thank the hosts. She did it gracefully. The warmth of her thanks exceeded the meal a little, and Tavere saw one of the older workers notice. He glanced at Tavere, embarrassed on her behalf, which made her want to leave as well.

She stayed. She ate very little more. When someone remarked that it must be difficult for Orasha to find everything so changed, Tavere said the room had spent most of the evening expecting her to be unchanged. The remark came out sharper than she intended.

The man who told the pool story looked down at his plate.

“I wasn’t trying to hurt her.”

“I know.”

He nodded without satisfaction. The conversation did not recover its earlier ease until a late guest arrived with a problem at the northern crossing and everyone had something practical to argue about.

On the way home, Tavere stopped outside Orasha’s room. There was no light beneath the door. She did not knock.

At her door the next morning, she found a cup waiting beside her own work place, made as she liked it. Orasha had asked Seluve what she drank. Tavere held the cup for a long time before beginning her work.

6.3 The hanging ends

The abandoned span lay beyond the inhabited crossings, where the gorge narrowed and the river could be heard more clearly than it could be seen.

They went at first light. Tavere carried tools and fresh line. Orasha carried one end of a survey rod, a light burden that made her look underemployed after the anchor. The bridge-keeper walked ahead, stopping whenever he found something on the path that ought to have been moved by the last person who passed.

His name was Theren. He had three fingers on his right hand and a whistle hanging against his chest. The whistle was pale, made from a fang, and looked too small to produce the sound Tavere had once heard it make.

He had not used it at this crossing. He told them so before either asked.

“The old favour is unspent.”

“How much does it hold?” Orasha asked.

“The strands we still have. For a little while. It won’t give us back the ones that fell.”

They came around the last shoulder of rock and saw the bridge.

One side hung low, its walking surface twisted toward the water. The near binding remained sound. Across the gorge, the far anchor seat was empty above a torn fan of silk. Temporary lines, long since weathered, ran from the remaining strands to a ledge below. They had kept the bridge from dragging itself entirely free. They had not made it passable.

Tavere knew the story. Someone had broken an oath while crossing, and the rupture had passed through the structure before the keeper could release the load. People had fallen. Theren had caught a line around his hand and lost two fingers when it tightened.

Orasha stood beside him, looking at the empty seat.

“This is the place.”

“Yes,” he said.

Her hand moved to the inside of her forearm, where an old mark lay beneath the travelling sleeve. Tavere watched the movement and wondered whether it hurt.

Theren began the inspection without ceremony. He made Tavere name him as witness before she stepped onto the near remnant, then made Orasha do the same. The bridge accepted their weight with a faint tightening through the surviving side rail.

“Only to the third join,” he said.

They went only that far. Tavere knelt and examined the old fibres. Some had darkened but remained strong. Others had become brittle where rain collected beneath the wrapping. She marked each bad length for replacement. Orasha measured the pull from the empty far seat to the surviving approach.

They would not take the new anchor across the broken span. It would go by the lower path, carried to the far ledge and lifted into place with a frame built there. Then the new binding would be joined, the temporary lines released in order, and the walking surface drawn back into its proper curve.

It sounded manageable while stated on solid ground.

The lower path was steep. The far ledge was narrow. Rain had undercut a section where carriers would need to turn. Theren made them walk it before discussing anything else. Halfway down, Orasha stopped to examine a crack in the rock. Tavere had passed it without noticing.

“That won’t take the frame’s foot.”

“The frame goes higher,” Tavere said.

“Then the lift drags across the edge.”

They argued over the rod, moving it between proposed positions. Theren sat on a stone and ate from a small packet while they did it. When Tavere asked for his judgment, he pointed at the place Orasha had rejected and said he had been waiting for one of them to notice.

“You could have told us,” Tavere said.

“I could tell you everything. You’d still have to look.”

He went on eating.

Orasha looked at Tavere. It was a quick look, almost the old one. Tavere smiled before remembering that the shared annoyance belonged to this morning, not another life.

They found a position farther back, where the frame could stand on sound rock if the lift were guided by an extra line. It required more material and another pair of hands. Theren agreed to it and finally put away his food.

Above them the forest rim offered an apparently easy route to the far seat. A human assistant who joined them at midday asked whether they could carry the anchor through the trees instead. Theren pointed out the boundary marks. The upper forest did not belong to them. They would not take a heavy load into it because the people below found their own path inconvenient.

The assistant asked whether anyone would notice.

Theren looked at him until he stopped asking.

Theren’s shelter stood under an overhang on the near side. He took them there to find an older survey of the seat.

The shelter was smaller than Tavere expected. A sleeping place, a dry shelf for tools, a hearth set where its smoke would not stain the bridge fibres, and a ledge holding an untidy collection of cups. One cup had no handle. Another had been repaired so often that little of its original rim remained.

“Visitors,” Theren said when Orasha looked at them. “Everyone thinks I need a cup.”

“Do you?”

“I need people to take away the one they replace.”

He found the survey beneath a folded cover and spread it on a board. Tavere held one corner while Orasha read the dimensions. A smear across the lower edge obscured a note. Theren supplied the missing words without looking.

“You remember all of it?” Tavere asked.

“I wrote it.”

“That doesn’t answer.”

He shrugged with the shoulder on his injured side. “I’ve had time.”

A basket beside the hearth contained small carvings, animals and people made from scraps too poor for useful work. Some had three fingers because Theren had grown tired of cutting five. Tavere picked one up. It was a goat with an expression of permanent offence.

“For children?”

“For anyone who takes one.”

Orasha chose a little figure carrying an enormous basket. She turned it in her hands, then put it back when Theren said there was no charge. Tavere reached for her pouch, then left it shut. Orasha was watching the keeper.

“Bring me a piece of wood from the eastern road,” Theren said. “Something I don’t have here.”

Orasha considered. “If I find a carrier returning.”

“If you find one.”

She took the figure.

Outside, Tavere asked whether he had anyone sharing the shelter.

“Usually someone waiting for passage. Not now.”

“Family?”

He looked at her. “You came to inspect an anchor.”

She felt foolish and went back to the drawing.

On the way to the broken span, Orasha said quietly, “He answered me because I asked about the wood.”

“I know.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

They walked in silence for a while. Then Tavere asked what kind of wood Orasha hoped to find, and Orasha described a dark timber she had seen in a market without knowing its name. The conversation carried them down the path more easily.

They ate beside the broken span. Orasha took the harder piece of fruit when Tavere offered a choice, then gave her a little more from her own packet in return. Tavere accepted it without looking away.

“Do you remember choosing this work?” she asked.

Orasha chewed before answering. “I know that I chose it.”

“From the mark?”

“From more than the mark.”

She touched her forearm again. “When I turn away from it, I know where I should be going.”

“Even if you want to go somewhere else?”

“Yes.”

Tavere looked at the hanging bridge. She had wanted Orasha to come back so badly that she had never asked herself what it would feel like to be brought back by a purpose that survived the person who had chosen it.

Theren rose and brushed crumbs from his clothes.

“Finish eating. We still have the upper join.”

Orasha waited until he was out of hearing.

“Has he always eaten before everyone else?”

“Longer than I’ve been alive.”

“I remember nothing of him, and I believe that completely.”

Tavere laughed. This time the laugh did not hurt.

6.4 A seal at the crossing

The surveyors arrived with good instruments and a bad authorization.

Tavere knew the instruments were good before she knew anything about the paper. One of the visitors set a level on a stone, checked it against a second sight, and refused to use the mark his companion had chosen because it lay on a loose surface. She liked him for that before he introduced himself.

His name was Jaleku. He was Oshiiran, with greying hair tied away from his face and a case of tools kept in better order than his clothes. His companion, a smooth-faced man called Vesan, carried the authorization in a waterproof roll. Three assistants followed with measuring poles and a folding frame.

They had come to assess a waterworks route that could use the restored crossing. There would be employment, materials, and access to skills useful to the valley’s own terraces. Tavere had spent years trying to improve an upper terrace whose water arrived generously in the wrong season and grudgingly when wanted. Jaleku understood the problem as soon as she described it.

“Show me the fall,” he said.

Vesan wanted the crossing confirmed first.

Theren examined the seal. In daylight it was merely a carefully made impression, but it bore the forms expected of the office named. He asked which witnesses had renewed the authority. Vesan read their names from the attached sheet.

One name was familiar to Tavere. It belonged to an office rather than a person she had met. She found herself relieved by that slight familiarity, then annoyed when Orasha asked to see the whole sheet.

“It doesn’t enlarge your work,” Tavere told her.

“It proposes using the anchor before the span has been released from temporary support.”

“Only for the survey.”

“With their frame.”

Jaleku looked at the folded equipment and nodded. “She’s right about the load. We can carry the instruments separately.”

Vesan smiled as though the concern had now been answered.

Theren named the intended crossing to the bridge. He stood with one foot on the near planks while he did it. Tavere felt the answering tension change beneath her hand. The side rail drew away from Vesan when he approached.

He stopped.

“It doesn’t know us,” he said.

“It was told your authority,” Theren replied.

“Perhaps it needs the witness present.”

“Then we wait.”

Vesan looked toward Tavere. She could see the opportunity slipping into a delay, perhaps into departure. The terrace work would be harder without the surveyor. It would remain possible, but possible had been the word used for years whenever the work was postponed.

“We could inspect this side while the seal is checked,” she said.

Jaleku agreed readily. Vesan did not. He explained that his employer had allotted a short interval, that the assistants were paid by the day, and that a route delayed here might be moved elsewhere.

“Then inspect this side today,” Tavere said.

Orasha watched her. Tavere disliked being watched when she was trying to be reasonable.

Tavere had not always wanted the terrace enlarged. When she was younger, she had thought the upper plots too exposed, too difficult to reach, and too far from the conversations she did not want to miss. She had volunteered for other work whenever the watering was assigned.

Then one of the older growers lost the use of a knee. Tavere took her place for a season and learned where the ground held moisture after the surface dried. She learned which plants tolerated the gusts that came along the gorge and which needed low screens. She learned to enjoy being above the noise for an hour, even while complaining about the climb.

The enlarged channel had been her proposal. Each season there was another repair more urgent, another use for the same hands. Nobody had rejected it. That had made waiting harder, because she could not become properly angry with anyone.

Now Jaleku stood looking at the fall with interest, and she felt the old plan quicken before he said anything encouraging.

She took him to the cistern first. He asked her to show him the worst overflow, not the best flow. That pleased her. She had prepared explanations for a visitor who would mistake a morning’s neat channels for the whole year.

At the lower bed, Seluve joined them carrying a basket of cut stems. She knew where Tavere had been overoptimistic and corrected the account without apology. Jaleku changed a mark on his drawing. Tavere thanked Seluve through her teeth.

After a while Seluve went back to work. Orasha had stayed at the crossing to check the anchor slings. Tavere found herself relieved to speak about something that did not require her to remember what the other person no longer remembered.

Jaleku asked to see the channel running.

Tavere lifted the small gate at the cistern. Water entered the upper run, clear over the first stone, cloudy where the bed had been disturbed. She walked beside it, holding her skirt clear of the narrow turn. The assistant followed with the measuring pole.

At the third bed the flow divided. One part continued neatly along the cut Tavere had shown them. The other found a depression beneath a mat of roots and disappeared.

“Under here,” she said.

Jaleku knelt. “How far?”

“It comes out at the lower turn.”

“All of it?”

“No.”

He pushed a finger into the damp soil and looked toward the drop. Tavere had known he would ask to see that too. She led him down the awkward steps, slower than usual because he did not know where the foothold narrowed.

The lost water emerged in three places. Two fed useful ground. The third ran against an old retaining face whose lower stones were stained dark. Tavere pointed to the crack she inspected each morning.

“If we cut straight across above,” she said, “we can take it clear.”

“And these beds?”

“We bring a branch down.”

“From where?”

She showed him. He stood looking at the proposed line, then asked the assistant for the pole. They measured once. Jaleku frowned and measured again with Tavere holding the far end.

“It will reach this one,” he said. “Not the other.”

Tavere knew enough to see it as soon as he marked the fall. She had measured from the top of the retaining stone and forgotten the depth the new channel would require. She looked at the lower bed. The woman who tended it had already moved her work twice to make room for Tavere’s repairs.

“We could raise the intake.”

“Yes. Then you lose capacity at the cistern.”

“Only a little.”

He looked at her. She heard herself and bent to examine the ground again.

The assistant suggested a shorter cut through the root mat. Tavere refused before he had finished. It would damage the oldest tree above them. She took him up to see where the roots entered and let him follow them down with his own hand. He withdrew the suggestion and asked whether the channel could curve round the outer side instead.

They tried that on the drawing. It was less elegant. It would take longer to clear, and a section would need support above the drop. Jaleku put his tool case down and began measuring again.

Tavere went for water. When she returned, he had made a small shaded square on the sheet.

“What is that?”

“A place to stand while you clean it. Unless you prefer hanging from the tree.”

She laughed. She had scraped that stretch with one foot in a crack for three seasons. Nobody she had asked for help had thought the foothold mattered to the design.

They stayed on the terrace until the shadows reached the upper beds. Jaleku spoke to the woman below about her watering times. She corrected Tavere’s account of the dry interval, adding two days which Tavere had spent at another crossing and had not known about. The assistant carried the pole back to the cistern. Tavere remained by the proposed cleaning place, fitting her foot against the rock and imagining being able to stand with both hands free.

Vesan remained near the papers. He spoke to Theren twice, privately, and was refused twice.

At dusk they compared the seal beneath the light used for such work. Its public impression had been copied well. The witnessing beneath it had not. The mark held the shape of an earlier authority without the renewal claimed on the attached sheet.

Jaleku stared at it.

“That is the document I was given.”

Theren did not accuse him of making it. He asked where it had been issued, who had handed it over, and whether anyone could answer for the renewal.

Vesan said the matter was more complicated than a bridge-keeper’s examination could establish. He was correct about that in a limited sense. There would be questions elsewhere, and people with reasons to give different answers. Theren barred the crossing while those questions travelled.

Tavere stood beside the instruments she had admired. She had wanted the document to be good because the level was good, because Jaleku listened, because a new channel might save the terrace. She could still feel the desire making arguments in her mouth.

Orasha asked where the surveyors’ equipment could be stored safely until they left.

Vesan turned toward her. “You seem satisfied.”

“No. I have an anchor to set, and your frame is in the way.”

Jaleku laughed once, without pleasure, and went to move it.

6.5 The place prepared

The surveyors left two days later.

Verification came from a clerk at the lower trading house who knew the current witness sequence and had a recent genuine document to compare. Vesan’s paper used an office that no longer possessed the authority it claimed. Its renewal had been copied from another kind of permission. Whoever made it had understood the outward forms better than the limits of their use.

Theren sent an account downstream. He did not hold the assistants, confiscate their instruments, or convene a gathering to hear every grievance against Oshiiran waterworks. He marked the authorization as refused at his crossing and told Vesan where further questions would be asked.

Jaleku remained long enough to help remove a temporary support his crew had placed near the terrace. Without him, Tavere would have taken it down in the wrong order. He showed her why, waited while she checked the load, and helped carry the released timber to dry ground.

“You can still use the fall we measured,” he said.

“Without your frame?”

“With a smaller lift. More hands. Less ambition.”

She looked at him.

He smiled tiredly. “For the channel. I don’t know enough about the rest of your life to advise.”

They worked out a rough arrangement on the back of a damaged wrapper. It would not provide everything she had imagined. It might make one difficult season easier. He left her the drawing and refused payment because the work had already been included in the day’s survey.

Vesan waited uphill with the assistants. He had stopped attempting to persuade Theren and begun describing the departure as his own decision. Nobody argued with him.

After the surveyors left, Tavere took Orasha to the lower market to buy wrapping for the lifting poles.

She could have sent someone else. Her shoulder was sound then, her work awaited her, and the market would be crowded. She wanted to spend an hour beside Orasha without offering a remembered place as a test. Buying wrapping seemed a sufficiently plain errand.

At the first stall, she answered a question meant for Orasha.

The seller wanted to know which thickness would suit the hand that held the rear guide. Tavere named the one they had used years before. Orasha asked to feel another. The seller passed it to her, and Tavere stood silent while she chose.

The new hands needed a different wrapping. Tavere had seen their length and still assumed the old grip.

They walked through the market with the roll between them. Orasha stopped at a stand of small tools, examined two, and bought neither. Tavere noticed that she looked at the maker’s hands before asking a question. It was a habit she did not remember. When she asked about it, Orasha said people who used a tool often showed where it would hurt by the places they protected on their own fingers.

“Who taught you that?”

“I don’t know.”

This answer no longer stopped the conversation quite so completely. Tavere looked at her own hand and tried to remember who had first taught her to keep her thumb behind a cutting edge. She remembered the pain more clearly than the person.

They ate at a stall near the steps. Orasha chose a dish Tavere would not have offered her because the old Orasha had disliked the sourness. She ate it with evident pleasure. Tavere bought a portion too and found that her own tastes had not changed enough to make it pleasant.

“You needn’t finish it,” Orasha said.

“I paid for it.”

“The seller won’t be less paid if you stop.”

Tavere laughed and gave the rest to a child who had been watching with open hope. The child ate it beside the stall, catching the crumbs in a cupped hand.

A woman at the next table recognized Orasha’s travelling clasp. She named a workshop beyond the coast. Orasha knew it. For several minutes they spoke of a street, a woman who repaired fine things badly and ordinary things beautifully, a lodging house with an inconveniently low doorway. Tavere listened. The names meant nothing to her, but the conversation had an ease she had been trying to manufacture all week.

The woman asked whether Orasha was returning there.

“Perhaps later. I want to go east first.”

“Alone?”

“With whoever is taking the road.”

Tavere looked down at the wrapping on her knee. She had listened to Orasha asking about the eastern road without imagining her taking it.

After the woman went, Tavere asked about the clasp. Orasha took it off and showed her the back, where the maker’s mark had been worn smooth.

“Was it a gift?”

“I bought it.”

“Do you remember?”

“Yes. I liked the way it fastened. The man kept trying to sell me the pattern on the front.”

She demonstrated the catch. Tavere tried it herself. It was good work, stronger than its small size suggested.

On the way home, the roll of wrapping began to uncoil. They stopped on the steps to gather it, laughing as the lower end escaped Tavere’s foot and rolled down to a trader who caught it against his basket. Orasha thanked him a little too generously and offered to carry the basket to the next landing. He accepted at once.

Tavere walked beside her while she carried it. The basket was heavy. Orasha did not appear to regret the offer. She asked the trader where the fruit had been grown and listened to an answer that lasted all the way up.

At the landing she set it down. The man gave them each a piece of fruit before they could leave. Tavere saw Orasha hesitate at the fresh courtesy and stepped in to ask him whether he would be at the market next week. The conversation moved on before another return could be proposed.

When they were alone, Orasha said, “I saw what you did.”

“Was it unwelcome?”

“No. I don’t want another basket.”

They laughed again. Tavere carried the wrapping for the last part of the climb. She had learned more about the woman beside her in that hour than in two evenings of asking what she recalled.

That afternoon Tavere returned to find the room beside hers swept, aired, and fitted with a new shelf.

A woven screen stood against the wall. Someone had mended the window cover. On the sleeping mat lay a folded cloth in a colour Orasha used to like.

Tavere stood in the doorway.

The woman who had brought water at the landing came past with a basket. Her name was Seluve, and she had known Tavere long enough to read her expression before Tavere decided what to do with it.

“Too much?”

“No.”

“We had the screen. It needed a place.”

“Where is Orasha?”

“At the store. Checking the lifting slings.”

Tavere touched the new shelf. It had been made from an old board, fitted neatly between existing supports. Inside the web, a room could become ready without anyone asking who had supplied each part. The pleasure of it struck her now with an unwelcome force. She had wanted Orasha to see the room and understand something from it that nobody had said aloud.

“She may leave after the bridge,” Tavere said.

Seluve set down the basket.

“Has she said otherwise?”

“No.”

“Then why are we putting your old cloth here?”

Tavere looked at the folded colour on the mat.

“I don’t know.”

Seluve waited.

“I do know.”

Neither of them moved for a little while. Then Seluve took the cloth and handed it to her.

“The room can be ready. It doesn’t have to be a question.”

Orasha came home before the meal. She had found a worn section in one sling and replaced it. She showed Tavere the damaged fibres, then asked whether they had enough fresh line to double the guide at the far ledge.

They did. Tavere went to fetch it. When she returned, Orasha stood in the prepared room, looking at the shelf.

“Who made this?”

“Several of us.”

“It’s well fitted.”

Tavere placed the line on the floor.

“How long did you mean to stay?”

Orasha turned from the shelf. “Until the work is done and the crossing is safe to leave.”

“And then?”

“There is a road east. The trader described a place where the low water exposes old stone steps. I would like to see it before the water rises.”

“Why?”

Orasha considered. “Because I haven’t.”

The answer was so slight that Tavere nearly resented it. A decade away, an oath in the body, a return that had filled the house with preparations, and afterward there was a place she wished to see because she had not seen it.

“Will you work there?”

“If there is work I want.”

“People?”

“I expect there will be people.”

She smiled. Tavere did not.

Orasha’s smile faded. “Have I misunderstood something?”

“No.”

The word came too quickly. Tavere sat on the edge of the mat and looked at the empty place where the cloth had been.

“I hoped you’d stay.”

Orasha remained standing. She seemed to be choosing an answer with care, which Tavere both needed and hated.

“I don’t know this as home.”

“I know.”

“I can learn names. I can learn where things are. That would not—”

“Don’t finish it.”

Orasha stopped.

Tavere pressed her palms against the mat. The fibres were familiar under her skin. She had repaired this mat twice, once after a child’s lamp burned its edge and once because she had kept postponing a loose join until it failed beneath a visitor.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

Orasha sat beside her, leaving enough space that their clothes did not touch.

They stayed there until someone called them to eat.

At the meal, Tavere told the household that Orasha would travel east after the crossing. People asked about the route. Someone knew the stone steps and described where to find good water. Seluve asked whether the room’s shelf should be left at its current height for the next guest, and two people immediately disagreed about who might use it.

The discussion moved on. Tavere found that she was hungry.

Later, while the dishes were being cleaned, Orasha picked up a basin and began to carry it outside. Tavere took the other handle. They emptied it together without speaking.

6.6 Across the old span

The day before the lift, they carried the anchor down.

Tavere had helped move heavy things often enough to be ashamed of how early she became frightened. The anchor looked manageable on its supports. Once suspended between the carriers, it seemed to possess intentions of its own. A small change in one person’s footing travelled through the poles and made everyone else adjust.

Orasha walked behind it holding the rear guide rope. Tavere went ahead to clear the narrow turns. They had rehearsed the calls on level ground. The first bend made the rehearsal feel absurdly comfortable.

“Stop.”

They stopped. One carrier’s heel had reached the edge of the path. He could not see it beneath the wrapped metal. Tavere put her foot beside his and told him where the firm ground began. He shifted a little, waited while the others took the change, and shifted again.

They rested at the first broad place. Nobody wanted to admit how much of the remaining path still lay below. Theren had gone ahead to inspect the far approach. He returned with mud on one knee and told them they would need to lay boards across a wash.

The boards were above them, stacked beside the frame. Two carriers went back while the others remained with the anchor. Tavere wanted to go as well and was told there were already enough hands for the boards. She stayed, useless for the moment, listening to the water.

Orasha sat on a rock with her palms turned upward. The guide had left red lines across them.

“You can use gloves,” Tavere said.

“I can’t feel it as well.”

“You’ll feel it later.”

Orasha smiled. “You can say that without sounding pleased.”

Tavere sat beside her. Below them the river moved past a red bar of stone. Something pale drifted around it and vanished into a faster channel. The air smelled of wet mineral and crushed leaves.

“Did you bring it all that way like this?”

“Not all. Carts where there were roads. A boat for part. The last carriers were very glad to hand it over.”

“Were you alone?”

“For some of it.”

Tavere waited. Orasha seemed to be choosing how much could be told without converting another decade into a list of things she could not remember.

“I had a companion after the boat. A woman taking tools to her brother. She hated waiting. We quarrelled about it every morning and ate together every night.”

“What became of her?”

“She reached her brother. I continued.”

“Do you miss her?”

“Yes.”

The answer startled Tavere with its simplicity. She had spent so much time trying to find the missing attachment that she had scarcely allowed room for the ones Orasha had made since losing it.

“Will you see her again?”

“I hope so. Her brother’s house is on a road I may use.”

Tavere looked toward the river. She could have asked the woman’s name. She did not, then felt ashamed and asked anyway. Orasha told her. The name meant nothing to Tavere and belonged, inconveniently, to someone real.

The carriers returned with the boards. They tested the crossing empty, then took the anchor over. At the far end the rear pole struck the rock. One man swore, another tried to compensate too quickly, and the load swung toward the outside.

Tavere raised two fingers in the old sign before she could call. Orasha eased the guide at the same moment, following the visible movement of the pole. The anchor settled.

For a heartbeat Tavere thought she had remembered.

“Good,” she said, too warmly.

Orasha looked at her, puzzled, then checked the rear wrapping. Tavere said nothing more. She wanted to keep the small event intact until she could examine it alone.

They reached the far ledge in the late afternoon. The anchor was set on timber rests below the empty seat. Every person examined the supports before letting go. Afterward they drank, stretched aching hands, and ate the food Seluve had packed. A carrier found a crushed fruit at the bottom of his bundle and accused the anchor of taking the better half of everything.

Orasha laughed. Tavere was so tired that she laughed long after the remark deserved it.

They returned uphill without the load. At the narrow turn, Tavere looked back and was astonished that they had brought it through. She wanted to tell someone who had not been there, to make them understand the small spaces, the changes of feet, the instant when the pole struck. Instead she caught up with Orasha and walked beside her for the rest of the climb.

That evening she almost asked about the sign. Orasha was eating with both hands wrapped around her bowl, visibly weary. Tavere decided it could wait. The possibility went with her to bed and remained there in the dark.

The rain began the next morning, before they raised the anchor.

It was not yet the heavy rain. It came in narrow veils across the gorge, vanishing on one side while it gathered on the other. The rock darkened. The silk lines acquired a sheen. Theren looked up at the rim and called for the cover to be brought over the lifting frame.

The timber rests stood where they had left them the previous afternoon. Tavere checked each one before the lift began. The rear guide had been rewound; Orasha ran it through her hands, pausing at the rubbed place where it had caught against the rock.

Now the anchor hung below the empty seat. The frame stood on sound rock farther back, as they had planned. A guide line held the metal clear of the lip. Tavere was on the far ledge beside Orasha. Theren and two workers waited across the gorge with the surviving bridge strands ready to be drawn into place.

The task was simple enough to see: raise the anchor another arm’s length, turn its shoulder into the cut seat, secure it, then bring the binding over. The difficulty lay in doing each part while the gorge offered a changing opinion of their arrangements.

Rain ran beneath Tavere’s collar. She wiped her hands on a dry patch of cloth and checked the guide knot.

Orasha watched the anchor.

“Another hand’s width.”

The men at the lifting frame turned the drum. The anchor rose.

“Stop.”

They stopped. Orasha leaned out with the positioning pole and moved the metal a fraction toward the seat. Tavere kept the guide taut. The pole slid along the wet surface, caught in the channel, and held.

Across the gorge, someone shouted.

Tavere looked up. A temporary line had begun to fray where it passed over an old edge. They had inspected it. They had put a protective sleeve around the rough place. The sleeve had shifted under the changed pull as the new anchor rose.

Theren called for the load to be held.

Tavere signalled to Orasha: two fingers turned inward, the old sign they had used as children when moving something too heavy between them. It meant take the near side, let mine go slack, trust that I had seen the place to put it.

Orasha stared at her hand.

The lost moment stretched. Tavere saw her own fingers as Orasha saw them, a gesture without a shared use.

“Hold your pole,” she said aloud. “I’m easing this line. Don’t follow it.”

Orasha held.

Tavere let the guide move slowly through her hands. The anchor turned just enough to relieve the old temporary line across the gorge. Theren’s workers reached it, pulled the sleeve back into place, and secured it. One raised a hand.

Tavere drew breath.

Orasha looked at her. “Say it next time.”

“Yes.”

They resumed the lift.

The anchor entered its seat badly. Its lower edge caught on a ridge left by the broken predecessor. Tavere had thought the ridge too shallow to matter. Under the hanging load it mattered immediately. The metal would not settle, and the frame could not hold it indefinitely while they discussed why.

They lowered it a little. Orasha took the chisel. Tavere held the light beneath the cover while she worked at the ridge, each stroke small because a large one might send a crack into the seat itself.

The rain thickened. One carrier asked whether they should abandon the lift until morning. Theren shouted from the other shore that the old temporary support was taking more water than he liked. If they lowered the anchor fully, they could retreat by the lower path, but they would leave the bridge hanging through the storm without the new binding. It might remain. It might not.

Orasha stopped chiselling and looked at Tavere.

Tavere wanted to say they had come too far to stop. She heard how foolish it would sound. Distance already travelled did not improve the rock.

“Can you dress it without going deeper?” she asked.

“Yes. I need time.”

She asked the frame workers how long they could hold. They examined the drum, the wedges, the ground beneath their feet. One said less than an hour. The other would not promise that much if the water continued rising along the lower path.

Tavere told Theren.

He touched the whistle at his chest and looked at the bridge.

They did not use it yet.

Orasha cut away the ridge. Tavere shielded the work as well as she could. Metal chips collected in the wet cloth below the seat. Her wrist cramped from holding the lamp, and she changed hands without moving the light. Orasha noticed and paused long enough for her to settle.

When the ridge was dressed, they raised the anchor again.

This time it seated. Tavere felt the contact through the guide line before she saw the shoulder settle against stone. Orasha drove the retaining pieces in sequence. The frame took less of the weight. The anchor stayed.

They began drawing the bridge binding across.

Halfway through, the old support on the near side failed.

It did not break with a clean snap. One strand went, then another, a rapid soft tearing almost lost beneath the rain. The hanging walking surface dropped several feet. A worker on the near side fell to his knees and was caught by the safety line at his waist. Theren hauled him back from the edge with his maimed hand wrapped in cloth to keep the line from cutting deeper.

The falling span pulled the new binding out of Tavere’s reach.

Orasha seized the guide before it whipped past. The pull dragged her toward the edge. Tavere caught her belt and planted a foot against the frame support. For a moment both of them were held by the belt, the support, and the grip Orasha had refused to release.

“Let it go,” Tavere said.

“The guide—”

“Let it go.”

Orasha opened her hands.

The line vanished over the lip. Tavere fell backward with her, striking her shoulder against the rock. The lamp broke. Darkness came close under the cover, though it was still daylight beyond it.

Across the gorge, Theren lifted the whistle.

Its sound was higher than Tavere remembered, a thin note that seemed to enter her teeth. The bridge answered. Surviving strands drew tight. The fallen walking surface rose partway, not to its old place but enough to stop dragging the far end toward the water. The new binding hung within reach of the positioning pole.

Theren lowered the whistle. A pale crack ran along its side.

“Now,” he called.

Orasha rose first. Blood ran from her palms. She wrapped them in cloth, took the pole, and caught the loop in the binding. Tavere helped draw it toward the anchor. Neither could have held it alone.

They brought the loop into the channel, set the first retaining turn, then the second. The bridge’s borrowed tension gave them a short interval in which the fibres could be arranged without the full pull tearing them away. Tavere worked by touch where the rain obscured sight. Orasha repeated each completed step aloud.

“First turn seated.”

“Second clear.”

“Draw the lower strand.”

The words made a narrow place in the storm where Tavere could keep working.

When the binding held, they eased the lifting frame and watched the anchor take the load. Its dark shoulder pressed into the dressed seat. No chip fell. No new line appeared in the rock.

The whistle’s tension began to fade. The bridge settled onto its repaired support with a long shudder.

They waited.

It held.

Tavere found that she was still gripping the positioning pole. Orasha put a hand over hers, not to offer comfort but to help pry her fingers loose.

The lower path was no longer safe. Water had taken part of the narrow turn where they had rested the anchor the day before. They would have to use the repaired span to leave the ledge.

Theren named the crossing, the witnesses, and the limited weight. No frame, no unused tools, one person at a time. The equipment could wait. The bridge had held an anchor and its binding; it had not yet been tested under walking feet.

The first worker crossed slowly. Tavere watched each step. The second followed. Then Orasha.

Tavere went last. Beneath her, the planks were wet and several remained twisted from the fall. She kept a hand on the side rail and did not look down until a gust turned her face toward the river. Red water moved far below, carrying pale branches that seemed too small to have belonged to trees.

Halfway across, her injured shoulder began to shake. She stopped, breathed, and continued.

At the near landing Orasha held out her uninjured forearm. Tavere took it and stepped onto stone.

Theren was looking at the cracked whistle. He wrapped it carefully and put it inside his clothes.

“Tomorrow,” he said, “we inspect everything.”

Nobody disagreed.

They did not reach the house until well after dark.

People met them on the road with lamps and dry covers. Tavere had been cold for so long that the first warm cloth against her neck made her shiver harder. She tried to answer three questions at once and found she could remember only the first.

Seluve took her tools. Someone else took Orasha’s. They were led inside, seated, given dry clothes, and told to stop explaining until they had eaten something.

Orasha’s hands had to be cleaned before she could hold a cup. Tavere sat beside her while a woman named Eshave unwound the wet cloths. The fibres had caught in the shallow cuts. Orasha watched without speaking. Only once, when a folded edge pulled free, did her jaw tighten.

Tavere reached toward her, then stopped before touching the injured hand. Orasha saw and offered her forearm instead. Tavere held it while Eshave worked.

“Were you always this quiet when hurt?” Eshave asked.

“I don’t know.”

The woman paused. “Of course. Forgive me.”

“You can ask. I simply can’t answer.”

Eshave finished one hand and began the other. Tavere looked at the long fingers, the split thumbnail, the new cuts where the guide had dragged. She could distinguish injuries made before she knew this woman from the ones she had been present for today. The distinction gave her no comfort, but it was clear.

Her own shoulder stiffened while she sat. When Eshave asked her to lift the arm, she discovered how little movement remained without pain. She made a joke about keeping the elbow low for a few days. Eshave did not laugh and asked what she had struck.

Tavere described the rock. She was made to undress enough for the bruise to be examined, which felt more intimate and more embarrassing than the embrace on the ledge. Orasha began to leave. Tavere asked her to stay, then wondered why she had asked.

Orasha stayed near the doorway, speaking to a child who wanted to know whether the bridge had screamed. Tavere could hear the careful answer: the fibres had made a sound when they tightened; people had shouted; the whistle had sounded; none of those was quite what the child imagined.

The child appeared disappointed. Orasha said the actual noise had been frightening enough. That satisfied him.

After the household quieted, Tavere lay awake. Each time she began to sleep, the guide line moved again through Orasha’s hands. She saw the edge, the belt, the wet stone under her own foot. She could not stop adjusting the sequence, trying to put them somewhere safer before it happened.

A light showed beneath Orasha’s door.

Tavere got up. She stood outside for some time before knocking.

Orasha was sitting on the mat, her wrapped hands in her lap. The little carving of the basket carrier stood beside the lamp.
