“Can’t sleep?” Tavere asked.

“No.”

“Your hands?”

“Some of it.”

Tavere sat against the wall, careful of her shoulder. They listened to the night air move through the window cover.

“Why did you hold it?” she asked.

“The line?”

“Yes.”

“It was going.”

“You were going with it.”

“I know that now.”

Tavere looked at her. She had been about to ask whether the oath had compelled it, whether the task in the body had overruled care for the body itself. Orasha’s answer was smaller and more familiar. Tavere had held foolishly too, in the instant before thought caught up.

“I should have told you to drop it sooner.”

“You told me. I dropped it.”

They fell quiet. The lamp hissed once. Orasha adjusted the wick with an awkward motion of her bandaged fingers, and Tavere took the tool gently from her.

“May I?”

“Yes.”

She trimmed the wick and gave it back. Orasha watched the steadier flame.

After a while she asked, “Did I like the dark?”

Tavere thought before answering. “Sometimes. You disliked being unable to see a room you’d just entered.”

“I still do.”

Tavere smiled. She did not say that this meant anything. They sat until the lamp burned lower, and when she returned to her own room she slept for several hours without waking.

6.7 The undertaking completed

The inspection took two days.

Tavere had expected relief to make the work easy. Instead every small defect frightened her. A shifted sleeve, a worn plank, a line that had taken more strain than planned: each suggested the thing that might have gone wrong while she stood above the river. Theren made her distinguish the urgent repairs from the ordinary ones and refused to let her replace sound material simply because it had frightened her.

Orasha’s hands healed beneath clean wrappings. She could not yet grip hard enough to do the heaviest work, which irritated her. She carried messages, measured distances, and watched Tavere climb down to retrieve the broken lamp.

“You can leave it,” she called.

“The metal can be used.”

“The river is not taking it this morning.”

Tavere looked up. Orasha was smiling, and this time she could answer the smile without searching it for an earlier one.

The first evening after the repair, Tavere woke at the sound of something falling in the court.

She was on her feet before she knew what she had heard. Her shoulder answered the movement with a pain sharp enough to turn her sideways. Outside, Seluve was gathering pieces of a broken jar while the child who had dropped it explained that the shelf had moved.

“The shelf is fixed.”

“It moved when I reached.”

“Then it will apologize when you have swept.”

Tavere stood in the doorway until Seluve looked up.

“Go back to bed.”

“I thought—”

“I know. Go.”

She could not. She went down to the water place and sat on the lowest step. The jar had made a perfectly ordinary noise. She had heard the frame giving way beneath it, the line striking stone, Theren’s shout. Her own hands were trembling now that there was nothing for them to do.

Orasha came out carrying a cup between both palms. She sat one step higher, leaving space.

“Is that for me?” Tavere asked.

“For drinking. I didn’t know whether you would want some.”

Tavere took it. The water was cool. She drank too quickly and coughed, making her shoulder hurt again.

“Don’t tell me to be careful.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“Everyone has.”

“They’ve seen you move.”

Tavere laughed unwillingly. Orasha took the cup back. Her wrappings were clean, but a small damp patch had appeared where one cut lay beneath them. Tavere looked at it, then at the court.

“Do you want that changed?”

“In a little while.”

They sat. Someone above began closing the window covers. A child protested that it was too early and received no answer. From another room came the sound of a tool being sharpened, an unhurried scraping with a pause between each stroke.

Tavere tried to follow the sound. On the fifth stroke she found herself back on the bridge. She pressed her feet against the step.

“I keep going over it.”

“The crossing?”

“The part where I should have called sooner.”

Orasha looked down into the cup. “I keep feeling the rope after I let go.”

Tavere turned toward her. Orasha opened her fingers as far as the wrappings allowed.

“It’s worse when I’m nearly asleep.”

They stayed until the sharpening stopped. Tavere had no advice. She had spent the day telling people which lines to leave alone and where to stand; now she was glad that Orasha had asked for nothing she could organize.

When they went inside, Orasha let her change the damp wrapping. Tavere worked at the knot with her good hand. The other woman held the cloth steady and moved when asked. Once the binding caught on a dried place and Orasha hissed through her teeth.

“Sorry.”

“Wet it.”

She did. They waited together until it loosened. Afterward Orasha helped her settle the support under her own sore arm. It took two attempts to find a position in which Tavere could lie down without pulling at the shoulder.

Seluve brought the lamp lower. Nobody began the story of the crossing again. When a cup knocked against a bowl in the next room, Tavere opened her eyes, saw the light on the wall and closed them.

On the second day, the worker who had fallen on the near side came to inspect the safety line that caught him.

He was called Relan. Tavere had known him through work for years and had never heard him speak so much at once. He described the fall to Theren, then to her, then to Orasha when she arrived with the measurements. Each account began at a different moment. In one, he had seen the support fail. In another, he had felt the planks move before hearing anything. He wanted to know whether his foot had caught the line or the line had caught him.

Theren showed him the worn place in the binding. Relan touched it, nodded, and began the account again.

Tavere was impatient until she saw his hand shake.

She took him along the near rail to check the joins. It was ordinary work, requiring attention without much strength. At the third join he stopped talking. At the fourth he asked whether the line should be replaced entirely.

“That section, certainly.”

“And the rest?”

“We inspect it.”

He did so with such care that she let him finish without hurrying him. When they reached the end, he asked to keep the worn piece. Theren cut it free after the new one was fitted and gave it to him without a speech.

At the far ledge, Orasha and Tavere counted the tools abandoned during the crossing. One chisel had fallen into a crack. They tried to retrieve it with a hook, failed, and left it where it lay. Orasha seemed more troubled by the chisel than Tavere expected.

“Yours?”

“It came with the anchor. The maker asked me to return it if I passed that way again.”

“Can another be sent?”

“Yes.”

They looked into the crack once more before leaving. Tavere knew the human impulse to keep trying because the thing was almost visible. She had no idea whether Orasha’s impulse came from the same place. She did not ask. She held the light while Orasha made one final attempt, and when it failed they both went back to the work above.

On the third morning they tested the crossing with a modest load. Then with the load it had been restored to carry. The bridge settled evenly. The new anchor remained quiet in its seat. Theren walked the span himself and stopped at the far end for so long that Tavere wondered whether he had found something.

When he returned, he said it was ready.

They witnessed the completion in the afternoon. The households who used the crossing sent people. The carriers who had brought the anchor down the lower path came. Seluve brought food because Theren would otherwise have eaten before everyone and pretended there had been no opportunity to arrange it differently.

Orasha stood beside the near binding and uncovered the old mark on her arm.

Tavere had seen it only briefly before. In the right light its shape was plain, a record of the undertaking that had drawn Orasha away and eventually brought her back. Theren named the anchor delivered and set. He named the span restored within the promised scope. The witnesses answered.

Orasha’s shoulders lowered.

It was a small movement. Tavere would have missed it if she had looked at the mark instead of the woman. Orasha turned her arm over, flexed her fingers, and looked toward the road beyond the landing.

Later, while people ate, Tavere asked whether it felt different.

“Yes.”

“How?”

“I can leave without feeling the direction beneath everything else.”

Tavere put down her cup.

Orasha saw the movement. “I don’t mean I am glad to leave you.”

“I know.”

She did know, which helped less than she had hoped knowledge would.

They walked home after the meal with Seluve and two children who had decided the repaired bridge belonged personally to them. The children ran ahead, returned, and ran ahead again. Orasha answered their questions about the anchor until one asked whether she would come back if they broke it.

“Don’t test that,” Tavere said.

The child looked disappointed at the loss of a useful experiment.

Seluve stopped Tavere before she went to her room.

“The terrace,” she said. “We need your answer.”

Tavere had forgotten the household was deciding how to divide its work for the next season. The bridge had occupied every conversation she attended, and Orasha every quiet interval between them. Other people had continued making plans.

They sat with the drawing Jaleku had left. The smaller channel could be cut with the hands available, but only if Tavere taught two others to maintain the upper beds rather than keeping the whole practice in her own head. She had objected to that description when it was first used. Now she looked at the drawing and saw how many marks she had made without explanation.

“I can show them.”

“Before the work begins.”

“Yes.”

“One of them wants to use the lower plot for something else.”

Tavere looked up sharply.

Seluve smiled. “There she is.”

They argued for a while. The new grower wanted a plant Tavere considered too thirsty for the exposed ground. Seluve said the woman knew that and had proposed a shaded trial bed. Tavere wanted to see the proposal herself. Seluve told her where it was and declined to repeat an argument on behalf of someone quite capable of making it.

The exchange left Tavere irritated in a familiar, manageable way. She went to the woman, listened, and discovered that the shaded bed would use a part of the terrace she had been leaving fallow because it was difficult to reach. They arranged to inspect it together after the bridge tools were put away.

On the way back, Tavere passed the room being prepared for the next visitors after Orasha. Seluve had already moved one shelf lower. A child stood inside explaining that the window cover needed a patch. Nobody had waited for Tavere to become ready for the room to change.

She went to fetch the patching material. When she returned, Orasha was holding the cover while the child marked the worn places. Tavere gave them what they needed and stayed to cut the pieces. The three of them finished before the evening meal.

At home, Tavere offered supplies for the eastern road. She had made a list: food that kept, a dry cover, spare thread, salve for Orasha’s hands, the name of a household at the next crossing. She began explaining how much she could provide, then heard herself adding that Orasha could stay until a better travelling party came through, until her hands were completely healed, until the weather settled.

Orasha listened to the first two conditions. At the third, she touched the edge of the list.

“I can travel in four days.”

“The road may—”

“I’ll ask at the lower landing.”

Tavere stopped.

Orasha offered to carry a parcel to the household at the next crossing, an ordinary service in return for the useful part of what Tavere had offered. The balance would be slightly generous; Tavere could hear it in the extra care she promised. She accepted anyway. Refusing every return would leave them with no easy way to give one another anything.

During the four days, Orasha found a travelling party.

Tavere learned this from the trader who brought the honey. He mentioned it while discussing the price of a replacement cover, assuming the household already knew. Tavere felt a moment’s unreasonable hurt. She had told Orasha to ask at the landing. Orasha had done exactly that.

She went down later to look at the party’s loads. There were three human traders, two carriers, and a woman travelling to join her sister. None seemed unusually reckless. One of the carriers had a foot sore enough to make Tavere wonder about their pace. The woman joining her sister asked sensible questions about water and refused a proposal to take an unfamiliar shortcut.

Orasha stood among them, listening. She had already learned their names. When one man joked about the valley people charging for every courtesy, she answered lightly enough to let him retreat from the remark without pretending she had not heard it.

Tavere watched the ease of it. Away from the household that remembered her, Orasha did not have to arrive as a loss.

The woman travelling to her sister asked Tavere whether she was joining them.

“No.”

“Only seeing her off?”

“Yes.”

The woman nodded and returned to checking her bundle. The exchange was ordinary. Tavere found it strangely freeing to be assigned so small a part in someone else’s journey.

On the way home Orasha asked whether she disliked the travellers.

“I came to see whether I should.”

“And?”

“I don’t.”

Orasha smiled. “That must have been disappointing.”

“A little.”

They climbed the steps slowly. Tavere’s shoulder still hurt. Orasha carried nothing with her injured hands, though she kept forgetting and reaching for small burdens before stopping herself.

At the upper landing they paused to watch a group of children practising the naming of witnesses for a crossing. The youngest kept naming himself because he preferred not to ask anyone else. The older children objected with the exaggerated authority of people only recently taught the rule.

Orasha laughed.

“You did that,” Tavere said.

“Did I?”

“Yes. You said you were paying attention.”

“What did you say?”

“That I was watching too.”

Orasha looked toward the children. “Then I was fortunate.”

Tavere heard the smooth generosity in the answer. She also heard its present kindness. For once she did not try to separate them before accepting it.

Tavere found the paper bird while clearing her work basket.

It had been crushed beneath a coil of line. One wing was folded the wrong way and the point softened from damp. She sat beside the basket and tried to restore it. The paper remembered the wrong fold more readily than the right one. She pressed it flat, began again, and became irrationally angry with the little object.

Orasha came past carrying the repaired cover.

“That one always turned,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Loosen the wing.”

“I know.”

Orasha stopped. Tavere put down the bird.

“I’m sorry. I do know. I can’t make it do what it did before.”

“May I?”

Tavere handed it over. Orasha worked the fold open with her bandaged fingers, then gave it back before throwing it. Tavere tried. The bird travelled a short distance and landed against the frame.

“Better,” Orasha said.

Tavere sat down again. For a while the two women watched the bird where it lay.

“I thought you remembered at the turn,” Tavere said.

“What turn?”

“With the anchor. I made a sign.”

Orasha thought back. “I saw the pole move.”

“Yes. I know that now.”

“I didn’t know you were asking me something.”

“I wasn’t. Not then.”

Tavere rubbed the side of her basket with one thumb. The repair Orasha had made was smoother than the old binding.

“I kept waiting for something to reach you. A place, or a thing I said. I knew it wasn’t how shedding worked. I kept waiting anyway.”

Orasha leaned the folded cover against the wall. She did not offer Tavere a consoling account of what might still be hidden somewhere.

“I have been afraid of disappointing you every time I answer,” she said.

Tavere looked up.

“The answers are easy until I see your face.”

“I’ve made you watch me.”

“Yes.”

A child ran through the court after a fallen spool. Neither woman moved until the child was gone.

Tavere set the basket down.

“Tomorrow,” she said, “tell me what you actually want for the road. I’ll stop packing for the person I remember.”

Orasha nodded. After a while she picked up the paper bird and set it on the shelf above the work frame, where nobody would crush it with useful things.

That evening Tavere went to Seluve and asked her not to bring out the old songs again before the departure. Seluve said she had already told the returning workers there would be no second welcome meal.

“I should have done it,” Tavere said.

“You were busy wanting one.”

Tavere smiled without pleasure. Seluve gave her a basket of work to take upstairs, and the ordinary instruction let her leave without being forgiven for everything at once.

For the last meal together, they went to a small terrace where traders cooked for people passing between crossings. Tavere had gone there with Orasha before their oaths. She chose it now for the food and wondered whether that was wholly true.

The cook was new. The old shelter had been replaced. The dish Tavere remembered was no longer made, but there was fish, hot bread, and a sour relish that Orasha liked enough to ask about.

They ate while rain moved across the far side of the gorge without reaching them. Orasha described a town where she had worked before her latest shedding. She did not remember the work itself. She had found notes in her own hand and a well-made tool in her pack, and had gone to ask people who did remember.

“Were they kind?” Tavere asked.

“Some. One woman was very angry. I had apparently promised to mend her roof before leaving.”

“Did you?”

“I mended it.”

“Was that what she wanted?”

“By the time I left, she wanted me to stop improving it. I had made the drain run onto her neighbour’s steps.”

Tavere laughed. Orasha’s own laughter followed, low and pleased. For a moment the old voice and the present voice met without demanding anything of Tavere.

The last day before packing, Tavere took Orasha to the repaired crossing without tools.

Theren was sitting beneath his shelter, working on another small carving. He looked at their empty hands and asked whether they had forgotten the reason for coming.

“We came to walk over it,” Tavere said.

“People usually do that on the way somewhere.”

“We are going to the far side.”

He nodded as though the destination met a minimum requirement. They named the witnesses and stepped onto the span.

It moved beneath them. The repaired curve was slightly different from the one Tavere remembered, less abrupt near the far end. Rainwater still dripped from the lower strands. A bird had begun building beneath one of the sheltered joins, and Theren had already complained about what the droppings would do to the binding.

Halfway across, Orasha stopped.

Tavere waited beside her. The river was lower than during the repair. She could see the ledge where they had stood, the empty frame above it, the line they had failed to retrieve caught against a rock below. In dry light the danger looked manageable in a way that offended her memory.

Orasha rested a wrapped hand on the rail.

“Does it feel different to you?” Tavere asked.

“Yes.”

“The crossing?”

“Being here without needing to do something before I can go.”

Tavere looked toward the far landing. She could ask her to remain a week. The repairs had tired them; there were places they had not visited; one pleasant afternoon could become another. She knew how to make the invitation sound harmless.

Instead she asked whether Orasha wanted to continue across.

“Yes.”

They went to the far side and sat on a low stone. Orasha unwrapped one hand to let the air reach it. The cuts had begun closing. Tavere watched her flex the fingers, then looked away before attention became inspection.

“You were frightened,” Orasha said.

“Yes.”

“When we were lifting. Before anything went wrong.”

“I kept seeing where people could fall.”

“I was glad you were there.”

Tavere accepted the words with difficulty. She wanted to put them beside older words, to make them prove a continuity. She let them remain where they had been said.

They sat until Theren called across to ask whether they meant to use the far side as a new household. Orasha called back that he had approved the destination. The keeper said nothing to that, and Tavere laughed all the way to the near landing.

Before they left, Theren gave them the small carving he had been working on. It was a bridge with one side much too low.

“For the room,” he said, handing it to Tavere. “So you remember to inspect the next one before you bring the anchor.”

She protested that they had inspected it. He began listing the things they had discovered afterward. Orasha excused them before the list could become the whole afternoon.

At home Tavere put the crooked bridge beside the older figure. She looked at the two pieces for a while. Then she moved the bridge to the common shelf where the children could handle it. Within minutes one was driving a carved animal over it and objecting to the slope.

“Complain to the maker,” Tavere said.

“I don’t like him.”

“He’ll want to know why.”

The child considered the work involved in that conversation and drove the animal over again.

On the last evening she packed the supplies.

The small carving lay beside them. She had brought it from the shelf without deciding to include it. It would fit easily between the folded cover and the food packet. Orasha might find it later. She might carry it, examine it, ask someone what kind of wood it was. Tavere imagined the object travelling where she would not and felt the temptation grow.

She took it out.

The parcel for the next household needed a better tie. She cut one, wrapped it firmly, and put the carving back on its shelf before finishing the pack.

6.8 The meal already begun

Orasha left after the morning rain.

The trading party had delayed while the lower steps dried. Tavere was grateful for the delay, then uncomfortable with her gratitude. Orasha used the extra hour to mend a torn edge on her own cover. Her bandaged hands moved more slowly than before. Tavere sat beside her winding spare line.

They had already said most of what could usefully be said. Conversation returned to the road, the supplies, the parcel, the household expecting it. Orasha asked whether a message could be left for Tavere at the lower trading house.

“Yes.”

“If I find someone coming back, I may send word.”

“I’d like that.”

Neither made it an undertaking.

When the call came from below, Tavere carried one of the packs to the landing. Orasha carried the other. At the landing Orasha turned toward the upper path. Tavere stopped beside her. They stood looking back at the house until the bundle counter called them down.

The carriers were different. A woman counted bundles while a boy tried to persuade her that a basket of fragile jars would be safest beneath his blanket. Someone had misplaced a walking stick. The ordinary confusion gave Tavere several unnecessary tasks she was glad to perform.

At last there was nothing left to adjust.

Orasha put down her pack and embraced her.

Tavere had wondered whether the embrace would be another fraction too generous. Once it happened, she could not measure it. She felt the travelling cloth beneath her hands, the warmth gathered in it, the narrow bones of a shoulder she had never held in quite this shape before.

Orasha stepped back.

“Thank you for the work.”

Tavere nodded. She could not yet trust her voice.

The party began moving. Orasha went with them, stopping once to help the boy settle the basket of jars where it would actually be safe. At the turn she looked back and raised a hand. Tavere raised hers.

Then the path took them out of sight.

Tavere remained until a carrier asked her to move because she was standing where his load needed to go. She apologized and went uphill.

The household had already begun the work of replacing the last walkway rail. Someone had brought the new cord from storage. Children were carrying short pieces of discarded fibre to a pile they had been told not to disturb and were therefore inspecting with great care. Seluve sat at the frame, speaking to a neighbour about the terrace channel Jaleku had drawn.

Tavere put down the empty carrying strap.

“Where?” she asked.

Seluve moved over.

She sat and took the loose end. The first join required two people to keep the tension even. Tavere held while Seluve worked, then they changed places. Their hands knew when the other needed slack. For a while Tavere found that familiarity almost unbearable. She kept working until it became simply useful again.

At midday the food was brought out. A jar of Sekogo honey stood among the dishes, its lid sticky from someone else’s impatient opening. One of the children asked who had put a thumbprint in it. Three adults denied responsibility before anyone accused them.

Tavere ate slowly. She kept listening for a step she would not hear. When the conversation moved toward the eastern road, Seluve let it move, neither steering it away nor making Tavere answer.

Later they returned to the rail. The new cord held a curve too high for the end post. Tavere loosened her side while Seluve shifted the join.

“More?” Seluve asked.

“A little.”

They drew it into place.
